INTRODUCTION

Before any man in this tale ever set eyes on a Spanish treasure-train, before oars struck time on a moonlit swell, before the Queen’s command or the Armada’s thunder rolled across the Western seas, there was—like the first smell of bread before it shows brown—a promise, quiet and domestic, sitting warm inside a list of names.

A book, like a great household, has its rooms. One opens first upon a long passage, and along it hang the labels of chambers yet unvisited: doors with salt-stained thresholds, doors that open on old courts and wind-worried combes, on Christmas boards laid with holly, on bridges that somehow contrive to dine like gentlemen, and on ships that go and come again as steadily as a tide and as strangely as a dream. Each title is a hook hung in the larder-beam: not meat itself, but the place where meat will be kept; not the song, but the throat that will sing it.

Listen, if you will, to how those doors sound even before they swing. There is the soft brush of a white bird’s wing—so slight a stirring that a man must learn to hear it beneath the common noises of a port town. There is the thicker, homelier sound of a son coming up the path the first time: boots on gravel, a latch lifted, a breath caught back at the smell of a familiar hearth. There is hounds’ music in Wales—horn, cry, and the many-voiced running of dogs—yet tangled with another running, more secret and more ancient, where men hunt with the hounds and yet run with the deer. Love, too, will cross men in two fashions, as wind crosses a chimney: sometimes whistling, sometimes choking it with smoke; and it will do so not in airy talk, but over tables and thresholds, with eyes that have watched the same pot boil for years and suddenly cannot bear the sight of it.

There will be Clovelly Court, not as a picture hung up for strangers’ coins, but as it stood when old words were still alive in the mouths that spoke them—words for field and cliff and fish, words whose very shape carried the taste of the place. The far-west combes will open their green mouths, and within them the sea will be heard always, even when it is not seen: a steady undertone like a great kettle beginning to sing, and above it the clear, small bells of birds and brook-water—an acoustic world that keeps the mind sane as surely as good food keeps the body. And against that, later, will come harsher sounds: iron, shouting, the manufactured din of crowds and commerce; for there is a kind of noise that is not music, only wear, and it will press in as surely as any enemy.

Mr. John Oxenham of Plymouth will have his true and tragical history, not as a printed moral, but as something that happened to a man with a name and a trade and a certain timbre in his voice when he swore. A noble brotherhood of the Rose will be founded—chivalry, yes, but also the close, workmanlike fellowship men make when they must share danger as they share bread. Amyas will keep a Christmas Day, and the very remembering of it should bring you the crackle of green wood, the scent of spice and ale, the knife’s steady rhythm through meat, and the blessed hush that falls when a room full of folk bows their heads at once. A Mayor of Bideford will bait his hook with his own flesh—so the saying will become more than a saying—and Eustace Leigh will meet the Pope’s Legate, where power speaks in careful phrases that do not fit the tongues of all who must obey them.

You shall see Bideford Bridge dine at Annery House—stone and mortar turned by story into a guest—and the Golden Hind come home again, bringing with her not only gold and gunpowder, but the sea’s smell ground into rope and tar, and the strange silence that sometimes falls on a quay when men count the missing before they count the gain. Salvation Yeo will slay the King of the Gubbings; Mr. John Brimblecombe will discover the nature of an oath; and there will be a most chivalrous adventure of the good ship Rose, where courage is not a painted word but the feel of wet deck underfoot and the salt burning in cracked lips.

They will come to Barbados and find no men therein, and the emptiness will have its own sound—a wind through palm-leaves without any answering human stir, like a kitchen after the feast when the last guest has gone and the embers tick down. They will take the pearls at Margarita, and what befalls at La Guayra will remind you that the same hands that pray can also bind; that mercy and cruelty often share the same language in different mouths. Spanish bloodhounds and English mastiffs will tear at each other; the Communion will be taken under the tree at Higuerote, where the leaves overhead will make a soft, living roof and the words of faith will sit strangely among mosquitoes and musket-smoke. The Inquisition in the Indies will show how dominion works not only by sword but by vocabulary—by who may name sin, who may name truth, and how quickly a man’s own speech can be made into evidence against him.

The banks of the Meta will appear, and Amyas will be tempted of the Devil—not as a mere allegory, but as temptation comes in life: with a voice that fits itself to your desire, speaking smoothly, as if it were only offering you what you have always deserved. They will take the gold-train and the great galleon; Salvation Yeo will find his little maid again; Amyas will come home the third time, and homecoming will not be a single moment but a circle, like the season’s turning—each return tasting of the last and yet different, because those who eat at the same board are not the same people year after year.

The Virginia fleet will be stopped by the Queen’s command; Admiral John Hawkins will testify against Croakers; the Great Armada will swell like a storm-line across the horizon, and the sea’s roar will become a history-book written in thunder. Amyas will throw his sword into the sea, and in that gesture you will hear the clean, final splash—metal leaving hand, pride leaving heart—and then, at the very end, the apple will fall westward.

So much, then, for the passage and its doors.

If you would go on, remember that every great voyage begins in a still room: in the scrape of a chair on flagstones, in the comforting bubble of broth, in the low talk that carries a dialect like a family heirloom—each local word a spice that cannot be replaced without changing the whole dish. And remember, too, how easily such things are drowned: not only by cannon and the sea, but by louder, cheaper noises—by the clatter of profit and the thin jingle of art sold as ornament. Yet as long as one person can still hear a white bird’s wing in the common air, and as long as a table can still be laid with care, the old sounds and old meanings are not entirely lost.

The doors stand ready.

“The hollow oak our palace is,
Our heritage the sea.”

If you have ever gone down into the sweet folds of North Devon, you will remember Bideford as a white-clustered town set upon a broad tide-river, its sands yellow as sifted meal when the water draws off, its old bridge arched again and again like a ribbed spine across the Torridge. In autumn, salmon waited under those arches for the first proud spates, and above them the town climbed the slope toward the west, where the uplands opened—red fields and green squares stitched together with hedgerows, and beyond them the salt-marshes and hazy flats where Torridge takes Taw by the hand and both go quietly out toward the bar. Always, behind all quietness, there was the other sound: the long Atlantic swell, like distant thunder that never quite ceased. Day and night the sea-wind moved through the streets—clean, briny, and cool—tempering frost in winter and the heavy inland heats of summer, and carrying with it the cries of gulls, the creak of cordage, the soft slap of oars, and the slow breathing of ships against the quay. In that mingled music, men grew up with their nerves tuned to tide and weather; and if the world had a harsh note in it, it was the inland clang, not this salt and measured chorus.

Bideford had stood there a long while—how long no man could swear to, save that it was old enough to have gathered into itself, generation after generation, mixed blood and mixed talk: Saxon and Norse, dark Briton from over the water, and all the stubborn seaward folk who keep their own ways and their own words, even while the court and the city coin new fashions. In those days, too, Bideford was not merely a pleasant country town with a few coasters nodding at the quay. It was a port of weight, a place that had sent ships against the Armada and later would send more northward than any port but London and Topsham. A small thing, some might say, beside the roaring, moneyed greatness that would come after; yet small things are often the first bright ring in a tree’s trunk, and the sap that makes later timber possible.

For from such western towns—Bideford, Dartmouth, Topsham, Plymouth when it was still petty—came the hard breed of seamen and gentlemen-adventurers who made England’s sea-story not in painted proclamations but in salt sweat, blood, and daring. They were men whose names still lay warm on west-country tongues: Drake and Hawkins, Gilbert and Raleigh, Grenville and Oxenham, and many more whom ink forgets though the sea remembers. And if the telling of their deeds now and then rises into a note too loud for a mere tale, blame the subject; there are lives that were meant to be sung.

On a bright summer afternoon in the year of grace 1575, a tall fair boy lingered along Bideford quay in his scholar’s gown, a satchel and slate in hand. He walked slowly, as boys will when the heart is elsewhere, letting his eyes drink in the shipping and the sailors. The river-smell was in his nostrils—wet rope, tar, fish-guts from the cleaning boards, oak timbers warmed by sun, and the sharp lift of cider from open doors. There was a clatter of barrels rolled across planks, the ring of an iron hook against a bollard, and above it all the steady hush-and-begin-again of the distant surf, a voice that could make a lad’s blood itch like nettles.

Just past the bottom of the High Street he came opposite one of the taverns that looked out upon the water. In the open bay-window sat merchants and gentlemen over their afternoon draught of sack; and outside, in the shade of the door, a knot of sailors had gathered, listening hard to a man who stood in the midst. The boy, alive as tinder to any sea-news, edged in among the sailor-lads who peeped and whispered under men’s elbows, and so caught the heart of a tale flung out in a loud, bold voice, thick with Devonshire and salted with oaths.

“If you don’t believe me, go and see, or stay here and grow all over blue mould. I tell you, as I am a gentleman, I saw it with these eyes—and so did Salvation Yeo there, through a window in the lower room; and we measured the heap, as I am a christened man—seventy foot long, ten foot broad, and twelve foot high, of silver bars, and each bar between a thirty and forty pound weight. And says Captain Drake: ‘There, my lads of Devon, I’ve brought you to the mouth of the world’s treasure-house, and it’s your own fault now if you don’t sweep it out as empty as a stock-fish.’”

“Why didn’t you bring some of they home, then, Mr. Oxenham?”

“Why weren’t you there to help to carry them? We would have brought ’em away, safe enough, and young Drake and I had broke the door abroad already, but Captain Drake goes off in a dead faint; and when we came to look, he had a wound in his leg you might have laid three fingers in, and his boots were full of blood, and had been for an hour or more; but the heart of him was that, that he never knew it till he dropped—and then his brother and I got him away to the boats, he kicking and struggling, and bidding us let him go on with the fight, though every step he took in the sand was in a pool of blood; and so we got off. And tell me, ye sons of shotten herrings, wasn’t it worth more to save him than the dirty silver? for silver we can get again, brave boys: there’s more fish in the sea than ever came out of it, and more silver in Nombre de Dios than would pave all the streets in the west country: but of such captains as Franky Drake, Heaven never makes but one at a time; and if we lose him, good-bye to England’s luck, say I; and who don’t agree, let him choose his weapons, and I’m his man.”

The speaker was a tall, sturdy man with a florid face half-hidden by a black beard, and dark eyes that never rested. He leaned against the tavern wall, legs crossed, arms akimbo, as if the whole street were his quarter-deck. To the boy he seemed not a man only but a story come to life—a magnifico, a prince of fortune. He wore, in defiance of the sumptuary laws, a suit of crimson velvet, somewhat worn at seam and elbow as though it had been tried against weather and rope. At his side hung a long Spanish rapier and a brace of daggers with gaudy hilts; rings flashed on his fingers; gold chains lay heavy at his neck; great earrings swung in his ears; and behind one of them a red rose was stuck jauntily among glossy black curls. On his head was a broad velvet Spanish hat, and in it, clasped not with a mere feather but with a great gold fastener, was set a whole Quezal bird—its fretted golden-green plumage shining like one solid jewel when the sun caught it. As he ended, he took off the hat and looked at the bird as if it were a banner.

“Look ye, my lads—did you ever see such a fowl as that before? That’s the bird which the old Indian kings of Mexico let no one wear but their own selves; and therefore I wear it,—I, John Oxenham of South Tawton, for a sign to all brave lads of Devon, that as the Spaniards are the masters of the Indians, we’re the masters of the Spaniards.”

A murmur of applause went round, broken by a doubtful voice that hinted the Spaniards were too many for them.

“Too many? How many men did we take Nombre de Dios with? Seventy-three were we, and no more when we sailed out of Plymouth Sound; and before we saw the Spanish Main, half were gastados, used up, as the Dons say, with the scurvy; and in Port Pheasant Captain Rawse of Cowes fell in with us, and that gave us some thirty hands more; and with that handful, my lads—only fifty-three in all—we picked the lock of the new world! And whom did we lose but our trumpeter, who stood braying like an ass in the middle of the square, instead of taking care of his neck like a Christian? I tell you, those Spaniards are rank cowards, as all bullies are. They pray to a woman, the idolatrous rascals! and no wonder they fight like women.”

“You’m right, captain,” sang out a tall gaunt fellow close by, “one westcountry-man can fight two easterlings, and an easterling can beat three Dons any day. Eh! my lads of Devon?

“For O! it’s the herrings and the good brown beef,
And the cider and the cream so white;
O! they are the making of the jolly Devon lads,
For to play, and eke to fight.”

The words dropped into the air like the smell from a kitchen window: herrings browned, beef salted and roasted, cider sharp as green apples, cream thick as peace. Around them the tavern’s own breath rose—hot sack, yeast, smoke, and spilled ale drying on boards. The boy heard it all as one thing: song, salt air, and hunger; the world’s far edges seemed to press close to the quay, as if you might step from Bideford straight into the Spanish Main.

“Come,” cried Oxenham. “Come along! Who lists? who lists? who’ll make his fortune?

“Oh, who will join, jolly mariners all?
And who will join, says he, O!
To fill his pockets with the good red goold,
By sailing on the sea, O!”

“Who’ll list?” shouted the gaunt man again. “Now’s your time! We’ve got forty men to Plymouth now, ready to sail the minute we get back, and we want a dozen out of you Bideford men, and just a boy or two, and then we’m off and away, and make our fortunes, or go to heaven.

“Our bodies in the sea so deep,
Our souls in heaven to rest!
Where valiant seamen, one and all,
Hereafter shall be blest!”

“Now,” said Oxenham, turning so the Quezal’s green-gold flashed, “you won’t let the Plymouth men say that the Bideford men daren’t follow them? North Devon against South, it is. Who’ll join? who’ll join? It is but a step of a way, after all, and sailing as smooth as a duck-pond as soon as you’re past Cape Finisterre. I’ll run a Clovelly herring-boat there and back for a wager of twenty pound, and never ship a bucketful all the way. Who’ll join? Don’t think you’re buying a pig in a poke. I know the road, and Salvation Yeo here, too—who was the gunner’s mate—as well as I do the narrow seas, and better. You ask him to show you the chart of it, now, and see if he don’t tell you over the ruttier as well as Drake himself.”

At that the tall gaunt fellow—Salvation Yeo—pulled from under his arm a great white buffalo horn, rough-etched all over with land and sea. He held it up, and the ring of men leaned in as one, drawn as if by scent.

“See here, boys all, and behold the pictur of the place, dra’ed out so natural as ever was life. I got mun from a Portingal, down to the Azores; and he’d pricked mun out, and pricked mun out, wheresoever he’d sailed, and whatsoever he’d seen. Take mun in your hands now, Simon Evans, take mun in your hands; look mun over, and I’ll warrant you’ll know the way in five minutes so well as ever a shark in the seas.”

The horn went from hand to hand. Oxenham, seeing eyes kindle and throats bob with desire, called through the open window for a great tankard of sack; and that went round too, warm and sweet, with a bite like spiced fruit. The boy, having edged himself into the inner ring, stood at last face to face with the man of the emerald crest and stole as many peeps as he could at the wonder. He watched the sailors, one after another, come forward after handling the horn and offer to join Oxenham. The horn seemed to work like a charm; not a few had their courage coaxed up as easily as hunger is stirred by the crackle of bacon on a pan.

When the group broke a little and Oxenham began to swagger into the tavern with his recruits, the boy’s longing overcame his shyness. He asked boldly for a nearer sight of the horn. It was granted at once, as if men who have seen strange coasts cannot deny a boy’s yearning.

Now the horn lay in his hands, cool and smooth in places where palms had worn it, and under his astonished gaze unrolled a whole world. There were cities and harbors, dragons and elephants, whales fighting sharks, plate-ships of Spain, islands with apes and palm-trees, each with its name written above, and here and there the plain promise: “Here is gold;” and again, “Much gold and silver;”—English words, no doubt, added by Oxenham’s own hand where greed most needed pointing. The boy turned it round and round, lingering as if the very turning were a voyage. He thought the owner richer than Khan or Kaiser. If he could but possess that horn—if he could keep that world in his hands—what else on earth would he need?

“I say, will you sell this?”

“Yea, marry, or my own soul, if I can get the worth of it.”

“I want the horn,—I don’t want your soul; it’s somewhat of a stale sole, for aught I know; and there are plenty of fresh ones in the bay.”

Then, after much fumbling, the boy pulled out a tester—the only one he had—and asked if that would buy it.

“That! no, nor twenty of them.”

The boy, thinking what a good knight-errant would do when treasure was refused him, lifted his chin and said, “Tell you what: I’ll fight you for it.”

“Thank ’ee, sir! Break the jackanapes’s head for him, Yeo,” said Oxenham, laughing.

“Call me jackanapes again, and I break yours, sir.” The boy’s fist came up quick and hard, his eyes fierce with the sudden dignity of youth.

Oxenham looked at him a moment, smiling as at a plucky dog. “Tut! tut! my man, hit one of your own size, if you will, and spare little folk like me!”

“If I have a boy’s age, sir, I have a man’s fist. I shall be fifteen years old this month, and know how to answer any one who insults me.”

“Fifteen, my young cockerel? you look liker twenty,” said Oxenham, and his glance ran admiringly over the lad’s broad limbs, keen blue eyes, curling golden locks, and round honest face. “Fifteen? If I had half-a-dozen such lads as you, I would make knights of them before I died. Eh, Yeo?”

“He’ll do,” said Yeo. “He will make a brave gamecock in a year or two, if he dares ruffle up so early at a tough old hen-master like the captain.”

There was a general laugh, Oxenham’s loudest among them; and then he bade the lad tell him why he was so keen after the horn.

“Because,” said he, looking up boldly, “I want to go to sea. I want to see the Indies. I want to fight the Spaniards. Though I am a gentleman’s son, I’d a deal liever be a cabin-boy on board your ship.” And having flung out his secret as if it were a weapon, he dropped his head again, ashamed and yet relieved, as one is after speaking aloud in a silent church.

“And you shall,” cried Oxenham with a great oath; “and take a galloon, and dine off carbonadoed Dons. Whose son are you, my gallant fellow?”

“Mr. Leigh’s, of Burrough Court.”

“Bless his soul! I know him as well as I do the Eddystone, and his kitchen too. Who sups with him to-night?”

“Sir Richard Grenville.”

“Dick Grenville? I did not know he was in town. Go home and tell your father John Oxenham will come and keep him company. There, off with you! I’ll make all straight with the good gentleman, and you shall have your venture with me; and as for the horn, let him have the horn, Yeo, and I’ll give you a noble for it.”

“Not a penny, noble captain. If young master will take a poor mariner’s gift, there it is, for the sake of his love to the calling, and Heaven send him luck therein.” And with the sudden, tidal generosity of true sailors, Yeo thrust the horn into the boy’s hands and walked away briskly, as if to outrun thanks before it could catch him.

“And now,” quoth Oxenham, turning back to the men with that air of command which comes as naturally to some as breath, “my merry men all, make up your minds what mannered men you be minded to be before you take your bounties. I want none of your rascally lurching longshore vermin, who get five pounds out of this captain, and ten out of that, and let him sail without them after all, while they are stowed away under women’s mufflers, and in tavern cellars. If any man is of that humor, he had better to cut himself up, and salt himself down in a barrel for pork, before he meets me again; for by this light, let me catch him, be it seven years hence, and if I do not cut his throat upon the streets, it’s a pity! But if any man will be true brother to me, true brother to him I’ll be, come wreck or prize, storm or calm, salt water or fresh, victuals or none, share and fare alike; and here’s my hand upon it, for every man and all! and so—

“Westward ho! with a rumbelow,
And hurra for the Spanish Main, O!”

After which oration Mr. Oxenham swaggered into the tavern, followed by his new men; and the boy took his way home up the hill, hugging his precious horn to his ribs as if it were warm-baked bread. The town sounds changed as he climbed: the quay’s cries and rope-creaks fell behind, and the steady Atlantic thunder came forward again, laid under everything like a drum kept far off. He trembled between hope and fear, blushing with a maidenly shame, and with that half-sense of wrong-doing which comes when a child has let slip, to a stranger, the darling wish he has hidden from those he loves.

This young gentleman was Amyas Leigh; and though he came of as good blood as any in Devon, and had lived all his life in what folk would call the very best society, he was not—saving for his good looks—what would now be named an “interesting” youth, still less a “highly educated” one. A little Latin had been driven into him by repeated blows as if it were a nail. Of books he knew none, save his Bible, his Prayer-book, the old “Morte d’Arthur” of Caxton that lay in the great bay-window in the hall, and the new English of Las Casas’ “History of the West Indies,” called “The Cruelties of the Spaniards,” lying beside it—words that burned on the tongue even when read aloud softly. He believed devoutly in fairies—pixies he called them—who changed babies and danced the mushroom rings on the downs. For warts and burns he went to the white witch at Northam for charms. He thought the sun moved round the earth, and that the moon had some kinship with a Cheshire cheese. He held that swallows slept all winter at the bottom of the horse-pond. He spoke, like Raleigh and Grenville and other “low persons,” in a broad Devonshire accent, and would have found it hard work to translate his own simplest home-sayings into courtly speech without losing their taste. There are words that carry a whole countryside’s weather in them; take them away, and you have the meaning perhaps, but not the life.

Yet, ignorant as any pert monitor of later days might call him, Amyas had been taught certain things thoroughly—after the old Persian fashion: to speak the truth and to draw the bow. He could do both. He could endure pain cheerfully. He believed it the finest thing in the world to be a gentleman; and by “gentleman” he understood not finery or idle pride, but the careful habit of causing needless pain to no human being, poor or rich, and of taking pride in giving up his own pleasure for the sake of those weaker than himself. He had been trusted for the last year with breaking a colt and with the care of a cast of young hawks his father had received from Lundy Isle; and through those rough, country tasks—coarse as they might seem to fine folk—he had learned patience, thoughtfulness, and the art of holding his temper as a cook holds a fire: not too fierce, not too slack, but steady under the hand. He had never had an “object lesson,” nor been taught to “use his intellectual powers,” but he knew the names and ways of every bird and fish and fly, and could read cloud-drift like an old sailor reads a chart, taking warning from a change in wind as from a change in a man’s voice.

He was, moreover, because of his extraordinary size and strength, the undisputed cock of the school and the most terrible fighter among all Bideford boys. In that brutal habit he took much delight; yet, strange as it may seem, he contrived to draw good out of it too, doing justice with a heavy hand among his fellows, succoring the oppressed, and hardly thinking he had done his day’s duty if he went home without beating a big lad for bullying a little one. The sailor-lads feared him; the town’s boys and girls, by a natural law, leaned on him and boasted of him.

For the rest, he never thought about thinking, or felt about feeling. His ambition was simple: to please his father and mother; to get, by honest means, the maximum of red quarrenders and mazard cherries when the season came; and—when he was big enough—to go to sea. He was not what many would now call a pious child; but he said his Creed and Lord’s Prayer night and morning, went to service every forenoon, read the day’s Psalms with his mother every evening, and had learned from both father and mother—shown later by his life—that it was infinitely noble to do right and infinitely base to do wrong. Beyond the Church Catechism he knew little theology; but he had, in his very bones, a plain, honest religion, like clean water drawn from a well—without much talk about it, and all the better for that.

So watch him now up the hill, the great horn hugged close, its scraped pictures hidden under his arm as if they might fly away. He was going home to tell his mother all that had passed—his mother, from whom he had never hidden anything in his life save only that sea-fever; and that only because he knew it would pain her, and because, prudent and sensible lad that he was, he had said to her that very afternoon, “there was no use hollaing till he was out of the wood.”

Five years had turned their wheel since last we looked upon North Devon; and now it was a clear, hushed morning in November, with such a cold brightness in the air that every sound seemed cut sharp as a knife, and yet carried kindly, as if the world itself were listening.

Bideford church-bells had no mind to keep their accustomed hours. They had been set to ring for the daily service; but instead of falling into sober measure, they broke out again and again into sudden, laughing peals, as though the ropes were pulled by boys who could not help their joy. The very town answered them: streets were hung with garlands and bright cloths; tapestry leaned from windows; the pool was thick with shipping dressed in flags; and the guns, great and small, spoke out in hearty thunder, that rolled away over the water and came back softened along the mud-flats and reeds.

The day smelt of smoke and wet wool and horses—every stable crammed, every yard alive with stamping, jingling tackle, grooms shouting, stirrups clinking against stone. Sir Richard Grenville’s house, hard by, was no longer a gentleman’s quiet dwelling, but a kind of cheerful siege: men running, boots thudding on boards, platters going in and out, drink poured without counting, saddles flung down, reins gathered up again. From the kitchen door there drifted the steady, honest breath of a great household at full stretch—meat roasting, bread just broken, ale warmed by the crush of bodies and the long work of the morning. Even if a man had come deaf, he might have known by that smell alone that the town kept holiday.

Up the little churchyard streamed the gentry of North Devon, and with them their wives and daughters, bright as the banners overhead—faces pale with cold and excitement, cheeks warmed by the walk, voices lowered and quick, shoes whispering through frost-stiff grass. Countess of Bath came in state, Sir Richard bare-headed beside her because her Earl was away with the Queen in London. Bassets from Umberleigh, Carys from Clovelly, Fortescues from Wear and Buckland and every quarter, Coles, Stukelys, St. Legers, Coffins, Coplestones even from Eggesford far off—each family bringing its own train, as if the whole shire had been poured out into that narrow place.

Sir John Chichester of Ralegh came after the old patriarchal manner, with his eight daughters in file behind him and three of his famous sons; and he met at the gate his cousin of Arlington, who seemed to have emptied a nursery into the street—daughters and nineteen sons, the last not yet past the town-hall while the first were already near the lychgate, laughing and making way for the elder branch. Within the church there was the usual busy justice of place and precedence: sour looks between high-born matrons, small pushes masked as courtesies, whisperings that stung more sharply than the November air. The churchwardens and sidesmen—who had never before been asked to marshal so glittering a company—bustled red-faced and breathless, rods in hand, until at last they were driven, almost to pleading, to beg help of Sir Richard himself: who was elder, who younger, who bore eight quarterings and who but four, lest a day of thanksgiving should end by setting half the county at feud. The old men were safely penned in the corporation pews; the young men sought only a corner where they might see the ladies.

And then, as if some hand had laid a cloth over the whole place, there fell a sudden silence. Heads turned. Eyes drew toward the door. Outside, a far-off music approached—flutes and hautboys, drums and trumpets—braying and screaming and thundering up the street with a merry violence which made the very stones seem to thrill; then it ceased at the church doors. Down went the churchwardens in a flutter; through the congregation ran a whisper like wind in dry leaves; and more than one woman’s breath broke into a sob that was half laugh, half prayer.

The wonder of the day entered, and the rector began—not the morning service, but the old thanksgiving appointed after a victory at sea.

Four weather-beaten mariners came first, their hard coats trimmed and knotted with ribbons by hands that had waited and feared and labored; but before them walked a figure at once strange and familiar: a beardless boy, yet built like a young Hercules, towering a head and shoulders above the crowd, golden hair falling loose upon his shoulders as if he had come out of some old song into the plain wooden church. Devon eyes devoured him. He went straight, with the others, as if drawn by a cord, up to the altar; and there the five knelt at the rails.

Then all the gaze of the church turned to one pew, where Mistress Leigh of Burrough hid her face between her hands. Her hood shook with the soundless trembling of joy, the way a tree trembles when the wind has passed through it and left it living.

For this was fellow-feeling, as it had been of old in merry England—county and town; and these were Devon men, and men of Bideford: Amyas Leigh of Burrough, John Staveley, Michael Heard, Jonas Marshall of Bideford, and Thomas Braund of Clovelly; and they, first of all English mariners, had sailed round the world with Francis Drake, and were come home to give God thanks.

It is a long tale how such a thing befell; and to make it plain we must go backward a little, almost to the place where we last set our foot.

A twelvemonth and more after Mr. Oxenham’s going, young Amyas had kept, for the most part, to the promise he had made—quiet enough, save for the sudden fierce outbursts that belong to all young male creatures, and to boys most of all who have any strength of blood and will. His book-learning mended little; his home-learning did. He grew daily more handy with bow and horse and sword—especially in the old buckler-play—when his father, going down upon business to the Exeter Assizes, took the gaol-fever from the prisoners (as too often in those days), sickened in the very court, and died within a week.

So Mistress Leigh was left a widow a little past forty, with God above her and a young lion-cub at her hand to be tamed for this life and the next.

She had loved her husband fervently and holily; and he—though scholar, courtier, soldier—had been a disappointed man. His estate had been pinched by his father’s folly and his own youth’s ambition, which carried him up to Court to spend both heart and purse upon a shadow. He had the gifts of many men, and lacked chiefly the gift of using them; and in him there was a certain shy pride, a delicate honor—call it what you will—which kept him always from playing the winning game in the very world whose prizes he desired; and so he revenged himself upon it by continual grumbling. Yet at last, by good fortune, he met at Elizabeth’s Court a fair young Miss Foljambe of Derbyshire, weary, as he was, of the world’s sins (though she had seen less of them); and they pleased each other so well that, though the Queen grumbled—because the lady dared to marry, and the gentleman dared to adore any one but herself—they were suffered to vanish from that little Babylon at Whitehall and settle at Burrough. In his wife he found a treasure, and knew it.

Mistress Leigh had been from her youth one of those noble old English churchwomen—without superstition, without severity—who are among the fairest features of that hard heroic time. Yet a melancholy had dwelt with her always, for memory carried her back to days when to be Protestant was to live with fire at one’s shoulder. She could remember, five-and-twenty years before, the burning of poor blind Joan Waste at Derby, and of Mistress Joyce Lewis too, a lady born. In dreams, even now, she heard again her mother’s bitter cries to God—either to spare her that torment, or to give her strength to endure it as others had endured.

For her mother had been one of Queen Catherine’s bedchamber women, the friend and disciple of Anne Askew; and she had sat in Smithfield, blood curdled, to see that hapless beauty of Henry’s Court carried in a chair—so broken by the rack—to her fiery doom beside her fellow-courtier Mr. Lascelles; while the heavens themselves, it seemed, spoke wrath and grief in thunder, and heavy drops hissed upon the crackling pile.

Therefore sadness hung about Mistress Leigh all her life, deepening in Queen Mary’s days when, being a known Protestant and heretic, she was forced to hide among the hills and caverns of the Peak, saved only by her husband’s tenants’ love and by his own bold vow that, good Catholic as he was, he would run through the body any constable, justice, priest—aye, bishop or cardinal—who dared serve the Queen’s warrant upon his wife.

So she escaped. Yet the shadow remained; and the hem of that dark mantle fell upon the young girl who had shared the hidings and the wanderings; who, when she came to be a wife, gave herself utterly up to God.

And yet—this is worth the saying—she did not give herself to God in any barren way that forgets hearth and neighbor. She gave herself to her husband and her children, and to the poor of Northam Town; and she was none the less welcome among Grenvilles and Fortescues and Chichesters and all gentle houses round, who honored her husband’s gifts and were glad of his wit. She had her private austerities—often gently rebuked by him—yet without one superstitious thought of buying favor with pain. Her spirit was schooled in the freest and loftiest doctrine of Luther; and that little mystic Alt-Deutsch Theologie was her counsel and her comfort, by day and night.

So now, when she stood alone, she was still lovely in face and figure; lovelier yet for the divine calm that brooded over look and word and gesture, a sweetness ripened by storm as well as sunshine, which this world had not given and could not take away. No wonder Sir Richard and Lady Grenville loved her; no wonder her children worshiped her.

And Amyas—when the first burst of grief had spent itself and he could see where he stood—felt that a new life had begun. His mother was no longer to think and act for him only; he must think and act for her. Therefore, the very day after his father’s funeral, when school-hours were done, he did not come straight home, but went boldly into Sir Richard Grenville’s house and asked to see his godfather.

“You must be my father now, sir,” he said, and stood to it.

Sir Richard looked at the boy’s broad strong face and swore a great and holy oath, “by oak, and ash, and thorn,” that he would be a father to him and a brother to his mother, for Christ’s sake. Lady Grenville took the boy by the hand and walked home with him to Burrough; and there the two fair women fell on each other’s necks and wept—one for the loss that had been, the other, by a prophetic instinct, for the like loss that was to come. Sweet St. Leger knew her husband’s fiery spirit would not leave his body in a quiet bed; death would find him, as he prayed almost nightly, sword in hand upon the field of duty and fame.

They vowed sisterhood and kept it. After that, things at Burrough went on much as before: Amyas rode, shot, boxed, wandered the quay at Sir Richard’s side; for Mistress Leigh was too wise to change one tittle of the training Amyas’s father had judged best for his younger son.

It was enough that her elder son had taken, of his own accord, the very way of life in which she would fain have molded both.

Frank—God’s wedding-gift to that pure love—had won honor early. He went from Bideford school to Exeter College, and there made friendship with Sir Philip Sidney and other young men of rank and hope. In the summer of 1572, traveling toward Heidelberg, he passed through Paris with letters to Walsingham at the English Embassy; by that chance he met Sidney again, and by that chance too he saved his own life (as Sidney did his) in the Massacre of St. Bartholomew. At Heidelberg he stayed two years, winning love and praise, refusing Sidney’s entreaties to follow him into Italy; for he scorned to burden his parents and so became tutor to two young German princes. After a time he took them down to Padua “to perfect them,” as he wrote home, “according to his insufficiency, in all princely studies.”

Friends he had everywhere: princes, magnificos, learned doctors—such letters and diplomas he carried back and forth that a lesser man would have been puffed up by the very weight of them. He tasted Italy as a careful man tastes a rare dish—eager, attentive, resolving to remember. He talked of sonnets with Tasso, of history with Sarpi; he listened, half awed, half unbelieving, to Galileo’s daring; he brought his pupils to Venice to have their portraits painted by Paul Veronese; he watched the merchant princes on the Rialto and the argosies, and saw Palladio’s palaces. He stood before Tintoretto’s huge tempests of color; and by private intercession in high places he was admitted to that sacred room where old Titian, long-bearded and undimmed of eye, lingered amid a pantheon of his own making, telling tales of Bellini and Raphael and Michael Angelo, of St. Peter’s building, Venice’s fires, Rome’s sack, and the kings and poets long gone to their account—showing too the brush which Francis the First had stooped to pick up for him.

Frank even went to Rome—much to the scandal of good Protestants at home—and saw the Pope of that day, whom Sidney (in his worldly frankness) calls a “right good fellow” not yet eaten to death. He saw the Vatican’s frescos; he heard Palestrina beneath St. Peter’s dome, presiding over the performance of his own music, and fell half in love with those luscious strains—till he woke, as from enchantment, by remembering that under that same dome men had given thanks to God for blood in Paris streets and shrieking women and heaps of insulted corpses.

A few months before his father died, he took his pupils back to Germany and was dismissed with rich gifts. Mistress Leigh’s heart beat high at the thought of his return; but within a month after the death came a long letter from Frank, full of the Alps and the Waldensian valleys, where he had talked with the Barbes about late persecutions; and full too of Latin quotations, as was his way. He wrote of Padua, where he had met the learned Stephanus Parmenius—Budaeus from his native place—who persuaded him to go down the Danube to Buda and see the learning for which Hungary was famed. He wrote again and again thereafter to the father whom he believed still living; but no answer reached him for nearly two years. At last, fearing calamity, he hurried home to find his mother widowed—and Amyas gone to the South Seas with Captain Drake of Plymouth.

Even then Frank was not allowed to remain long in Devon. Sir Richard, to whom idleness seemed unrighteous, had him up and doing before six months were out, and sent him off to Court to Lord Hunsdon. There, being as delicately fair as Amyas was huge and strong, he found place in some office of the Queen’s household by Carew’s help and Sidney’s and Leicester’s; and he basked in the full sunshine of favor, ladies’ eyes, chivalries and euphuisms of Gloriana’s fairyland, and in the close friendship of Sidney—returned with honor in 1577 from his delicate mission to Vienna, under color of condoling with the new Emperor Rodolph, while in truth he carried the hopes and fears of Protestant leaders abroad. Frank found him in 1579 as lovely and loving as ever, at five-and-twenty acknowledged among the most remarkable men in Europe—patron of letters, counselor of warriors and statesmen, confidant of William of Orange and Languet and Du Mornay and the rest.

Mistress Leigh, who had long since learned to have no self, but to live in her children, submitted without a murmur. She only said, smiling, to her stern friend—

“You took away my mastiff-pup, and now you must needs have my fair greyhound also.”

“Would you have your fair greyhound, dear lady,” Sir Richard answered, “grow into a tall true Cotswold dog that can pull down a stag of ten, or one of those smooth-skinned poppets which Florence ladies lead about with a ring of bells round its neck and a flannel farthingale over its loins?”

So she yielded; and after a few months she was repaid by a letter sent through Sir Richard—written by none other than Gloriana herself—thanking her for “the loan of that most delicate and flawless crystal, the soul of her excellent son,” praising him beyond my room to set down, and exalting the poor mother above Cornelia: for Cornelia had shown her jewels and kept them; but this woman, having two as precious, had lent both to country and Queen, who held herself indebted and would repay as became both giver and receiver. The sweet mother bedewed the letter with holy tears and laid it in the cedar-box that held her household treasures, beside Frank’s diplomas and recommendations—Latin which she spelled patiently over though she understood not a word, hoping always to light upon that dear phrase excellentissimus noster Franciscus Leighius Anglus, which was all in all to a mother’s heart.

But why did Amyas go to the South Seas?

For two causes—each of which has driven many a lad farther and worse: first, because of an old schoolmaster; secondly, because of a young beauty. Take them in order.

Vindex Brimblecombe—once servitor of Exeter College, Oxford, and commonly called Sir Vindex after the fashion of the time—was master of the grammar-school at Bideford. He was in truth a godly, kind-hearted pedant at root; but like many schoolmasters in those days of the birch, his heart had been toughened by long license to inflict pain upon those weaker than himself. It may be healthful enough for the sufferer, being short and bodily; but for the executioner it is too often a certain way to scrape away chivalry and tenderness, and sometimes even self-command. Sir Vindex felt, in his way, that it was now his duty to take especial care of the fatherless boy whom he strove to cram with qui, quae, quod; but the fruit of that new responsibility was chiefly that Amyas’s floggings increased rapidly—from two a week to one each day—not without consequences to Sir Vindex in the end.

All this while Amyas never once lost sight of his darling desire—the sea. When he could not wander on the quay and stare at the shipping, or go down to the pebble-ridge at Northam and sit there, devouring with hungry eyes the great broad ocean that seemed to beckon him outward into boundless space, he consoled himself in school by drawing ships and imaginary charts upon his slate instead of minding his “humanities.”

It fell out one afternoon that he was deep in a map—a bird’s-eye view of an island—with a great castle on it and at the gate a dragon terrible to see; while in the foreground came what was meant for a gallant ship with a great flag aloft, but which, being crowded with a forest of lances, looked more like a porcupine carrying a signpost. At the roots of those lances he had made many little round o’s for heads, whereby was signified Amyas and his schoolfellows about to slay the dragon and rescue the beautiful princess imprisoned in the enchanted tower. To behold this marvel, the boys at the desk must needs club their heads together, the more boldly because Sir Vindex, after dinner as his custom was, lay back in his chair and slept the sleep of the just.

Amyas slept as a man sleeps when the body is spent and the mind refuses the bargain. His limbs lay heavy, as if the long road and the sea-air had salted them into stiffness; but behind his shut eyes the day kept moving—faces, voices, a hand lifted in warning—till his mother and Frank, bending now and then over the pillow, saw by the quick twitching of his brows that he was chased by thoughts he could not master.

It was the name of John Oxenham that had taken hold of him, like a burr in wool. All the evening, in the bay-windowed room where that man had once sat living, Amyas had found himself calling back every turn of the lost adventurer’s head, each half-smile and sudden stillness, as though memory were a dish he could not leave until it was scraped clean—though scraping only brought up more taste of it, more craving. He wondered at himself, and the wondering made it worse; and when he lay down, the fancy did not die, but rose up hotter in his sleep.

He was, he knew not how, sailing westward ever, up the wake of the sinking sun, chasing a tiny sail that was Oxenham’s. A painful certainty pressed on him—that unless he overtook her in time, some horror would be done and could not be undone. Yet his ship would not sail. The bows were clogged with sargasso weed, those long, slimy coils that looked like a sea-serpent’s hair, dragging and clinging, dead weight and living menace together. He strained, he willed, he shouted without sound, and still the vessel only wallowed as the sun went down and the sea turned to ink.

Then the moon rose, and with it came Oxenham’s ship close aboard—too close, as if it had been behind him all along. Her sails were torn and snapping like old linen in a gale; pitch streamed down her sides in black tears; the bulwarks looked soft with rot. And along the mainyard there hung a line of dark shapes, swaying gently. A line of hanged men.

He tried to cry out, and could not. From the yard-arm, above him, John Oxenham’s corpse looked down with grave-bright eyes and beckoned—beckoned and pointed, striving to speak, and failing, his lips moving like a fish’s in air. And always the finger pointed, not onward into that glittering west, but backward—back along their course.

Amyas turned his gaze the way the dead man commanded; and behold, behind him the Andes shone in the moon, sharp as knife-edges under snow. He knew then, with the helpless knowledge that comes in dreams like a blow, that he was in the South Seas again, and all America lay between him and home. Still the corpse pointed back and back, yearning in its agony to tell some secret that could not be said; till Amyas sprang up with a shout that tore him awake.

He lay in his little coved chamber at Burrough, the gray of autumn morning stealing in as if through wet wool. His heart hammered like a smith at iron. He tried to sleep again and could not; after an hour’s tossing he rose, dressed, and went down for a bathe on the beloved pebble ridge, as a man goes to cold water to drive out fever.

Passing his mother’s door, he could not help but look in. In the dim light he saw the bed; the pillow had not been pressed. His mother, in her long white night-gear, knelt at the far end by her prie-dieu, so intent that even the dawn seemed a thing she would not acknowledge till her soul had finished its work.

He slipped in without a word and knelt beside her. She turned, smiled, and put her arm around him—quiet, steady, as if he were a child again brought in from wind and fright—and then went on with her prayers. Why not? He knew they were for him, and so he prayed too; for her, and for poor lost John Oxenham and all the vanished men who had followed him as one follows a bell into fog.

At last she rose and stood above him, parted the yellow hair from his brow, and looked long into his face. There was no need for speech; between these two there was nothing to hide, and nothing that could not be known without words. When at last the gaze broke, she stooped and kissed him on the brow. As she turned away, a tear fell and splashed warm on his skin.

Her bare feet showed beneath the hem. He bent and kissed them again and again; then looked up, half laughing at himself, half pleading—

“You have such pretty feet, mother!”

In a woman’s instant instinct she hid them. She had been a beauty once; her hair was gray now and the roses long faded, but there was a beauty left that belonged to bone and bearing and the clear courage of her eyes.

“Your dear father used to say so thirty years ago.”

“And I say so still. You always were beautiful; you are beautiful now.”

“What is that to you, silly boy? Will you play the lover with an old mother? Go take your walk, and think of younger ladies, if you can find any worthy of you.”

So the son went out, and the mother returned to her prayers, as to bread-making—quiet, daily, necessary, and done for love.

He walked down to the pebble ridge where the bay’s surges, after centuries of beating, had built a rampart of gray boulders—two miles long, cunningly curved and smoothed and fitted, as if some giant mason had laid each stone by hand. Inside it lay a sheet of rich turf, alluvial and level, protected from the spring and autumn tides. Amyas drew in the salt air with the greedy delight of a young sea-dog; it seemed to scrape his lungs clean. He stripped, plunged into the breakers, and dived and rolled, letting the cold bite and the foam buffet him till his blood ran bright and his dream began to lose its claws.

Then he heard himself hailed from the shore. Looking up, he saw on the top of the stony rampart the tall figure of his cousin Eustace.

Amyas was half-disappointed; for, love-lorn rascal, he had been walking down dreaming of Rose Salterne, and had no wish for a companion to interrupt such sweet folly on the way back. Still, it was three years since he had seen Eustace. He scrambled out and dressed, while his cousin paced the turf inside the ridge.

Eustace Leigh was the son of a younger brother of Leigh of Burrough, one who had more or less cut himself off from his kin and country by remaining a Papist. Born so, he had turned Protestant under Edward, Papist again under Mary, and—honor to him—had stopped there, too honest to turn once more with the wind when Elizabeth came in, as so many did. He lived withdrawn in a great rambling dark house on the Atlantic cliffs in Moorwinstow parish, still called Chapel, not far from Sir Richard Grenville’s Stow.

The penal laws troubled him little. They seldom troubled any man who did not make conspiracy part of his creed; and even for those, the council, for England’s peace, kept a tighter hand upon spiritual zealots than the zealots liked. Jesuits and seminary priests came and went at Chapel House as quietly as owls. They found sport in secrecy, like schoolboys in a forbidden pantry: little dens in a lonely turret, trap-doors, a hidden chamber under the roof where mass might be said. When Moorwinstow’s zealous parson, having discovered what all knew already, complained to Sir Richard and demanded the act of the fourteenth of Elizabeth be enforced, Sir Richard rated him like a fantastical Puritan and bade him mind his own business unless he wished the place made too hot for himself. The parson, who found Mr. Thomas Leigh paid tithes well enough, learned to bow his head in the house of Rimmon like Naaman—eating Mr. Leigh’s dinners when invited, and ignoring old Father Francis opposite him, dressed as a layman and calling himself the young gentleman’s pedagogue.

Yet those same priests held a lien on Mr. Thomas Leigh’s conscience—lands once belonging to Hartland Abbey. The rents burned in his purse. He half believed he would be lost for holding them; but not wholly, and therefore he did not give them up. Like many, he compromised one wrong with another: petted foreign priests, listened—or pretended not to listen—to their practisings, and gave up a son as a sin-offering, to be carried overseas to Douay or Rheims or Rome, to be trained as a seminary priest. One such scapegoat was Eustace, sent out to be made—so the harsh phrase went—a liar at Rheims.

A fair liar he had become; not a bad fellow at the core, but shaped by those who had chosen him for what they called a “vocation”—a certain trembling fear of the unseen that can be cultivated like fungus in a cellar, and with it that sly bend toward deceit which too often grows in superstitious men. He was tall and handsome, light of complexion, with a great upright forehead and a very small mouth. His face wore a dry set look, as if holding itself in by force; it was always trying to get free into proper dimples and smiles, because one ought to have Christian love, and if one loved one ought to be cheerful, and cheerful people smiled; therefore he would smile, and tried. But his charity prepense looked as little winning as malice prepense; and had he not been handsome, many a woman who raved about his sweetness might have likened his frankness to a skeleton’s in fetters, and his smiles to the grin thereof.

He had come back to England a month before, under the proclamation that those with children and wards beyond seas should register their names and call them home within four months. He stayed now with his father at Chapel, yet with private matters to transact for the society that had bred him; one such matter had brought him to Bideford the night before.

Now he sat beside Amyas on the pebbles and looked him over from the corners of his eyes very gently, as if afraid to hurt him—or even the flies on his back. Amyas faced him full, laughing, and held out a lion’s paw. Eustace took it rapturously, and a great shaking of hands followed: Amyas gripping with a round fist and a quiet quiver that said, I am glad to see you; Eustace pinching hard with straight white fingers and sawing the air up and down as if to cry, Don’t you see how glad I am?

“Hold hard, old lad,” said Amyas. “Before you break my elbow. And where do you come from?”

“From going to and fro in the earth, and from walking up and down in it,” said Eustace, with a small smile and a nod of mysterious self-importance.

“Like the devil, eh? Well, every man has his pattern. How is my uncle?”

If there was one man Eustace dreaded above another, it was Amyas. Not only because Amyas could have killed him with one blow; but because there are natures that do not rejoice in strength outside themselves—strength makes them irritable, then fearful, then spiteful, as if expecting the strong to do what they know they would do were they in the strong man’s place. And further, Eustace could not help feeling that Amyas despised him. Three years had not changed that memory: Amyas had never argued with him, because Amyas treated his haunted questions of saints and spirits and the unseen as “wind and moonshine,” and his cousin as half-baked. Eustace knew it, knew too that he had been wronged by it; and a secret voice in him whispered, Let us see if he finds me a match now—at which thought he fell at once into inward contrition for self-seeking, as if the very wish to be thought wise were a sin to be scourged.

Yet he spoke smoothly enough.

“Ah, my dearest cousin! How disappointed I was this morning to find I had come a day too late to witness your triumph. But I hastened to your home as soon as I could, and learning from your mother that I should find you here, hurried down to bid you welcome again to Devon.”

“Well, old lad, it does look natural to see you.” Amyas pulled on his jerkin and jerked the wet hair from his eyes. “I often used to think of you walking the deck o’ nights. Uncle and the girls are all right, then? Is the old pony dead yet? And how’s Dick the smith, and Nancy? Grown a fine maid by now, I warrant. ’Slid, it seems half a life since I was away.”

“And you really thought of your poor cousin? Be sure he too thought of you, and offered up nightly his weak prayers for your safety—to those saints to whom would that you—”

“Halt there, coz. If they’re half as good fellows as you and I take them for, they’ll help me without asking.”

“They have helped you, Amyas.”

“Maybe. I’d have done as much for them, I’m sure, if I’d been in their place.”

“And do you not feel, then, that you owe a debt of gratitude to them; and above all to her, whose intercessions have, I doubt not, availed for your preservation? Her—the star of the sea—the all-compassionate guide of the mariner?”

“Humph!” said Amyas. “Here’s Frank. Let him answer.”

As he spoke, up came Frank, and after greetings sat down beside them on the ridge, the tide hissing and sucking below as if it were frying something in a giant pan, and the gulls crying overhead with that wild, clean, knife-edge music that makes a man’s thoughts feel aired out and honest.

“I say, brother,” Amyas began at once, “here’s Eustace trying already to convert me, and telling me I owe all my luck to the Blessed Virgin’s prayers.”

“It may be so,” said Frank. “At least you owe it to the prayers of that most pure and peerless virgin by whose commands you sailed; the sweet incense of whose orisons has gone up for you daily, and for whose sake you were preserved from flood and foe, that you might spread the fame and advance the power of the spotless championess of truth, and right, and freedom—Elizabeth, your queen.”

Amyas answered the rhapsody with a loyal chuckle. Eustace smiled meekly; but his answer had a thin edge.

“I at least am certain I speak truth when I call my patroness a virgin undefiled.”

Both brothers’ brows clouded at once. Amyas, lying back on the pebbles, spoke quietly up toward the gulls as if to them rather than either cousin—

“I wonder what the Frenchman whose head I cut off at the Azores thinks by now about all that.”

“Cut off a Frenchman’s head?” said Frank.

“Yes, faith; and so fleshed my maiden sword. I’ll tell you. ’Twas in some tavern. George Drake and I had gone in, and there sat this Frenchman with his sword on the table, ready for a quarrel—I found after he was a noted bully. He begins loud enough about this and that; but after a while, by instigation of the devil, out he vents a dozen slanders against her majesty’s honor, one atop of another. I was ashamed to hear them, and I should be more ashamed to repeat them.”

“I have heard enough of such,” said Frank. “They come mostly through lewd rascals about the French ambassador, bred—God help them—among the filthy vices of that Medicean court where the Queen of Scots had her schooling, and who can only perceive in virtuous freedom a cloak for licentiousness like their own. Let the curs bark. Honi soit qui mal y pense is our motto, and shall be for ever.”

“But I didn’t let the cur bark. I took him by the ears to show him into the street. Whereon he got to his sword and I to mine; and a near chance I had of never bathing on the pebble ridge more. For the fellow did not fight with edge and buckler like a Christian, but had some newfangled French devil’s device of scryming and foining with his point—ha’ing and stamping and tracing at me—till I thought I should be full of eyelet holes ere I could close.”

“Thank God you are safe,” said Frank. “I know that play well enough. Dangerous enough it is.”

“Of course you know it; but I didn’t—more’s the pity.”

“Well, I’ll teach it thee, lad, as well as Rowland Yorke himself—

‘Thy fincture, carricade, and sly passata,
Thy stramazon, and resolute stoccata,
Wiping maudritta, closing embrocata,
And all the cant of the honorable fencing mystery.’”

“Rowland Yorke? Who’s he?”

“A roystering rascal in London, turning profit by teaching that art; and like enough to have his mortal thread clipped in a tavern brawl, as thy Frenchman. But how did you escape his pinking iron?”

“How? Had it through my left arm before I could look round. And then I got mad, and leapt on him, caught him by the wrist, and had a fair side-blow; and fortune willed it, off tumbled his head upon the table, and there was an end of his slanders.”

“So perish all her enemies!” said Frank.

Eustace, who had been trying not to listen, rose.

“I trust you do not number me among them?”

“As you speak, I do, coz,” said Frank. “But for your own sake, put faith in the true report of those who have daily experience of their mistress’s excellent virtue—as they have of the sun’s shining and the earth’s bringing forth fruit—and not in the tattle of a few cowardly back-stair rogues who would curry favor with the Guises. Come; we will say no more. Walk round with us by Appledore, and then home to breakfast.”

But Eustace declined. He had immediate business, he said, in Northam town, and then in Bideford; and so left them. The brothers lounged a while longer on the beach, letting the sea’s steady roar—old, unbought music, not some hired fiddling for a lord’s supper—wash the sharpness out of their talk; and then walked across the smooth turf toward the little white fishing village two miles above the bar, where Taw and Torridge meet and gossip together in brackish swirls.

Now it came to pass that Eustace had told them he was going to Northam; but he did not tell them that his true point was the same as theirs, namely Appledore. Therefore, after satisfying his conscience by going as far as the nearest house in Northam village, he struck away sharp to the left across the fields, repeating Heaven knows what to the Blessed Virgin as he went. By that crookedness—such as crooked spirits are prone to, Jesuits especially—he went miles out of his road, and only outwitted himself.

For Amyas and Frank, going straight like honest men across the turf, came into Appledore opposite the little Mariner’s Rest just in time to see what Eustace had taken such pains to hide: four of Mr. Thomas Leigh’s horses at the door, held by the groom with saddles and mail-bags on, and on three of them mounted Eustace and two strange gentlemen.

“There’s one lie already this morning,” growled Amyas. “He told us he was going to Northam.”

“And we do not know that he has not been there,” Frank suggested, with that bland fairness which could, when it chose, be sharper than any insult.

“Why, you’re as bad a Jesuit as he, to help him out with such a fetch.”

“He may have changed his mind.”

“Bless your pure imagination, my sweet boy,” said Amyas, laying his great hand on Frank’s head and mimicking their mother’s tone. “Come, dear Frank, let’s step into this shop and buy a pennyworth of whipcord.”

“What do you want with whipcord, man?”

“To spin my top, to be sure.”

“Top? How long hast had a top?”

“I’ll buy one then, and save my conscience. But the upshot of this sport I must see. Why mayn’t I have an excuse ready-made as well as Master Eustace?”

So saying, he pulled Frank into the little shop, unobserved by the party at the inn-door. The place smelled of hemp and tar and smoked fish—cordage, hooks, and sea-gear hanging like dry ribs from beams; a faint sweetness of apples stored somewhere in back; and beneath it all that briny, living odor that clings to any room near a working tide.

“What strange cattle has he been importing now?” Amyas murmured, peering through the window-crack. “Look at that three-legged fellow, trying to get aloft on the wrong side. How he claws at his horse’s ribs, like a cat scratching an elder stem!”

The three-legged man was tall and meek-looking, bedizened in gorgeous garments with a great feather and a sword so long and broad it differed little in size from the thin stiff shanks between which it wandered unhappily.

“Young David in Saul’s weapons,” said Frank. “He had better not go in them, for he certainly has not proved them.”

“Look, if his third leg is not turned into a tail! Why does not someone in charity haul in half-a-yard of his belt for him?”

And all this while, what had become of Rose Salterne, that same young breaker of hearts whom as yet no one had properly seen?

She was lodged, none too willingly, in the little farm-house by the mill, deep down in the green hollow of Combe, halfway betwixt Stow and Chapel, where the valley held its own weather and its own sound—water forever at work, wheel forever complaining, and the brook’s thin singing threading through the thicker hush of fern and ash. She had been sent there to be out of the way of all the brave stir at Bideford; and though her temper was too sweet to sour outright, she kept a kind of Martinmas Lent, abstaining from pleasure as if it were flesh, and taking her penance with a pout.

The days were long in that glen. Even the house’s homely business—skimming cream, turning cheeses in their cloths, warming yesterday’s loaf before the peat so the crust would crack and breathe again—could not fill all the hours. The mill had its own constant laboring music: the click and slap of the wheel-paddles, the low thunder of stones, the dusty cough of meal shaken through a sieve. It ought to have comforted her, that honest, old English sound; yet loneliness makes even good noises sharp.

So it came to pass that, though she had a Protestant’s suspicion of Eustace Leigh and something near an aversion besides, she could not bring herself to refuse him speech when he contrived, by one pretended errand or another, to visit the mill almost daily. At first her uncle and aunt looked stiff upon it, and her aunt took care to be a third voice at every talk; but Mr. Leigh’s son was a gentleman’s son, and a good customer besides, and it was ill-manners to be outright rude to the neighboring squire’s house. And Rose, for all her dutiful looks, carried that quiet wilfulness which often gets its own way by seeming only to ask. Indeed, she had been wise enough to beg her aunt never to leave her alone with him—because, as she put it, she “couldn’t a-bear the sight of Mr. Eustace, only she must have somebody to talk to down here.”

Her aunt, being a plain countrywoman with a head full of dairy reckonings and winter stores, concluded that townfolks’ fashions must be different, and that people knew their own minds best, and so let the matter slide.

Eustace, for his part, understood very well that the reef upon which his hopes were likeliest to split was the difference of creed between them. Therefore he kept his private opinions tucked away, like a knife in a boot, and showed instead the smooth side of himself: buying milk still warm from the cow, or flour newly bolted, and carrying it away only to give it to the old women of Moorwinstow, who agreed among themselves that, Papist or no, he was “a godly young man enough.” He even went so far, after taking counsel with Campian and Parsons upon certain plots then whispering through the country like wind under doors, as to decide that all three should go to church the next Sunday.

So Messrs. Evan Morgans and Morgan Evans, having crammed the rubrics as a scholar crams his Latin, behaved in a fashion orthodox and unexceptionable; and Eustace likewise, to the great wonder of the parish, answered and stood and knelt as if he had been bred to it. He went home afterward with a secret triumph, flattering himself that he had taken in parson, clerk, and people; not knowing—in his simple unsimplicity, and cunning foolishness—that each goodwife was saying to her neighbor, over her bread and small beer, “He turned Protestant? The devil turned monk! He’s only after Mistress Salterne, the young hypocrite.”

But if the two Jesuits found it expedient to reconcile themselves outwardly to the powers that were, they were none the less busy, in private, in contriving their overthrow.

Since April they had been hunted and hidden, playing at hide-and-seek from shire to shire, and now lay quiet as cats in a hedge, waiting for expected news from Ireland to give them their cue—waiting for a great rising of the West to sweep from her throne that stiff-necked, excommunicate usurper who called herself Queen of England. Their hope—like the rainbow’s foot that always seems to touch ground a field beyond your reach—skipped before them in those days as it has in many since: that England’s heart was truly theirs, and would return to the bosom of Rome, and that Elizabeth would be led in chains to the Old Man of the Seven Hills.

At times their language upon this point terrified old Mr. Leigh. One day, as Eustace came to his father’s private room after his usual descent to the mill, he heard voices lifted high. Parsons blustered as was his wont; Mr. Leigh answered peevishly, as men do when fear wears a thin coat of anger; and Campian—who was in truth the gentlest-tempered of men—tried to lay oil upon the troubled water. Eustace, for the good of the cause (as he would have named it), stopped outside and listened.

“My excellent sir,” Mr. Leigh was saying, “does not your very presence here show how I am affected toward the holy cause of the Catholic faith? But I cannot, in the meanwhile, forget that I am an Englishman.”

“And what is England?” said Parsons. “A heretic and schismatic Babylon, whereof it is written, ‘Come out of her, my people, lest you be partaker of her plagues.’ Yea, what is a country? An arbitrary division of territory by the princes of this world, who are naught, and come to naught. They are created by the people’s will; their existence depends on the sanction of him to whom all power is given in heaven and earth—our Holy Father the Pope. Take away the latter, and what is a king? The people who have made him may unmake him.”

“My dear sir, recollect that I have sworn allegiance to Queen Elizabeth!”

“Yes, sir, you have, sir; and, as I have shown at large in my writings, you were absolved from that allegiance from the moment that the bull of Pius the Fifth declared her a heretic and excommunicate, and thereby to have forfeited all dominion whatsoever. I tell you, sir, what I thought you should have known already, that since the year 1569, England has had no queen, no magistrates, no laws, no lawful authority whatsoever; and that to own allegiance to any English magistrate, sir, or to plead in an English court of law, is to disobey the apostolic precept, ‘How dare you go to law before the unbelievers?’ I tell you, sir, rebellion is now not merely permitted, it is a duty.”

“Take care, sir; for God’s sake, take care!” said Mr. Leigh. “Right or wrong, I cannot have such language used in my house. For the sake of my wife and children, I cannot!”

“My dear brother Parsons, deal more gently with the flock,” put in Campian. “Your opinion, though probable, as I well know, in the eyes of most of our order, is hardly safe enough here; the opposite is at least so safe that Mr. Leigh may well excuse his conscience for accepting it. After all, are we not sent hither to proclaim this very thing, and to relieve the souls of good Catholics from a burden which has seemed to them too heavy?”

“Yes,” said Parsons, half-sulkily, “to allow all Balaams who will to sacrifice to Baal, while they call themselves by the name of the Lord.”

“My dear brother, have I not often reminded you that Naaman was allowed to bow himself in the house of Rimmon? And can we therefore complain of the office to which the Holy Father has appointed us—to declare to such as Mr. Leigh his especial grace, by which the bull of Pius the Fifth (on whose soul God have mercy!) shall henceforth bind the queen and the heretics only; but in no ways the Catholics, at least as long as the present tyranny prevents the pious purposes of the bull?”

“Be it so, sir; be it so. Only observe this, Mr. Leigh, that our brother Campian confesses this to be a tyranny. Observe, sir, that the bull does still bind the so-called queen, and that she and her magistrates are still none the less usurpers, nonentities, and shadows of a shade. And observe this, sir, that when that which is lawful is excused to the weak, it remains no less lawful to the strong. The seven thousand who had not bowed the knee to Baal did not slay his priests; but Elijah did, and won to himself a good reward. And if the rest of the children of Israel sinned not in not slaying Eglon, yet Ehud’s deed was none the less justified by all laws human and divine.”

“For Heaven’s sake, do not talk so, sir! or I must leave the room. What have I to do with Ehud and Eglon, and slaughters, and tyrannies? Our queen is a very good queen, if Heaven would but grant her repentance, and turn her to the true faith. I have never been troubled about religion, nor any one else that I know of in the West country.”

“You forget Mr. Trudgeon of Launceston, father, and poor Father Mayne,” said Eustace, slipping in at last; and Campian added softly—

“Yes, your West of England also has been honored by its martyrs, as well as my London by the precious blood of Story.”

“What, young malapert?” cried Mr. Leigh, turning upon his son with sudden relief, glad to find a safer target for his vexation. “Are you too against me, with a murrain on you? And pray, what the devil brought Cuthbert Mayne to the gallows, and turned Mr. Trudgeon—he was always a foolish hot-head—out of house and home, but just such treasonable talk as Mr. Parsons must needs hold in my house, to make a beggar of me and my children, as he will before he has done.”

“The Blessed Virgin forbid!” said Campian.

“The Blessed Virgin forbid? But you must help her to forbid it, Mr. Campian. We should never have had the law of 1571, against bulls, and Agnus Deis, and blessed grains, if the Pope’s bull of 1569 had not made them matter of treason, by preventing a poor creature’s saving his soul in the true Church without putting his neck into a halter by denying the queen’s authority.”

“What, sir?” almost roared Parsons. “Do you dare to speak evil of the edicts of the Vicar of Christ?”

“I? No. I didn’t. Who says I did? All I meant was, I am sure—Mr. Campian, you are a reasonable man, speak for me.”

“Mr. Leigh only meant,” Campian soothed, “that the Holy Father’s prudent intentions have been so far defeated by the perverseness and invincible misunderstanding of the heretics, that that which was in itself meant for the good of the oppressed English Catholics has been perverted to their harm.”

“And thus, reverend sir,” Eustace added quickly, eager to stand again in his father’s good graces, “my father attaches blame, not to the Pope—Heaven forbid!—but to the pravity of his enemies.”

“And it is for this very reason,” said Campian, “that we have brought with us the present merciful explanation of the bull.”

“I’ll tell you what, gentlemen,” said Mr. Leigh, who, like other weak men, found courage as soon as his adversary seemed inclined to make peace, “I don’t think the declaration was needed. After the new law of 1571 was made, it was never put in force till Mayne and Trudgeon made fools of themselves, and that was full six years. There were a few offenders, they say, who were brought up and admonished, and let go; but even that did not happen down here, and need not happen now, unless you put my son here (for you shall never put me, I warrant you) upon some deed which had better be left alone, and so bring us all to shame.”

“Your son, sir, if not openly vowed to God, has, I hope, a due sense of that inward vocation which we have seen in him,” said Parsons, “and reverences his spiritual fathers too well to listen to the temptations of his earthly father.”

“What, sir, will you teach my son to disobey me?”

“Your son is ours also, sir. This is strange language in one who owes a debt to the Church, which it was charitably fancied he meant to pay in the person of his child.”

Those last words struck Mr. Leigh where he was tenderest. His face worked; and, breaking all bounds, he swore at Parsons, who stood foaming with righteous rage.

“A plague upon you, sir, and a black assizes for you, for you will come to the gallows yet! Do you mean to taunt me in my own house with that Hartland land? You had better go back and ask those who sent you where the dispensation to hold the land is, which they promised to get me years ago, and have gone on putting me off, till they have got my money, and my son, and my conscience, and I vow before all the saints, seem now to want my head over and above. God help me!” And the poor man’s eyes filled outright.

Now Eustace was stung at last; for, after all, he was an Englishman and a gentleman, and there is a point at which even devotion begins to smell like a bargain ill-kept. He spoke kindly enough, but firm.

“Courage, my dearest father.
