Remember that I am still your son, and not a Jesuit yet; and whether I ever become one, I promise you, will depend mainly on the treatment which you meet with at the hands of these reverend gentlemen, for whom I, as having brought them hither, must consider myself as surety to you.”

If a powder-barrel had burst in their faces, the Jesuits could scarcely have looked more blank. Campian stared at Parsons; Parsons at Campian; and then Parsons, recovering breath first, snapped—

“Sir! do you know, sir, the curse pronounced on those who, after putting their hand to the plough, look back?”

Eustace was one of those impulsive men who dare raise the devil and then fear him; and he tried to pass his boldness off by winks and signs toward his father, as much as to say he spoke only to quiet an old man’s terrors. But Campian was too startled, and Parsons too hot, to take his hint; and Eustace, having stepped out, must needs stand.

“All I read is, Father Parsons,” he answered, “that such are not fit for the kingdom of God; of which high honor I have for some time past felt myself unworthy. I have much doubt just now as to my vocation; and in the meanwhile have not forgotten that I am a citizen of a free country.” And with that he took his father’s arm and led him out.

Those last words hit hard. All their schoolmen’s subtlety, all their ciphers, dispensations, and indulgences, were on this side of the Channel but a cloud-castle, a painted mist, which one touch of the constable’s staff could scatter. “A citizen of a free country!” That was the stumbling-stone; and they looked at one another in real perplexity.

At last Parsons spoke, low and keen. “There’s a woman in the wind. I’ll lay my life on it. I saw him blush up crimson yesterday when his mother asked him whether some Rose Salterne or other was still in the neighborhood.”

“A woman!” said Campian. “Well, the spirit may be willing though the flesh be weak. We will inquire into this. The youth may do us service as a layman; and if aught should happen to his elder brother (whom the saints protect!) he is heir to some wealth. In the meanwhile, our dear brother Parsons will perhaps see the expediency of altering our tactics somewhat while we are here.”

And then began between them one of those long consultations in which two men, sent together by the wise method of their order, each served as bridle and spur to the other—Campian to soften, Parsons to drive; and each, if need be, could give counsel almost contradictory to his fellow, without seeming changeable, because “two are better than one.”

The upshot was this: that, in a day or two—during which Mr. Leigh and Eustace made their amends, and outward peace was kept—Father Campian asked Father Francis, the household chaplain, to grant him as a special favor the hearing of Eustace’s usual confession on the following Friday.

Poor Father Francis dared not refuse so great a name, and consented with an inward groan, knowing well what such favor meant: to be gently undermined, to have family secrets drawn out like a fishbone from the throat, and the Jesuits’ power increased as his waned. For between the regular priesthood and the Society in England there ran an armed neutrality, which wanted little to become open war.

Campian heard Eustace’s confession; and by questions such as the confessional breeds—questions that creep and circle, drawing closer each time like a hawk’s shadow—found, to his satisfaction, that the young man was what Campian would have called “in love.” He smiled, and set himself briskly to discover who the lady might be.

Had he said plainly, “I feel for you; if your desire be lawful, I will help you,” he might have had the whole of Eustace’s heart at once. But he had been trained to distrust, to take the basest motive as the likeliest spring of action, and therefore took the crooked road—expecting the worst and prying for it. He began his delicate tortures, and Eustace, knowing well the father’s cunning and yet delaying surrender as long as he could, dodged like a hare before the stoat: now pretending stupidity, now surprise, now scruple, now anger. Was she married or single? Catholic or heretic? English or foreign? Each question brought Campian a step nearer.

At last, when Campian—finding the case not so black as he had supposed—asked something of her worldly wealth, Eustace saw a door and sprang at it.

“Even if she be a heretic, she is heiress to one of the wealthiest merchants in Devon.”

“Ah,” said Campian thoughtfully. “And she is but eighteen, you say?”

“Only eighteen.”

“Ah. Well, my son, there is time. She may be reconciled to the Church: or you may change.”

“I shall die first.”

“Ah, poor lad! Well; she may be reconciled, and her wealth may be of use to the cause of Heaven.”

“And it shall be of use. Only absolve me, and let me be at peace. Let me have but her,” he cried, with a sudden raw hunger in his voice, as if he begged not for doctrine but for bread. “I do not want her wealth—not I! Let me have but her, and that but for one year, one month, one day!—and all the rest—money, fame, talents, yea, my life itself, hers if it be needed—are at the service of Holy Church. Ay, I shall glory in showing my devotion by some special sacrifice—some desperate deed. Prove me now, and see what there is I will not do!”

So Eustace was absolved. And afterward Campian, as if he had been waiting for that very cry, said quietly—

“This is indeed well, my son: for there is a thing to be done now, but it may be at the risk of life.”

“Prove me!” Eustace answered at once, impatient to be set to any task that might purchase peace.

“Here is a letter brought me last night; no matter from whence. You can understand it better than I, and I longed to show it you, but that I feared my son had become—”

“You feared wrongly, then, my dear Father Campian.”

Campian put the paper before him and translated the cipher.

“This to Evan Morgans, gentleman, at Mr. Leigh’s house in Moorwinstow, Devonshire. News may be had by one who will go to the shore of Clovelly, any evening after the 25th of November, at dead low tide, and there watch for a boat, rowed by one with a red beard, and a Portugal by his speech. If he be asked, ‘How many?’ he will answer, ‘Eight hundred and one.’ Take his letters and read them. If the shore be watched, let him who comes show a light three times in a safe place under the cliff above the town; below is dangerous landing. Farewell, and expect great things!”

“I will go,” said Eustace. “To-morrow is the 25th, and I know a sure and easy place. Your friend seems to know these shores well.”

“Ah—what is it we do not know?” said Campian, with a little mysterious smile, as if the sea itself were in their keeping. “And now?”

“And now,” said Eustace, with the sudden boyish daring of a man who wants approval as much as absolution, “to prove to you how I trust you, you shall come with me, and see this—the lady of whom I spoke—and judge for yourself whether my fault is not a venial one.”

“Ah, my son, have I not absolved you already? What have I to do with fair faces?” Campian answered; yet he rose. “Nevertheless, I will come; both to show you I trust you, and it may be to help toward reclaiming a heretic, and saving a lost soul. Who knows?”

So they set out together. The hill between Chapel and Stow mill lifted them above the valley’s steady water-speech, and the air there had that clean edge of late autumn, smelling of turned earth and woodsmoke. And, as it was appointed, they had scarce reached the top when up the lane came Mistress Rose Salterne herself, bright as a berry against the hedges, in all the glories of a new scarlet hood.

From beneath it her large dark languid eyes sent soft lightnings—no sermon, no rubric, no papal bull could have matched their swiftness—straight through poor Eustace’s heart and marrow. Up she tripped on delicate ankles and tiny feet, tall, lithe, and graceful, a true West-country lass; and as she passed them with a blush and courtesy, even Campian, who had vowed himself against vanity, turned to look back at the innocent creature. Her long dark curls, after the country fashion then, rolled down from beneath the hood below her waist; and it seemed to Eustace that those glossy nets had taken his very soul.

“There,” he whispered, trembling. “Can you excuse me now?”

“I had excused you long ago,” said Campian kindly. “Alas, that so much fair red and white should have been created only as a feast for worms!”

“A feast for gods, you mean!” Eustace cried; and the very homely absurdity of Campian’s pious phrase struck his common sense like a clap. Then, as if to escape the rebuke he knew he had earned, he burst out—

“Will you let me return for a moment? I will follow you. Let me go!”

Campian saw refusal would be wasted breath, and only nodded. Eustace darted away across a field, cut the corner of hedge and ditch, and met Rose full at the next bend of the road.

She started, and a little shriek slipped from her like a bird’s call.

“Mr. Leigh! I thought you had gone forward.”

“I came back to speak to you, Rose—Mistress Salterne, I mean.”

“To me?”

“To you I must speak, tell you all, or die!” He pressed up close. She shrank, startled by the heat in him.

“Do not stir; do not go, I implore you! Rose, only hear me!” And, fiercely and passionately, he caught her hand and poured out the whole story of his love, heaping her with every fantastic epithet that a young man, fed on sonnets and fever, can coin.

Much of it Rose had heard before—Bideford had its gallants, and posies, and verses enough—but there was a quiver now in his voice, and a hard fire in his eye, that made her draw back by instinct as from something that might burn.

“Let me go!” she said. “You are too rough, sir!”

“Ay!” he answered, and seized both her hands now, as if he could hold her by sheer will. “Rougher, perhaps, than the gay gallants of Bideford, who serenade you, and write sonnets to you, and send you posies. Rougher, but more loving, Rose! Do not turn away! I shall die if you take your eyes off me! Tell me—tell me, now, here—this moment—before we part—if I may love you!”

“Go away!” she cried, struggling, and bursting into tears. “This is too rude. If I am but a merchant’s daughter, I am God’s child. Remember that I am alone. Leave me; go! or I will call for help!”

Eustace had somewhere heard, or read, that such words in a woman’s mouth were but a fashion of speaking, and meant the very opposite of what they said; and he held her hands the more fiercely, staring into her face with hungry, blinded eyes. But Rose was in earnest. Her next shriek rang out sharp and clear along the lane, startling the birds in the hedge; and in that moment he knew—if he would keep his own good name—that he must let her go.

Yet there was one question, for the answer to which he would have risked his very life.

The next morning Amyas Leigh was nowhere in sight.

Not that he had ridden out to fling himself into the Torridge for a lover’s cure, nor sat sulking on a bank to count his losses with the lap of water for company. He had simply saddled early and gone straight toward Stow, to Sir Richard Grenville; and his mother, reading him as women read weather, guessed at once the errand—an Irish commission, a new field in which a man might spend his anger without confessing its true name. She sent Frank after him, to fetch him home, or at least to make him pause and taste his own purpose before he swallowed it whole.

Frank took horse and rode ten miles and more; and because there were no inns upon that road then—nor, truth to tell, are there many now—and because there lay yet another stretch of hill and wind ahead, he turned down toward Clovelly Court to ask, in the old fashion of the country, meat for himself and mash for his beast at Mr. Cary’s door.

When he walked in unbidden, as gentlemen did in those days when hospitality was as sure as the tide, into the long dim wainscoted hall, the first thing he saw was Amyas—mighty in limb and appetite—set at the board as if he meant to conquer grief by main strength. A great pasty lay before him, its crust already broken like earth after the plough; and he was alternately burying his face in it and the pasty in his face, as if sorrow had sharpened him to a keener hunger. Over against him, young Will Cary knelt on the opposite bench, elbows on the table, leaning forward with that earnest grace boys have when they think themselves men, and delivering sentence in a fierce low voice.

“Hillo! lad,” cried Amyas, mouth full and eyes brightening at the sight of Frank. “Come hither and deliver me out of the hands of this fire-eater, who, I verily believe, will kill me, if I do not let him kill some one else.”

“Ah, Mr. Frank,” said Will Cary, rising half up and flushing with pleasure, for he held Frank as all the young gentlemen thereabout held him—an oracle of fashion and chivalry—“welcome. I was longing for you too. I wanted your counsel on half-a-dozen matters. Sit down, and eat. There is the ale.”

“None so early, thank you,” said Frank, drawing near the board; for the hall smelled of peat-ash and warmed oak and that good, thick savor which rises when baked meat and pepper and browned crust have sat a while and breathed into the air.

“Ah no!” said Amyas, and plunged his face into the tankard with a gurgle, then lifted it and mimicked Frank’s sober tone: “Avoid strong ale o’ mornings. It heats the blood, thickens the animal spirits, and obfuscates the cerebrum with frenetical and lymphatic idols, which cloud the quintessential light of the pure reason. Eh? young Plato, young Daniel, come hither to judgment! And yet, though I cannot see through the bottom of the tankard already, I can see plain enough still to see this—that Will shall not fight.”

“Shall I not, eh? Who says that?” cried Will, striking the table so that the trenchers jumped. “Mr. Frank, I appeal to you. Only hear me.”

“We are in the judgment-seat,” said Frank, settling himself and setting knife to the pasty’s firm crust. It broke with a faint crackle, letting out a hot breath of minced beef, herbs, and rich brown gravy that clung to the air like a remembered song. “Proceed, appellant.”

“Well then—I was telling Amyas—that Tom Coffin, of Portledge; I will stand him no longer.”

“Let him be, then,” said Amyas. “He could stand very well by himself when I saw him last.”

“Plague on you—hold your tongue. Has he any right to look at me as he does whenever I pass him?”

“That depends on how he looks,” said Amyas. “A cat may look at a king, provided she don’t take him for a mouse.”

“Oh, I know how he looks, and what he means too, and he shall stop, or I will stop him. And the other day, when I spoke of Rose Salterne—”

“Ah!” groaned Frank, and set his teeth into his portion as if to keep down impatience with the mouthful. “Eve’s apple again!”

“—never mind what I said—he burst out laughing in my face; and is not that a fair quarrel? And what is more, I know that he wrote a sonnet, and sent it to her to Stow by a market woman. What right has he to write sonnets when I can’t? It’s not fair play, Mr. Frank, or I am a Jew, and a Spaniard, and a Papist; it’s not!”

“My dear knight of the burning pestle,” said Amyas, wiping a smear of gravy from his lip with the back of his hand, “I have a plan, a device, a disentanglement, according to most approved rules of chivalry. Let us fix a day, and summon by tuck of drum all young gentlemen under the age of thirty, dwelling within fifteen miles of the habitation of that peerless Oriana.”

“And all ’prentice-boys too,” cried Amyas suddenly, and dived again into the pasty.

“And all ’prentice-boys,” Will caught up, half-laughing in spite of himself.

“The bold lads shall fight first with good quarterstaves in Bideford Market till all heads are broken; and the head which is not broken, let the back belonging to it pay the penalty of the noble member’s cowardice. After which grand tournament—to which that of Tottenham shall be but a flea-bite and a batrachomyomachy—”

“Confound you and your long words, sir,” said Will, reddening. “I know you are flouting me.”

“Pazienza, Signor Cavaliere,” answered Amyas, with mock gravity. “That which is to come is no flouting, but bloody and warlike earnest. For afterwards all the young gentlemen shall adjourn into a convenient field, sand, or bog—bog will be best, for no man can run away if he be up to his knees in soft peat—and there, stripping to our shirts, with rapiers of equal length and keenest temper, each shall slay his man, catch who catch can; and the conquerors fight again, like a most valiant main of gamecocks as we are, till all be dead and out of their woes; after which the survivor, bewailing before heaven and earth the cruelty of our fair Oriana, and the slaughter which her basiliscine eyes have caused, shall fall gracefully upon his sword, and so end the woes of this our lovelorn generation. Placetne Domini? as they used to ask in the Senate at Oxford.”

“Really,” said Will, mortified, “this is too bad.”

“So is,” said Frank quietly, cutting through the pasty’s thick heart where meat and spice were packed tight, “your fighting Mr. Coffin with anything longer than a bodkin.”

“Bodkins are too short for such fierce Bobadils,” returned Amyas. “They would close in so near, we should have them falling to fisticuffs after the first bout.”

“Then let them fight with squirts across the market-place,” said Frank, and his tone—still mild—rang with a law that belonged to more than grammar and Latin. “For by heaven and the queen’s laws, they shall fight with nothing else.”

“My dear Mr. Cary,” Frank went on, and his voice softened into that winning music which made men listen against their will, “do not fancy that I cannot feel for you, or that I, as well as you, have not known the stings of love and the bitterer stings of jealousy. But does it not seem to you a dreadful thing to waste selfishly upon your own quarrel that noble wrath which—if Plato says true—is the root of all virtues, and which has been given you, like all else you have, that you may spend it in the service of her whom all bad souls fear and all virtuous souls adore—our peerless queen? Who dares, while she rules England, call his sword or courage his own, or any one’s but hers? Are there no Spaniards to conquer, no wild Irish to deliver from their oppressors, that two gentlemen of Devon can find no better place to flesh their blades than in each other’s valiant and honorable hearts?”

“By heaven!” cried Amyas, smiting the board with the flat of his hand so the tankards rang like small bells. “Frank speaks like a book. And for me, I do think Christian gentlemen may leave love quarrels to bulls and rams.”

“And that the heir of Clovelly,” said Frank, smiling a little, “may find nobler examples to copy than the stags in his own deer-park.”

“Well,” said Will, subdued and yet still prickling with pride, “you are a great scholar, Mr. Frank, and you speak like one. But gentlemen must fight sometimes, or where would be their honor?”

“I speak,” said Frank, and a touch of pride came into him now, “not merely as a scholar, but as a gentleman, and one who has fought ere now—and to whom it has happened to kill his man (on whose soul may God have mercy); but it is my pride to remember that I have never yet fought in my own quarrel, and my trust in God that I never shall. For as there is nothing more noble and blessed than to fight in behalf of those whom we love, so to fight in our own private behalf is a thing not to be allowed to a Christian man, unless refusal imports utter loss of life or honor; and even then—though I would not lay a burden on any man’s conscience—it may be better not to resist evil, but to overcome it with good.”

“And I can tell you, Will,” said Amyas, suddenly grave beneath his jesting, “I am not troubled with fear of ghosts; but when I cut off the Frenchman’s head, I said to myself, ‘If that braggart had been slandering me instead of her gracious majesty, I should expect to see that head lying on my pillow every time I went to bed at night.’”

“God forbid!” said Will, and shuddered. “But what shall I do? For to the market to-morrow I will go, if it were choke-full of Coffins, and a ghost in each coffin of the lot.”

“Leave the matter to me,” said Amyas. “I have my device as well as scholar Frank here; and if there be, as I suppose there must be, a quarrel in the market to-morrow, see if I do not—”

“Well, you are two good fellows,” said Will, finding comfort where young men always find it—in company and noise. “Let us have another tankard in.”

“And drink the health of Mr. Coffin, and all gallant lads of the North,” said Frank. Then, wiping his fingers and glancing keenly at Amyas, he added, “And now to my business. I have to take this runaway youth here home to his mother; and if he will not go quietly, I have orders to carry him across my saddle.”

“I hope your nag has a strong back, then,” laughed Amyas; yet there was that under his laugh which said he meant to ride his own way. “But I must go on and see Sir Richard, Frank. It is all very well to jest, but my mind is made up.”

“Stop,” said Will quickly. “You must stay here to-night; first, for good fellowship’s sake; and next, because I want the counsel of our Phoenix here—our oracle, our paragon. There, Mr. Frank, can you construe that for me? Speak low, though, gentlemen both; there comes my father. You had better give me the letter again.”

Even as he spoke, the sound of boots and spurs came down the passage; with it the lighter sounds that went everywhere with the old squire—hawk-bells tinkling, the soft pad and sudden sniff of spaniels, and, beyond these, the steady winter-murmur of the sea far below Clovelly, a breathing that filled the pauses between words and made the hall feel older than any man in it.

Old Mr. Cary strode in, weather in his face and comfort in his bones, and looked from one to another with the blunt kindness of a man who measured the world in acres, cattle, and honest stomachs.

“Eh, company here? Young men, you are always welcome, and such as you. Would there were more of your sort in these dirty times! How is your good mother, Frank, eh? Where have I been, Will? Round the house-farm, to look at the beeves. That sheeted heifer of Prowse’s is all wrong; her coat stares like a hedgepig’s. Tell Jewell to go up and bring her in before night. And then up the forty acres—sprang two coveys and picked a leash out of them. The Irish hawk flies as wild as any haggard still, and will never make a bird. I had to hand her to Tom and take the little peregrine. Give me a Clovelly hawk against the world, after all; and—heigh ho—I am very hungry! Half-past twelve and dinner not served? What, Master Amyas, spoiling your appetite with strong ale? Better have tried sack, lad; have some now with me.”

He finished his tale as if it were all one thread—beasts, birds, and boys—then settled on the great bench within the chimney. He set his hawk on the perch above his head; the hawk shifted, bells chiming softly, and blinked in the warm draught. The cockers curled themselves in the peat-ashes’ heat. Will knelt to tug off his father’s boots, and the old man lectured him between grunts to mind his corns.

“Come, Master Amyas,” said he, turning his head and sniffing as the smell of the kitchen drifted nearer—hot fat hissing somewhere, and the clean sharpness of onions cut for broth. “A pint of white wine and sugar, and a bit of a shoeing-horn to it ere we dine. Some pickled prawns now, or a rasher off the coals, to whet you?”

“Thank you,” said Amyas; “but I have drunk a mort of outlandish liquors, better and worse, in the last three years, and yet never found aught to come up to good ale; which needs neither shoeing-horn before nor after, but takes care of itself—and of all honest stomachs too, I think.”

“You speak like a book, boy,” said old Cary; “and after all, what a plague comes of these newfangled hot wines and aqua vitaes which have come in since the wars, but maddening of the brains and fever of the blood?”

“I fear we have not seen the end of that yet,” said Frank. “My friends write me from the Netherlands that our men are falling into a swinish trick of swilling like the Hollanders. Heaven grant they may not bring home the fashion with them.”

“A man must drink, they say, or die of the ague, in those vile swamps,” said Amyas. “When they get home here, they will not need it.”

“Heaven grant it,” said Frank. “I should be sorry to see Devonshire a drunken county; and there are many of our men out there with Mr. Champernoun.”

“Ah,” said Cary, “there, as in Ireland, we are proving her majesty’s saying true, that Devonshire is her right hand, and the young children thereof like the arrows in the hand of the giant.”

“They may well be,” said Will, glancing proudly at Amyas, “when some of them are giants themselves.”

“He will be up and doing again presently, I’ll warrant him,” said old Cary.

“And that I shall,” quoth Amyas, his eyes lighting with that boyish blaze which in him could turn, in a breath, to iron. “I have been devising brave deeds; and see in the distance enchanters to be bound, dragons choked, empires conquered—though not in Holland.”

“You do?” asked Will, a little sharply, because a young man’s jealousy has ears like a bat in a dusk hall.

“Yes,” said Amyas, and let the jest drop as if it were only a cap he had worn for sport. “I go to what Raleigh calls the Land of the Nymphs. Another month, I hope, will see me abroad in Ireland.”

“Abroad? Call it rather at home,” said old Cary, pleased; “for it is full of Devon men from end to end, and you will be among friends all day long. George Bourchier from Tawstock has the army now in Munster, and Warham St. Leger is marshal; George Carew is with Lord Grey of Wilton (poor Peter Carew was killed at Glendalough); and after the defeat last year, when that villain Desmond cut off Herbert and Price, the companies were made up with six hundred Devon men and Arthur Fortescue at their head; so that the old county holds her head as proudly in the Land of Ire as she does in the Low Countries and the Spanish Main.”

“And where,” asked Amyas, “is Davils of Marsland, who used to teach me how to catch trout when I was staying down at Stow? He is in Ireland too, is he not?”

“Ah, my lad,” said Mr. Cary, and the warmth of the chimney seemed to fall away a little. “That is a sad story. I thought all England had known it.”

“You forget, sir. I am a stranger. Surely he is not dead?”

“Murdered foully, lad! Murdered like a dog—and by the man whom he had treated as his son, and who pretended, the false knave, to call him father.”

“His blood is avenged?” asked Amyas, and the words came out as if they were drawn across a whetstone.

“No, by heaven, not yet! Stay—don’t cry out again. I am getting old. I must tell my story my own way.” He took breath, and somewhere behind the wall the kitchen began a new clatter—lids lifted, pans set down—domestic thunder going on as if no man’s death could interrupt a dinner. “It was last July—was it not, Will? Over comes to Ireland Saunders, one of those Jesuit foxes, as the Pope’s legate, with money and bulls and a banner hallowed by the Pope and the devil knows what beside; and with him James Fitzmaurice—the same fellow who had sworn on his knees to Perrott, in the church at Kilmallock, to be a true liegeman to Queen Elizabeth, and confirmed it by all his saints and such a world of his Irish howling that Perrott told me he was fain to stop his own ears. Well, he had been practising with the King of France, but got nothing but laughter for his pains, and so went over to the Most Catholic King and promises him to join Ireland to Spain and set up Popery again and what not. And he—thinking, I suppose, it better Ireland should belong to him than to the Pope’s bastard—fits him out and sends him off on such another errand as Stukely’s—though I will say, for the honor of Devon, if Stukely lived like a fool, he died like an honest man.”

“Sir Thomas Stukely dead too?” cried Amyas.

“Wait a while, lad, and you shall have that tragedy afterwards. Well—where was I? Oh. Fitzmaurice and the Jesuits land at Smerwick with three ships, choose a place for a fort, bless it with their holy water and their moppings and their scourings and the rest of it, to purify it from the stain of heretic dominion; but in the meanwhile one of the Courtenays—a Courtenay of Haccombe it was—lying at anchor near by in a ship of war of his, cuts out the three ships and cuts off the Dons from the sea. John and James Desmond, with some small rabble, go over to the Spaniards. Earl Desmond will not join them, but will not fight them neither, and stands by to take the winning side; and then in comes poor Davils, sent down by the Lord Deputy to charge Desmond and his brothers, in the queen’s name, to assault the Spaniards. Folks say it was rash of his lordship: but I say, what could be better done? Every one knows there never was a stouter or shrewder soldier than Davils; and the young Desmonds, I have heard him say many a time, used to look on him as their father. But he found out what it was to trust Englishmen turned Irish.

“Well—Desmonds find out on a sudden that the Dons are such desperate paladins, it is madness to meddle, though they are five to one; and poor Davils, seeing there is no fight in them, goes back for help, and sleeps that night at a place called Tralee. Arthur Carter of Bideford, St. Leger’s lieutenant—stout as Davils himself—sleeps in the same bed with him; the lacquey-boy, who is now with Sir Richard at Stow, on the floor at their feet. But in the dead of night, who should come in but James Desmond, sword in hand, with a dozen ruffians at his heels, each with his glib over his ugly face and his skene in his hand. Davils springs up in bed and asks but this—‘What is the matter, my son?’—whereon the treacherous villain, without giving him time to say a prayer, strikes at him—naked as he was—crying, ‘Thou shalt be my father no longer, nor I thy son! Thou shalt die!’ and at that all the rest fall on him. The poor little lad (so he says) leaps up to cover his master with his naked body, gets three or four stabs of skenes, and so falls for dead; with his master and Captain Carter, who were dead indeed—God reward them! After that the ruffians ransacked the house, till they had murdered every Englishman in it, the lacquey-boy only excepted, who crawled out, wounded as he was, through a window; while Desmond—if you will believe it—went back, up to his elbows in blood, and vaunted his deeds to the Spaniards, and asked them—‘There! Will you take that as a pledge that I am faithful to you?’ And that, my lad, was the end of Henry Davils—and will be of all who trust to the faith of wild savages.”

“I would go a hundred miles to see that Desmond hanged!” cried Amyas, and the tears ran down his face unashamed, dropping into the crumbs by his plate like sudden rain on a hard path. “Poor Mr. Davils! And now—what is the story of Sir Thomas?”

“Your brother must tell you that, lad,” panted old Cary. “I am somewhat out of breath.”

“And I have a right to tell it,” said Frank, and there was a strange lightness in him, like a man setting a bitter thing in a jest to make it go down. “Do you know that I was very near being Earl of the bog of Allen, and one of the peers of the realm to King Buoncompagna, son and heir to his holiness Pope Gregory the Thirteenth?”

“No, surely!”

“As I am a gentleman. When I was at Rome I saw poor Stukely often; and this and more he offered me on the Pope’s part—so he said—if I would oblige him in two little matters: being reconciled to the Catholic Church, and joining the invasion of Ireland.”

“Poor deluded heretic,” said Will Cary, with the easy zeal of youth, “to have lost an earldom for your family by such silly scruples of loyalty!”

“It is not a matter for jesting, after all,” said Frank. “But I saw Sir Thomas often, and I cannot believe he was in his senses, so frantic was his vanity and ambition; and all the while, in private matters, as honorable a gentleman as ever. However, he sailed at last for Ireland with his eight hundred Spaniards and Italians; and what is more, I know the King of Spain paid their charges. Marquis Vinola—James Buoncompagna, that is—stayed quietly at Rome, preferring that Stukely should conquer his paternal heritage of Ireland for him while he took care of the bona robas at home. I went down to Civita Vecchia to see him off; and though his younger by many years, I could not but take the liberty of entreating him, as a gentleman and a man of Devon, to consider his faith to his queen and the honor of his country. There were high words between us. God forgive me if I spoke too fiercely, for I never saw him again.”

“Too fiercely to an open traitor, Frank?” said Amyas hotly. “Why not have run him through?”

“Nay,” answered Frank, “I had no clean life for Sundays, Amyas; so I could not throw away my week-day one. And as for the weal of England, I knew it was little he would damage it, and told him so. At that he waxed utterly mad, for it touched his pride, and swore that if the wind had not been fair for sailing, he would have fought me there and then; to which I could only answer that I was ready to meet him when he would. And he parted from me, saying, ‘It is a pity, sir, I cannot fight you now; when next we meet, it will be beneath my dignity to measure swords with you.’

“I suppose he expected to come back a prince at least—Heaven knows. I owe him no ill-will; nor, I hope, does any man. He has paid all debts now in full, and got his receipt for them.”

“How did he die, then, after all?” asked Amyas.

“On his voyage he touched in Portugal. King Sebastian was just sailing for Africa with his new ally, Mohammed the Prince of Fez, to help King Abdallah, and conquer what he could. He persuaded Stukely to go with him. There were those who thought he, as well as the Spaniards, had no stomach for seeing the Pope’s son King of Ireland. Others said he thought an island too small for his ambition, and must needs conquer a continent. I know not why; but he went. They had heavy weather in the passage; and when they landed many soldiers were sea-sick. Stukely, reasonably enough, counselled they should wait two or three days and recruit; but Don Sebastian was so mad for the assault that he must needs have his veni, vidi, vici; and so ended with a veni, vidi, perii. For he, Abdallah, and his son Mohammed all perished in the first battle at Alcasar; and Stukely, surrounded and overpowered, fought till he could fight no more, and then died like a hero with all his wounds in front. And may God have mercy on his soul.”

“Ah!” said Amyas. “We heard of that battle off Lima, but nothing about poor Stukely.”

“That last was a Popish prayer, Master Frank,” grumbled old Cary, recovering breath and temper together.

“Most worshipful sir,” said Frank gently, “you surely would not wish God not to have mercy on his soul?”

“No—eh? Of course not. But that’s all settled by now, for he is dead, poor fellow.”

“Certainly, my dear sir. And you cannot help being a little fond of him still.”

Old Cary’s rough face softened against his will, like a hard crust that gives when the knife is warm. “Eh? why, I should be a brute if I were not. He and I were schoolfellows, though he was somewhat the younger; and many a good thrashing have I given him, and one cannot help having a tenderness for a man after that. Beside, we used to hunt together in Exmoor, and have royal nights afterward into Ilfracombe, when we were a couple of mad young blades. Fond of him? Why, I would have sooner given my forefinger than that he should have gone to the dogs thus.”

“Then, my dear sir,” said Frank, “if you feel for him still, in spite of all his faults, how do you know that God may not feel for him still, in spite of all his faults? For my part—without believing in that Popish Purgatory—I cannot help holding with Plato, that such heroical souls, who have wanted but little of true greatness, are hereafter by some strait discipline brought to a better mind; perhaps—so ancients have held with the Indian Gymnosophists—by transmigration into the bodies of those animals whom they have resembled in their passions; and indeed, if Sir Thomas Stukely’s soul should now animate the body of a lion, all I can say is that he would be a very valiant and royal lion; and also doubtless become in due time heartily ashamed and penitent for having been nothing better than a lion.”

“What now, Master Frank?” cried Cary, half laughing, half vexed. “I don’t trouble my head with such matters. I say Stukely was a right good-hearted fellow at bottom; and if you plague my head with any of your dialectics and propositions and college quips and quiddities, you sha’n’t have any more sack, sir. But here come the knaves—and I hear the cook knock to dinner.”

Dinner came in with the solid ceremony of the house—platters borne, lids lifted, steam rising. There was meat browned at the edges and glistening where the fat had basted it; there was bread with a crust like new-tanned leather and a crumb that yielded sweet; there were those pickled prawns the old man had boasted of, sharp as sea-wind on the tongue; and over all the comfort-sound of knives at work and tankards set down, and the low talk that is as much part of a meal as salt.

Afterward—after a madrigal or two, and one of Frank’s Italian songs, which ran sweetly under the rafters like a bird’s call and made the hall listen for a moment as if even the sea below had hushed to hear—the ladies rose and went.

Then Will Cary drew his chair close to Frank’s and, with the furtive haste of a boy hiding a burn, slipped a dirty letter into his hand.

“This was left for me,” he whispered, “by a country fellow this morning. Look at it and tell me what I am to do.”

Frank opened it and read, while the peat crackled and the hawk-bells chimed above, and the sound of the world outside—the wind, the distant wash of the Atlantic—pressed in at the shutters like a warning spoken in a tongue older than any of theirs:

“Mister Cary, be you wary  
By deer park end to-night.  
Yf Irish ffoxe com out of rocks  
Grip and hold hym tight.”

“I would have showed it my father,” Will breathed, “but—”

Far westward, where Devon and Cornwall clasp hands above the Atlantic, the land breaks suddenly into hidden combes—green cuts in the high table—each opening by a stone throat to the sea. All day they look like peace itself: oak-woods packed rich and brown inland, furze nearer the salt, then short turf, then the black iron cliffs shouldering out into the rollers in jagged castles and spires. In the bottom of each combe lies a strip of meadow, fat as a well-fed cow; a trout stream runs it like a bright seam, winding and doubling as though it cannot make up its mind to quit the hills; and beside it stands the gray mill, with water humming and sparkling round the wet wheel, a sound as old as the place—steady, consoling, domestic.

Yet the same valley that smells of bruised fern and warm hay by day ends at night in a mouth of teeth. At the cove’s edge the rock lies up in shark-tooth ridges, black and sharp, with here and there a pink thread of shell-sand caught in the cracks, and white foam lacing everything as the surge breathes in long, parallel lines toward the west. To landward, soft, sown, and sheltered; to seaward, a waste of roller and reef that gives nothing to the fisherman but danger, and to the shipwrecked nothing at all.

Only one of those mouths will take a boat with any mercy, because a long wall of rock breaks the Atlantic’s shoulders and makes a small lee. That mouth is Marsland—Lucy Passmore’s place, the White Witch’s place; and to Marsland, as Sir Richard Grenville guessed, the Jesuits had bent their secret steps.

But before their coming, two women were already there under an October moon: Rose Salterne, heated with fear and longing, and Lucy Passmore herself, solid as a boundary-stone, sharp as a thorn, and bustling with that kind of courage which is half anger and half mother-wit.

“You be safe enough here to-night, miss,” Lucy said, as if she had hired the very darkness to do her bidding. “My old man’s snoring sound abed; and there’s no other soul sets foot here o’ nights—save it be mermaids now and then. Goodness, Father—where’s our boat? It ought to be up here on the pebbles.”

Rose, drawing her cloak closer against the damp that smelled of kelp and cold iron, pointed down the beach. The boat lay upon a strip of sand nearer the sea, dark as a beetle in moonshine.

“Oh, the lazy old villain!” Lucy scolded, and her voice—high, brisk, all edges—cut clean through the hush of the cove. “He’s been round the rocks after pollock, and never taken the trouble to hale her up. I’ll trounce him for it when I get home. I only hope he’s made her fast, that’s all! He’s more plague to me than ever my money will be. O deary me!”

Down she went, skirts gathered, and Rose after her, stumbling on the round shingle that rattled like poured beans. The sea lay strangely still; no roller broke within the little wall of rock. Even the gulls, which by day screamed above the cliffs like quarrelling fishwives, were asleep on the ledges. It was one of those autumn silences which are not empty at all, but so full of minute sound—drips, far trickles, the faint sifting of sand—that the ear feels it like a pressure.

“Iss, ’tis fast, sure enough,” Lucy muttered, stooping to the rope. “And the oars aboard too! Well, I never! Oh, the lazy thief, to leave they here to be stole! I’ll just sit in the boat, dear, and watch mun, while you go down to the say; for you must be all alone to yourself, you know, or you’ll see nothing. There’s the looking-glass. Now go, and dip your head three times, and mind you don’t look to land or sea before you’ve said the words, and looked upon the glass. Be quick—it’s just upon midnight.”

She coiled her ample body in the stern-sheets like a broody hen in a basket, and Rose went faltering down the last strip of sand between two rock walls. On her left the cliff rose sheer and threw a deep shade; on her right the lower rock took the moon full in the face, and the sea-weed hung from it in purple festoons, wet and glossy, smelling rankly of salt and life. Those streaming fronds shaded cracks and crevices fit, in a girl’s fancy, for anything that had never been christened.

The breeze had died. The cove held its breath. Rose, stripped and shivering, stood a moment with the mirror cold against her palm and the sea’s cold smell in her mouth—metallic and clean, like a knife newly whetted. She listened for some proof that she was not the only living thing in that hollow. A faint bleat, as of a new-dropped lamb, came thinly from above; she started and stared up. Then a wail from the cliffs, like a child in pain, and another answering from the opposite rock. Snipe passing in the dark; an otter calling to her brood—common enough if you had been born to the place and knew its tongues. But Rose did not know the cliff-language; she knew only that the sounds found her heart and pinched it.

Lucy had warned her: fear nothing. If the rite were done duly, no harm could come.

Rose waded in.

The water bit like teeth; she gasped, and the sound seemed loud as a shout in that hush. She went as far as she dared, mirror held up, and then stopped as if a hand had caught her. A ring of pale fire ran round her waist. Each limb, each moving finger, each trembling knee, was bathed in lambent light. The sea itself, stirred by her, kindled into innumerable sparks; little globes of living flame swam and broke and drifted away, and the clear water below her shone as though star-dust had been shaken into it. She could see every shell crawling on the sand at her feet, every rock-fish darting in and out of the crannies and staring up with broad bright eyes. The great brown oar-weed, half seen in the glimmer, waved like long hands beckoning her down into its cold bowers.

For one instant she would have fled—not from witchcraft, but from the simple terror of being made so bright in such a dark place, as if the sea itself had chosen her for its lantern. But she had gone too far to retreat without confessing herself a coward to Lucy, and—worse—to her own superstition.

Hastily she dipped her head three times. Water ran in icy threads down her spine; her teeth clicked. She hurried back to the marge and, through her dripping locks, lifted the mirror to the moonlight and whispered the charm, careful, as she had been told, not to glance to land or sea until the last word was said:

“A maiden pure, here I stand,
Neither on sea, nor yet on land;
Angels watch me on either hand.
If you be landsman, come down the strand;
If you be sailor, come up the sand;
If you be angel, come from the sky,
Look in my glass, and pass me by;
Look in my glass, and go from the shore;
Leave me, but love me for evermore.”

She had scarcely finished; her eyes strained into the mirror, where nothing showed but the sparkle of drops shaken from her own tresses, when the cove’s silence split at once—pebbles rattling under hurried feet, horses slipping and striking sparks from stone.

She darted into a cavern in the higher rock, where sea-weed lay wet as soaked rope and barnacles roughened the walls. There she dragged on her clothes with hands that could scarcely obey her, and crouched, holding her breath, while the men came straight to the boat.

Four of them. Two had leaped from their horses and turned the beasts loose to scramble away; the other two ran the boat down with quick, practised pushes.

Then, out of the stern-sheets, rose Lucy Passmore like a fat ghost in a nightcap, and shrieked in her shrillest treble, loud enough to startle the sleeping gulls:

“Eh! ye villains, ye roogs, what do ye want staling poor folks’ boats by night like this?”

The whole party recoiled, and one, in sheer panic at the sudden apparition, turned to run up the beach shouting, “’Tis a marmaiden—a marmaiden asleep in Willy Passmore’s boat!”

“I wish ’twere any sich good luck,” Rose heard a man whimper—Will Passmore himself. “’Tis my wife, oh dear!” and he cowered as if expecting a blow.

He received it duly. Lucy, leaping out, cuffed him soundly and thundered that he should go home to bed; then planted herself before the strangers with all the belligerence of a goose defending a pond.

Rose, peeping from her crevice, saw what Lucy had seen: among the men was that ungainly Welsh gentleman whose calling she had guessed too clearly already; and Lucy, loyal in her fierce, rude way, held “Popish skulkers” in honest horror—unless paid to tolerate them.

In vain Father Parsons stormed; in vain Campian pleaded with gentle voice and scholar’s Latin; in vain Mr. Leigh’s groom swore and fidgeted and tried to look brave. Lucy stood with hands on hips and eyes like coals.

“No,” she cried. “As I am an honest woman and loyal! This is why you left the boat down to the shoore, you old traitor, you, is it? To help off sich noxious trade as this out of the hands of her majesty’s quorum and rotulorum? Eh? Stand back, cowards! Will you strike a woman?”

The speech, as usual, was invitation rather than warning; for she seized an oar and swung it round and round with a hiss through the air, the wood whistling in the moonlight. The sound of it—clean, circular, merciless—made even armed men step back. At last she brought it down across Father Parsons’s shins with such a crack that he retreated howling, hopping on one foot on the shingle.

“Lucy, Lucy!” shrieked her husband in a Devon falsetto, “be you mazed? Be you mazed, lass? They promised me two gold nobles before I’d lend them the boot!”

“Tu?” Lucy flung at him, the syllable full of scorn as if it were a stone. “And do yu call yourself a man?”

“Tu nobles! tu nobles!” he squealed again, hopping about just out of oar’s reach.

“Tu? And would you sell your soul under ten?”

“Oh, if that is it,” cried Campian, between a laugh and a wince, “give her ten, give her ten, brother Pars—Morgans, I mean; and take care of your shins, Offa Cerbero, you know—Oh, virago! Furens quid faemina possit! Certainly she is some Lamia, some Gorgon, some—”

“Take that, for your Lamys and Gorgons to an honest woman!”

The oar flashed again. Campian’s thin legs were cut from under him; he went down in the shingle, and Lucy, in a fury half comic and half terrible, mounted astride his trunk and cried, breathless:

“Ten nobles, or I’ll kep ye here till morning!”

Ten nobles clinked into her palm at last. The sound of coins—heavy, satisfying, unmistakably real—seemed to thicken the air and bring the whole strange business back from sorcery into bargain. Lucy pocketed them with the swift neatness of a housewife tucking away spice, and in the same breath ordered the boat run down and done with it.

They thrust it into the water. It passed close by Rose’s hiding-place, so near that she could smell the tar and wet wood, and hear water gurgle under the keel. Her heart hammered so loudly she feared it would betray her. Lucy followed them close—not solely for her fee, Rose guessed, but to hold the night in her own hands.

And Lucy, shrewd as a ferret, had seen the dark cavern by the water’s edge and guessed what it sheltered. As the boat moved, she planted her ample body directly across the cavern mouth, grumbling loud enough to cover any movement within—at her husband, at the strangers, and most of all at Mr. Leigh’s groom. To him she prophesied Launceston gaol and the gallows with a relish that made the serving-man’s teeth chatter. He dared not answer her. He dodged and pleaded and kept one of the priests between himself and her, as if the very line of her eye could blight him again.

The boat was launched; the men shoved off. Rose, in the dark behind Lucy’s skirts, could hear the oars bite, the low creak of rowlocks, the faint splash that travelled out into the still cove like a whispered secret.

Then a new sound came—a faint halloo from the beach, and after it the clatter of a horse plunging down the pebbles. In another minute a rider swept along the sand and pulled up so hard that the horse sat back on its haunches. The man dropped rather than leaped from the saddle.

The serving-man, heartened by something he understood at last—steel against steel—drew and sprang upon the newcomer, then recoiled as if struck.

“God forgive me, it’s Mr. Eustace! Oh, dear sir, I took you for one of Sir Richard’s men! Oh, sir, you’re hurt!”

“A scratch—a scratch!” Eustace Leigh almost moaned; and his voice, usually so smooth, was broken, raw, as if the night air had rasped it. “Help me into the boat, Jack. Gentlemen, I must with you.”

“Not with us, surely, my dear son—vagabonds upon the face of the earth?” said Campian, pity softening even his scholar’s jest.

“With you, for ever. All is over here. Whither God and the cause lead—” He staggered toward the boat.

As he passed the cavern, the moonlight struck his face, and Rose saw it—ghastly, bleeding, half bound up with a handkerchief that could not hide the convulsions of rage, shame, and despair twisting it out of all its usual beauty. His eyes glared wildly round; and once, straight into the dark where she crouched.

Their gazes met.

The look was so keen, so dreadful, that Rose, forgetting herself utterly, was on the very edge of shrieking aloud. The night, the charm, the phosphorescent sea—all of it seemed suddenly childish beside that human glance.

“He has overlooked me!” she said within herself, shuddering, remembering his threat.

“Who has wounded you?” asked Campian.

“My cousin—Amyas—and taken the letter!”

“The devil take him, then!” cried Parsons, stamping in fury on the sand.

“Ay, curse him—you may! I dare not!” Eustace panted. “He saved me—sent me here!” And with a groan he tried again to enter the boat.

Lucy Passmore, all bluster a moment before, melted at the sight of blood as any kitchen-woman might soften when a child burns its hand. Her woman’s heart broke through her loyalty and her greed.

“Oh, my dear young gentleman,” she cried, “you must not goo forth with a grane wound like to that. Do ye let me just bind mun up—do ye now!” and she came forward, already fumbling as if for clean linen.

Eustace thrust her back with a violence that was half shame.

“No! better bear it. I deserve it—devils! I deserve it! On board, or we shall all be lost—William Cary is close behind me!”

At that name, the air itself seemed to tighten. The boat was thrust out faster than ever it had moved, oars striking harder, the little splashes suddenly sounding loud as pistol-pricks. In moments it was round the rocks and out of sight.

Only just in time; for, scarcely had the darkness swallowed it when the rattle of horsehoofs came again above.

“That rascal of Mr. Leigh’s will catch it now, the Popish villain!” Lucy said aloud, with a sharp relish that was as much fear as malice. Then, dropping her voice, she bent toward the cavern. “You lie still there, dear life, and settle your sperrits. You’m so safe as ever was rabbit to burrow. I’ll see what happens, if I die for it!”

She squeezed herself up through a cleft to a higher ledge, her skirts scraping rock, and from that perch she could spy down into the meadow where the stream ran—a silver thread, noisy now in Rose’s ears, every little rush and chuckle sounding too clear in the night.

“There mun is!” Lucy whispered fiercely, watching. “In the meadow, trying to catch the horses! There comes Mr. Cary! Goodness, Father, how a rid’th! He’s over wall already! Ron, Jack! ron then! A’ll get to the river! No, a wain’t! Goodness, Father! There’s Mr. Cary cotched mun! A’s down, a’s down!”

“Is he dead?” Rose asked below, hugging her knees, sick with dread.

“Iss, fegs, dead as nits!—No, a bain’t! A’s up now. Suspose he was hit wi’ the flat. Whatever is Mr. Cary tu? Telling wi’ mun, a bit. Oh dear, dear, dear!”

“Has he killed him?” Rose whispered.

“No, fegs, no! Kecking mun, kecking mun, so hard as ever was futeball! Goodness, Father, who did ever? If a haven’t kecked mun right into river, and got on mun’s horse and rod away!”

Down Lucy came again, breathless with the tale, her face shining in moonlight with sweat and excitement.

“And now then, my dear life, us be better to goo hoom and get you sommat warm. You’m mortal cold, I rackon, by now.” She spoke briskly, as if warmth could be summoned by naming it: a fire of furze that crackled clean instead of smoking, a mug of ale with spice if she had it, bread thick-cut, butter salty and firm—anything solid to drive the sea out of a girl’s bones. “I was cruel fear’d for ye; but I kept mun off clever, didn’t I, now?”

“I wish—I wish I had not seen Mr. Leigh’s face!” Rose said. The words tasted bitter as brine.

“Iss, dreadful, weren’t it, poor young soul; a sad night for his poor mother!”

“Lucy, I can’t get his face out of my mind. I’m sure he overlooked me.”

“Oh then! who ever heard the like o’ that?” Lucy answered, half laughing to drive the fear away. “When young gentlemen do overlook young ladies, ’tain’t thikketheor aways, I knoo. Never you think on it.”

“But I can’t help thinking of it,” Rose said, and the stubborn tremble in her voice was worse than tears. The mirror’s cold edge seemed still to press her palm though she no longer held it. “Stop. Shall we go home yet? Where’s that servant?”

“Never mind; he wain’t see us, here under the hill.” Lucy peered into the dark as if she could bully it into giving up its secrets. “I’d much sooner to know where my old man was. I’ve a sort of a forecasting in my inwards, like, as I always has when aught’s gwain to happen—as though I shuldn’t zee mun again, like, I have, miss. Well—he was a bedient old soul, after all, he was.” Then, with sudden practical indignation: “Goodness, Father! and all this while us have forgot the very thing us come about! Who did you see?”

“Only that face,” Rose answered, shivering as the memory seized her again.

“Not in the glass, maid? Say then—not in the glass?”

“Would to heaven it had been! Lucy, what if he were the man I was fated to—”

“He?” Lucy barked, contempt returning as a shield. “Why, he’s a praste—a Popish praste—that can’t marry if he would, poor wratch.”

“He is none; and I have cause enough to know it!”

And because there was no other soul in the world to whom she could pour such a story at that hour, Rose—still damp, still shaken, still smelling the sea’s coldness on her skin—told Lucy everything of yesterday’s meeting: the threat, the uncanny sense of being seen through and through, the weight of another’s will pressing on her like a hand.

Lucy listened with the greedy patience of one who takes in a tale as she might take in a stew—tasting for what is meat, what is bone, what is spice, and what might poison.

“He’s a pretty wooer!” she said at last, with a snort. “Be a brave maid, then—be a brave maid—and never terrify yourself with his unlucky face. It’s because there was none here worthy of ye, that ye seed none in glass. Maybe he’s to be a foreigner, from over seas, and that’s why his sperit was so long a coming. A duke, or a prince to the least, I’ll warrant, he’ll be, that carries off the Rose of Bideford.”

But flattery, like sugar on bitter herbs, could not change the taste. Rose could not wipe that fierce face from her eyes. The combe’s sounds—the mill-hum she knew in childhood, the stream’s bright chatter, the soft drag of weed on rock, and, worst of all, the remembered whistle of Lucy’s oar—went with her like a strange music, half natural and half bewitched.

She reached home safely and crept to bed undiscovered. And when morning came, as morning would, it found her laid up with something very like a fever—born of cold sea-water, terror, and excitement. The phantom only grew stronger in her mind. She used all her woman’s tact and self-restraint to keep from betraying, by look or exclamation, what she had dared that night in Marsland cove, where the sea had burned around her like fire and men’s feet had sounded like fate upon the stones.

After a fortnight’s weakness she recovered and returned to Bideford. But before she came there, Amyas was far across the seas on his way to Milford Haven, as shall be told in the chapters that follow.

It had been too late and too black, the night before, to take the measure of Stow. Now October laid it open—clear as a clean trencher set down at noon—while the house woke into its own familiar din: doors thudding soft on their hinges, the far clink of iron in the yard, pigeons chuckling under the eaves, and the wind making a slow, sea-salted music along the walls.

Sir Richard and Amyas walked the south terrace toward eleven of the forenoon. Amyas had slept deep till luncheon-time—till an hour ago, indeed—his youth taking rest as naturally as a hearth takes flame when kindling is offered. Sir Richard had been out at six, down in the valley, giving his blood its morning heat with a brace of terriers and sundry badgers, and coming home with that keen, clean smell about him which men bring from wet fern and turned earth.

Old Stow House stood—stood then, though in later years it would be transformed and pulled down and sold off like yesterday’s feast gone cold; but that future was not yet, and what mattered now was the old building itself, as it had grown by degrees like an oak: part fortress, part dwelling, a long, rambling creature of stone. On three sides the old walls still rose with their dark mouths for casting fire and stone, relics of days when every supper might be eaten under threat of steel. But the southern court, safe enough now, had been tamed into a flower-garden, terraced and clipped into proud shapes—yews and hollies shorn as neatly as a cook trussing a capon, knots of flowers arranged with a pedant’s pleasure in pattern, the very earth made obedient.

To the east the old grim keep—ruined in the Wars of the Roses—had given place to Tudor richness: broad windows for light, and stonework that seemed less to forbid a stranger than to receive him, if he came in peace. The house, like the age, wore two minds at once: the old warlike caution and the new appetite for breadth and brightness.

Below the terrace the land fell away steep and generous, and the prospect had the full English flavor of it—salt and sweet together. At one turn, over the western walls, the ocean showed blue and broken with white, sails passing like pale moths across a darker cloth. At the next, the valley unrolled in layers: park after park, avenues set with great trees already yellowing, woodland in autumn thick as spice, purple heather on the moors, and, higher still, a black old earthwork furze-grown on its conical peak. Eastward, closing the valley, Kilkhampton church-tower stood up, tall and grave, bearing on its stones the offerings and memorials of Grenvilles piled century upon century, like dishes added to a long family board.

A soft yellow haze lay over everything, as if the day had been basted with light. Far below, red cattle lowed to each other and brushed off flies with their tails in the rivulet. In the horse-park to the right, colts whinnied as they played, and from the Queen’s Park opposite, the stallions answered in deeper voices, fainter with distance. From the woods came the hoarse thunder of a rutting stag, and from farther up the valley a rival’s trumpet of defiance—sound carrying clean in that clear air, the whole place stitched together by its voices: sea-wind, beasts, birds, and the far-off human stir of a great household at work.

And close at hand, upon the terrace before the house, Lady Grenville sat among spaniels and golden-haired children. She was St. Leger of Annery, beautiful as the day was calm, and she looked down on her children, and then up at her husband, and out again across the wide, kind West, until happiness itself seemed almost too full a draught.

Yet above all that ease and brightness Amyas and Sir Richard paced, back and forth, long and earnest, as men walk when they come to the turn of a life and must set a foot down wisely. Their steps had a rhythm of their own upon the stone; and under the words, another sound kept time—the unspoken knowledge that childhood had loosened its hold, and manhood was calling, harsh and high.

“Yes,” said Sir Richard at last, when Amyas had told him plainly—bluntly, as he did most things—the whole tale of Rose Salterne and of his brother. “Yes, sweet lad. Thou hast chosen the better part, thou and thy brother also, and it shall not be taken from you. Only be strong, lad, and trust in God that He will make a man of you.”

“I do trust,” said Amyas; and the words had not the thinness of a lesson learned, but the steadiness of something tasted and found nourishing.

“Thank God,” Sir Richard answered, “that thou hast taken from my heart the one great anxiety for thee, from the day thy good father, who sleeps in peace, committed thee to my hands. For all best things, Amyas, become, when misused, the very worst; and the love of woman—because it is able to lift a man’s soul to the heavens—is also able to drag him down to hell. But thou hast learnt better. Our old German forefathers had the right of it: Sera juvenum Venus; ideoque inexhausta pubertas.”

He went on, with a heat that was not cruelty but hard-won certainty, against the French and Italian fashion—boys forever dangling at women’s sleeves, thinking themselves men only when they could smooth and flatter, toying with maids and wives alike as if hearts were dice to be thrown and pocketed. Vanity bred from it, and expense; envy, heart-burning; hatred and murder often enough. And even when no blood came of it, something worse: a man’s reverence squandered until the wife at last got only the dry scrapings—the caput mortuum—of what should have been kept whole for her alone. Then she became an ornament for his table, a means to beget heirs, and no more.

“May God,” he said, “who has saved me from that death in life, save thee also.”

As he spoke he looked down toward his wife upon the terrace, and she—as if she had heard the very turn of his thought—lifted her face and smiled up. The smile was so quiet and so full that Sir Richard’s sternness broke open, and his countenance shone as if a cloud had parted.

Amyas looked at them both and sighed; and then, as if to keep his own heart from dwelling too long on what he had renounced, he turned short to business.

“And I may go to Ireland to-morrow?”

“You shall sail in the Mary for Milford Haven, with these letters to Winter. If the wind serves, bid the master drop down the river to-night and be off; for we must lose no time.”

“Winter?” said Amyas, the old anger quickening. “He is no friend of mine, since he left Drake and us so cowardly at the Straits of Magellan.”

“Duty must not wait on private quarrels, even just ones. But he will not be thy general. When thou comest to the marshal or the Lord Deputy, give either of them this letter, and they will set thee work—and hard work too, I warrant.”

“I want nothing better.”

“Right, lad. The best reward for having wrought well already is to have more to do. He that has been faithful over a few things must find his account in being made ruler over many. That is the true and heroical rest, the only rest worthy of gentlemen and sons of God. As for those who look—in this world or the next—for idleness, hoping God shall feed them with pleasant things as with a spoon, I count them cowards and base, though they call themselves saints and elect.”

“I wish you could persuade my poor cousin of that.”

“He has yet to learn what losing his life to save it means. Bad men have taught him—and I fear these Anabaptists and Puritans at home teach little else—that the one great business is to save a man’s own soul after he dies, each for himself; and that, not divine self-sacrifice, is the one thing needful.”

“I think men are inclined enough already to be selfish, without being taught that.”

“Right, lad. If I could hang up such a teacher on high as an enemy of mankind and a corrupter of youth, I would do it gladly. Is there not cowardice and self-seeking enough in the hearts of us fallen sons of Adam, that these false prophets, with their baits of heaven and terrors of hell, must make our dirtiest vices into heavenly virtues? Farewell to chivalry and desperate valour; farewell to patriotism and loyalty; farewell to England and the manhood of England—if once it become the fashion for our preachers to bid every man, as the Jesuits do, take care first of what they call the safety of his soul. Then every man will be afraid to die at his post, because he will fear he is not fit to die.”

The words rang on the terrace like a blade struck sharp against stone, and beneath them, as if in answer, came the steady domestic sounds from the house: the clatter of a trencher somewhere within, a child’s laughter cut short by a nurse’s murmur, the faint scold of a peacock. Life went on with its common music while great doctrines were weighed that might send men to their deaths.

“Amyas,” Sir Richard ended, “do thou do thy duty like a man—to thy country, thy queen, and thy God—and count thy life a worthless thing, as did the holy men of old. Do thy work, lad, and leave thy soul to the care of Him who is just and merciful in this, that He rewards every man according to his work. Is there respect of persons with God? Now come in, take the letters, and to horse. And if I hear of thee dead there at Smerwick fort, with all thy wounds in front, I shall weep for thy mother, lad; but I shall have never a sigh for thee.”

They went in. Sir Richard turned again to his writing among books and despatches and warrants; for though he had not yet gathered on himself all the offices that later custom flung upon a great man at once, there were few matters of weight in which his hand was not felt, few great men with whom he did not correspond.

But the day had more to bring.

At the threshold they met the old steward, searching the passages as if the walls themselves might offer him counsel.

“There is a manner of roog, Sir Richard—a masterless man—at the door; a very forward fellow, and must needs speak with you.”

“A masterless man? He had better not speak to me unless he is in love with gaol and gallows.”

“Well, your worship,” the steward persisted, “I expect that is what he does want, for he swears he will not leave the gate till he has seen you.”

“Seen me? Halidame! he shall see me—here and at Launceston too—if he likes. Bring him in.”

“Fegs, Sir Richard, we are half afeard. With your good leave—”

“Hillo, Tony,” cried Amyas, laughing, “who was ever afeard yet with Sir Richard’s good leave?”

“What, has the fellow a tail or horns?”

“Massy no: but I be afeard of treason for your honour; for the fellow is pinked all over in heathen patterns, and as brown as a filbert; and a tall roog, a very strong roog, sir, and a foreigner too, and a mighty staff with him. I expect him to be a manner of Jesuit, or wild Irish, sir; and indeed the grooms have no stomach to handle him, nor the dogs neither; or he had been under the pump before now. They that saw him coming up the hill swear he had fire coming out of his mouth.”

“Fire out of his mouth?” Sir Richard said, dryly. “The men are drunk.”

“Pinked all over? He must be a sailor,” said Amyas; for the sea has its own signatures on a man’s skin. “Let me out and see him; and if he needs putting forth—”

“Why, I dare say he is not so big but he will go into thy pocket. Go, lad, while I finish my writing.”

Amyas went out, and at the back door, leaning on his staff, stood a tall, raw-boned, ragged man. His skin showed through the rents of his clothes, marked with blue and black patterns—strange work, like map-lines on flesh—and his face was baked brown as a nut. The air about him carried the sourness of old tar and long-wet cloth, with that sharp, human smell of a man who has lived hard and washed seldom because water has been for drinking, not for comfort.

“Hillo, lad,” said Amyas. “Before we come to talk, thou wilt please to lay down that Plymouth cloak of thine.” And he nodded at the cudgel, which the West-country seamen commonly called by that name.

“I’ll warrant,” said the steward behind him, “that where he found his cloak he found purse not far off.”

“But not hose or doublet,” Amyas answered, eyeing the man’s rags. “So the magical virtue of thy staff has not helped thee much. Put it down, man, and speak like a Christian, if thou be one.”

“I am a Christian, though I look like a heathen; and no rogue, though a masterless man, alas. I want nothing—deserving nothing—and only ask to speak with Sir Richard, before I go on my way.”

There was something in the man’s voice—stately, yet humbled—as if he had once been proud and had learned what breaks pride without breaking the backbone. It drew Amyas in spite of himself.

“Whither go you? And whence come you?”

“From Padstow Port, sir, to Clovelly town, to see my old mother, if indeed she be yet alive—which God knoweth.”

“Clovelly man!” cried the grooms, as if a kinship had been declared over a shared loaf. “Why didn’t thee say thee was Clovelly man?”

The steward, half softened by county fellowship, asked, “What’s thy mother’s name then?”

“Susan Yeo.”

“What, that lived under the archway?” said a groom.

“Lived?” the man repeated, and the word came out carefully, as if he were tasting it for truth.

“Iss, sure; her died three days since, so we heard, poor soul.”

He stood still. Not a sob, not a sway—only a quietness that was more dreadful than crying, like a candle snuffed without a wisp of smoke. Then he said, very low, in Spanish, as if that tongue had become the place where grief lodged easiest: “That which is, is best.”

“You speak Spanish?” Amyas asked, more and more taken.

“I had need to do so, young sir. I have been five years in the Spanish Main, and only set foot on shore two days ago. And if you will let me have speech of Sir Richard, I will tell him that at which both the ears of him that heareth it shall tingle. And if not, I can but go on to Mr. Cary of Clovelly, if he be yet alive, and there disburden my soul. But I would sooner have spoken with one that is a mariner like to myself.”

“And you shall,” said Amyas. “Steward, we will have this man in; for all his rags, he is a man of wit.”

“I only hope he ben’t one of those Popish murderers,” muttered the steward, keeping distance as if the stranger carried infection.

“Popish, old master? There’s little fear of my being that. Look here!” The man dragged back his rags and showed a ghastly scar encircling his wrist and winding up the forearm—a rope-burn made eternal, a signature burned into him by pain.

“I got that on the rack,” he said quietly, “in the Inquisition at Lima.”

“O Father! Father!” cried the steward, suddenly penitential, “why didn’t you tell us that you were a poor Christian?”

“Because I have had naught but my deserts; and but a taste of them either, as the Lord knoweth who delivered me. I wasn’t going to make myself a beggar and a show on their account.”

“By heaven, you are a brave fellow,” Amyas said; and he felt, with a sailor’s quick sympathy, how the man’s voice sat in his throat like something long held back. “Come along straight to Sir Richard’s room.”

So they went in to the library, where Sir Richard sat among his papers, the room smelling of ink and leather and old vellum—dry scents, but honest, and to a man of action as necessary as salt in meat.

“Hillo, Amyas,” Sir Richard called up, “have you bound the wild man already, and brought him in to swear allegiance?”

Before Amyas could answer, the stranger fixed his eyes upon him.

“Amyas?” he said. “Is that your name, sir?”

“Amyas Leigh is my name, at your service, good fellow.”

“Of Burrough by Bideford?”

“Why then? What do you know of me?”

“Oh sir, sir—young brains and happy ones have short memories; but old and sad brains too long ones often. Do you mind one that was with Mr. Oxenham, sir? A swearing reprobate he was—God forgive him, and hath forgiven him too, for His dear Son’s sake—one, sir, that gave you a horn, a toy with a chart on it?”

“Soul alive!” Amyas cried, and caught his hand. “And are you he? The horn? Why, I have it still, and will keep it to my dying day. But where is Mr. Oxenham?”

“Yes,” said Sir Richard, rising, his face tightening as the name stirred older anxieties. “Where is Mr. Oxenham? You are over-hasty in welcoming your old acquaintance, Amyas, before we have heard whether he can give honest account of himself and of his captain. There is more than one way by which sailors may come home without their captains—as poor Mr. Barker of Bristol found to his cost. God grant there have been no such traitorous dealing here.”

“Sir Richard Grenville,” said the man, and there was iron under his humility, “if I had been a guilty man to my noble captain—as I have been to God—I had not come here this day to you, from whom villainy has never found favour, nor ever will; for I know your conditions well, sir. Trust in the Lord: if you will be pleased to hear me, you shall know mine.”

“Thou art a well-spoken knave,” Sir Richard answered. “We shall see.”

“My dear sir,” Amyas whispered, “I will warrant this man guiltless.”

“I verily believe him to be,” Sir Richard returned; “but this is too serious a matter to be left on guess. If he will be sworn—”

At that the man bowed his head a little. “If it would please Sir Richard, I would rather not be sworn.”

“But it does not please me, rascal! Did I not warn thee, Amyas?”

“Sir,” the man said, and now he stood straight, proud as a mast despite the rags, “God forbid that my word should not be as good as my oath. But it is against my conscience to be sworn.”

“What have we here? Some fantastical Anabaptist, wiser than his teachers.”

“My conscience, sir—”

“The devil take it and thee! I never heard a man yet begin to prate of his conscience but I knew he was about to do something more than ordinarily cruel or false.”

“Sir,” the man replied coolly, “do you sit here to judge me according to law, and yet contrary to the law swear profane oaths, for which a fine is provided?”

Amyas expected thunder. But Sir Richard, with a grim self-command, pulled a shilling from his purse and laid it on the table. The coin rang on the wood like a small bell.

“There—my fine is paid, sirrah, to the poor of Kilkhampton. But hearken all the same. If thou wilt not speak an oath, thou shalt speak on compulsion; for to Launceston gaol thou goest, there to answer for Mr. Oxenham’s death, on suspicion whereof—and of mutiny causing it—I will attach thee and every soul of his crew that comes home. We have lost too many gallant captains of late by treachery of their crews; and he that will not clear himself on oath must be held for guilty, and self-condemned.”

“My good fellow,” Amyas broke in, unable to let it stand, “why, for such fantastical scruples, peril not only your life but your honour, and Mr. Oxenham’s also. For if you be examined by question, you may be forced by torment to say that which is not true.”

“Little fear of that, young sir,” the man answered, and his mouth turned with a hard, knowing humour. “I have had too much of the rack already, and the strappado too, to care much what man can do unto me. I would heartily that I thought it lawful to be sworn; but not so thinking, I can but submit to the cruelty of man—though I did expect more merciful things, as a most miserable and wrecked mariner, at the hands of one who hath himself seen God’s ways in the sea, and His wonders in the great deep. Sir Richard Grenville, if you will hear my story—may God avenge on my head all my sins from my youth up until now, and cut me off from the blood of Christ, and, if it were possible, from the number of His elect—if I tell you one whit more or less than truth. And if not, I commend myself into the hands of God.”

Sir Richard smiled, half in scorn, half in reluctant admiration. “Well, thou art a brave ass, and valiant, though an ass manifest. Dost thou not see, fellow, how thou hast sworn a ten-times bigger oath than ever I should have asked of thee? But this is the way with your Anabaptists, who by hatred of forms and ceremonies show of how much account they think them, and then bind themselves, out of fantastical self-will, with heavier burdens than ever lawful authorities laid on them for the commonweal. But what do they care for the commonweal, so they can save—so they fancy—each man his own dirty soul for himself? However, thou art sworn now with a vengeance. Go on with thy tale: and first, who art thou, and whence?”

“Well, sir,” the man said, unmoved, “my name is Salvation Yeo, born in Clovelly Street, in the year 1526, where my father exercised the mystery of a barber-surgeon, and a preacher of the people since called Anabaptists—for which I return humble thanks to God.”

“Fie!” said Sir Richard. “Thou naughty knave; return thanks that thy father was an ass?”

“Nay,” Yeo replied, “but because he was a barber-surgeon. I myself learnt a touch of that trade, and thereby saved my life, as I will tell presently. And I do think a good mariner ought to have all knowledge of carnal and worldly cunning—even to tailoring and shoemaking—that he may turn his hand to whatsoever may hap.”

“Well spoken, fellow,” Sir Richard allowed. “But let us have thy text without thy comments. Forwards!”

“Well, sir. I was bred to the sea from my youth, and was with Captain Hawkins in his three voyages which he made to Guinea for negro slaves, and thence to the West Indies.”
