“Then thrice thou wentest to a bad end,” Sir Richard said sharply, “though Captain Hawkins be my good friend; and the last time to a bad end thou camest.”

“No denying that last, your worship,” Yeo answered. “But as for the former, I doubt—about the unlawfulness, I mean; being the negroes are of the children of Ham, who are cursed and reprobate, as Scripture declares, and their blackness testifies, being Satan’s own livery; among whom therefore there can be none of the elect, wherefore the elect are not required to treat them as brethren.”

Sir Richard’s contempt flared like tinder catching. “What a plague of a pragmatical sea-lawyer have we here? I doubt not, thou hypocrite, that though thou wilt call the negro’s black skin Satan’s livery when it serves thy turn to steal him, thou wilt find out sables to be Heaven’s livery every Sunday and set up a godly howl unless a parson preach in a black gown, Geneva fashion. Out upon thee! Go on with thy tale, lest thou finish thy sermon at Launceston after all.”

“It is virtue, yea virtue, gentlemen, that maketh gentlemen…”

So ran the old sentence, oft mouthed in hall and chamber, and not the worse for being mouthed; for the age that delighted in such brave sounding words could, in the next breath, shoulder a pike, steer by dead-reckoning through a dirty night, or keep counsel as close as a cellar-door. If any man, reading what follows, should call it fantastical, let him remember that those who spoke thus of love and honor were the same who could count a tide, bargain for powder, and take a town. They could season their talk with roses and arrows, and yet smell treason on the wind as keenly as they smelt a fresh roast from the kitchen.

Amyas Leigh could not sail when he would. The south-west freshened and held, not as a kindly breath behind canvas, but as a hard palm set full against the bay; three parts of a gale, as the seamen said, dead into the mouth of it, with the bar moaning and the shingle hissing like frying fat. So the Mary Grenville was got down the river into Appledore pool, lying there ready as a dish laid for serving the instant the household hears the step at the door—only the step was the change of wind, and it tarried.

Amyas went quietly home; and when his mother, stern in sorrow and duty, started on pillion behind the old serving-man to ride to Clovelly—where Frank lay wounded—Amyas rode in with her as far as Bideford. The town was in its market stir: the lowing of cattle at one end, the clack of women’s tongues at the other, and between them the constant thud of hoof and wheel. Over it all rose the gulls’ sharp crying and, from a smithy, the ring of iron upon iron—good, honest sound that tells a man where he is, and steadies the mind better than any fine music played for money.

They had scarce entered the High Street when a little procession came down it, drawing eyes from doors and windows as a scent draws noses. Will Cary led—clad cap-a-pie in shining armor, sword on thigh, helmet at saddle-bow—so gallant that even the fishwives, with hands in brine and herring-scale, paused to grin and nudge. Behind him, on country ponies, rode four or five stout serving-men with lances and baggage and their own long-bows, swords, and bucklers; and behind all, in a horse-litter, was Frank Leigh himself, to Mrs. Leigh’s joy, looking pale and fine and very much alive.

He protested, with the airy obstinacy that made his mother angry and proud in one breath, that his wounds were but flesh-wounds, the dagger having turned against his ribs; that he must see the last of his brother; and that, with her leave, he would not go home to Burrough, but take up his lodging with Cary in the Ship Tavern by the Bridge-foot. And so he did, setting himself upon a couch like a young prince in exile, and holding his levee in state, mobbed by the gossips of the town and fed with their questions; to which he returned answers seasoned with white fibs—more pepper than salt—as to who had brought him into that sorry plight.

But in the meanwhile, he and Amyas, with that quick brotherly understanding which needs few words, laid a plot of their own, and set it afoot the next day—market-day—when the whole country-side came in as bees to a hive.

First, the innkeeper, under Amyas’s orders, began such a bustle of roasting, boiling, and frying as had not been heard in the annals of the Ship. The kitchen became a little world of heat and savor: spits creaked under their loads; fat fell hissing upon coals and sent up a sweet smoke that clung to rafters and hair and cloak; lids clattered; knives thudded on blocks; and the cook’s voice, sharp as vinegar, kept time with the scullions’ bare feet slapping the flags. Great joints were basted till they shone like varnished oak; capons browned and crackled; onions went down into pots with herbs from some hedgerow garden, and a handful of spice—dear, foreign, and used sparingly, as if each grain had a story. All through the house, the smell travelled, creeping under doors and along passages, making even sober men’s mouths water and their tempers soften, for hunger and ill-humor are near kin.

Next, Amyas went out into the market and invited, one by one and apart, as many of his old schoolfellows as Frank pointed out to him, to a merry supper and a “rowse” upon it. Each young gentleman came with his own hope tucked close, as a man carries a secret letter in his doublet; and each, entering, found himself—much to his disgust—set at table with six rivals, to none of whom he had spoken for half a year. Yet all were too well bred to let the Leighs see their teeth. Frank lay at the head on his couch, pale still, but bright-eyed; Amyas took the bottom; and, by filling every mouth with good things—meat that needed chewing, bread that had a crust worth cracking, and ale that made the throat grateful—they spared their guests the pain of speaking till the wine should loosen their tongues and warm their hearts.

Amyas and Frank, with provoking good-humor, talked and joked and told stories, as if silence were the merriest music in the world. Will Cary, who caught mirth as tinder catches flame, melted into one jest and then another; tried to make Mr. Coffin laugh and only got a bow; tried to make Mr. Fortescue laugh and only got a frown; and, unabashed, turned his light artillery upon the waiters till he drove them out, bursting with laughter, into the noisy haven of the kitchen, where the spit’s squeal and the pot’s bubble would cover their mirth better than any curtain.

So far so good. The cloth was drawn; sack and sugar came in—warm, sweet, and fragrant, like a cordial for chilled courage; and “Queen and Bible” was duly drunk with all honors, the cups clinking like small bells in a chapel. With the drink came the room’s new sound: chairs scraped, voices rose, the first hard edges of silence rounded off, as stones in a stream are rounded by continual water.

Frank watched his moment as a cook watches the instant when a sauce turns and must be taken from the fire.

“I have a toast, gentlemen—here it is. ‘The gentlemen of the Irish wars; and may Ireland never be without a St. Leger to stand by a Fortescue, a Fortescue to stand by a St. Leger, and a Chichester to stand by both.’”

That toast forced each of the three to drink and return thanks and pay compliments to the other houses, and in so doing to speak aloud to those whom they had lately scowled at in silence. The ice cracked. Young Fortescue, warmed in spite of himself, proposed “Amyas Leigh and all bold mariners;” and Amyas answered with blunt kindness that he wished to know no better fortune than to sail round the world again with the present company as fellow-adventurers, and so give the Spaniards another taste of the men of Devon.

Wine slid down sweetly; and the old saying proved itself true again, that it makes the lips of them that were asleep to speak. Now each man began talking like a rational Englishman to him at his elbow. The room filled with a better music than any hired fiddler—an honest weaving of voices, with the occasional burst of laughter that had in it the clean crack of breaking wood rather than the thin tinkle of courtly affectation.

“And now, gentlemen,” said Frank, seeing the moment ripe, “let me give you a health which none of you, I dare say, will refuse to drink with heart and soul as well as with lips;—the health of one whom beauty and virtue have so ennobled, that in their light the shadow of lowly birth is unseen…”

He went on, piling praise upon praise with that airy, dangerous grace of his, like a man scattering rose-leaves over hot embers. Each guest turned red, and pale, and red again, and looked at the others as if to say, “Lift your glass, and I will dash it out of your hand.” Then Frank, with sweet effrontery, drank:

“The health of the Rose of Torridge, and a double health to that worthy gentleman, whosoever he may be, whom she is fated to honor with her love!”

“Well done, cunning Frank Leigh!” cried Will Cary, clapping his hand upon the table till the cups jumped. “None of us dare quarrel with you now, however much we may sulk at each other. For there’s none of us, I’ll warrant, but thinks she likes him best; and so we are bound to believe you’ve drunk our healths all round.”

“And so I have,” said Frank; “and what better thing can you do, gentlemen, than drink each other’s healths all round likewise; and so show yourselves true gentlemen, true Christians, ay, and true lovers?”

Then he launched into one of those swift, fantastical harangues of his—Cupid, Psyche, Graces and Furies—turning jealousy into nettle and love into rose, and arguing, with a courtier’s tongue and a soldier’s nerve, that their common worship should bind them into friendship. The words ran on like a clever cook’s hand through a feast—now sharp, now sweet, now rich—till the listeners, taken unawares, had no leisure to kindle their tempers.

But when he paused, weak from his wounds, to breathe, a haughty murmur rose. More than one young gentleman took it for impertinent interference in his right to make a fool of himself. Mr. Coffin, who had sat bolt upright and stared at the opposite wall as if it were safer than any face, now rose, and with a look meant to be unconcerned walked toward the door. Another minute and the feast might have turned Lapithae indeed.

Frank’s heart and head never failed him.

“Mr. Coffin!” he cried, in a tone that compelled the other to turn; and so brought him under the power of a face too imploring, tender, and earnest for malice to dwell long upon. “My dear Mr. Coffin! If my earnestness has made me forget, even for a moment, the bounds of courtesy, I entreat you to forgive me. Do not add to my heavy griefs, heavy enough already, the grief of losing a friend. Only hear me patiently to the end; and then, if you are still incensed, I can but entreat your forgiveness again.”

Now Coffin, in truth, had never been to Court; and he was jealous of Frank’s Court talk and Court clothes and Court company. Being the eldest there, and scarce two years younger than Frank, he was nettled at being classed with lads of eighteen. Had Frank but hinted at superiority, all had been lost. Instead, he sued like a beggar for mercy; and Coffin, true-hearted enough, and having known him since either could walk, could do nothing but sit again, stiffly at first, and then with his anger melting as butter melts when brought too near the fire.

“Believe me,” Frank went on, more earnestly than ever, “I no more arrogate to myself a superiority over you than does the sailor hurled on shore by the surge fancy himself better than his comrade still battling with the foam. For I too, gentlemen,—let me confess it, that by confiding in you I may win you to confide in me,—have loved, ay and do love, where you love also.”

There was a stir; brows drew down; but he hurried on, turning their very frowns into fuel.

“Is it wonder that the sun which has dazzled you has dazzled me? I have learnt to love you for loving what I love, and to admire you for admiring that which I admire. Will you not try the same lesson? What breeds closer communion than allegiance to the same queen, duty to the same father, adoration of the same God? And shall not worship of the same beauty be likewise a bond? Let each see in his rival not an enemy but a fellow-sufferer…”

So he pleaded that they should make their rivalry an emulation in virtue: loyalty to queen, valor against foes, courtesy and mercy to the oppressed; and then, higher yet, that they should bind themselves in charity, vowing to abide by the lady’s choice and to serve him whom she should honor, as faithful worshippers of one divinity. And with that he ended:

“…as I do here promise to be the faithful friend, and, to my ability, the hearty servant, of him who shall be honored with the love of the Rose of Torridge.”

He ceased, and for a space there was no sound but the faint crackle of the hearth and, from without, the far-off cry of a hawker in the street—common noises, and yet in that hush each one seemed to hear his own blood.

At last young Fortescue spoke, half smiling.

“I may pay you a left-handed compliment, sir; but it seems to me that you are so likely, in that case, to become your own faithful friend and hearty servant—if you have not borne off the bell already while we have been asleep—that the bargain is hardly fair between such a gay Italianist and us country swains.”

“You undervalue yourself and your country,” said Frank quickly. “But set your mind at rest. I know no more of that lady’s mind than you do, nor shall I know. For the sake of my own peace, I have made a vow neither to see her, nor to hear—if possible—tidings of her, till three full years are past. Dixi?”

Mr. Coffin rose at that, as if a cord had been cut.

“Gentlemen, I may submit to be outdone by Mr. Leigh in eloquence, but not in generosity. If he leaves these parts for three years, I do so also.”

“And go in charity with all mankind,” cried Cary, leaping up. “Give us your hand, old fellow. If you are a Coffin, you were sawn out of no wishy-washy elm-board, but right heart-of-oak. I am going too—Amyas here can tell—to Ireland away, to cool my hot liver in a bog, like a jack-hare in March. Come, give us thy neif, and let us part in peace. I was minded to have fought thee this day—”

“I should have been most happy, sir,” said Coffin stiffly.

“—But now I am all love and charity,” Cary rattled on. “Can I have the pleasure of begging pardon of the world in general, and thee in particular? Does any man wish to pull my nose; send me an errand; make me lend him five pounds; ay, make me buy a horse of him, which will be as good as giving him ten? Come along! Join hands all round, and swear eternal friendship, as brothers of the sacred order of the—of what, Frank Leigh? Open thy mouth, Daniel, and christen us!”

“The Rose,” said Frank quietly, seeing his love-philtre work, and minded to strike while the iron was hot.

“The Rose!” cried Cary, catching Coffin’s hand with his right and Fortescue’s with his left. “Come, Mr. Coffin! Bend, sturdy oak! ‘Woe to the stiff-necked and stout-hearted!’ says Scripture.”

And somehow—whether it was Frank’s chivalrous pleading, or Cary’s broad fun, or Amyas’s good wine and the strong meat that had lined their stomachs and steadied their brains, or that native nobleness which lies in young men’s hearts if their elders will but trouble to call it out—the whole party came in to terms. They shook hands round the table and vowed, on the hilt of Amyas’s sword, to make fools of themselves no more—at least by jealousy—but to stand by each other and by their lady-love, and neither grudge nor grumble though she danced with, flirted with, or married whom she would. And because the honor of their peerless dame and of the brotherhood named after her must be spread through all lands, they would each go home and ask their fathers’ leave—easy enough in those brave times—to go abroad wheresoever there were “good wars,” and there emulate the courage and courtesy of Walter Manny and Gonzalo Fernandes, Bayard and Gaston de Foix. Sidney was a hero at five-and-twenty; why not they?

Frank watched and listened with one of those quiet smiles of his—his eyes smiling even when his lips were still—and only said:

“Gentlemen, be sure that you will never repent this day.”

“Repent?” cried Cary. “I feel already as angelical as thou lookest, Saint Silvertongue. What was it that sneezed?—the cat?”

“The lion, rather, by the roar of it,” said Amyas; and in a moment, as if the sound had pricked his seaman’s instinct for hidden things, he made a dash at the arras behind him. “Why, here is a doorway here! and—”

He rushed under the hanging, through an open door behind, and returned dragging out, by the head, Mr. John Brimblecombe.

And who was Mr. John Brimblecombe? If you have forgotten him, you have done almost what every one in that room had done. Yet you may recall a certain fat lad, son of the schoolmaster, whom Sir Richard, three years before, punished for tale-bearing by sending him—not to Coventry, but to Oxford. That was he. He was now one-and-twenty and a Bachelor, having learned such things as were then taught, with more or less success; and he was hanging about Bideford again, meaning after Christmas to return and read divinity, that he might become a parson and shepherd souls in his native land.

In person he was exceedingly like a pig—yet not like every pig; and here, if the savor of a feast has already made you attentive to flesh, fat, and the honest appetites of the body, you will understand the comparison the better. He was not like the lank Devon swine of that day, ill-shaped as hedgerow monsters; nor like the furry, grizzled little wild rooklers, game-flavored and quick-eyed, that still grunted in sounders about Swinley Down and Braunton woods, Clovelly glens and Bursdon moor. No: he was prophetic—face, figure, and complexion—of those great pigs that a later time would boast of as science’s triumph.

A Fisher Hobbs pig of twelve stone, set upon its hind-legs—that was Jack Brimblecombe, and nothing else: the plump mulberry skin with a few scattered black bristles; the sleek tightness as if he might burst with his own good keeping; the little toddling legs; the dapper bend in the small of the back; the cracked squeak of a voice; the low upright forehead and tiny eyes; the round, self-satisfied jowl; and, best of all, the sensitive cocked nose that was ever on the watch for a savory smell—yet, while sniffing for the best, contented with the worst. A pig of self-helpful and serene spirit; fatting fast while other pigs’ ribs stared through their skins.

Christmas afternoon fell early, as it always did upon that far western edge of England where daylight seemed a thing carried in the hands, and set down gently before it was snatched away again by the sea. Even-song had ended in Bideford, and the folk poured out as if the church door were the mouth of some warm oven: men with their cheeks reddened by hymns and ale, children already sticky-fingered with spice-cakes, lovers walking close in the lane as if the very air were too cold to waste between them. From open windows came the busy clatter of platters and trenchers; the scent of baking, of fried apples, of beer foaming up in brown jugs; and underneath all, the small domestic music of Christmas—knives on boards, laughter, the thud of a stool set down, the scrape of a fiddler’s bow beginning to try its strings.

One woman only went apart from that cheerful tide.

She was wrapped close in a black muffler, her maid behind her, and she walked swift as duty, sad as hunger, toward the long causeway and bridge that led to Northam. Sir Richard Grenville and his wife, meeting her, turned and overtook her with gentle haste.

“You will come home with us, Mrs. Leigh,” Lady Grenville said, laying a hand upon her sleeve as one might upon a dear, trembling bird. “And spend a pleasant Christmas night?”

Mrs. Leigh’s smile came and went like the thin light over the river. She touched Lady Grenville’s arm—grateful, and yet as if she could not bear to lean—and pointed westward.

“I cannot well spend a merry Christmas night,” she said, “while that sound is in my ears.”

They looked where she pointed.

The sky above them was fading from soft blue into a ash-gray, and a star or two blinked out, shy as children peeping through a door-crack. In the west the downlands and Raleigh woods met Abbotsham’s, and the last of the sun had left there a strange, delicate glory—white flakes as if the blue had been webbed and turf’d with frost-light, iridescent stains like the shifting colors on a dying dolphin, and, low on the rim of the world, a long band of grassy green.

It was dead calm. Not a breath stirred the river reeds. And yet—listen—there was a sound, a vast one, not sharp but weighty: a deep, steady roar that did not belong to the town at all, but came from beyond it, as if the earth itself had turned in sleep and groaned. It hovered over down and wood, over salt-marsh and river, like the roll of a thousand wheels, the tramp of endless armies, or, truer than both, the thunder of a mighty surge upon the boulders of the pebble ridge.

“The ridge is noisy to-night,” Sir Richard said, turning his head as a man turns when he hears distant carts upon a road. “There has been wind somewhere.”

“There is wind now,” Mrs. Leigh answered, and her voice tightened as cord tightens, “where my boy is—God help him!”

They all knew what she meant. The Atlantic storm had sent its herald inland: that smooth ground-swell whose voice you may hear two miles from shore on a calm day, telling you, before the first breath touches your cheek, that the west has begun to heave. To-morrow, perhaps, the pebbles would be rattling like shot, leaping ridge-high, and the advancing wave would fling them, round as cannon-balls, far up on the marsh in its rage.

“God help my boy,” she said again, like a prayer that would not be finished.

“God is as near him by sea as by land,” said Sir Richard, who had long practised speaking comfort as he practised command—plainly, and with faith in it.

“True,” she answered; “but I am a lone mother; and one that has no heart just now but to go home and pray.”

So she went on up the lane, the muffler drawn close, the maid’s steps small behind her. And all that night, between her prayers, she listened—listened as women listen who have fed children and watched fevers and stood with candle in a doorway for the sound of late feet—until the low thunder of the surge was swallowed, long before sunrise, in the nearer thunder of the storm itself.

And where was Amyas, while his mother counted the sea’s voice as if it were beads?

Amyas was sitting bareheaded in the stern of a boat in Smerwick Bay, the spray whistling through his curls, his teeth shining as he laughed into the gale.

“Pull, and with a will, my merry men all!” he shouted. “And never mind shipping a sea. Cannon-balls are a cargo that don’t spoil by taking salt-water.”

Christmas Eve had been one of those black, still nights which feel too warm for winter, as if the year were holding its breath. But now the western gale had been roaring for twelve hours upon the Irish coast. The short light was failing fast. Behind him a line of billows leapt and broke into flying mist; beside him green foam-fringed pillars rushed up the black rock and fell again in cataracts of white, as if the cliffs themselves were being washed and scoured for judgment. Before him the bay lay deep and sheltered—yet even that shelter was only a pause, a bowl held under a pouring roof.

For out at sea, four miles off and more, began a slope of thick gray cloud, like a low ceiling laid upon the world. It stretched over their heads, inland and away, cutting the cliffs at mid-height and hiding all the Kerry mountains. Under that awful roof the storm howled without stopping, sweeping before it the gray salt spray and great sponges of foam until land and sea alike were hazy, dim, dun—no horizon, no distance, only a roaring nearness.

Let it howl. There was more than weather at work. Along the sand-hills, in the evening gloom, red sparks flashed that never fell from heaven. The fort which the invaders, with their golden dreams, had christened Fort del Oro, held San Josepho and eight hundred of the foe; and over it flaunted the hated golden flag of Spain. Three nights ago Amyas and Yeo and the rest of Winter’s shrewdest hands had slung four culverins out of the Admiral’s main deck, floated them ashore, and dragged them up to the battery among the sand-hills. Now the question was plain: could Spanish and Italian condottieri hold their own on British ground against men of Devon?

Small blame to Amyas if, for that hour, he thought less of Burrough Court than of those quick bright flashes—sand-hill answering fort, fort answering sand-hill—where Salvation Yeo laid his eighteen-pound shot with deadly patience. He watched, with that cool bitter smile of triumph that sits strangely upon an honest face, the sand fly from the gabions as if the earth itself were being peppered, and the wickerwork crack and spill. The battery was out of ball. For four hours they had been firing stones, as hungry men chew crusts when meat is gone.

Now Amyas’s boat ran in through the surf at a cove where landing could be dared. They drove her up as if they cared not whether she staved, and scrambled over the sand-hills with each man carrying a brace of shot slung across his shoulder, the cold iron wet and heavy, chafing the straps, each step sucking and sliding in sand that would not hold. Into the trenches Amyas leapt, and called out to Yeo with a cheer that tasted of Devon ale and Christmas mirth even there, among salt and powder.

“More food for the bull-dogs, Gunner, and plums for the Spaniards’ Christmas pudding!”

“Don’t speak to a man at his business, Master Amyas,” Yeo growled, without lifting his eye from his work. “Five mortal times have I missed; but I will have that accursed Popish rag down, as I’m a sinner.”

“Down with it, then; nobody wants you to shoot crooked. Take good iron to it, and not footy paving-stones.”

“I believe, sir,” said Yeo, and his voice had that obstinate superstition sailors carry like a knife in their pocket, “that the foul fiend is there, a-turning of my shot aside, I do. I thought I saw him once: but, thank Heaven, here’s ball again. Ah, sir, if one could but cast a silver one! Now, stand by, men!”

The great gun spoke—one blunt, belly-deep roar, and then the hiss and rush of the shot. Amyas’s heart leapt in him with it.

And glory! The great yellow flag of Spain, streaming in the gale, lifted clean into the air, flagstaff and all, as if the very wind had taken it by the throat; then it pitched wildly down headforemost, far to leeward.

A hurrah broke from the sailors, and the soldiers in the other camp answered it; the sound rolled up under the cloud-roof like a wave striking stone. But before the echoes died, a tall officer sprang upon the fort’s parapet with the fallen flag in his hand. He reared it upon his lance-point and held it there against the gale while, within, they raised a new staff.

A dozen long-bows were bent in an instant—cords creaking, arrows nocked and drawn.

“Shame, lads!” Amyas shouted from behind. “Stop, and let the gallant gentleman have due courtesy!”

So they stopped, and Amyas, springing up on the battery’s rampart, took off his hat and bowed. The Spaniard—or Italian—held his post until relieved; then, free of his charge, he returned the bow with the same grave politeness, and went down.

By this time darkness had thickened. Firing slackened on all sides. Salvation and his brother gunners covered up their slaughtering tackle with tarpaulins and retired, and the men, after their scant supper of biscuit—provisions running so short that each mouthful seemed to accuse the next—lay down under arms among the sand-hills and grumbled themselves to sleep. The wind tore at their cloaks; the sand crept into their collars and teeth; and now and then, as a gust shifted, you could hear the fort’s life too—distant shouts muffled by storm, a hammer’s tap, the squeal of a pulley.

Amyas had volunteered the watch till midnight. He paced up and down in the gusty darkness, exchanging a word now and then with the sentinel, while the world seemed made only of three things: the low boom of surf beyond the spit, the nearer moan of wind through sand-grass, and the faint clink of arms as men slept with hands upon their weapons.

After an hour or more two men came into the battery, talking busily together. One wore complete armor, that made a dull music of metal when he moved; the other was wrapped in the plain short cloak of a man of pens and peace. Yet their talk was neither of culverin nor sally, but of English hexameters—of metres, of syllables, of the very shape of speech, as if words too were weapons to be set upon a parapet.

Amyas listened, puzzled, half amused, half vexed that any man could find sport in such matters while the storm and the Spaniard were so near. But the two talked on: of learned fashions at court and college; of men who would force the queen’s English to march in foreign shoes; of lines that hopped and stumbled upon the tongue like a lame dog over stones.

The armored man’s voice was quick, bold, impatient—Raleigh’s voice, with laughter in it like a knife’s flash. The cloaked man’s speech had a softer, grave cadence—Spenser’s, the secretary’s, full of thought that seemed to weigh on him like wet wool.

Raleigh was railing, half in jest and half in earnest, at those who would “reform” English poetry by making it ape the ancients, as if the old land had not its own tune. Spenser defended, then faltered, then defended again; and Raleigh tossed him a new taunt as a cook tosses a pancake—careless, dexterous, sure it will land.

“Tut, tut, Colin Clout,” Raleigh said at last, “much learning has made thee mad. A good old fishwives’ ballad jingle is worth all your sapphics and trimeters—hey? do you mind that precious verse?”

“But, dear Wat, Homer and Virgil—”

“But, dear Ned, Petrarch and Ovid—”

“But, Wat, what have we that we do not owe to the ancients?”

“Ancients, quotha? Why, the legend of King Arthur, and Chevy Chase too, which even your fellow-sinner Sidney cannot deny stirs his heart like a trumpet-blast though he hear it from a blind fiddler. Speak well of the bridge that carries you over, man! Did you find your Redcross Knight in Virgil, or such a dame as Una in old Ovid? No more than you did your Pater and Credo, you renegado baptized heathen, you!”

Spenser answered with something about “divine antiquity,” and “imitate afar,” and Raleigh laughed him down, declaring that if Homer was blind, then by that rule Spenser ought to poke out his own eye; and that hexameters on English roads went twitching and stumbling “like a three-legged terrier over a pebble-bank.”

Amyas, as he paced, began, half to warm his own courage and half because Christmas makes the tongue remember old sweetness, to sing under his breath. The wind snatched at the tune, carrying bits of it away and bringing others back. Yet the words held, simple and stubborn, like a hearth-song that will not be blown out:

“As Joseph was a-walking
He heard an angel sing—
‘This night shall be the birth-night
Of Christ, our heavenly King.

His birth-bed shall be neither
In housen nor in hall,
Nor in the place of paradise,
But in the oxen’s stall.

He neither shall be rocked
In silver nor in gold,
But in the wooden manger
That lieth on the mould.

He neither shall be washen
With white wine nor with red,
But with the fair spring water
That on you shall be shed.

He neither shall be clothed
In purple nor in pall,
But in the fair white linen
That usen babies all.’

As Joseph was a-walking
Thus did the angel sing,
And Mary’s Son at midnight
Was born to be our King.

Then be you glad, good people,
At this time of the year;
And light you up your candles,
For His star it shineth clear.”

Raleigh stopped short, as if struck by the plainness of it, and called out in the darkness with a harsh tenderness he would not confess.

“There, Edmunde Classicaster! Does not that simple strain go nearer to the heart of him who wrote The Shepherd’s Calendar than all your outlandish ‘Wote ye why his mother with a veil hath covered his face?’ Why dost not answer, man?”

There was a pause. Even the wind seemed for an instant to listen, as it does when it changes its note among the grass.

Then Spenser spoke, quietly, with shame in it.

“Because I was thinking rather of the rhymer than the rhyme. Good heaven! how that brave lad shames me—singing here the hymns which his mother taught him, before the very muzzles of Spanish guns; instead of bewailing, as I have done, the love I held, I doubt not, as dear as he holds his. This is his welcome to the winter’s storm; while I, who dream of heavenly inspiration, can but see therein an image of mine own cowardly despair. Pah! away with frosts, icicles, and tears, and sighs—”

“And with hexameters and trimeters too, I hope,” Raleigh cut in. “And all the trickeries of self-pleasing sorrow.”

“I will set my heart to higher work,” Spenser said, as if making a vow to himself, “than barking at the hand which chastens me.”

“Wilt put the lad into the Faerie Queene, then, by my side?” Raleigh cried. “He deserves as good a place there as ever a Guyon, or even Lord Grey your Arthegall. Let us hail him. Hallo! young chanticleer of Devon! Art not afraid of a chance shot, that thou crowest so lustily upon thine own mixen?”

“Cocks crow all night long at Christmas, Captain Raleigh, and so do I,” Amyas called back cheerfully. “But who’s there with you?”

“A penitent pupil of yours—Mr. Secretary Spenser.”

“Pupil of mine?” Amyas said, laughing. “I wish he’d teach me a little of his art; I could fill up my time here with making verses.”

“And who would be your theme, fair sir?” Spenser asked.

“No ‘who’ at all,” Amyas answered, honest as bread. “I don’t want to make sonnets to blue eyes, nor black either: but if I could put down some of the things I saw in the Spice Islands—”

“Ah,” Raleigh said, and Amyas could hear him grin in the dark, “he would beat you out of Parnassus, Mr. Secretary. Remember, you may write about Fairyland, but he has seen it.”

“And so have others,” Spenser replied. “It is not so far off from any one of us. Wherever is love and loyalty, great purposes, and lofty souls, even though in a hovel or a mine, there is Fairyland.”

“Then Fairyland should be here, friend,” Raleigh said. “For you represent love, and Leigh loyalty; while, as for great purposes and lofty souls, who so fit to stand for them as I, being—as my enemies and my conscience swear—ambitious and proud as Lucifer’s own self?”

“Ah, Walter, Walter,” Spenser murmured, “why wilt always slander thyself thus?”

“Slander? Tut.—I but give the world fair warning. Ask Leigh here—he has but known me a fortnight—whether I am not vain as a peacock, selfish as a fox, imperious as a bona roba, and ready to make a cat’s paw of any man if there be a chestnut in the fire; and yet the poor fool cannot help loving me, running my errands, taking my schemes and dreams for gospel, and believing that I shall be the man in the moon some day, and he my big dog.”

“Well,” Amyas said, half apologetic, for he could not bear a friend’s self-mocking, “if you are the cleverest man in the world what harm in my thinking so?”

Raleigh’s laugh snapped.

“Hearken to him, Edmund! He will know better when he has outgrown this callow trick of honesty, and learnt of the great goddess Detraction how to show himself wiser than the wise by pointing out the fool’s motley peeping through the philosopher’s cloak. Go to, lad! slander thy equals, envy thy betters; pray for an eye that sees spots in every sun, and for a vulture’s nose to scent carrion in every rose-bed. If thy friend win a battle, say he threw away men; if he lose, hint he sold it; if he rise, argue favor; if he fall, argue justice. Believe nothing, hope nothing, but endure all things—even to kicking—if aught may be got thereby; so shalt thou be clothed in purple and fine linen, and sit in kings’ palaces, and fare sumptuously every day.”

“And wake with Dives in the torment,” Amyas answered. “Thank you for nothing, captain.”

“Go to, Misanthropos,” Spenser said, reproving Raleigh with a quiet firmness. “Thou hast not yet tasted the sweets of this world’s comfits, and thou railest at them?”

“The grapes are sour, lad,” Raleigh muttered.

“And will be to the end,” Amyas said, “if they come off such a devil’s tree as that. I really think you are out of your mind, Captain Raleigh, at times.”

“I wish I were,” Raleigh returned; “for it is a troublesome, hungry, windy mind as man ever was cursed withal. But come in, lad. We were sent from the lord deputy to bid thee to supper. There is a dainty lump of dead horse waiting for thee.”

“Send me some out, then,” Amyas said, matter-of-fact as if the talk had been of nets and tides. “And tell his lordship that, with his good leave, I don’t stir from here till morning, if I can keep awake. There is a stir in the fort, and I expect them out on us.”

“Tut, man! their hearts are broken. We know it by their deserters.”

“Seeing’s believing. I never trust runaway rogues. If they are false to their masters, they’ll be false to us.”

“Well, go thy ways, old honesty,” Raleigh called, half admiring and half vexed. “And Mr. Secretary shall give you a book to yourself in the Faerie Queene—‘Sir Monoculus, or the Legend of Common Sense,’ eh, Edmund?”

“Monoculus?” Spenser echoed, amused despite himself.

“Ay, Single-eye, my prince of word-coiners—won’t that fit? Give him the Cyclops head for a device. Heigh-ho! They may laugh that win. I am sick of this Irish work; were it not for the chance of advancement I’d sooner be driving a team of red Devons on Dartside; and now I am angry with the dear lad because he is not sick of it too. What plague business has he to be paddling up and down, contentedly doing his duty, like any city watchman? It is an insult to the mighty aspirations of our nobler hearts—eh, my would-be Ariosto?”

“Ah, Raleigh!” Spenser’s voice softened. “You can afford to confess yourself less than some, for you are greater than all. Go on and conquer, noble heart! But as for me, I sow the wind, and I suppose I shall reap the whirlwind.”

“Your harvest seems come already; what a blast that was!” Raleigh said, as a gust struck them. “Hold on by me, Colin Clout, and I’ll hold on by thee. So! Don’t tread on that pikeman’s stomach, lest he take thee for a marauding Don, and with sudden dagger slit Colin’s pipe and Colin’s weasand too.”

Their footsteps and their voices stumbled away into the dark, leaving Amyas to his pacing, with the wind for company and the Spanish fort for neighbor.

He turned Raleigh’s wild words over and over, as a housewife turns a cake on the griddle to see if it is burning. There was, he began to think, some strange savor in cleverness that sat ill upon the stomach; some sourness hidden in the sweetest comfits. He thanked God he was neither scholar, courtier, nor poet—only a plain man who could hear a carol and feel it warm him.

The promised “dainty” came at last: a lump of horseflesh, boiled or stewed till it had given up what little richness it owned. The smell rose blunt and rank in the wet air; yet to hunger it was as if venison had been set down. Amyas said grace over it, ate it cleanly and with gratitude, and went back to his post. This time he kept his mouth shut, for the night was drawing on, and there was no need to tell the Spaniards that any man was awake and watching.

The hours went by, slow as a pot that will not boil. All lights were out in battery and shipping. The only sounds of living things were the monotonous tramp of the sentinels and, now and then, a grunt from the men sleeping under arms some twenty yards to the rear. The wind’s note changed continually, as if it were trying different pipes; it whistled through the sand-grass, moaned along the gun-barrels, and now and again dropped low enough that Amyas could hear, far off, the sea’s deeper breathing.

He began to think of his mother, and how she might be spending her Christmas—whether she had tasted even a mouthful, or only sat with folded hands and listened to the ridge’s thunder. He thought of Frank, and wondered what brave court festival his brother might be attending, amid bright lights and sweet music and gay ladies; how he was dressed; whether he remembered, even for a moment, the dark Atlantic shore. Amyas said his prayers and his creed. Then he tried not to think of Rose Salterne, and of course thought of her the more, as a man forbidden to taste will taste with his mind.

He paced to and fro, peering often over the strip of sand-hill that lay between him and the fort. All was blank and black. And then the rain began, furious and slanting, as if flung from buckets; it beat upon helmet and cloak, it hissed on the sand, it ran cold down Amyas’s neck and wrists, and the whole world smelled of wet salt and trampled grass.

Suddenly, amid the wind’s loud whispering, he caught another sound: a rustle in the harsh sand-grass—not altogether like the wind’s hand. Then a soft sliding noise, as of something that had slipped down a bank and drawn sand after it.

Amyas stopped. He crouched beside a gun, and laid his ear to the rampart. Through the rain and the storm he heard it clearly now: the cautious, broken rhythm of approaching feet—whether rabbits or Christians he could not yet tell; but his heart, trained by danger, guessed the latter.

So it came about—by no great thunderclap at first, but by the slow, daily settling of a man into comfort—that Don Guzman began to lose a battle he had not meant to fight.

He lay at Bideford on parole in quarters kinder than any he had known for many a month: a roof that did not leak, linen that smelled of sun and lye, and—chiefest temptation of all—Sir Richard Grenville’s table, where meat was not measured like powder in a siege, but came up steaming, glistening, lavish as a harvest-home. Don Guzman took all as a soldier of fortune must: with a stiff pride, a quick eye, and a mind always counting exits.

Until one evening, not a month after his coming, old Salterne the Mayor was bidden to supper.

At first the Spaniard’s soul shrugged within its fine black doublet. These English, with their heresies and their blunt manners, would set a tradesman above the salt among gentlemen, as if a man’s hands—ink-stained from ledgers, tarred from bales and casks—could be washed clean by napery and a silver spoon. Yet Don Guzman was gentleman enough to understand that Grenville was gentleman enough to do nothing uncouth by his own light. So he sat, and endured the strange equality, and watched.

And the Mayor, though he had sat all day at his desk and reckoned apprentices and cargoes, spoke neither foolishly nor vulgarly. He gave the Spaniard every due sign of his rank—never fawning, never forgetting—yet held his own opinions with a quiet obstinacy Don Guzman had seen before in the merchant princes of Genoa and Venice. A man might buy and sell wool and wine, and yet keep within him a kind of sovereignty.

When supper ended, Salterne invited Grenville with proper humility to his own house on the morrow, and then turned to the Don and asked, as frankly as a man weighing coin, whether a nobleman of Spain would condescend to sit at the board of a simple merchant. The cheer, he said, would be fit for any rank, whatever the company.

Don Guzman, who was both captive and bored, accepted. Amusement is a meat that a prisoner chews long.

He found the promise kept. The Mayor’s house had the busy smell of a trading man’s prosperity: spice-sacks in the buttery, dried herbs hanging in the kitchen beams, and from the yard a faint sharpness of fish and river-mud, for the Torridge was near and always speaking. The very sound within was different from Grenville’s great rooms: not the hollow reverberation of stone and distance, but the snug clatter of domestic life—platters laid down, trenchers scraped, a fire that sighed and snapped as it ate its own fuel. It was a kinder music. Don Guzman, who had lived amid camps and galleys and courts, noticed it without meaning to; the ear takes its own nourishment.

Salterne set good sack before him, amber in the cup, fragrant as baked apples and summer dust. But the Don drank sparingly—more water than wine—abstemious as a monk where it served him, for drink loosens more than tongues. Then Salterne tried a second net: questions, prudent and well-cooked as any dish. Peru, the Spanish Main, the Moluccas, China—every far shore that an English merchant’s mind, newly kindled under Elizabeth’s sun, could hunger toward.

Don Guzman parried as a fox runs—quick, silent, never showing his belly. His answers gave sound and wind, with little substance.

In the midst of that fencing, Mistress Rose Salterne came in—the Rose of Torridge, so called by those who watched her as men watch a bright thing in water. She heard what they spoke of and, artless enough, began to ask her own questions, not as a merchant would, for profit, but as a girl with imagination and a heart that leaned forward to any far-off wonder. To her Don Guzman could not help answering. It is easier to refuse a hook than a hand held out with honest curiosity.

So the evening ran on, pleasant as a long meal where each course comes when appetite is ripe for it. The Don spoke of lands that smelled of sugar and smoke, of cities where the air was heavy with orange blossom and incense, of seas whose night was alive with strange lights. He told it so that Rose heard not only words but climates: she could almost taste the salt on a wind that had never blown in Devon.

And because the human heart is a kitchen that will breed desire out of idleness and good feeding, Don Guzman—idle as captives must be, and full of Sir Richard’s bread and beef—began, for mere sport at first, to look about him for some one to love.

Lady Grenville, being nearest, came first into his thoughts; and he put her away again with a self-denial he admired in himself. Sir Richard was his host. Besides, danger is sweetest when it is forbidden; and that sweetness he denied himself with a flourish, like a man pushing back a dish he has already tasted.

So, having banished that peril, he let another into the vacant place: Rose Salterne, with her innocent freshness, her fondness for admiration that yet did not smell of craft. “The train of the peacock,” he said to himself, “and the heart of the dove.” The mingling of vanity and virtue, bright feathers and soft breast, charmed him. And then a panic of prudence seized him—if he could persuade her to love none but him, might he not become fool enough to love none but her? He resolved to keep out of her way.

But days are long in captivity. And Lady Grenville, when she was at home, was dull enough to talk of nothing but her husband; and when she rode to Stow she left the Don alone in one corner of the great house, with silence thick as cold broth. What was he to do but lounge down to the butt-gardens, show off his black cloak and feather, watch the shooting, play rackets with the younger men, bowls with the elder, and let himself be invited—again and again—to the Mayor’s table?

There he ruled the roast, as he loved to do, not only by rank but by the power of having something to say. For these were the day-dawn hours of English commerce. In Bideford parlors and quay-side warehouses men were dreaming aloud—projects of voyages and companies, patents and settlements, all the new world’s edges gleaming like a knife-blade. The talk ran on Turkey merchants and Levant privileges, on voyages to Guinea for musk and civet, gold and grain, on new doors opening where old powers fell. Even the household sounds took their rhythm from it: the scratching of a quill, the creak of a bale-hoist, the ring of a cooper’s hammer. The town’s very air had that restless brine-and-woodsmoke odor that clings to places where men are always fitting out for elsewhere.

Don Guzman, listening and measuring, saw what his countrymen saw: that these islanders were not merely traders counting money by candlelight. They hated fighting and loved profit, yet could fight, when pressed, with a dogged terror that had teeth in it. Their ships went armed, their crews trained from childhood to use steel and shot, and their consciences, once persuaded by policy and Scripture that they were right, did not easily soften.

One evening, stung by their boasts, he waxed hot. He had hinted, civilly enough, that if Englishmen came where they had no right they might be sent back again. The answer came like a volley across a deck:

“We’ll see that, sir.”

“Depends on who says ‘No right.’”

“You found might right when you claimed the Indian seas,” said another. “We may find right might when we try them.”

“Try them then, gentlemen,” he snapped, “and find the sacred flag of Spain as invincible as ever was the Roman eagle.”

“We have, sir. Did you ever hear of Francis Drake?”

“Or of George Fenner at the Azores, one against seven?”

“Or John Hawkins at St. Juan d’Ulloa?”

“Insolent burghers!” Don Guzman cried, and rose to go.

Old Salterne rose too, not reddening like the younger men, but steady as a man accustomed to bargaining. “Sir,” he said, “we are plain men and have forgotten ourselves somewhat. Put it down to my wine. Insolent we never meant to be—least of all to a noble gentleman and a foreigner.”

But the Don would not be pacified. He walked out into the night air, calling himself ass and blinkard for having lowered himself to such company, conveniently forgetting that he had baited the quarrel.

Next day Salterne came, prompted by Mammon and by curiosity, and begged pardon again; promised that none of the rude speakers should be asked to meet him henceforth, if he would honor the house once more.

Don Guzman allowed himself to be appeased, and went the next evening, sneering inwardly as he went.

“Fool that I am,” he thought, “that girl has bewitched me. Go I must, and eat my share of dirt, for her sake.”

He went, and with cunning offered Salterne a sweet morsel: that he had taken such a fancy to him, and felt bound by his courtesy, that he might tell him things he would not mention to every man. Spaniards, he hinted, were jealous not of single traders, but of any general attempt to rob them of their hard-earned wealth. Yet for one man here and there, in the meantime, there were opportunities—oh, plentiful—if he would be wise and quiet.

Salterne, shrewd as he was, had his weak point: the hunger to know how the Spanish monopoly was fenced, and where a hand might slip in for profit. The Don touched it, and felt the line quiver.

And so—so blind does money make men—the Mayor began to set Rose herself to draw out the Spaniard, never fearing that she might be drawn in. For he held it impossible that she would ever marry a Popish Spaniard, as impossible as marrying the man in the moon; and equally impossible that Don Guzman would ever marry a burgher’s daughter, any more than he would marry a negress. Religion on one side, pride of blood on the other—two walls, Salterne thought, high as a fortress.

And as for love without marriage—if the thought ever crossed him, it died in laughter. He would have staked every farthing on Rose’s virtue, and gave the Don credit, too, for a certain human fondness for keeping his throat and the parts adjoining in one piece.

Thus, for weeks and months, the merchant’s parlor became the Don’s favorite haunt. He saw the Rose of Torridge daily, and the Rose of Torridge heard him. And she—poor child—had never seen such a man. He carried himself with a high-bred grace that reminded her of Frank Leigh, and yet there was a manlier mould beneath it. He had enough proud self-assertion to make a woman feel him superior, and yet tact enough to keep it from running into the bragging for which Spaniards were so often and so justly blamed. He had marvels to tell by flood and field as many as Amyas and more, and told them with a fluent, glowing eloquence that simple, modest Amyas did not possess. Besides, he was there—present as the smell of warm bread—while the Leighs were absent.

So she amused herself with the only person that came to hand; and in those days, with young gallants gone to wars in the Netherlands and Ireland, many a lady did the same. Don Guzman grew welcome at houses for miles round, and took their welcomes freely. Fair young dames gave him attention enough to turn a wiser head; and Rose might have escaped, had not Sir Richard delicately let him know that though English ladies were freer in manner than Spanish, brothers were as jealous, and women’s honor at least as unstormable, as in the land of duennas.

Don Guzman took the hint. He kept on good terms with the country gentlemen as with their daughters; and, to tell truth, found profit in seeming intimate with many ladies, that Salterne might never fancy he had any peculiar predilection for Mistress Rose.

Yet Salterne’s parlor lay nearest, and remained his commonest road. There he would discourse for hours—of deserts and cannibals, of men whose heads grew beneath their shoulders—till Rose’s fancy ran like a child turned loose in a meadow. And he seasoned those tales with scraps of mercantile knowledge, little savory morsels that kept Salterne hungry, as salt keeps a man thirsty: just enough to make it worth his while to invite the Spaniard again.

And certainly the stories were worth hearing. He had been everywhere, or seemed so. Born in Peru, sent home to Spain at ten; brought up in Italy; soldier in the Levant; adventurer to the East Indies; again in America—first the islands, then Mexico. Back to Spain, then Rome, then Ireland. Shipwrecked; captive among savages; peering down volcano-craters; hanging about courts; fighting Turks, Indians, lions, elephants, alligators, and whatever else the world could throw at a man. At five-and-thirty he had seen enough for three lives, and knew how to plate his experience so that others would feast on it.

He had been in the horrors of Famagusta; had escaped he hardly knew how from the victorious Turks, from flaying and impalement, from the likelihood of ending as a Janissary. He had been at the Battle of the Three Kings; had seen Stukely borne down by lances, unconquered even in death; had held on his knee the head of the dying King of Portugal.

And one evening—when the fire burned low and the house had sunk to that late hush where only the clock and the river can be heard—he spoke to Rose, not of wonders, but of himself.

“What have I left,” he said, “but a heart trampled as hard as the pavement? Whom have I to love? Who loves me? I have nothing to live for but fame—and even that is denied me here, a prisoner in a foreign land.”

“Have you no kindred?” Rose asked, pity softening her voice.

“My two sisters are in a convent,” he answered. “They had neither money nor beauty; so they are dead to me. My brother is a Jesuit, so he is dead to me. My father fell by Indian hands in Mexico. My mother—penniless—carries fans and lapdogs for some princess in Seville, no better born than herself. And I—devil!—I have lost even my sword. So fares the house of De Soto.”

He meant to be pitied, and he was. Rose’s heart, that had never been trained to distrust a sorrow spoken well, went out to him as naturally as a hand goes toward a child’s burn.

Then, with the ease of a man who knows his audience, he would turn the talk. When Rose was present, he told Italian tales that were pathetic—lovers parted, women wronged, tears like pearls; and Rose wept over sorrows of Juliet and Desdemona and many another tale before ever England saw them on a stage. When Rose was absent, he told racy stories to the Bideford worthies, till their ribs shook with laughter; but those I will not repeat.

So the months went on. What need of more words? Before a year was out, Rose Salterne was far more in love with Don Guzman than he with her. Both suspected each other’s mind, yet neither hinted at the truth—she from fear, he from sheer Spanish pride of blood.

For he began to perceive a hard choice: if he meant to profit by this passion, he must either compromise his noble blood by marrying a heretic burgher’s daughter, or see all his labor thrown away.

He had found, with astonishment and then with pleasure, that old English custom of saluting ladies on the cheek at meeting—a thing which Romish visitors, with coarser imaginations, slandered. He had seen, too, more than one red-faced burgher, smelling of onions, press his lips to Rose’s velvet cheek in that common fashion; and jealousy, sharp as vinegar, had risen in the Don, though he would have bitten his tongue out sooner than confess it.

So one day he offered his own salute.

He chose his moment when she was alone. Something—perhaps conscience, perhaps policy—warned him against doing it under her father’s eye.

To his astonishment she drew back, prompt and quiet. “No, sir. You should know my cheek is not for you.”

“Why,” he said, anger choked down but not swallowed, “it seems free enough to every counter-jumper in the town!”

Was it love, or innocence, that made her answer apologetically? “True, Don Guzman; but they are my equals.”

“And I?”

“You are a nobleman, sir, and should recollect that you are one.”

He forced a sneer. “It is a strange taste to prefer the shopkeeper.”

“Prefer?” She tried to laugh, and the laugh sounded thin. “It is a mere form among us. They are nothing to me, I can tell you.”

“And I then—less than nothing?”

Rose reddened to the roots of her hair, yet held her ground. “And why should you be anything to me? You have condescended too much already, sir, in giving us many a—many a pleasant evening. You must condescend no further. You wrong yourself, and me too. No, sir; not a step nearer! I will not. A salute between equals means nothing; but between you and me—if you do not leave me this moment, I will complain to my father.”

“Do so, madam!” he cried. “I care as little for your father’s anger as you for my misery.”

“Cruel!” Rose said, trembling from head to foot.

“I love you, madam!” Don Guzman flung himself at her feet as if shot through the heart. “I adore you! Never speak to me of difference of rank. I have forgotten it—forgotten all but love, all but you. You see where my pride is gone. I plead as suppliant, beggar—though I may be one day a prince, a king! Ay, a very Lucifer of pride to all save you; to you a wretch who grovels and cries, ‘Have mercy on me—on my loneliness, my homelessness, my friendlessness.’ Ah, Rose—madam, forgive the madness of my passion—you know not the heart you break. Cold Northerns, you little dream how a Spaniard can love. Love? Worship, rather—as I worship you; as I bless the captivity that brought me sight of you, and the ruin that first made me rich. Saints and Virgin! do my own tears deceive me, or are there tears too in those radiant orbs?”

Rose recovered herself in a sudden spasm of fear, as if she had heard a door unlatch in the dark. “Go, sir,” she cried, “and let me never see you more!” And as a last chance for life—so it seemed to her—she darted out of the room.

“Your slave obeys you, madam, and kisses your hands and feet for ever and a day,” said the Spaniard; and drawing himself up, he walked serenely out of the house.

Upstairs, behind a window, Rose—poor fool—peeped after him. Her heart sank as she watched his jaunty, careless air, as if all that rapture had been only a practiced speech spoken for sport.

How much of his rhapsody was honest and how much premeditated, who can say? She began to fancy it was all set and studied when she found that he had taken her at her word and set foot no more within her father’s door.

Then she reproached herself as the cruelest of women; resolved that if he died she would be his murderess; watched at the window for him to pass and hoped—oh, foolish hope—that he might look up; and the instant he turned the corner she hid herself again, burning with terror at being seen.

So it went, and so it goes. One love-making is much like another, I suppose, since that first blessed marriage in Paradise, where love came ready-made from Heaven and needed no speeches at all.

And truly it is like fiddling while Rome burns to spend many more pages upon the sorrows of poor little Rose Salterne, while the destinies of Europe hang upon the marriage-talk between Elizabeth and Anjou; while Sir Humphrey Gilbert stirs heaven and earth—and Devon, of course, as the most important portion of that earth—to quicken his dormant patent, which will give to England, in due time, Newfoundland, Nova Scotia, Canada, and the Northern States; and to Humphrey Gilbert himself something better than a new world—another world, and a crown of glory that does not fade.

It was the spring of 1582—3, that harsh hinge of the year when March will not yet loosen winter’s grip, but only hardens it into a sharper edge. The sky lay like gray curd above black mountain teeth; the wind came thin and dry over a wide, sour bog, and carried with it the smell of iron water and peat—old decay, old cold, and the faint acrid tang of last year’s heather burned by desperate hands. In the waste, where red and yellow frost-stains still bruised the ground, a single brown knoll rose like a loaf left too long in the ashes. On it a few leafless oaks, clipped by constant weather, stretched mossed arms over a pool that shivered and crisped in the gale; and from beside that pool, now and again, came a cry—mournful, long-drawn—borne away and returned by the wind as if the bog itself were trying to remember a voice and could not hold it.

Along the bog’s rim, choosing their footing between crumbling rock and treacherous green springs that sucked at the boot, pushed a company of English soldiers. Their gear was dulled by weather and service—helmets stained, quilted jerkins dark with old rain, arquebuses steadied on shoulders, pikes trailing and clinking. They were men who, two years before, had worked the guns at Smerwick; men who had learned to eat hard biscuit wet with ditch-water, to sleep with their muskets as if with wives, and to keep marching when the belly made its own complaints.

Two captains rode in front on shaggy ponies. The taller wore armor that had taken on the country’s own complexion—rust and peat and salt; the other sat in a bright inlaid cuirass and plumed helmet, sash clean enough to look like a banner, sword-hilt glittering with gold. It was finery made for torchlight and a court’s close air, a strange thing on a meagre garron picking its way through bog-holes.

Beside them, tethered by a cord to a pikeman’s wrist, trotted a bare-legged Irish kerne. His ragged saffron mantle flapped, his “glib” of uncombed hair fell over his brow, and his eyes slid right and left with fear sharpened into sullenness. He was their guide in the hunt for Baltinglas. If he lied, the bog would not be the worst of his grave.

“A pleasant country, truly, Captain Raleigh,” said the dingier officer to the gay one, glancing at the plume as it dipped and recovered against the wind. “I marvel you had the heart, after escaping it to Whitehall, to come back and spoil that brave suit with bog-water.”

“A pleasant country, my friend Amyas. What you say in jest, I say in earnest.”

Amyas laughed without mirth. “Hillo! Our tastes have changed places. I am sick of it already, as you foretold. Would to Heaven I could hear of some adventure Westward-ho! and find these big bones swinging in a hammock once more. What has made you so suddenly in love with rock and mire, that you come tramping them with us? I thought you had spied out the nakedness of the land long ago.”

“Bog and rock?” Raleigh’s voice warmed as if he were speaking of a dish he meant to perfect. “What is needed here but prudence and skill, justice and law? The soil—see you not?—is fat enough, if men were here to till it. And these rocks—who knows what they hold? I hear of gold and jewels found already; and Daniel, my brother Humphrey’s German assayer, swears these stones are of the very kind that yield silver in Peru. Give me but some few square miles of this wilderness, and in seven years I would make it blossom, by God’s help.”

“Humph!” Amyas eyed him sidelong. “If you can bake bread out of bog, I might be inclined to stay.”

“So you shall, and be my agent,” Raleigh returned lightly. “Get in my mine-rents and corn-rents and fishery-rents. Can you keep accounts, old knight of the bear’s-paw?”

“Well enough,” said Amyas, “for short reckonings like yours will be on the profit side. No, no. I’d rather carry lime all my days from Cauldy to Bideford than pass another twelve-month in the land of Ire. There is a curse upon the face of the earth.”

“There is no curse upon it,” Raleigh answered, with that fearless edge that later would be called irreligion by men who mistook bluntness for blasphemy, “save the old one of man’s sin—‘Thorns and thistles it shall bring forth to thee.’ Root them up, and you plough away nature’s curse and schoolmen’s fables. Till the ground like a man, and wheat comes where the briar stood.”

“It is sword and bullet that are needed here, before plough and harrow,” Amyas muttered. “Clear away some of the curse. Until more of these Irish lords are gone where the Desmonds are, there is no peace for Ireland.”

“Not so far wrong, I fear.” Raleigh’s gaze slid over the bog as if already measuring fields and rents. “And yet—Irish lords? These traitors are better English blood than we who hunt them down. When Yeo here slew Desmond, he let out no more Irish blood than if he had slain the Lord Deputy.”

“His blood be on his own head,” said Yeo, marching behind with the steady anger of a man who has been paid too long in hunger and lead. “He looked as wild as the worst of them—more shame to him. The ancient here had nigh cut off his arm before he told who he was; and then, your worship, there being a price on his head, and he like to bleed to death too—”

“Enough,” Raleigh broke in. “Thou hast done what was given thee to do.”

He fell quiet. The march went on, boots and hoofs making that soft, sucking music peat gives back—a damp applause that never quite becomes a sound. And Raleigh, as often happened when the world offered him a mirror, turned inward.

Strange, he thought, that noble Normans could sink into savages—Hibernis ipsis hiberniores. Was there some uncivilizing venom in the air? Or was it only that a man, set far from law and equal speech, grows used to hearing no “no,” and begins to believe his own desires are statutes?

He glanced at Amyas’s broad shoulders, at the guide’s hungry eyes, at the soldiers’ set faces; and then, with a sudden sting that came not from wind but conscience, he remembered his own craving for Desmond’s lands. What would he be, when he had them? Would his children, fattened on power as on rich meat, sink downwards as those barons sank? Would the genius of tyranny find soil even in him?

And yet—could he not do justice and love mercy? Could he not plant, build, sow—make the desert valleys laugh with corn? He imagined barns sweet with new hay, smoke from hearths, the steady domestic clatter of a country at peace: the grind of quern-stones, the hiss of oats turned on a hot iron, milk foaming in pails, the safe sounds that mean life is being fed. He longed—oddly, fiercely—for those wholesome noises, as if the ear could be nourished like the mouth, and as if this land’s misery were not only in its hunger but in its sound: the sudden scream, the war-cry, the musket’s crack, the thin wail in the wind where there ought to be a child’s laughter and the steady, sane creak of cart-wheels.

Yes—away from courts, among a people who might bless him rather than grin at him—what days might be his! But was that only ambition wearing an angel’s mask?

He broke off, startled. “Leigh—what noise was that?”

“An Irish howl, I fancied,” said Amyas, reining in. “But it came off the bog. It may be only a plover.”

“Something not quite right,” said the ancient, his voice lowering as if the air itself listened. “They tell ugly stories here of pucks and banshees and such. There it is again—wailing like a woman. They say the banshee cried all night before Desmond was slain.”

“Perhaps then,” Raleigh answered, though the hair at his nape prickled against his gorget, “this one is crying for Baltinglas, for his turn may come next—not that I believe old wives’ tales.”

Amyas leaned toward the guide. “Shamus. Do you hear that cry in the bog?”

The kerne made his face a sullen wall. “Shamus hear naught. Perhaps—what you call him?—fishing in ta pool.”

“An otter, he means,” said Amyas; then, as the cry rose again, clearer, with a human break in it: “Stay. Did you not hear it, Shamus? It was a woman’s voice.”

“Shamus is shick in his ears ever since Christmas.”

“Shamus will go after Desmond if he lies,” Amyas said flatly. Then, to the ancient: “Take a few men and see what it is. There may be some poor soul taken by robbers, or starving, as I have seen many.”

“And I too,” the ancient began, with that weary soldier’s philosophy that pins disaster on everybody and nobody. “Poor wretches; and by no fault of their own or ours neither. But if their lords will fall to quarrelling, and then drive each other’s cattle and waste each other’s lands—sir, you know—”

“I know.” Amyas cut him short. “Why dost not take the men and go?”

The old soldier swallowed. “Cry you mercy, noble captain, but—I fear nothing born of woman.”

“Well, what of that?” Amyas’s mouth twitched.

“But these pucks, sir. The wild Irish do say they haunt the pools; and they do no harm when you go toward them; but when you are past, they jump on your back like apes—and who can tackle that manner of fiend?”

“Then by thine own showing,” Raleigh laughed, “thou may’st go safely enough and see. If the puck jumps on thee as thou comest back, run in with him, and I’ll buy him of thee for a noble—or keep him in a cage and make money in London showing him for a monster.”

“Good Heaven forefend, Captain Raleigh! You talk rashly!” The ancient drew a breath, then squared himself with a scrap of bravado.

“‘Where duty calls  
To brazen walls,  
How base the slave who flinches—’

Lads—who’ll follow me?”

“Thou askest for volunteers as if thou wert to lead a forlorn hope,” Amyas said. “Pull away at the usquebaugh, man, and swallow Dutch courage, since thine English is oozed away. Stay. I’ll go myself.”

“And I with you,” Raleigh said, swinging down from his pony. “As the queen’s true knight-errant, I am bound to be behindhand in no adventure. Who knows but we may find a wicked magician just going to cut off the head of some saffron-mantled princess?”

“Oh, sirs, sirs, to endanger your precious—”

“Pooh,” Raleigh interrupted. “I wear an amulet, and have a spell of art-magic at my tongue’s end, whereby neither can a ghost see me, nor I see them. Come with us, Yeo, the Desmond-slayer, and we will shame the devil—or be shamed by him.”

“He may shame me, sir,” Yeo answered, “but he will never frighten me. But the bog, captains—”

“Tut! Devonshire men, and heath-trotters born, and not know our way over a peat moor!”

So the three went off, leaving the troop to watch the guide with hands nearer to knife-hilts than they admitted.

They splashed and scrambled half a mile, boots breaking through the skin of the bog into cold black water that smelled of long-soaked wood and old smoke. The cry grew louder as they neared, not like the call of bird or beast, but with the shaped sorrow of a throat that had learned words and now could only wail.

“That’s neither ghost nor otter,” Yeo said under his breath, “but a true Irish howl, as Captain Leigh said; and I’ll warrant Master Shamus knew it long ago.”

Now they could catch the words—Ochone, Ochonorie—rising and falling like a keening song. They climbed the knoll, slipped between boulders slick with lichen, and in a moment came full upon the sound’s source.

A young girl sat on a stone. She was, as such poor creatures were, sluttish and unkempt; the yellow mantle hung on her in folds that might once have been decent, but now were stained with bog-water and the gray of long want. Yet her face, under the tangles of black hair she tore at, was fair enough—fair, too, with that quick fairness that hunger sharpens rather than softens. She threw her head back and sent out again that long cry, less like speech than like the baying of a hound whose master will not come.

On her knees lay the head of a man in the long soutane of a Romish priest. One look at the set of his limbs told them what they had already tasted in the air: he was dead.

For a heartbeat none spoke. Raleigh, who could turn any scene into a picture and then into a thought, felt the bleakness pierce him—bitter sky, shapeless bog, stunted trees, and this savage young grief crouched over a body in utter desolation. The wind took her wail and scattered it, as if even sorrow here must be thinned to nothing.

Yeo, tougher of nerve and less given to reverence for any man in a cassock, broke the spell. “Shall I go and search the fellow, captain?”

“Better,” Amyas said, and knelt by the body.

Raleigh went to the girl and spoke gently in English. She looked up at armor and plume with wide, stunned eyes, then shook her head as if the language were a locked door, and bent again to her crying.

Raleigh laid a hand on her arm and eased her upright—not roughly, but with a careful firmness, as one lifts a pot from the fire so it will not spill. She did not resist; her grief had made her heavy and empty at once.

Amyas and Yeo bent over the corpse. It was a large man, coarsely featured, but wasted until the skin clung to bone. Hunger had hollowed him like a scooped crust. His hands and legs were cramped, his trunk bowed as if cold had folded him in upon himself. The stench was not of rot—death had come too cleanly for that—but of long privation: sour sweat, stale wool, and that faint sick-sweetness that clings to those who have been without food too long.

Yeo drew back the clothes from the thin breast. The girl screamed and wept, but still made no move to strike him; her sorrow was older than her anger.

“Ask her who it is,” Amyas said. “Yeo, you know a little Irish.”

Yeo asked. The girl’s eyes flashed, then shuttered; she made no answer.

“The stubborn jade won’t tell,” Yeo muttered. “If she were but a man, I’d make her soon enough.”

“Ask her who killed him.”

Yeo questioned again, listening close. “No one, she says. And I believe her, for I find no wound. The man has been starved, sirs, as I am a sinful man. God help him, though he is a priest. Yet—he seems full enough down below. What’s here? A big pouch, stuffed full of somewhat.”

“Hand it hither,” said Amyas.

They opened it. Not a crust, not a grain—only papers. Papers, papers; and then a parchment. They unrolled it with fingers stiffened by wind.

“Latin,” Amyas said. “Construe, Don Scholar.”

Raleigh took it, and his face changed as if the bog’s cold had touched some deeper place. He read a moment—then drew a sharp breath.

“Is it possible?” he said softly. “This is indeed a prize. This is Saunders himself.”

Yeo sprang back from the body as if it had turned to snake. “Nick Saunders, the Legate, sir?”

“Nicholas Saunders,” Raleigh replied, eyes still on the script, “the Pope’s legate.”

“The villain!” Yeo’s voice broke with a soldier’s thwarted fury. “Why did he not wait for me to have the comfort of killing him? Dog!” He kicked the corpse.

“Quiet!” Amyas snapped, half turning toward the girl as she shrieked at the insult. “Remember the poor wench.”

Raleigh, half to himself, went on, not without a strange pity threaded through his contempt. “Yes—Saunders. Misguided fool, and this is the end. To this thou hast come with thy plotting and conspiring, thy boasting, consecrated banners and bulls, Agnus Deis and holy waters, the blessing of all saints and angels, and thy Lady of the Immaculate Conception. Thou hast called on the heavens to judge between thee and us—and here is their answer.”

He glanced at something clenched in the dead man’s hand. “What is that, Amyas? Give it me.”

A pastoral epistle—addressed to Ormond and the nobles of Ireland. Raleigh’s lip curled as he read the opening—“To all who groan beneath the loathsome tyranny of an illegitimate adulteress”—and the usual flood of venom. He murmured, almost with the dryness of a scholar tasting a spoiled sauce: “Bah. And this, forsooth, thy last meditation. Incorrigible pedant. Victrix causa Diis placuit, sed victa Catoni.”

He ran his eye through other sheets—great promises from Pope and Spain, frantic slanders against Elizabeth and the chief servants of the realm, threats of endless fire for those who would not submit, sainting of rebels and traitors newly hanged, arguments for rebellion, for the nullity of English law, for the right to kill a prince deemed heretic. Words upon words—dominant words meant to choke out the small native words of common life: the plain language of bread and milk and hearth, the unrecorded speech by which mothers comfort children. It struck Raleigh—briefly, uncomfortably—that here, too, power hid itself in a lexicon: one tongue declaring another damned, one set of names claiming the right to define all others. And yet what could any ink do against hunger? Saunders had carried a library where he should have carried a loaf.

With a gesture of disgust, Raleigh crammed the papers back into the pouch.

They took it with them and walked back to the company. The wind still tore at their cloaks; the bog still made its sucking, patient sound. Behind them, the girl’s keening rose once more, then dwindled as distance swallowed it, as if the land were trained to eat voices.

They remounted, and marched on toward the Desmond lands; and the girl was left alone with the dead.

An hour passed.

Then another Englishman stood by the same wailing girl, and around him a dozen shock-headed kernes, skeans at thigh and javelins in hand, shifted their tawny rags and joined their lamentation to hers. Their cries rose and fell together, a bitter chorus that made the empty place ring; and in that wild music—so unlike the ordered sounds of a tended farmyard—there was a kind of acoustic rule: grief as a communal duty, sorrow as a language all could speak when other words failed.

The Englishman was Eustace Leigh—still a layman, yet still about the work which had become his penance and his hope. Two years of intrigue and labor from end to end of Ireland had worn him. His brow was more wrinkled, his features harder. The scar upon his face, and the slight distortion that came with it, lay hidden under a bushy beard from all but his own memory; and he never forgot it, nor forgot who had given it.

He had wandered with Desmond in moor and moss for months, hunted like a beast. Now he had been on his way to James Fitz-Eustace, Lord Baltinglas, to bring news of Desmond’s death, and with him a remnant of the clan—men too stout-hearted, or too deeply stained with crime, to seek the English mercy which, as so often happens, is easiest to those who most distrust it.

And now he stood looking down upon what was left of Ireland’s most sacred personage: the man who, Eustace had once hoped, would regenerate the island, bring the West under that gentle yoke in which, he believed, united Christendom labored for the commonweal of the universal Church.
