There lay Saunders; and with him, in that bog, lay Eustace’s dreams—dreams he had spoken aloud as certainties: that the land would rise as one man, even to the baby at the breast; that the Saxon heretic would be swept into the sea; that Spain and the Pope and the Jesuits’ wealth would stand behind him; that noblemen pledged to die and serfs ready to worship would make victory inevitable.

Starved to death in a bog.

It was a verdict plain enough for any man who could taste reality; but not for Eustace Leigh. Failure was an accident, a momentary clouding of providence in a wicked world whose prince, all knew, was the devil. Indeed—was it not proof of righteousness? When had the true faith not been trampled? If one cleansed one’s eyes of carnal impatience, what was this but martyrdom?

“Blest Saunders,” Eustace murmured, and the words came out with a tremor, half prayer and half hunger—for sanctity, for meaning, for a language that would make suffering intelligible. “Let me die the death of the righteous, and let my last end be like this. Ora pro me, most excellent martyr, while I dig thy grave upon this lonely moor, to wait there for thy translation to one of those stately shrines which, cemented by the blood of such as thee, shall rise restored toward heaven, and make this land once more the Isle of Saints.”

They buried the corpse there in the sour earth. A few prayers were said hastily, words clipped by fear and wind. Then Eustace was gone—not now to find Baltinglas, for it was more than his life was worth. The girl had told him of English soldiers who had passed; he knew they would reach the earl before he did. The game was up; all was lost.

So, as a desperate resource, he turned back toward the last place he would be looked for. After a month of disguises and hiding, he found himself again in his native Devon; while Fitz-Eustace, Viscount Baltinglas, took ship for Spain, having got little by his famous argument to Ormond for joining Rome—“Had not thine ancestor, blessed Thomas of Canterbury, died for the Church of Rome, thou hadst never been Earl of Ormond.” The premises were sound enough, for the Ormond lands had been granted by Henry II to expiate that turbulent murder; but the conclusion was of a piece with the rest of his party’s logic.

And now the road draws us back to Raleigh and Amyas as they jog on, their troop strung out behind them like a dark thread across the pale bog. It is but three days since they met, and they have much to speak of.

Amyas, as the march shows, is sliding fast into Raleigh’s old opinion of Ireland; while Raleigh, under the sudden inspiration of a possible grant of Desmond’s lands, looks on bog and rock transfigured by his hopes as if a rainbow had laid its colors upon them. He looked at all things so—noble fellow—even thirty years after, when he was old, worn, and ruined. Well for him had it been otherwise, had his heart grown old with his head.

Amyas, who knows nothing of Desmond’s lands, cannot make sense of the change.

“Why, what is this, Raleigh?” he said. “You are like children in the market-place, and nothing pleases you. You wanted to get to court, and you have got there; and are lord and master, I hear, or near it already. And as soon as fortune stuffs your mouth full of sweet-meats, do you turn informer on her?”

Raleigh laughed—short, as if the sound itself did not satisfy him—and was silent a moment. The wind made speech costly; it stole consonants and left words ragged.

“And how is your friend Mr. Secretary Spenser,” Amyas asked, “who was with us at Smerwick?”

“Spenser?” Raleigh answered. “He has thriven even as I have. And he has found, as I have, that in making one friend at court you make ten foes. ‘Oderint dum metuant’ is no motto for him—nor for me. I want to be great—great I am already, they say, if a prince’s favor can swell the frog into an ox; but I want to be liked, loved. I want to see people smile when I enter.”

“So they do, I’ll warrant,” Amyas said drily.

“So do hyenas,” Raleigh returned. “They grin because they are hungry, and I may throw them a bone.” He glanced at Amyas with sudden warmth. “I’ll throw you one now, old lad—or rather a good sirloin of beef, for the sake of your smile. That’s honest at least, whosoever else is not. Have you heard of my brother Humphrey’s new project?”

“How should I hear anything,” Amyas answered, sweeping the waste with his hand, “in this howling wilderness?”

“Kiss hands to the wilderness then,” Raleigh said, and in his voice came a note like spice in plain broth—sharp, enlivening, dangerous. “Come with me to Newfoundland.”

“You—to Newfoundland?”

“Yes. I to Newfoundland, unless my little matter here be settled at once. Gloriana does not know it and shall not till I am off. She would send me to the Tower, I think, if she caught me playing truant. I could hardly get leave to come hither; but I must out and try my fortune. I am over ears in debt already, and sick of courts and courtiers. Humphrey must go next spring and take possession of his kingdom beyond seas, or his patent expires. With him I go—and you too, my circumnavigating giant.”

And as they rode—wind hissing through helmet-rims, leather creaking, the bog’s soft suck keeping time—Raleigh poured out to Amyas the whole design of the Newfoundland venture: harbors and fisheries, plantations and patents, the taking of a land by ink and oar and hunger for new beginnings. His words were brisk as a cook’s when the fire is right and the knife is sharp: full of measures and means, of what could be salted down for winter, what could be gathered fresh from the sea, what could be made to feed both purse and pride. Behind them, Ireland’s wail faded into the wind; before them, the road went on, and the world’s larger appetite drew them westward.

Every soul in Bideford—aye, and every stranger who has but once smelt the river at low tide, brackish and weedy, with the tar of boats and the sharp bite of oakum—learns soon enough that the town is not built round its market, nor its church, nor even its quay, but round its bridge. The bridge stands there as if it had been set down by a hand older than masons’ craft: a backbone of stone spanning the Torridge, taking into itself the tramp of hoof and boot, the creak of cart-wheels, the cries of fishwives, the bell of the parish, the soft slap of water under the arches, and the hiss of wind through the parapets. It is the town’s ear as much as its spine; for on a still day you can tell by the bridge’s hum whether the river runs full or thin, whether the tide comes in easy or fretting, whether peace sits on the houses or quarrel.

And if men were honest, they would confess that Bideford Bridge has never been a mere work of stone. It has been, by turns and all at once, a thing blessed and a thing feared: a bridge that saved souls by letting pilgrims pass dry-foot; a bridge that took alms and gave them again; a bridge that paid for schooling; a bridge that could, so the old tell it, feel the weight of a liar’s foot more sharply than a true man’s. Folk will still whisper, lowering their voices as if the very arches might be listening, how the first stones were carried away each night from the place the builders chose, and set down—without the mark of crowbar or rope—half a mile lower, where the sand lies treacherous and shifts like flour under a baker’s palm. Sir Richard Gurney, parson of the parish, fretted himself into sleeplessness over that theft, dreaming of devils busy in his sheepfold; and then, at last, came his vision—an angel, so the tale goes, who spoke plain sense with heavenly authority: build it where the stones go of their own will, for there alone is foundation amid the false, sliding bed.

Bishop Grandison of Exeter, a man who knew that bodies have their claims as surely as souls, made the work holy by proclamation: indulgence and benediction, and “participation in all spiritual blessings for ever,” to any who would help bridge that dangerous ford. Thus did Bideford, with a piety not untouched by prudence, contrive to “make the best of both worlds.”

What queer sayings have grown up about the bridge since then! That its foundations are laid upon wool, and yet it quivers under a horse’s lightest step; that though it bears three-and-twenty arches, a certain William Alford—another Milo, if you like to believe it—carried for a wager four bushels, salt-water measure, all the length thereof. Stranger still (and truer), that the bridge is, in law, an esquire in its own right, bearing arms like a gentleman—ship and bridge on a plain field—and owning lands and tenements in many parishes, by which it has founded charities, built schools, and fought suits at Westminster as stoutly as any flesh-and-blood litigant.

But of all its works, the one that concerns us most is this: it has given dinners. Yearly dinners. And for that purpose, being (as Bideford folk say with a wink) a bridge of a very good palate, it kept the best-stocked cellar of wines in all Devon—bottles lying cool as river-stones, casks breathing out their oak and their long, sleepy sweetness in the dark.

To one of these bridge dinners, in the year of our Lord 1583, were bidden all the notabilities of Bideford; and with them Mr. St. Leger of Annery, whose house sat close by, looking down upon park and river, and whose open hand was so busy in his lifetime that he saved his children, after him, from the temptation of riches by leaving them none to tempt.

It fell out, as often at table where men’s tongues are warmed, that a trustee of the bridge and Mr. St. Leger came to words—civilly, but with heat—upon a matter of great weight to Devon stomachs: whether a salmon caught below the bridge was better or worse than one caught above. No argument can live long when a fish is in question and honest appetites are present; proof must be had. Mr. St. Leger swore that since the bridge had fed him well, he would feed the bridge in return; he offered five pounds that he would find them, out of the pool below Annery, a salmon as firm and flaky as the Appledore fish they had just eaten; and then, in that overflow of hospitality which is as sudden as the lifting of a lark, he bade the whole company dine with him at Annery three days thence, and bring each man a wife or daughter. Don Guzman being at table, he was invited too.

So it came to pass that there was a mighty feast in the great hall at Annery, such as had been rare since Judge Hankford feasted Edward the Fourth there. The house itself seemed to remember old banquets: oak-panelling dark with age, a hearth wide enough to roast an ox, and the air full of mingled scents—wood-smoke, rushes strewn newly underfoot, rosemary bruised by skirts, and the rich, steady perfume of meat brought to the board.

Servants went and came with that soft, trained swiftness that adds its own music to a feast: trenchers set down, knives laid, cups filled. Pewter flashed; napery whitened the long table like a river of linen. There was no starving of the eye nor cheating of the nose. Dishes arrived steaming—capons browned and crackling, their skin salted just so; pasties whose crusts had been pinched and glazed to a deep shine; platters of river-fish set in order, the flesh pearled and ready to lift in clean flakes with the knife; herbs chopped fine and strewn; bowls of sharp sauces—verjuice and mustard, and one sweet-sour with fruit that made the mouth water before it touched the tongue. When the salmon came—pink as dawn under its skin, and smelling of clean river and salt—it drew a murmur from those who knew how to praise without words.

While every one ate their best and drank their worst, Rose Salterne and Don Guzman played a more dangerous game than any wager over fish. They pretended not to see each other, and watched each other all the more. Rose, at least, had to keep her eyes bridled; for not only did her father sit at the table, but just opposite her were two young men newly returned on furlough from her Majesty’s Irish army—Mr. William Cary and Mr. Arthur St. Leger—whose soldierly faces and quick, measuring looks made a girl feel as if even her silence could be questioned.

Rose and the Spaniard had not exchanged one word in six months, though they had met many times. Don Guzman did not avoid her company—save in her father’s house. There he obeyed her too carefully, as if she were air: always the stateliest of salutations on coming and going, never a glance that could be accused in open day. Yet whenever he was in her presence elsewhere, he set himself—deliberate as a cook seasoning a dish—to show his best: more witty, more eloquent, more full of romantic wonder and far-off tales than ever he had been. He had tried one way and failed; now he tried another, more subtle and more perilous.

For in his mind Rose had deserved punishment for daring to refuse a Spanish nobleman; and what punishment, he thought, could sting more sharply than the withdrawal of his smiles—the closing of his sunshine? There was vanity in that, but cunning too. He knew well how the world values a man at the price he puts upon himself, and how women—especially proud women—are tempted to pay dearly for what seems withheld.

So he did not stoop to gross tricks, such as paying attention to another woman—too coarse a bait, that might have raised disgust or despair but would never have brought Rose to his feet. Instead, he showed her, quietly and without fuss, that he could do without her. And Rose, poor soul, began at once to ask herself why. What secret treasure did he possess that made him independent of her, while she could not say she was independent of him? Was there some mystery at his heart? Some great ambition? Some knowledge he kept locked away like spice in a chest? Some capacity of love beyond common men? And worse—was he not, in all things, greater than she: wiser, richer-hearted, better-born? If his wealth could but mend her poverty—

Step by step she was being led to sue, as a pauper, to the very man she had once dismissed. The temptation of being the keeper of some hidden sanctuary—of knowing what other women did not, of feeling herself set apart—grew too strong for her, as it grows strong for most. She tired of her mask. At table that day, between the clatter of knives and the thick talk of men, Don Guzman saw her unease and downcast looks; and thinking, in his conceit, that she was punished enough, he let his eyes steal to hers now and then. When she dropped her gaze at meeting his, he was not abashed; he only watched her blush, watched her grow more silent, more abstracted, as if her very breath were busy with his name.

Then he, too, played at abstraction—till at last he caught her looking at him to see whether he looked at her. In that instant he knew she was snared. With his eyes he asked, Do you forgive me? And she stopped short in talk with her neighbour, faltered, lowered her eyes, and then lifted them again—searching his—so he might ask it once more. What could she do but answer with all her face, with the bend of her pretty neck: And do you forgive me in turn?

Upon that, Don Guzman burst forth bright as a bird at dawn, and became the life of the company—story, jest, repartee flowing from him like wine from a tilted cup. And Rose knew, with a tremor of guilt that was half delight, that she was the cause of his sudden joy. She blamed herself, and yet her heart leapt. She longed for the feast to end that she might flee home and hide herself alone with sweet fancies whose reality she dared not face.

After dinner the company drifted out to the great terrace. It was a fair sight: the stately house behind, warm stone in the midsummer glare; before them the park with old oaks thick as history, deer moving like brown shadows between trunks, and the broad, land-locked river spread below like a lake of hammered brightness. The air held the scent of hot grass, distant water, and the faint sour-sweet breath of fermenting ale from within. Laughter rose and fell in little bursts. Dresses rustled; ribbons flickered; women walked in twos and threes, peering, gossiping, praising, pricking one another with smiles. The day had its own soundscape: the far caw of rooks, the small insistence of insects, the soft thud of hooves in turf, and beneath it all the steady, soothing voice of the river—nature’s music, not the harsh hammering that later ages would call progress and sell as triumph.

Mrs. St. Leger, the hostess, did the honours with a kindly state; beside her moved fair Lady Grenville, a presence that drew eyes as flame draws moths. Rose kept apart, as if she could find a corner of shadow on a sunlit terrace, longing to laugh or cry, she knew not which.

“Our pretty Rose seems sad,” said Lady Grenville, coming to her with an ease that made kindness look like grace. “Cheer up, child! We want you to come and sing to us.”

Rose answered she scarce knew what, and obeyed as if she were walking in a dream.

She took the lute and sat down on a bench beneath the house, where the wall gave a strip of shade and the stone held a coolness against her skirts. The others gathered round her, voices softening, as if music demanded a gentler tongue.

“What shall I sing?” she asked.

“Let us have your old song, ‘Earl Haldan’s Daughter.’”

She shrank from it. It was loud and dashing, ill-matched to her thoughts; and Frank—Frank—had praised it in happier days long gone. The words felt ominous in her mouth. Yet she dared not refuse, for fear that refusal might call notice where no one had yet suspected. So she began, forcing steadiness into her fingers and voice—

“It was Earl Haldan’s daughter,
She look’d across the sea;
She look’d across the water,
And long and loud laugh’d she;
‘The locks of six princesses
Must be my marriage-fee,
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Who comes a wooing me?’

“It was Earl Haldan’s daughter,
She walk’d along the sand;
When she was aware of a knight so fair,
Come sailing to the land.
His sails were all of velvet,
His mast of beaten gold,
And ‘hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat,
Who saileth here so bold?’

“‘The locks of five princesses
I won beyond the sea;
I shore their golden tresses,
To fringe a cloak for thee.
One handful yet is wanting,
But one of all the tale;
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Furl up thy velvet sail!’

“He leapt into the water,
That rover young and bold;
He gript Earl Haldan’s daughter,
He shore her locks of gold;
‘Go weep, go weep, proud maiden,
The tale is full to-day.
Now hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Sail Westward-ho, and away!’”

As the last note died, a measured voice, foreign in accent and yet trained to please, slid into the hush and made it tremble.

“In the East,” said Don Guzman, “they say the nightingale sings to the rose; Devon, more happy, has nightingale and rose in one.”

“We have no nightingales in Devon, Don Guzman,” Lady Grenville answered, lightly, as if to clip his compliment before it could fly too near Rose. “But our little forest thrushes sing, as you hear, sweetly enough to content any ear. What brings you away from the gentlemen so early?”

“These letters,” he said, holding them up. “They have just been put into my hand; and as they call me home to Spain, I was loath to lose a moment of that delightful company from which I must part so soon.”

“To Spain?” half-a-dozen voices echoed at once; for the Don was a general favourite.

“Yes; and thence to the Indies. My ransom has arrived, and with it the promise of an office. I am to be Governor of La Guayra in Caracas. Congratulate me on my promotion.”

A mist swam over Rose’s eyes. The Spaniard’s voice sounded hard, almost flippant, as if the whole world were a gaming-table and this but a throw of dice. Did he care for her at all? And if he did—was it hopeless? Heat rose in her cheeks, and she feared every woman there must see it. In a nervous haste—when silence would have been wisdom—she blurted out, to turn attention from herself:

“And where is La Guayra?”

“Half round the world,” he answered, “on the coast of the Spanish Main. The loveliest place on earth, and the loveliest governor’s house—set in a forest of palms at the foot of a mountain eight thousand feet high. I shall only want a wife there to be in paradise.”

“I do not doubt you may persuade some fair lady of Seville to accompany you thither,” said Lady Grenville.

“Thanks, gracious madam; but the truth is, that since I have had the bliss of knowing English ladies, I have begun to think they are the only ones on earth worth wooing.”

“A thousand thanks for the compliment; but I fear none of our free English maidens would like to submit to the guardianship of a duenna. Eh, Rose? How should you like to be kept under lock and key all day by an ugly old woman with a horn on her forehead?”

Rose turned so scarlet that Lady Grenville understood at once and would have steered the talk away; but before she could, some burgher’s wife blundered in with a commonplace about the jealousy of Spanish husbands, and another—giggling—spoke something more true than delicate of West Indian masters and fair slaves.

Don Guzman reddened. “Ladies,” he said, with a tight pride, “believe me these are but calumnies of ignorance. If we be more jealous than other nations, it is because we love more passionately. If some of us abroad are profligate, it is because they—poor men—have no helpmate, which, like the amethyst, keeps its wearer pure. I could tell you stories, ladies, of the constancy and devotion of Spanish husbands, even in the Indies, as strange as ever romancer invented.”

“Can you?” cried Lady Grenville. “Then we challenge you to give us one at least.”

“I fear it would be too long, madam.”

“The longer the more pleasant, señor. How can we spend an hour better this afternoon, while the gentlemen within are finishing their wine?”

In those days, when books were fewer and the tongues of travellers served as libraries, story-telling was an art as valued as carving at table; and as Don Guzman’s skill in it was known, the ladies drew in, chairs and benches were fetched, skirts settled, fans stilled. The terrace quieted into an attentive ring, the river’s murmur below and the faint clink from within making a distant accompaniment. Don Guzman seated himself in the midst with a proud humility, at Lady Grenville’s feet, and began:

“Your perfections, fair and illustrious ladies, must doubtless have heard how Sebastian Cabota, some five-and-forty years ago, sailed forth by commission of my late master, the Emperor Charles the Fifth, to discover the golden lands of Tarshish, Ophir, and Cipango; but being in want of provisions, stopped at the mouth of that mighty South American river to which he gave the name Rio de la Plata, and sailing up it discovered the fair land of Paraguay. Yet you may not have heard how, on the bank of that river, at the mouth of the Rio Terceiro, he built a fort which men still call Cabot’s Tower; nor, perhaps, the strange tale which will ever make that tower a sacred spot to all true lovers.

“For when Cabota returned to Spain the year after, he left in his tower a garrison of a hundred and twenty men under Nuno de Lara, Ruiz Moschera, and Sebastian da Hurtado—old friends and fellow-soldiers of my invincible grandfather Don Ferdinando da Soto; and with them a jewel more precious than Spain ever owned: Lucia Miranda, the wife of Hurtado, who, famed in the emperor’s court no less for wisdom and modesty than for unrivalled beauty, had flung aside palace pomp to marry a poor adventurer and follow him round the world.

“Mangora, cacique of the neighbouring Timbuez Indians—whose friendship Lara had contrived to win—cast eyes upon this fair creature; and no sooner saw than he coveted, no sooner coveted than he plotted with the devilish subtilty of a savage to seize by force what he knew he could never gain by right. She soon discovered his passion—she was wise enough (what every woman is not) to know when she is loved—and telling her husband, kept as much as she could out of her new lover’s sight. Meanwhile the savage pressed Hurtado to visit him and bring his lady. Hurtado, suspecting the snare yet fearing to offend, excused himself courteously on duty; and Mangora, mad with desire and disappointment, began to plot against his life.

“So passed several weeks, till food grew scarce; and Don Hurtado and Don Ruiz Moschera, with fifty soldiers, were sent up the river on a foraging party. Mangora saw his hour and leapt at it.

“The tower, ladies—so I have heard from those who have seen it—stands on a knoll where two rivers meet; on the land side stretches a dreary marsh covered with tall grass and bushes, a fit place for ambuscade. There Mangora hid four thousand Indians about the tower, while he himself went boldly up, followed by thirty men laden with grain, fruit, game, and all the delicacies his forests could afford.

“With smiling face he told the unsuspecting Lara his sorrow for the Spaniards’ want; begged him to accept the provision; and was, as he had expected, invited within to taste the wines of Spain.

“In went he and his thirty fellow-bandits; and the feast continued—songs and libations—far into the night. Often Mangora looked round; at last he asked boldly for fair Miranda; but she had shut herself in her lodging, pleading illness.

“A plea, fair ladies, which little availed. For no sooner had the Spaniards retired to rest—leaving, by I know not what madness, Mangora and his Indians within—than they were awakened by the cry of fire, the explosion of their magazine, and the inward rush of the four thousand from the marsh outside.

“Why pain your gentle ears with details of slaughter? A few fearful minutes sufficed to exterminate my bewildered and unarmed countrymen, to bind the only survivors—Miranda, the innocent cause of the tragedy, and four other women with their infants—and to lead them away in triumph through the forest towards the Indian town.

“Stunned by the suddenness of the evil, and more by thought of the worse to come, Miranda travelled all night and at daybreak was brought before the Indian king to receive her doom. Judge her astonishment when she saw it was not Mangora.

“A ray of hope flashed. She asked where he was.

“‘He was slain last night,’ said the king; ‘and I, his brother Siripa, am now cacique of the Timbuez.’

“It was true. Lara, maddened with drink, rage, and wounds, caught up his sword, rushed into the fight, singled out the traitor, slew him on the spot; and then, forgetting safety in revenge, continued to plunge his sword into the corpse, heedless of blows, till he fell pierced with a hundred wounds.

“A ray of hope, as I said, flashed across Miranda for a moment; but the next she found she had been freed from one bandit only to be delivered to another.

“‘Yes,’ said the new king in broken Spanish; ‘my brother played a bold stake and lost. But it was worth the risk. You cannot be his queen now; you must content yourself with being mine.’

“Miranda, desperate, answered with fierce taunts against his treachery and crime, asking how he could dream the wife of a Christian Spaniard would become the mistress of a heathen savage—hoping, unhappy lady, to exasperate him into killing her at once. But in vain. She only prolonged her misery. Whether her virtue and beauty awed him; whether some ambition moved him to make her wisdom serve his ends; or whether—though I will scarce believe it of one of those dark children of the devil—some pride within him made him too proud to take by force what he could not win by persuasion, certain it is the savage answered her with smiles; bade his men unbind her; told her she was no slave, and it lay with her alone to become sovereign of him and his vassals; he gave her a hut to herself, loaded her with ornaments, and for weeks treated her with courtesy—as miraculous as love itself—almost as if he had been a cavalier of Castile.

“Three months and more passed in this misery; each day Miranda grew more desperate of deliverance, seeing nearer and nearer some hideous end. When one day, going with the cacique’s wives to draw water from the river, she saw on the opposite bank a white man in a tattered Spanish dress, drawn sword in hand. No sooner did he spy her than, shrieking her name, he plunged into the stream, swam across, landed at her feet, and clasped her. It was—credible or no—Don Sebastian himself, returned with Ruiz Moschera to the tower to find only a charred and bloodstained heap.

“He guessed, as by inspiration, what had passed and whither she was taken; and without a thought of danger—like a true Spanish gentleman and a true Spanish lover—darted alone into the forest, guided by his loyal heart, and found again his treasure, still unstained, still his.

“Who can describe the joy—and then the terror—of that meeting? The Indian women fled. For ten short minutes the lovers were left together, and life was one long kiss. But what to do they knew not. Inland was the enemy. He would have swum with her across the river and tried; but his strength, worn with hunger and travel, failed. He drew her back with difficulty, sat down by her, and awaited doom with prayer—the first and last resource of virtuous ladies, as weapons are of cavaliers.

“Alas for them! May no true lovers ever have to weep over joys so soon lost, after being so hardly found! For ere a quarter of an hour had passed, the women returned with all the warriors of the tribe. Don Sebastian, desperate, would fain have slain his wife and himself; but his hand sank again—and whose would not but an Indian’s?—as he lifted it against that fair and faithful breast. In a few minutes he was surrounded, seized from behind, disarmed, and carried in triumph to the village. And if you cannot feel for him in that misery, fair ladies—who have known no sorrow—yet I, a prisoner, can.”

He paused, as if the tale had tightened his throat; and though no one spoke, the terrace did not fall silent—rather it seemed to listen harder: to the river’s voice below, to the birds in the oaks, to the distant laughter from the hall where men still drank, careless of how words can bind more surely than ropes. And Don Guzman, pausing, did not forget—no, not for the space of one breath—to see whether Rose Salterne’s eyes were on him. They were.

“The spirits of your fathers
Shall start from every wave;
For the deck it was their field of fame,
And ocean was their grave.”

The room in Plymouth where Mistress Hawkins sat of an evening had a smell that clung like a memory to oak: salt dried into woollen cloaks; tar that never quite left a sailor’s hands; beeswax from the panels; and, underneath it all, the faint metallic tang that rose whenever Master Adrian Gilbert opened his little packet of earths and stones and set them near the hearth to show her what he loved best—proof held in the palm.

He had been speaking long, as men do when they believe the world will yield at last to a clever fire and a finer word. His face, fair and thoughtful, was sharpened by furnace-light even when no furnace burned; his eyes had that weary redness of one who has watched a crucible too often and trusted too much to what might be coaxed out of dust.

“So you see, my dear Mistress Hawkins,” he said, and took up again the lump he had set upon her table as if it were a loaf whose crumb might be read, “having the silver—luna itself, as your own eyes show you—beside the ores of lead, manganese, and copper; and above all this gossan, as the Cornish call it, which I hold not merely the matrix of ore, but the very crude form and materia prima of all metals—you mark me? If my recipes, which I had from Doctor Dee, succeed only half so well as I expect, then I refine out the luna, lay it by, and transmute the remaining ores into sol, into gold. Whereupon Peru and Mexico become superfluities, and England the mistress of the globe. Strange, no doubt; distant, no doubt: but possible, my dear madam—possible!”

Mistress Hawkins, who had listened with the patience of one who had fed too many hungry mouths to be dazzled by promises of plenty, shook her head. Her hands, not old but worn, were folded close, as if she kept them from trembling.

“And what good to you if it be, Master Gilbert?” she answered softly. “If you could find a stone to turn sinners into saints, now—yet naught save God’s grace can do that; and that last seems ofttimes over long in coming.”

She sighed; and the sound was like the tide when it draws away from a shore and leaves more exposed than one wishes to see.

“But indeed, my dear madam, conceive now—the Comb Martin mine thus becomes a gold mine, perhaps inexhaustible; yields me wherewithal to carry out my North-West patent; meanwhile my brother Humphrey holds Newfoundland, and builds me fresh ships year by year—for the forests of pine are boundless—for my China voyage.”

“Sir Humphrey has better thoughts in his dear heart than gold, Master Adrian,” she said, with a steady look that did not flatter. “A very close and gracious walker he has been this seven year. I wish my Captain John were so too.”

Gilbert’s mouth tightened; ambition and conscience struggled there like two wrestlers in a narrow lane.

“And how do you know I have naught better in my mind’s eye than gold? Or, indeed, what better could I have? Is not gold the Spaniard’s strength—the very mainspring of Antichrist? By gold only, therefore, can we out-wrestle him. You shake your head; but say, dear madam—for gold England must have—which is better: to make gold bloodlessly at home, or take it bloodily abroad?”

“Oh, Master Gilbert, Master Gilbert!” She lifted her eyes, and there was fear in them not for herself but for the shape her town had taken. “Is it not written that those who make haste to be rich pierce themselves through with many sorrows? God’s blessing is not on it all.”

“Not on you, madam? Be sure brave Captain John Hawkins’s star told me a different tale, when I cast his nativity.—Born under stormy planets, truly, but under right royal and fortunate ones.”

“Ay,” she answered, “I am a simple body, and you a great philosopher; but I hold there is no star for the seaman like the Star of Bethlehem; and that goes with ‘peace on earth and good will to men,’ and not with such arms as that, Master Adrian. I can’t abide to look upon them.”

Her finger pointed up to one of the carved bosses in the ribbed oak roof, where the fatal crest shone with gilt and paint—a half-Moor, bound. The gold leaf caught the candle, and for an instant it seemed to move, as if the bound man breathed.

“Ah, Master Gilbert!” Her voice thickened, as if words had to pass through brine. “Since first he went to Guinea after those poor negroes, little lightness has my heart known; and the very day that crest was set up in our grand new house, as the parson read the first lesson, there was this text in it: ‘Woe to him that buildeth his house by iniquity, and his chambers by wrong. Shalt thou live because thou closest thyself in cedar?’ And it went into my ears like fire, and into my heart like lead. When the parson went on, ‘Did not thy father eat and drink, and do judgment and justice? then it was well with him,’ I thought of good old Captain Will; and—I tell you, Master Gilbert, those negroes are on my soul from morning until night! We are all mighty grand now, and money comes in fast, but the Lord will require the blood of them at our hands yet. He will!”

The room itself seemed to answer her with its trophies. On the sideboard were Spanish cups and salvers that had once held sweet wine and now held nothing but reflected candle-flame. Strange birds, dried skins, charts with ragged coasts scratched in ink, rough drawings of bays and bars: all the world brought home and nailed, as it were, to a Devon wall. Over the fireplace hung old Captain Will Hawkins’s portrait—ruddy, bold, and of an age when men could be fierce and yet feel simpler in their fierceness. Beside it, the Spanish ensign taken at Rio de la Hacha drooped in folds that smelled faintly of smoke even after fifteen years.

Gilbert, who could talk boldly of nations and metals, was a little abashed before a woman’s plain terror of judgment.

“My dearest madam,” he said at last, “who can prosper more than you? If your husband copied the Dons too closely once or twice in that matter—which I do not deny—was he not punished at once when he lost ships, men, all but life, at St. Juan de Ulloa?”

“Ay, yes,” she answered; “and that did give me a bit of comfort, especially when the Queen—God save her tender heart—was so sharp with him for pity of the poor wretches; but it has not mended him. He is growing fast like the rest now, Master Gilbert—greedy to win, and niggardly to spend (God forgive him!), and always fretting and plotting for some new gain, and envying and grudging at Drake, and all who are deeper in the snare of prosperity than he is. Gold, gold, nothing but gold in every mouth—there it is!”

Her tone rose, not in anger but in grief, like a kettle beginning to sing because the fire will not be denied.

“Ah! I mind when Plymouth was a quiet little God-fearing place as God could smile upon. But ever since my John, and Sir Francis, and poor Master Oxenham found out the way to the Indies, it’s been a sad place. Not a sailor’s wife but is crying ‘Give, give,’ like the daughters of the horse-leech; and every woman must drive her husband out across seas to bring her home money to squander on hoods and farthingales, and go mincing with outstretched necks and wanton eyes; and they will soon learn to do worse than that, for the sake of gain. The Lord’s hand will be against their tires and crisping-pins, their mufflers and farthingales, as it was against the Jews of old. Ah, dear me!”

As she spoke, the house’s sounds laid their own music under her words: the low hiss of a settling log; the faint creak of timber; outside, from the street, the distant clink of a harness chain and the thin, uneasy laughter of men loosed from duty. Plymouth’s harbour had its own voice too—water slapping stone, a stray gull, the hoarse call of a watch—sounds that could soothe when they were honest and measured, and could gnaw at the mind when they multiplied into shouts and hammering and the restless din of profit. Mistress Hawkins heard it all as if it were a psalm turned sour.

Gilbert sat looking at her a while in silence. He was not a hard man; he was rather one whose head ran before his heart could follow, and whose heart then blamed itself for its own hunger.

“The Lord be with you, dearest lady,” he said at last. “Strange, how you women sit at home to love and suffer, while we men rush forth to break our hearts and yours against rocks of our own seeking! Ah well! were it not for Scripture, I should have thought that Adam, rather than Eve, had been the one who plucked the fruit of the forbidden tree.”

“We women, I fear, did the deed nevertheless,” she said; “for we bear the doom of it our lives long.”

“You always remind me, madam, of my dear Mistress Leigh of Burrough, and her counsels.”

“Do you see her often? I hear of her as one of the Lord’s most precious vessels.”

“I would have done more ere now than see her,” he answered, and coloured like a boy at his own confession, “had she allowed me. But she lives only for the memory of her husband and the fame of her noble sons.”

Even as he spoke, the door opened; and a gust of night-air came in with a smell of wet rope and cold sea. A man stepped across the threshold wrapped in a rough sea-gown, as if the very spray had stitched it to him.

Adrian Gilbert turned pale.

“Amyas Leigh! What brings you hither? How fares my brother? Where is the ship?”

“The Golden Hind is gone on to Dartmouth, with Master Hayes,” Amyas said. His voice was hoarse with wind and want of sleep. “Your brother is well, Master Gilbert. I came ashore here, meaning to go north to Bideford ere I went to London. I called at Drake’s just now, but he was away.”

“The Golden Hind?” Mistress Hawkins cried, astonished into warmth. “Yet welcome ever, sir. This is a great surprise, though. Captain John did not look for you till next year.”

Amyas did not answer. He stood as a man stands who has carried a weight too long and is frightened to set it down lest it crush someone else.

“Something is wrong!” Adrian’s voice sharpened. “Speak!”

Amyas tried, but could not. The silence thudded like a sail flogging.

“Will you drive a man mad, sir?” Adrian started up, and in a desperate tenderness held the candle close to Amyas’s face as if light could force truth to show itself. “Has the adventure failed? You said my brother was well.”

“He is well,” Amyas whispered.

“Then what—Why do you look at me in that fashion, sir?”

Amyas’s lip quivered. He laid his hand on Adrian’s shoulder; the touch was heavy with pity.

“Your great and glorious brother, sir, is better bestowed than in settling Newfoundland.”

“Dead?” Adrian shrieked, and the word seemed to strike the carved ceiling and fall back shattered. “Dead?”

“He is with the God whom he served.”

“He was always with Him, like Enoch. Parable me no parables, if you love me, sir!”

“And, like Enoch, he was not; for God took him.”

Adrian clasped his hands over his forehead and leaned against the table as if he would hold his skull together.

“Go on,” he said at last, very low. “Go on. God will give me strength to hear all.”

Then Amyas spoke; and while he did, the room filled with another world—cold seas and unruly men, the snap of canvas, the groan of hulls, and in between the ghastly cheer men make when they are afraid and will not confess it. He told of the ruffian crews caught up at hazard; of mutinies and conspiracies to carry off ships; of plundering fishing vessels as if hunger excused theft; of desertions multiplying daily; of licenses granted by the general to the lazy and fearful to return home. When Amyas reached that, Adrian groaned aloud.

“From him? Conspired against him? Deserted from him? Dotards, buzzards! Where would they have found such another leader?”

“Your illustrious brother, sir,” Amyas answered with soldierly respect and a sailor’s bluntness, “if you will pardon me, was a very great philosopher, but not so much of a general.”

“General, sir? Where was braver man?”

“Not on God’s earth, but that does not make a general, sir. If Cortés had been brave and no more, Mexico would have been Mexico still. The truth is, sir, Cortés, like my Captain Drake, knew when to hang a man; and your great brother did not.”

It was said without malice; only the hard truth of command, which is as unpalatable as unsalted bread and yet as necessary.

Amyas went on. He told how they set sail from St. John’s to discover the southward coast; and how Sir Humphrey, chivalrous to his own hurt, chose to go in the little Squirrel of ten tons, “overcharged with nettings, fights, and small ordnance,” not only because she could nose into creeks, but because he had heard a taunt among the men that he feared the sea. That small pride, that large courage—both were of the same Devon blood, and both carried their own doom.

Then came woe upon woe. Seven days after they left Cape Raz, their biggest ship, the Delight, made a last brave revel, as if men could drum and pipe death away. “Most part of the night,” Amyas said, quoting the words of Hayes, “like the swan that singeth before her death, continued in sounding of trumpets, drums, and fifes; also winding of the cornets and hautboys; and, in the end of their jollity, left off with the battle and doleful knells.” Next day she struck—Golden Hind and Squirrel sheering off just in time—upon unknown shoals. All but fourteen were lost; and among them Budaeus, Frank’s learned friend, swallowed up as quietly as if the sea had always owned him.

Those who escaped, after cold and famine and the gnawing misery of wet clothes that would not dry, were cast on shore again in Newfoundland; and at last, worn out with hunger and want of garments, the crews of the two remaining ships persuaded Sir Humphrey to turn for England on the thirty-first of August.

“And on that very instant,” Amyas continued, “even in winding about, we beheld close alongside—so Master Hayes writ it—a very lion in shape, hair, and colour; not swimming, but sliding on the water, with his whole body; who passed along, turning his head to and fro, yawning and gaping wide, with ugly demonstration of long teeth and glaring eyes; and to bid us farewell—coming right against the Hind—he sent forth a horrible voice, roaring or bellowing as doth a lion.”

Adrian’s face tightened as if he tasted iron.

“And the devil it was, doubtless,” he said; “the roaring lion who goes about seeking whom he may devour.”

“He has not got your brother, at least,” Amyas answered.

“No,” Mistress Hawkins said through her tears; and there was no scoffing in her, for those were days when the unseen pressed close upon the seen. “He roared for joy to think how many poor souls would be left still in heathen darkness by Sir Humphrey’s death. God be with that good knight, and send all mariners where he is now!”

Amyas’s voice sank. The room’s small noises—coal shifting, candle-wick ticking—seemed suddenly too loud against the wide silence of what he told.

“When we were off the Azores,” he said, “the storms came heavier than ever, with terrible seas breaking short and pyramid-wise. On the ninth of September the Squirrel nearly foundered and yet recovered; and the general, sitting abaft with a book in his hand, cried out to us in the Hind so oft as we did approach within hearing: ‘We are as near heaven by sea as by land,’ reiterating the same speech—well beseeming a soldier resolute in Jesus Christ, as I can testify he was.

“The same Monday, about twelve of the clock, or not long after, the frigate being ahead of us in the Golden Hind, suddenly her lights were out; and withal our watch cried, the general was cast away, which was true; for in that moment the frigate was devoured and swallowed up of the sea.”

So it ended. Not with trumpet, nor with any grand last word heard by the land, but with a light snuffed as easily as a candle in a draught.

“Oh, my brother! my brother!” Adrian moaned, and the scholar’s grief in him broke into the boy’s. “The glory of his house, the glory of Devon!”

“Ah! what will the Queen say?” Mistress Hawkins whispered.

“Tell me,” Adrian asked, mastering himself by sheer will, “had he the jewel on when he died?”

“The Queen’s jewel? He always wore that,” Amyas answered, “and his own posy too—Mutare vel timere sperno. He wore it; and he lived it.”

“Ay,” Adrian said, staring as if he could see the little ring glinting in the black water, “the same to the last.”

“Not quite the same,” Amyas said. “He was a meeker man latterly than he used to be. As he said himself once, a better refiner than any whom he had on board had followed him close all the seas over, and purified him in the fire. And gold seven times tried he was, when God, having done His work in him, took him home at last.”

After that there were no more words worth spending. The venture had failed utterly; Adrian was ruined; and Amyas had lost his own stake with the rest. Adrian rose at last, pale and stiff, and begged leave to retire; he must collect himself.

“Poor gentleman,” Mistress Hawkins said when he was gone. “It is little else he has left to collect.”

“Or I either,” Amyas answered, with a bitter breath that tried to be a laugh and could not. “I was going to ask you to lend me one of your son’s shirts, and five pounds to get myself and my men home.”

“Five? Fifty, Captain Leigh!” she cried, as if the very notion stung her pride. “God forbid that John Hawkins’s wife should refuse her last penny to a distressed mariner, and he a gentleman born. But you must eat and drink.”

“It’s more than I have done for many a day worth speaking of,” Amyas said.

So she set food before him—plain Plymouth plenty, yet to a man fresh off starvation it might as well have been a banquet laid by angels. There was a great manchet loaf, its crust browned close and crackling; a dish of salt beef warmed through with onions so their sweetness rose up and took hold of the air; a pottage thickened with pease and a knob of butter melted in, making a glossy pool; and a sharp little cheese that smelled of the cow and the clean straw. She poured him ale—dark, strong, and honest—its head standing like foam on a wave; and the sound of it, glugging into the cup, was comfort in itself, as if the house spoke: here is shore, here is hearth, here is something that will not sink.

As he ate, the room softened. Even the hard trophies on the walls seemed less like stolen splendours and more like burdensome heirlooms. Mistress Hawkins sat opposite, and while he tore bread with hands still roughened by rope, she told him what she knew of his mother—Mistress Leigh—whom Adrian Gilbert had seen a few months before in London. The names and places were like familiar herbs stirred into broth, restoring some taste of home.

And then, naturally, she turned to news from Bideford.

“And by the by, Captain Leigh,” she said, lowering her voice as if the very boards might carry tales, “I’ve sad news for you from your place; and I had it from one who was there at the time. You must know a Spanish captain, a prisoner—”

“What, the one I sent home from Smerwick?” Amyas asked, and his spoon paused.

“You sent? Mercy on us! Then perhaps you’ve heard—”

“How can I have heard? What?”

“That he’s gone off, the villain.”

“Without paying his ransom?”

“I can’t say that; but there’s a poor innocent young maid gone off with him—one Salterne’s daughter—the Popish serpent!”

Amyas went white under his weather-tan.

“Rose Salterne,” he said, as if the name were a cut, “the Mayor’s daughter—the Rose of Torridge!”

“That’s her.” Mistress Hawkins half rose in alarm, for he had fallen back in his chair as if shot through. “Bless your dear soul, what ails you?”

He forced his breath to steady, ashamed of his weakness and yet not master of it.

“You’ll forgive me, madam,” he said, striving to smile. “I’m weak from the sea; and your good ale has turned me a bit dizzy, I think.”

“Ay, yes—’tis too heavy till you’ve been on shore a while. Try the aqua vitae; my Captain John has it right good; and a bit too fond of it too, poor dear soul, between whiles—Heaven forgive him!”

And before he could refuse she poured him strong brandy cut with water and made him drink it down. The spirit burned a path to his stomach and left heat in his throat, but it did not burn away the image that had risen in him at her words: Rose—warm and living—gone into the cold mouth of a foreign tongue, into a world where even love would be named in another lexicon, and her own Devon speech might be mocked or silenced. He lay down when she sent him, as obedient as a child, but sleep would not come. The house’s gentle night-sounds—distant harbour-calls, the soft settling of beams, the mutter of a city dreaming of gain—wrapped him in a net that held his body and left his mind beating against its knots.

When grey morning came at last, he rose with eyes that felt full of sand, took what Mistress Hawkins gave—money for the road, a clean shirt for shame’s sake, and kindness that asked no payment—and set his face toward Bideford.

In those years it was no light undertaking to ride from Plymouth northward into Devon, unless a man chose to lose half a day by wide detours and safer lanes. For between a traveller and his destination lay a tract of moor and broken down where authority went thin as watered ale, and where a sort of petty prince—no anointed king, yet feared as if crowned—kept his own rough court. Folk called him, when he could not hear, the King of the Gubbings; and the name, like a fishmonger’s insult flung with relish, clung because it carried shame in it and because the country itself seemed to answer to it: a Scythia within England, so men said, a heathen patch on Christian soil, near Brent-Tor on the skirts of Dartmoor.

Amyas Leigh, therefore, rode out of Plymouth as if he meant to tilt at an army. He sat in full armor on a good horse, with lance and sword, and, slung ready besides, two great dags. Behind him came Salvation Yeo and five or six North Devon men, old comrades from the Irish work, home on furlough—head-pieces over their brows, quilted jerkins on their backs, pikes and swords in hand; and Yeo with arquebuse and match as carefully tended as a cook’s slow fire. Two sumpter ponies, patient as any dairy-cow, carried their baggage.

They meant to sleep at Lydford; but horses had to be bought, straps mended, petty delays paid for in coin and temper, and they did not start till noon. Night slid down as they reached the frontier of that lawless country. Sunset threw a wild glare over the heath—a high tableland where the wind ran clean and hard, banked on the right by Dartmoor’s crags and swelling hills, and sloping away toward Brent-Tor, which reared up like a black cone of some dead volcano, topped with a tiny church. Far down the waste, thin blue threads of smoke rose from scattered dens: the Gubbings’ homes, if holes might be called so. More than once Amyas called a halt, staring across furze and pony-shapes in the distance, asking himself whether they were only moor-beasts or men posted like wolves to watch a fold.

Laughing at such fears is easy in a later, louder age that fancies itself “civil” because its noises are constant and its roads are mapped. But on that down, where the only steady sound was the wind’s low grind through heath and stone, every foreign clink or shout struck the mind like a thrown pebble into still water. Their ears were as wary as their eyes; and within two miles they learned that caution was not cowardice.

Midway on the down stood a wayside inn—more a lump of lichen-spotted granite than a house, with paper patched into broken panes, and a rotting thatch pinned down by stones and straw-banks as if the roof had to be bullied into staying. Behind it sprawled a rambling court of barns and walls. Pigs nosed among muck; barefoot children grunted and rolled in a brotherhood of dirt.

At the door, as if lost in admiration of the last orange light touching the moor, stood the innkeeper—brawny and sodden, blear-eyed, six feet of brutishness. He held up his hose with one hand for want of points, and clawed at elf-locks in which feathers stuck—witnesses enough, for any who cared to read them, that he had slept lately on straw and had geese enough to lend him their down in more ways than one.

He saw Amyas’s armored figure crest the rise, counted the party with a slow blink, and gave a grunt. Then, with the sleepy howl of a man who keeps his words for oaths, he called:

“Mirooi!—Fushing pooale!”

A strapping lass came out at once—Mary, they named her—her working dress as scant as the times allowed countrywomen, green bodice over red petticoat, both too little for modesty and too stained for pride. She brought him a fishing-rod and basket. He examined the footlink and snarled.

“Don vlies’ gone!”

“May be,” Mary answered, without much concern. “Shouldn’t hay’ left mun out to coort. May be old hen’s ate mun off. I see her chocking about a while agone.”

He took the news with an oath and a cuff at her head. She ducked, and, as quick as a cook’s spatula when grease flames, brought her own blow home upon his shock of hair. He shuffled away howling, accustomed to such domestic music.

“Tell Patrico!”

Mary ran in, combed her hair with fingers that knew more of dough and soot than silk, slipped stockings over her legs and her best gown over her dirt, and waited the guests with the practiced face of one who can smile while counting what may be stolen.

Amyas’s men drew long faces at the place—“mucksy,” one muttered—but chose its roof over a bivouac on the edge of the enemy’s smoke. So the cooking began; and in that kitchen, as in many a poor house, the day’s fear was managed the only way fear can be managed—by fire and food.

The old hen who had swallowed the dun fly was killed, plucked, and set to roast; her skin hissed and tightened above the coals, fat spitting like small shot on the hearthstones. Black Dartmoor mutton was slapped on the gridiron; it sizzled and smoked, and—forced by that fiery torment into confession—betrayed itself to every honest nose as stolen red-deer venison. Outside, the wind kept up its moaning sermon along the eaves; inside, the crackle and hiss of meat, the clink of knives, and the low talk of hungry men laid a domestic spell over the savage land.

Amyas had put his horse and ponies into a shed where he found neither lock nor key, and came back grumbling, uneasy for the steed’s safety. The baggage was heaped in a corner. He stretched his legs before a turf fire while Salvation Yeo, whose conscience had an iron edge, planted himself at the door and watched the yard as if watching for Amalekites. The men, seized with a sudden zeal for household management, pressed close to the cooking—perhaps because Mary held the knife, and the air around her was thick with smoke, salt, and warm fat.

Presently Yeo stepped in.

“There’s a gentleman just coming up, sir, all alone.”

“Ask him to make one of our party, then, with my compliments,” Amyas said.

Yeo went out and returned.

“Please, sir, he’s gone in back ways, by the court.”

“Well, he has an odd taste, if he makes himself at home here.”

Out went Yeo again, and back he came in high excitement, as if the very soundscape had shifted under his feet and warned him.

“Come out, sir; for goodness’ sake come out. I’ve got him. Safe as a rat in a trap, I have!”

“Who?”

“A Jesuit, sir.”

“Nonsense, man!”

“I tell you truth, sir. I went round the house, for I didn’t like the looks of him as he came up. I knew he was one of them villains the minute I saw him—by the way he turned in his toes, and put down his feet so still and careful, like as if he was afraid of offending God at every step. So I put my eye between the wall and the dern of the gate, and I saw him knock at the back door and call ‘Mary!’ quite still, like any Jesuit; and the wench flies out ready to eat him; and ‘Go away,’ I heard her say, ‘there’s a dear man;’ and then something about a ‘queer cuffin’’—that’s a justice in these canters’ thieves’ Latin; and with that he takes out a somewhat—I’ll swear it was one of those Popish Agnuses—and gives it her; and she kisses it and crosses herself and asks him if that’s the right way, and then puts it into her bosom, and he says, ‘Bless you, my daughter;’ and then I was sure of the dog. And he slips to the stable, peeps in, and when he sees no one there, in he goes; and out I go, and shut to the door, and back a cart up against it, and call one of the men to watch the stable, and the girl’s crying like mad.”

“What a fool’s trick, man!” Amyas burst out, hurrying after him. “How do you know he is not some honest gentleman, after all?”

“Fool or none, sir; honest gentlemen don’t give maidens Agnuses. I’ve put him in; and if you want him let out again, you must come and do it yourself. My conscience is against it, sir. If the Lord’s enemies are delivered into my hand, I’m answerable. ’Tis written, ‘If any let one of them go, his life shall be for the life of him.’”

Amyas dragged the cart back, opened the stable-door, and began a string of apologies—only to find his cousin Eustace Leigh, trapped indeed, sitting with a face half foolish, half venomous, like a fox uncovered at last by the terriers.

For a moment neither spoke. Then Amyas, trying to keep it light, said:

“Well, cousin hide-and-seek, how long have you added horse-stealing to your other trades?”

“My dear Amyas,” Eustace replied, very meek, “I may surely go into an inn stable without intending to steal what is in it.”

“Of course, old fellow,” Amyas answered, mollified. “I was only in jest. But what brings you here? Not prudence, certainly.”

“I am bound to know no prudence save for the Lord’s work.”

“That’s giving away Agnus Deis, and deceiving poor heathen wenches, I suppose,” growled Yeo.

Eustace turned on him roundly.

“Heathens? Yes, truly; you Protestants leave these poor wretches heathens, and then insult and persecute those who, with a devotion unknown to you, labor at the danger of their lives to make them Christians. Mr. Amyas Leigh, you can give me up to be hanged at Exeter, if it shall so please you to disgrace your own family; but from this spot neither you, no, nor all the myrmidons of your queen, shall drive me, while there is a soul here left unsaved.”

“Come out of the stable, at least,” Amyas said. “You don’t want to make the horses Papists as well as the asses, do you? Come out, man, and go to the devil your own way. I sha’n’t inform against you; and Yeo here will hold his tongue if I tell him, I know.”

“It goes sorely against my conscience, sir; but being that he is your cousin—of course—”

“Of course; and now come in and eat with me; supper’s just ready, and bygones shall be bygones, if you will have them so.”

Whatever forgiveness Eustace felt—or did not feel—he knew what he ought to enact, and he knew also that a meal shared might serve the Church as well as any sermon. So he went in. Yet he never forgot the scar upon his cheek; and Amyas could not meet his eye but Eustace fancied he saw Amyas’s gaze slide to it, and peeped up from under his lids to catch, if he could, some triumph on that honest face.

They ate. The venison—mutton turned deer by theft and fire—came off the gridiron in dark strips, seared without, red within, smelling of smoke and marrow. Cider, sour as the place, went round. And as mouths were filled and bellies warmed, their talk, guarded at first, loosened and drifted back to old lanes and old rooms, to boyhood haunts and kin: uncles, aunts, cousins spoken of like dishes remembered—some sweet, some bitter, all flavored with time.

Then Eustace, with a suddenness that showed his heart had been crouching like a hare in fern, asked Amyas why he rode to Bideford while Frank and his mother were in London.

“To tell you the truth,” Amyas said, “I cannot rest till I have heard the whole story about poor Rose Salterne.”

“What about her?” Eustace cried, and the words tore out of him.

“Do you not know?”

“How should I know anything here? For heaven’s sake, what has happened?”

Amyas told him; and as he spoke he wondered at the other’s eagerness, for he had never once suspected what lay beneath Eustace’s guarded piety.

Eustace shrieked aloud.

“Fool, fool that I have been! Caught in my own trap! Villain, villain that he is! After all he promised me at Lundy!”

He sprang up, stamped up and down, gnashing his teeth, tossing his head and clutching at the empty air with that dreadful blind gesture of despair still hunting for what it knows is lost. The room rang with it. Even the fire seemed to crackle louder, as if startled.

Amyas sat thunderstruck. His first impulse was to demand: Lundy? What knew you of him? What had he—or you—to do there? But pity, for once, beat curiosity.

“Oh, Eustace! And you then loved her too?”

“Don’t speak to me! Loved her? Yes, sir, and had as good a right to love her as any one of your precious Brotherhood of the Rose. Don’t speak to me, I say, or I shall do you a mischief!”

So Eustace knew of the brotherhood as well. Amyas felt the question rise like bile; he swallowed it down. If Eustace knew, he knew; and what harm in that compared with the harm already done?

“My good cousin,” Amyas said, forcing his voice steady, “why be wroth with me? If you really love her, now is the time to take counsel with me how best we shall—”

Eustace cut him off. Conscious he had betrayed more than one secret, he stopped, drew himself together by a great effort, and eyed Amyas from under his brows with his old down-look.

“How best we shall do what, my valiant cousin?” he asked, his voice meaning and half-scornful. “What does your most chivalrous Brotherhood of the Rose purpose in such a case?”

Amyas, nettled, stood on his guard.

“What the Brotherhood of the Rose will do, I can’t yet say. What it ought to do, I have a pretty sure guess.”

“So have I,” Eustace flung back. “To hunt her down as you would an outlaw, because forsooth she has dared to love a Catholic; to murder her lover in her arms, and drag her home again stained with his blood, to be forced by threats and persecution to renounce that Church into whose maternal bosom she has doubtless long since found rest and holiness!”

“If she has found holiness,” Amyas answered, “it matters little to me where she has found it; but that is the very point that I should be glad to know for certain.”

“And you will go and discover for yourself?”

“Have you no wish to discover it also?”

“And if I had, what would that be to you?”

“Only,” Amyas said, grinding down his temper, “that, if we had the same purpose, we might sail in the same ship.”

“You intend to sail, then?”

“I mean simply, that we might work together.”

“Our paths lie on very different roads, sir!”

“I am afraid you never spoke a truer word,” Amyas said. “In the meanwhile, ere we part, be so kind as to tell me what you meant by saying that you had met this Spaniard at Lundy?”

“I shall refuse to answer that.”

“You will please to recollect, Eustace, that however good friends we have been for the last half-hour, you are in my power. I have a right to know the bottom of this matter; and, by heaven, I will know it.”

“In your power? See that you are not in mine!” Eustace shot back. “Remember, sir, that you are within a—within a few miles, at least, of those who will obey me, their Catholic benefactor, but who owe no allegiance to those Protestant authorities who have left them to the lot of the beasts which perish.”

Amyas’s anger rose hot and quick. He wanted only a touch more to seize Eustace by the shoulders and shake him till the crookedness rang out. But to take him was to doom him; to doom him was to drag disgrace and death upon the family. Remembering others’ mercy shown once before, he held his hand.

“Take me,” Eustace said, with cold courage, “if you will, sir. You, who complain of us that we keep no faith with heretics, will perhaps recollect that you asked me into this room as your guest, and that in your good faith I trusted when I entered it.”

The plea was thin, but it bit because it sounded like honor. Amyas went to the door, flung it open, and bowed.

“Walk out,” he said, “and go to the devil, since you seem set on ending your days in that company.”

Eustace vanished into the night.

Amyas stood a moment listening. The moor-wind pressed its long breath against the house; somewhere a loose shutter tapped a dull beat. In such quiet, even a man’s thoughts sound loud, and his own words—devil, honor, duty—seemed to echo back altered, as if the very land translated them into a harsher dialect.

“Pooh!” he said to himself at last. “I can find out enough, and too much, I fear, without the help of such crooked vermin. I must see Cary; I must see Salterne; and I suppose, if I am ready to do my duty, I shall learn somehow what it is. Now to sleep; to-morrow up and away to what God sends.”

He called down the passage:

“Come in hither, men, and sleep here. Haven’t you had enough of this villainous sour cider?”

The men came yawning and flung themselves on the floor. Amyas laid himself on the settle, his saddle for a pillow.

“Where’s Yeo?”

No one knew. He had gone out to say his prayers and had not returned.

“Never mind,” Amyas muttered, suspecting some plot of vigilance in the old Puritan. “He’ll take care of himself, I’ll warrant him.”

“No fear of that, sir,” one of the tars answered; and soon four were snoring in concert round the fire while Amyas drifted into sleep, the turf-smoke in his nostrils and the taste of venison still on his tongue.

It was about midnight when he started up—no, rather fell backward, upsetting saddle, settle, and then table, under the belief that ten thousand flying dragons had burst at the window with howls. The “dragons,” rushing past in the dark, proved only terror-stricken geese flapping and screaming around the corner of the house. But the sound that had scared them did not cease. Another moment made it plain: a sharp fight in the courtyard, and Yeo hallooing for help.

Out tumbled the men, swords in hand. They burst the back door, stumbled over pails and pitchers, and rushed into the yard. There was Yeo, his back against the stable-door, sword and buckler up, holding his own against a dozen men.

The night became a kind of brutal orchestra: geese screaming, chickens screaming, pigs screaming, donkeys braying in outrage, Mary screaming from an upper window; and, as if drawn by the racket, plovers wheeled overhead, their wild cries sewing the noise to the dark. The court rang with it all—the acoustic misery of a place where even terror had a chorus.

But as Amyas charged in, the ruffians broke. They fled over a low hedge at the far end of the yard and vanished into the moor like smoke sucked into wind.

“Are you hurt, Yeo?”

“Not a scratch, thank Heaven! But I’ve got two of them, the ringleaders, I have. One of them’s against the wall. Your horse did for t’other.”

The wounded man was lifted—huge, nearly Amyas’s own size. Yeo’s sword had gone through him. He groaned and choked, fighting for air like a fish on stones.

“Carry him indoors. Where is the other?”

“Dead as a herring, in the straw. Have a care, men, have a care how you go in! the horses are near mad!”

After some struggling and soothing words that did little, they brought the dead man out. No pulse, no breath.

“Carry him in too, poor wretch,” Amyas said. “And now, Yeo, what is the meaning of all this?”

Yeo told it quickly. He could not get it out of his head that Eustace had come for the horses. He had seen the innkeeper sneak off when they arrived; expecting a night-attack, he had taken his lodging in the stable.

As he expected, the attempt came. The door was opened—how, he could not say, though he had fastened it inside—and two fellows slipped in and began to loose the beasts. Yeo seized the big one; the man drew a knife and broke away; the horses, terrified, kicked right and left. One man fell. The other ran out calling for help, with Yeo at his heels.

“Whereon,” said Yeo, “seeing a dozen more on me with clubs and bows, I thought best to shorten the number while I could, ran the rascal through, and stood on my ward; and only just in time I was, what’s more. There’s two arrows in the house wall, and two or three more in my buckler, which I caught up as I went out, for I had hung it close by the door, you see, sir, to be all ready in case.”

So the cunning old Philistine-slayer spoke as they went in after the wounded man, stepping from yard-noise into kitchen-stink—blood, sweat, and the cooling reek of roast.

They had scarcely ducked through the low doorway into the back-kitchen when fresh shouting rose inside. Amyas ran forward, struck his head on the lintel, and blinked through the sparks in his eyes to see an old acquaintance held fast, one sailor on each side.

With one arm thrust into his doublet sleeve, the other bare in a none-too-clean shirt; holding up his hose with one hand and a candle with the other—lighted, it seemed, to show his own confusion—stood Mr. Evan Morgans, alias Father Parsons. His face was fiery with rage; his half-dressed figure, between disordered cloth and swelling pride, looked (as Yeo told him bluntly) like “the very moral of a half-plucked turkey-cock.” Behind him, dressed and composed, stood Eustace Leigh.

“We found the maid letting these here two out by the front door,” said one of the sailors.

Amyas’s eyes narrowed.

“Well, Mr. Parsons,” he said, “and what are you about here? A pretty nest of thieves and Jesuits we seem to have routed out this evening.”

“About my calling, sir,” Parsons answered stoutly. “By your leave, I shall prepare this my wounded lamb for that account to which your man’s cruelty has untimely sent him.”

On the floor the wounded ruffian heard the priest’s voice and moaned thickly for the “Patrico.”

“You see, sir,” said Parsons, with pompous certainty, “the sheep know their shepherd’s voice.”

Six weeks, give or take, had hardly turned since the duel, when trouble came padding up the drive to the great house at Stow on a pair of frightened legs.

The miller from Stow, cap in his hands and his face as pale as flour, begged for the bloodhounds.

Rose Salterne had vanished in the night.

There are disappearances that leave the house tidy, as if a woman had merely stepped out to gather herbs and been delayed by gossip; and there are disappearances that leave a taste in the mouth like burnt fat, the sense that something has been spoiled past mending. This was the latter. Her little fineries—those bright trifles a girl will treasure even when her pockets are empty—lay where they always lay. Nothing seemed stolen. Only some linen was gone; and old Anthony the steward, who could smell a lie as readily as he could smell sour ale, hinted broadly that missing linen had a way of turning up in other people’s boxes.

They found, under her chamber window, a slight mark in the earth where a foot had pressed—light as a hare’s, and gone as quickly. That was enough. The keepers were fetched; the serving-men ran down, and behind them trailed all the idle lads of the parish, eager as boys at a fair—because a maiden-hunt was sport to such as had never been hunted.

They spoke, as men will, with their hearts already made up. Rose must be the wronged one; the miller and his wife the hard old heathens who had driven a tender creature to fling herself from cliff or drown in the sea. Meanwhile the women of Stow, with a sterner relish for scandal, agreed to a voice that the hussy had “gone off” with some bad fellow, and that pride had met its fall.

The bloodhound was brought—leashed, of course; for even the simplest countryman knows what a true hound will do if you loose him on a scent and let him reason it out with teeth. They laid him to the mark. He gave a little premonitory whimper, like a kettle beginning to sing before the boil; then lifted his great bell-mouth, and the sound rolled through garden and lane and down into the hollow places like a church note made flesh.

He started.

The keeper, towed and panting, stumbled after him; and the whole rabble streamed behind, boots scuffing, breath sawing, voices cracking jokes that sounded thin against the steady music of the hound. Past the garden gate, up the lane, over the rise and out upon the downs, where the wind carried every noise farther than it had any right to go—the baying, the men’s curses, the clatter of borrowed staves. In a countryside without factories and stamp-mills, a sound could rule a man’s mood: a calm stream could soothe; a hunting-cry could turn even decent fellows cruel with excitement. So it did now.

Straight away the hound drew them towards Marslandmouth. There, at the door of Lucy Passmore’s cottage, the whole posse comitatus pulled up, lungs aflame.

Lucy’s door was fast.

Lucy herself—where was she?

Round the threshold her goats stood crying to be milked, their thin bleats fretting the air with hunger and habit; and from the slope above came answer from her donkeys, those shaggy grey philosophers of the down, who had wandered to their own sweet will and now brayed back at the bloodhound in a burst of discordant harmony, as if making sport of human urgency.

“They’m laughing at us, keper,” one of the lads said, winded and red. “They neddies. Sure enough, we’m lost our labor here.”

But the bloodhound did not agree. He worked about the door awhile, nose low, then turned away down the glen with the certainty of one who reads what men cannot. He never checked till he reached the margin of the sea.

“They’m taken water,” a voice declared, with that grim satisfaction men feel when a mystery resolves into something they can blame. “Let’s go back, and rout out the old witch’s house.”

“’Tis just like that old Lucy,” another cried, “to lock a poor maid into shame.”

Indignation makes a fine cloak for greed. They went back and attacked the cottage—not with any warrant but the loud consent of the crowd—and ransacked it from hearth to rafters, half in wrath and half in the hope of plunder. Yet plunder there was none. Lucy had decamped with all her movable wealth; only the huge black cat remained, crouched among the embers. At the sight of the bloodhound it vanished up the chimney; some swore they smelt brimstone in its wake. Seen again outside, it was chased into the woods, and there, no doubt, lived long and happily as a scourge to the rabbits of the glen.

The goats and donkeys, bewildered and bawling, were driven up to Stow. The crowd straggled home, their brief fury cooling into a shamefaced silence—and with it a smaller fear of what Sir Richard Grenville might say.

Sir Richard, when he returned, sold the donkeys and goats and gave the money to the poor, promising to refund the same if Lucy came back and yielded herself to justice. Lucy did not return. Her cottage, which the neighbours thereafter passed wide as they would a plague-pit, stood empty and began to crumble. Fern and damp worked their slow cookery on the stones; the roof sank; the walls leaned. Only the little stream went on murmuring from pool to pool—the lone voice that broke the silence of that glen for many a year, steady as a lullaby, as if it remembered a language the people had forgotten.

A few days later Sir Richard, riding from Bideford to Stow, looked in at Clovelly Court and dropped, with a careless “by the by,” a piece of news that made Will Cary spring from his chair as if the floor had turned to flame.

“And there is no clue?” asked old Cary; for Will had no words.

“Only this,” said Sir Richard. “Some fellow prowling about the cliffs that night saw a pinnace running for Lundy.”

Will went out without excuse. Within half an hour he had three or four armed servants in a trawling-skiff and was away for Lundy, the oars thumping and the tide hissing past the bow like a whisper that would not be stilled. He did not return for three days. When he came back, the salt had dried white on his clothes and his eyes were gritty with sleeplessness; but he brought news.

An elderly man, seemingly a foreigner, had for months been lodging in a part of ruined Moresco Castle on Lundy, where one John Braund lived—farmer, fisherman, smuggler, and whatever else fed seven hungry children and paid arrears of rent. Some weeks back a younger man, a foreigner too, had joined him from a ship. The ship came and went—Flushinger, or Easterling, the fishermen thought—and the young man in her. Then, a few days ago, a lady and her maid, a stout woman, had come with him up to the castle and spoken long in secret with the elder man. They had slept there; and in the morning all three sailed away. The fishermen on the beach had heard the young man call the other “father.” The old one was still and priest-like; more they did not know, or did not choose to know.

Old Cary and Sir Richard sent Will again, this time with the parish constable of Hartland—within whose huge parish, for its sins, lay Lundy ten miles out at sea. They returned not with Rose, nor even with the foreign priests, but with the body of poor John Braund dragged into the coils of law. He was examined, and the examiners found him afflicted with extreme deafness and loss of memory; which was convenient, perhaps, for a man who had lived by keeping his ears open and his mouth shut. Fruitless as the questioning was, it ended as such things do: he was dispatched to Exeter gaol on a charge of “harboring priests, Jesuits, gipsies, and other suspect and traitorous persons.”
