There are prisons that punish by iron; Exeter punished by air. The place was a stew of human breath, damp straw, and sickness. In it, the soundscape itself was misery: coughing that never ended, moans that rose and fell like a foul tide, chains that clinked time into men’s bones. John Braund, taken from the clean wind of Lundy into that pestiferous den, lasted a week before the gaol-fever took him. He died raving. His secret—if he had one—perished with him; and nothing remained but suspicion, like a bad smell that lingers after the pot is scoured.

That Rose had gone off with the Spaniard few doubted. But whither, and in what character? On the last point, mercy was in short supply. Many a Bideford dame, who had hated Rose simply for being beautiful, found a full cupboard of wickedness to feed her tongue; and many a country lady, who had always declared that “the girl would be brought to ruin by the absurd notice, beyond what her station had a right to,” now felt herself proved prophetic. Every ambitious young maiden aspired to step into the place Rose had left.

So Bideford, busy as a kitchen at fair-time, went on as well without Rose as it had done with her—perhaps better, in its own opinion. A few still kept her in their minds as one keeps a scent of summer apples in winter; but the bustle of each day swept such tenderness away, and her place knew her no more.

Will Cary did not go on so easily. For a while he was like a man out of his wits. He loaded himself with superfluous reproaches—first, he said, he had brought Rose into disgrace; then he had driven her into the Spaniard’s arms. St. Leger, being sensible enough, tried in vain to comfort him: the attachment must have been long before the quarrel; it would have ended so sooner or later; old Salterne’s harshness had hastened the catastrophe more than Cary’s wrath; and, lastly, that a girl who had eloped with a Spaniard was not worth troubling their heads about.

Will would not be so comforted. He wrote to Frank at Whitehall, telling the whole truth, calling himself all fools and villains, and begging forgiveness. Frank replied with a letter that Will read as if it were scripture: Will had no reason to accuse himself; such strange attachments were due to a synastria, a sympathy of the stars ruling each person’s destiny, and to fight it was to fight the heavens. Frank, as Rose’s brother, was bound to believe—nay, to assert at sword’s point if need were—that the incomparable Rose of Torridge could make none but a worthy and virtuous choice; and to the man she had honoured by her affection was due, Spaniard and Papist though he might be, all friendship, worship, and loyal faith for evermore.

Honest Will took it for gospel, never guessing what it had cost Frank to write those sentences—what agony of despair, what fearful suspicions, what bitter prayers must have been kneaded into those smooth words, as a cook kneads salt into dough that will not rise.

Will showed the letter triumphantly to St. Leger. St. Leger, wise enough, gainsaid no word aloud; but he was wise enough too to believe, in secret, that Frank looked on the matter in quite another light. He only said, “The man is an angel as his mother is!” and for a few days the matter might have dropped—if one had not stepped forward with no mind to let it drop.

That was Jack Brimblecombe, now curate of Hartland town, passing rich on forty pounds a year.

He came in upon Will Cary with his small pig’s eyes earnest, his face set in that obstinate honesty which makes a fool of fear.

“I hope no offence, Mr. William,” said he; “but when are you and the rest going after—after her?”

The name stuck in his throat.

Cary stared. “What’s that to thee, Catiline the blood-drinker?” he asked, trying to laugh it away.

“What? Don’t laugh at me, sir. It’s no laughing matter. I drank that night naught worse, I expect, than red wine. Whatever ’twas, we swore our oaths, Mr. Cary; and oaths are oaths, say I.”

“Of course, Jack, of course. But to go to look for her—and when we’ve found her, cut her lover’s throat. Absurd, Jack. Even if she were worth looking for, or his throat worth cutting. Tut, tut—”

Jack did not blink. The room was quiet; Cary could hear, beyond the shutters, the soft domestic noises that make a house feel safe—some pan being set down, a distant murmur of servants, a crackle of wood. The ordinary world went on, indifferent to vows.

After a silence Jack asked, very simply, “How far is it to the Caracas, then, sir?”

“What is that to thee, man?”

“Why, he was made governor thereof, I hear; so that would be the place to find her?”

“You don’t mean to go thither to seek her?” Cary forced a laugh, as if laughter could bar a door.

“That depends on whether I can go, sir; but if I can scrape the money together, or get a berth aboard some ship—why, God’s will must be done.”

Cary looked hard at him, to see whether he had been drinking or had gone mad; but those little eyes were sane and sober. It is a terrible thing, sometimes, to find conscience in another man’s face; it makes one’s own prudence taste like stale bread.

“Well,” Jack went on, in his stupid steadfast way, “it’s a very bad look-out. But mother’s pretty well off, if father dies, and the maidens are stout wenches enough, and will make tidy servants, please the Lord. And you’ll see they come to no harm, Mr. William, for old acquaintance’ sake, if I never come back.”

Cary could find no answer. Heroism in a fat, vulgar curate shamed him more than any knight-errant could have done, because it offered no romance to hide behind.

“And, Mr. William,” Jack added, “you know me for an honest man, I hope. Will you lend me five pound, and take my books in pawn for them, just to help me out?”

“Are you mad, or in a dream? You will never find her.”

“That’s no reason why I shouldn’t do my duty in looking for her, Mr. William.”

“But, my good fellow, even if you get to the Indies, you will be clapped into the Inquisition and burnt alive, as sure as your name is Jack.”

“I know that,” said Jack, and his voice went dull with dread. “And a sore struggle of the flesh I have had about it; for I am a great coward, Mr. William—a dirty coward—and always was, as you know. But maybe the Lord will take care of me, as He does of little children and drunken men. And if not, Mr. Will—I'd sooner burn, and have it over, than go on this way any longer, I would!”

And with that the poor fellow burst out blubbering.

“What way, my dear old lad?” Cary asked, softened in spite of himself.

“Why—not—not to know whether—whether she’s married to him or not—her that I looked up to as an angel of God, as pure as the light of day; and knew she was too good for a poor pot-head like me; and prayed for her every night, God knows, that she might marry a king, if there was one fit for her—and I not to know whether she’s living in sin or not, Mr. William. It’s more than I can bear, and there’s an end of it. And if she is married to him—they keep no faith with heretics; they can dissolve the marriage, or make away with her into the Inquisition—burn her, Mr. Cary, as soon as burn me, the devils incarnate!”

A shudder ran through Cary. That plain, ugly possibility—Rose not merely lost, but trapped—had never struck him with such force. Jack’s fear had given it a voice, and once spoken aloud it seemed to fill the room.

“Yes,” Jack hurried on, “or make her deny her God by torments, if she hasn’t done it already for love to that— I know how love will make a body sell his soul, for I’ve been in love. Don’t you laugh at me, Mr. Will, or I shall go mad!”

“God knows,” said Cary, “I was never less inclined to laugh at you in my life, my brave old Jack.”

Jack’s face broke. “Is it so, then? Bless you for that word!” He held out his hand.

“But what will become of my soul, after my oath, if I don’t seek her out—just to speak to her, to warn her, for God’s sake, even if it did no good; just to set before her the Lord’s curse on idolatry and Antichrist, and those who deny Him for the sake of any creature—though I can’t think He would be hard on her—for who could? But I must speak all the same. The Lord has laid the burden on me, and done it must be. God help me!”

“Jack,” Cary said slowly, “if this is your duty, it is others’.”

“No, sir, I don’t say that. You’re a layman; but I am a deacon, and the chaplain of you all, and sworn to seek out Christ’s sheep scattered up and down this naughty world—and that innocent lamb first of all.”

“You have sheep at Hartland, Jack, already.”

“There’s plenty better than I will tend them, when I am gone; but none that will tend her, because none love her like me, and they won’t venture. Who will? It can’t be expected, and no shame to them.”

“I wonder what Amyas Leigh would say to all this, if he were at home?”

“Say? He’d do. He isn’t one for talking. He’d go through fire and water for her—you trust him, Will Cary—and call me an ass if he won’t.”

“Will you wait, then, till he comes back, and ask him?”

“He may not be back for a year and more.”

“Hear reason, Jack. If you will wait like a rational and patient man, instead of rushing blindfold on your ruin, something may be done.”

“You think so!”

“I cannot promise; but—”

“But promise me one thing. Do you tell Mr. Frank what I say—or rather, I’ll warrant, if I knew the truth, he has said the very same thing himself already.”

“You are out there, old man; for here is his own handwriting.”

Jack read Frank’s letter and sighed bitterly. “Well, I did take him for another guess sort of fine gentleman. Still, if my duty isn’t his, it’s mine all the same. I judge no man; but I go, Mr. Cary.”

“But go you shall not till Amyas returns. As I live, I will tell your father, Jack, unless you promise; and you dare not disobey him.”

“I don’t know even that, for conscience’ sake,” Jack muttered, uncertain.

“At least you stay and dine here, old fellow,” said Cary, seizing the only leash he could find, “and we will settle whether you are to break the fifth commandment or not, over good brewed sack.”

Now a good dinner was what Jack loved, and loved too often in vain. He submitted for the nonce. Cary thought, when Jack went, that he had talked him round. Jack kept away for a week.

Then he returned, brisk and bright as a man with a plan. “I have settled all, Mr. Will. The parson of Welcombe will serve my church for two Sundays, and I am away for London town, to speak to Mr. Frank.”

“To London? How wilt get there?”

“On Shanks his mare,” said Jack, pointing to his bandy legs. “But I expect I can get a lift aboard a coaster so far as Bristol, and it’s no way on to signify, I hear.”

Cary tried in vain to dissuade him, and then, half in pity and half in shame, forced on him a small loan. Away went Jack, with his poor bundle and his great vow; and Cary heard nothing for three weeks.

At last Jack walked into Clovelly Court again just before supper-time, thin and leg-weary. He sat down among the serving-men till Will appeared. Will brought him up above the salt, made much of him—as a man will who sees another wasted by roads and purpose—and after supper asked in private how he had sped.

“I have learnt a lesson, Mr. William,” Jack said. “I’ve learnt there is one on earth loves her better than I—if she had but had the wit to have taken him.”

“But what says he of going to seek her?”

“He says what I say—Go! and he says what you say—Wait.”

“Go? Impossible! How can that agree with his letter?”

“That’s no concern of mine. Of course, being nearer heaven than I am, he sees clearer what he should say and do than I can see for him. Oh, Mr. Will, that’s not a man—he’s an angel of God; but he’s dying, Mr. Will.”

“Dying?”

“Yes, faith, of love for her. I can see it in his eyes, and hear it in his voice. But I am of tougher hide and stiffer clay, and so you see I can’t die even if I tried. But I’ll obey my betters, and wait.”

And Jack went home that very evening, weary as he was, despite all entreaties to spend the night. Yet he left behind him in Cary’s mind a restlessness that would not be quieted. It lay there like yeast, working in the dark, swelling thought into thought until a small touch would make the whole rise sudden and irresistible.

That touch came, as such touches often do, by what men call chance.

One murky day Cary was lounging on Bideford quay, listening to the harbour noises—the slap of water against piled timber, the creak of rope, the cry of gulls that cut the air clean as a knife—when up came Mr. Salterne.

Cary had shunned him of late, partly from delicacy, partly from dislike of his supposed hard-heartedness; but now they met full, and Cary could not pass without speaking.

“Well, Mr. Salterne, and how goes on the shipping trade?”

“Well enough, sir,” Salterne answered, “if some of you young gentlemen would but follow Mr. Leigh’s example, and go forth to find us stay-at-homes new markets for our ware.”

“What? You want to be rid of us, eh?”

“I don’t know why I should, sir. We sha’n’t cross each other now, sir, whatever might have been once. But if I were you, I should be in the Indies about now, if I were not fighting the queen’s battles nearer home.”

“In the Indies? I should make but a poor hand of Drake’s trade.”

The talk dropped there, as talk does when two men skirt the edge of what matters. But Cary did not forget the hint; it rang in him like a remembered refrain.

So, later, when he told Amyas Leigh—making an end, as he said, of a long story—his decision had already been simmering to a boil.

“If you are minded to take the old man’s offer, so am I,” said Cary. “Westward-ho with you, come foul come fair.”

“It will be but a wild-goose chase, Will.”

“If she is with him, we shall find her at La Guayra. If she is not, and the villain has cast her off down the wind, that will be only an additional reason for making an example of him.”

“And if neither of them are there, Will, the Plate-fleets will be; so it will be our own shame if we come home empty-handed. But will your father let you run such a risk?”

“My father?” Cary laughed, and the laugh had more bitterness than mirth. “He has just now so good hope of a long string of little Carys to fill my place, that he will be in no lack of an heir, come what will.”

“Little Carys?”

“I tell you truth. I think he must have had a sly sup of that fountain of perpetual youth which our friend Don Guzman’s grandfather went to seek in Florida; for some twelvemonth since he must needs marry a tenant’s buxom daughter. Mistress Abishag Jewell has brought him one fat baby already. So I shall go—back to Ireland, or with you—but somewhere. I can’t abide the thing’s squalling, any more than I can abide seeing Mistress Abishag sitting in my poor dear mother’s place and informing me every other day that she is come of an illustrious house, because she is—or is not—third cousin seven times removed to my father’s old friend, Bishop Jewell of glorious memory. I had three-parts of a quarrel with the dear old man t’other day; for after one of her peacock-bouts, I couldn’t for the life of me help saying that as the Bishop had written an Apology for the people of England, my father had better conjure up his ghost to write an apology for him, and head it, ‘Why green heads should grow on gray shoulders.’”

“You impudent villain! And what did he say?”

“Laughed till he cried again—and told me if I did not like it I might leave it; which is just what I intend to do. Only mind—if we go, we must needs take Jack Brimblecombe with us, or he will surely heave himself over Harty Point, and his ghost will haunt us to our dying day.”

“Jack shall go. None deserves it better.”

There followed a long consultation on practical matters—ships, money, men, and the slow patience of waiting for the right wind. They concluded that Amyas should go up to London and sound Frank and his mother before any further steps were taken. The other brethren of the Rose were scattered far and wide, each at his post; St. Leger had returned to his uncle; and it would be unfair, as well as a delay, to demand from them all the fulfilment of the vow.

“And besides,” Amyas said, with the homely wisdom of a man who had seen too many gentlemen spoil good work, “too many cooks spoil the broth, and half-a-dozen gentlemen aboard one ship are as bad as two kings of Brentford.”

With which maxim he departed next morning for London, leaving Yeo with Cary, and leaving behind him, in the quiet house by the sea, a sense of things set moving—like a pot newly put upon the fire, whose first small whisper foretells the boil.

“He is brass within, and steel without,
With beams on his topcastle strong;
And eighteen pieces of ordinance
He carries on either side along.”

Take boat at Whitehall-stairs, as Amyas did, and let the Thames draw you down with its brown, tide-smelling breath—tar, bilge-water, and the faint sweetness of malt from riverside brewhouses mingling under the heavier reek of London smoke. The river has its own music: the slap of oars, the creak of thole-pins, chains clinking, hawkers calling from the stairs, and, over all, that unending undernote of water worrying the stones. Even now, when men talk of new worlds, the old world’s soundscape is what steadies the heart. You pass under the shadow of London Bridge, where the current frets and mutters through the starlings like an animal held too long in a pen; and so, with the tide, to Deptford Creek.

There, drawn up high and dry on the sedgy bank, lies the famous ship—Drake’s Pelican—resting like an old fighting-man with his scars shown openly, unashamed. She stands embalmed in river-air and memory; and nailed upon her mainmast are verses and epigrams in her praise, as though words might serve for caulking against time. Here Elizabeth herself once came, banqueted aboard, and made Drake knight with her own hand. Men still tell, with a kind of awe that half believes in lucky planets, how the plank-bridge broke under the press and a hundred tumbled down together, yet none were harmed—as though Fortune had held out her apron to catch them.

And Fortune, this afternoon, seems again at work; for the old ship is dressed for revel. Flags busy the masts; laughter, the scrape of benches, and the bright clatter of pewter come through windows that were once gun-ports; and, more persuasive than any music, savory steams drift out into the river air. The Pelican has long served as a show and, better yet, as a dining-hall for the city’s jovial folk; and to-day a great feast is toward. White-aproned waiters hurry to and fro—quick-footed as gulls—across the lucky bridge from the Pelican Inn, bearing trenchers, pots, and covered dishes; and with them go gay serving-men with the city badge at shoulder, bright as a cockade. The lord mayor gives a dinner to certain gentlemen of Leicester’s party, men who have set their minds on foreign discoveries; and where should such talk be fed more fitly than on the very deck that once carried it round the world?

Look at the company. It is a table worth a chronicler’s ink. If you be an American, look—look well—and reverence these faces; for out of such brows and hands, such stubborn appetites for risk and such patient accounts of victual and venture, your fatherland will one day be made.

At the head sits the lord mayor—Sir Edward Osborne, clothworker, old now, but with the same prompt chivalry that leapt from a window on London Bridge into roaring tide to save the infant who became his wife. Age has not softened him; it has only set the temper. He has the look of a man who has handled both bales and bills, who can taste the honesty of a measure as surely as he can taste a sauce. On his right is my Lord Earl of Cumberland; on his left sits Walter Raleigh; and the three speak low together of vast rich countries still undiscovered between Florida and the River of Canada. Raleigh’s speech runs half on science, half on enchantment, spiced with quotations from Doctor Dee the conjuror; and though such seasoning pleases Cumberland (who has tried many adventures by proxy, and failed in all—because he would send other men to do what he praised), it has less savor to Osborne, who has eaten too much of the world to be fed on words alone. So Raleigh draws in, for witness and weight, the quiet student at his side, Richard Hakluyt of Oxford.

But Hakluyt is deep in talk with a reverend elder, long white beard flowing near to his waist, his face furrowed as if every storm he ever sailed through had taken a turn at carving him. It is Anthony Jenkinson, the great Asiatic traveller, discoursing of reindeer-sledges and Siberian steppes, and of fossil ivory dug by the Tungoos from the ice-cliffs of the Arctic sea—plain proof, he maintains, of Noah’s flood. He speaks, and one hears behind his words the wind’s thin whistle over snow, the crunch of runners, the hush of a world where sound itself is precious. Next to him sits Christopher Carlile, Walsingham’s son-in-law—a valiant captain, a man who will one day drink good wine with a certain Bishop of Carthagena. For now he is busy with Alderman Hart the grocer, Sheriff Spencer the clothworker, Charles Leigh—Amyas’s merchant-cousin—Aldworth the mayor of Bristol, and William Salterne, alderman thereof and cousin to our friend at Bideford. For Carlile, and Secretary Walsingham too, have helped them heart and soul these last two years to gather money for Humphrey and Adrian Gilbert’s great adventures to the North-West; one of which Carlile himself was to have sailed, though for some reason he did not.

Opposite is a knot scarcely less notable: Martin Frobisher and John Davis, the pioneers of the North-West passage, in talk with Alderman Sanderson the geographer and setter forth of globes; with Mr. Towerson, Sir Gilbert Peckham, Captain John Winter (old acquaintance), and, last, Philip Sidney himself. Sidney, with his accustomed courtesy, has given up his rightful place toward the head, that he may have the virtuosi about him like a little court of his own; and with him are Mr. Edward Dyer and Mr. Francis Leigh. They talk of the North-West; and Sidney laments that he is tied to diplomacy and courts, and envies old Frobisher in pretty compliments.

“It’s all very fine,” says Frobisher, “to talk of here—sailing on dry land with a good glass of wine afore you; but you’d find it another guess business, knocking about among icebergs with your beard frozen fast to your ruff, Sir Philip—specially if you were a bit squeamish about the stomach.”

“That were a slight matter,” Sidney answers, “if by it I could win the honor her majesty bestowed on you, when her own ivory hand waved farewell from Greenwich windows.”

“Well, sir,” Frobisher returns, “folks say you’ve no reason to complain of lack of favors, seeing you’ve no reason to deserve lack; and if you can get ’em by staying ashore, don’t you go to sea to look for more, say I. Eh, Master Towerson?”

Towerson’s gray beard wags grim assent; but at that moment a waiter enters, breathless with haste and importance, smelling of hot bread and ale-foam.

“Please my lord mayor’s worship, there is a tall gentleman without would speak with the Right Honorable Sir Walter Raleigh.”

“Show him in, man,” says Osborne. “Sir Walter’s friends are ours.”

Amyas enters and stands for a moment in the doorway, as if the warmth and the smell of the feast—roasted meats, pepper, wine, the sharpness of vinegar on greens—have struck him like a memory rather than a welcome.

“Captain Leigh!” cry half a dozen voices.

“Why did you not walk in, sir?” says Osborne. “You should know your way well enough between these decks.”

“Well enough, my lords and gentlemen. But, Sir Walter—you will excuse me—” And he gives Raleigh a look that needs no tongue.

Raleigh turns pale as death. He rises at once and follows Amyas into an adjoining cabin. They are together scarce five minutes. Then Amyas comes out alone.

In few words he tells them the sad tale we already know. While he speaks, the sounds of the feast—knife on trencher, laughter, the jangle of cups—seem to falter, as if even oak and iron must listen. Ere the story ends, noble tears glisten on some of those stern faces; and the savory steam that had promised comfort now hangs in the air like a reproach, as though food itself had grown ashamed to ask a man to swallow.

“The old Egyptians,” says Sir Edward Osborne, “when they banqueted, set a corpse among their guests, for a memorial of human vanity. Have we forgotten God and our own weakness in this our feast, that He Himself has sent us thus a message from the dead?”

“Nay, my lord mayor,” says Sidney softly, “not from the dead, but from the realm of everlasting life.”

“Amen!” answers Osborne. “But, gentlemen, our feast is at an end. There are those here who would drink on merrily, as brave men should, in spite of private losses; but none here can drink with the loss of so great a man still ringing in his ears.”

So it is. Many at that table have suffered by the failure of the expedition; yet they forget their own hurt under the weight of Sir Humphrey’s death. The feast breaks up sadly and in haste; men rise with chairs scraping like a sudden squall; and each asks his neighbor, “What will the queen say?” Even the river seems louder as they spill out upon the bank—the splash of wherries, the crying of watermen, the city’s ceaseless grind; how thin and brazen it sounds beside a good man gone.

Raleigh returns after a few minutes, silent, pressing honest hands as he passes, and goes out to call a wherry, beckoning Amyas to follow. Sidney, Cumberland, and Frank go after in another boat, leaving the two to talk apart of the sad details.

They land at Whitehall-stairs. Raleigh, Sidney, and Cumberland go toward the palace; and the two brothers turn to their mother’s lodgings.

All the way, Amyas has been shaping words in his mind for Frank about Rose Salterne; but now that the moment comes, courage fails him. He longs that Frank will open the matter first. Frank too shrinks from it—more because he does not know that Amyas has passed through Bideford, nor that he knows aught of Rose’s disappearance. So each is trapped by tenderness, and by fear of what pain the telling will bring their mother.

They go upstairs. It is a relief to both to learn she is at the Abbey; for it is for her sake they dread what must be said. So they stand in the bay-window looking on the river. Light is fading. They speak of indifferent things, and yet look earnestly at each other’s faces, for it is now three years since they met.

Years and events have widened the contrast between them. Frank smiles with affectionate pride at Amyas: no longer the rollicking handy sailor-lad, but a self-confident, stately warrior, reason firm, temperate will, endurance and foresight set in every movement—such a man as Drake and Grenville might begin to fashion, and Raleigh and Gilbert finish. His long locks are cropped close now, but lips and chin are covered with a rich golden beard. His face is browned by a thousand suns and storms; a long scar—trophy of some Irish fight—crosses his right temple. His huge figure has gained breadth with its height; and the hand he lays on the window-sill is hard and massive as a smith’s, as if it had kneaded rope and hammered iron into obedience.

Frank lays his own hand upon it and sighs; and Amyas starts at the contrast. Frank’s fingers are slender, bloodless, all but transparent, like a scholar’s rather than a man’s who should ride and fence at court. Amyas looks anxiously into his brother’s face. It has changed. The brilliant red is still on either cheek, but the white is dull and opaque; lips pale, features sharpened; eyes glittering with an unnatural fire. When Frank tells Amyas he looks aged, Amyas cannot help thinking the words fit Frank more truly.

To keep from seeing what is plain, Amyas talks on of buildings, asking name after name as though stone and timber could distract the heart.

“And so this is old Father Thames, with his bank of palaces?”

“Yes,” says Frank. “His banks are stately enough; yet, you see, he cannot stay to look at them. He hurries down to the sea; and the sea into the ocean; and the ocean Westward-ho, forever. All things move Westward-ho. Perhaps we may move that way ourselves some day, Amyas.”

“What do you mean by that strange talk?”

“Only that the ocean follows the primum mobile of the heavens, and flows forever from east to west. Is it strange to think of that, when I am but now come from a party where we drank success to Westward-ho?”

“And much good has come of it!” Amyas breaks out. “I have lost the best friend and noblest captain upon earth, not to mention all my little earnings, in that same confounded gulf of Westward-ho.”

“Yes,” Frank answers quietly. “Sir Humphrey Gilbert’s star has set in the West—why not? Sun, moon, and planets sink into the West: why not meteors of this lower world? why not a will-o’-the-wisp like me, Amyas?”

“God forbid, Frank!”

“Why then? Is not the West the land of peace and dreams? Do not our hearts tell us so when we look on the setting sun, and long to float away with him upon golden-cushioned clouds? They bury men with their faces to the East. I should rather have mine turned to the West when I die; for I cannot but think some divine instinct made the ancient poets guess Elysium lay beneath the setting sun. It is bound up in man’s heart, that longing for the West. I complain of no one for fleeing away thither beyond the utmost sea, as David wished to flee and be at peace.”

“Complain of no one for fleeing thither?” asks Amyas. “That is more than I do.”

Frank looks at him, questioning; and then—

“No. If I had complained of any one, it would have been of you just now, for seeming tired of going Westward-ho.”

“Do you wish me to go, then?”

“God knows,” says Frank, after a pause that is almost a struggle for breath. “But I must tell you now, I suppose, once and for all. That has happened at Bideford which—”

“Spare us both, Frank; I know all. I came through Bideford on my way hither; and came hither not merely to see you and my mother, but to ask your advice and her permission.”

“True heart! noble heart!” Frank cries. “I knew you would be stanch!”

“Westward-ho it is, then?”

“Can we escape?”

“We?”

“Amyas, does not that which binds you bind me?”

Amyas starts, and holds Frank by the shoulders at arm’s length; and as he does, he feels through the fine cloth how little there is beneath—arms but skin and bone.

“You? Dearest man, a month of it would kill you!”

Frank smiles, and tosses his head in his pretty way, as though he could shake off sickness like a troublesome curl.

“I belong to the school of Thales, who held the ocean mother of all life; and I feel no more repugnance at returning to her bosom than Humphrey Gilbert did.”

“But, Frank—my mother?”

“My mother knows all; and would not have us unworthy of her.”

“Impossible! She will never give you up!”

“All things are possible to them that believe in God, my brother; and she believes. Besides, Doctor Dee—the wise man—gave her this summer I know not what prognostics and diagnostics concerning me. I am born, it seems, under a cold and watery planet, and need, if I am to be long-lived, to go nearer to the vivifying heat of the sun, and there bask out my little life, like a fly on a wall. He has bidden me spend no more winters here in the East; but return to our native sea-breezes, there to warm my frozen lungs; and he has filled my mother’s fancy with stories of sick men given up for lost in Germany and France, yet renewed—like serpent or eagle—by going to Italy, Spain, and the Canaries, that she will be readier to let me go than I to leave her alone. And yet I must go, Amyas. Not merely that my heart pants, as Sidney’s does, as every gallant’s ought, to make one of your noble choir of Argonauts—replenishing the earth and subduing it for God and the queen; not merely that love calls me—love tyrannous, strengthened by absence, deepened by despair; but honor, Amyas—my oath—”

He cannot finish. A violent fit of coughing seizes him; he leans on Amyas’s shoulder while Amyas cries out—

“Fools, fools that we were—that I was, I mean—to take that fantastical vow!”

“Not so,” says a gentle voice behind them. “You vowed for the sake of peace on earth, and good-will toward men, and ‘Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called the children of God.’ No, my sons: be sure such self-sacrifice as you have shown will meet its full reward at the hand of Him who sacrificed Himself for you.”

They turn. Their mother stands there, just come in—quiet as a chapel after the street—her very presence changing the room’s sound, as if harshness dared not speak above a whisper.

“Oh, mother! mother!” says Amyas. “And do you not hate the very sight of me—come here to take away your first-born?”

“My boy, God takes him, and not you. And if I dare believe in such predictions, Doctor Dee assured me that some exceeding honor awaited you both in the West—to each according to his deserts.”

“Ah!” says Amyas bitterly. “My blessing, I suppose, will be like Esau’s—to live by my sword; while Jacob here, the spiritual man, inherits the kingdom of heaven and an angel’s crown.”

“Be it what it may, it will surely be a blessing, as long as you are such, my children, as you have been. At least my Frank will be safe from the intrigues of court and the temptations of the world. Would that I too could go with you, and share in your glory!”

And then, with one of her most winning smiles, she lays her head upon Amyas’s breast and looks up.

“Come now. I have heard of heroic mothers who went forth with their sons to battle, and cheered them on to victory. Why should I not go with you on a more peaceful errand? I could nurse the sick, if there were any. I could perhaps have speech with that poor girl, and win her back more easily than you. She might listen to words from a woman—a woman, too, who has loved—which she could not hear from men. At least I could mend and wash for you. I suppose it is as easy to play the good housewife afloat as on shore? Come, now!”

Amyas looks from one to the other, and his heart sinks; for their courage is all love, and love does not measure cannon.

“God only knows which of the two is less fit to go. Mother! mother! you know not what you ask. Frank! Frank! I do not want you with me. This is a sterner matter than either of you fancies; one that must be worked out, not with kind words, but with sharp shot and cold steel.”

“How?” cry both together, aghast.

“I must pay my men, and pay my fellow-adventurers; and I must pay them with Spanish gold. And what is more, I cannot, as a loyal subject of the queen’s, go to the Spanish Main with a clear conscience on my own private quarrel, unless I do all the harm my hand finds to do, by day and night, to her enemies, and the enemies of God.”

“What nobler knight-errantry?” says Frank, forcing cheer; but Mrs. Leigh shudders.

“What! Frank too?” she says, half to herself; and her sons know what she means. Amyas’s warlike life—honorable and righteous as she knows it—she has borne as a sad necessity; but that Frank, gentle Frank, should become a man of blood, is more than her heart can face at first. That youthful duel of his he has concealed from her, knowing her feeling. She cannot bear to yoke that spirit to battlefield ferocity and carnage. And yet, she thinks, is this but another self-willed idol she must renounce, one by one? Catching at her last hope, she says—

“Frank must at least ask the queen’s leave to go; and if she permits, how can I gainsay her wisdom?”

So the talk drops, sadly enough—like a song cut short. The river outside goes on, indifferent; its noises—oar-splash, call, bell—threading in through the casement, as if the world will not hush even for grief.

But now a fresh perplexity begins in Frank’s soul. At first Amyas takes it for jest; then it nettles him when he finds it earnest. Frank looks forward to asking the queen’s permission with abject despondency and terror. Two or three days pass before he can make up his mind even to seek an interview; and he spends the time making interest with Leicester, Hatton, and Sidney, as if he sued for reprieve from the scaffold.

So says Amyas, adding that the queen cannot cut his head off for wanting to go to sea.

“But what axe so sharp as her frown?” says Frank, lugubrious as a man condemned.

Amyas begins to whistle, rudely and provokingly.

“Ah, my brother, you cannot comprehend the pain of parting from her.”

“No, I can’t. I would die for the least hair of her royal head—God bless it!—but I could live very well from now till Doomsday without ever setting eyes on the said head.”

“Plato’s Troglodytes regretted not that sunlight which they had never beheld.”

Amyas, not understanding the conceit, makes no answer; and there the matter rests for the time.

At last Frank gains his audience. When he returns, two hours later, he is pale and exhausted, as if he has walked against a strong wind.

“Thank Heaven, it is over! She was very angry at first—what else could she be?—and upbraided me with having set my love so low. I could only answer that my fatal fault was committed before the sight of her had taught me what was supremely lovely and only worthy of admiration. Then she accused me of disloyalty in having taken an oath which bound me to service of another than her. I confessed my sin with tears; and when she threatened punishment, pleaded that the offence had avenged itself heavily already—for what worse punishment than exile from the sunlight of her presence, into the outer darkness which reigns where she is not? Then she was pleased to ask me how I could dare, as her sworn servant, to desert her side in such dangerous times as these; and asked how I should reconcile it to my conscience, if on my return I found her dead by the assassin’s knife. At which most pathetic demand I could only throw myself at once on my knees and her mercy, and so awaited my sentence. Whereon, with that angelic pity which alone makes her awfulness endurable, she turned to Hatton and asked, ‘What say you, Mouton? Is he humbled sufficiently?’ and so dismissed me.”

“Heigh-ho!” yawns Amyas.

“If the bridge had been stronger,
My tale had been longer.”

“Amyas! Amyas!” cries Frank, solemn as a preacher. “You know not what power over the soul has the native and God-given majesty of royalty—awful enough in itself—when to it is added the wisdom of the sage and the tenderness of the woman. Had I my will, there should be in every realm not a salique, but an anti-salique law—whereby no kings, but only queens should rule mankind. Then would weakness and not power be to man the symbol of divinity; love, and not cunning, the arbiter of every cause; and chivalry, not fear, the spring of all obedience.”

“Humph! There’s some sense in that,” says Amyas. “I’d run a mile for a woman when I would not walk a yard for a man; and—Who is this our mother is bringing in? The handsomest fellow I ever saw in my life!”

He is not far wrong. Mrs. Leigh enters with a companion—none other than Mr. Secretary, Amyas’s acquaintance at Smerwick Fort; alias Colin Clout, alias Immerito, alias Edmund Spenser. Some half-jesting talk has been passing between poet and saint; for as they come in she says, with a smile that is a little forced—

“Well, my dear sons, you are sure of immortality, at least on earth; for Mr. Spenser has been vowing to me to give your adventure a whole canto to itself in his ‘Faerie Queene’.”

“And you no less, madam,” says Spenser. “What were the story of the Gracchi worth without Cornelia? If I honor the fruit, I must not forget the stem which bears it. Frank, I congratulate you.”

“Then you know the result of my interview, mother?”

“I know everything, and am content,” says Mrs. Leigh.

“Mrs. Leigh has reason to be content,” says Spenser, “with that which is but her own likeness.”

“Spare your flattery to an old woman, Mr. Spenser. When, pray, did I”—and she casts a most loving look at Frank—“refuse knighthood for duty’s sake?”

“Knighthood?” cries Amyas. “You never told me that, Frank!”

“That may well be, Captain Leigh,” says Spenser; “but believe me, her majesty—so Hatton assures me—told him this day no less than that by going on this quest he deprives himself of that highest earthly honor, which crowned heads are fain to seek from their own subjects.”

Spenser does not exaggerate. Knighthood is yet the prize of merit only; so valuable that Elizabeth herself has said, when asked why she did not bestow a peerage upon some favorite, that having already knighted him she had nothing better to bestow. It will remain for young Essex to begin the order’s degradation in his hapless Irish campaign, and for James to complete it by raising money through the sale of baronetcies—an hereditary knighthood, laughing-stock of the day, and, however venerable it may since have grown, reflecting little honor on its first possessors.

Land—land—land.

It lay far off to the south-west, a long indigo stroke between a sea turned wine-red by the sinking sun and a sky burnished like heated brass. After weeks of bilge-stink, sweat-sour hammocks, and the medicinal reek of tar and salt pork, the very thought of earth had a taste: cool water that did not come from a cask, fruit that bled sweetness instead of brine, shade that did not smell of damp canvas. Men who had been counting their ribs in the dark crawled up as if dragged by a rope of hope; men still strong enough to stand dropped to their knees and gave thanks, their voices breaking in the wind.

That wind—steady, warm, faithful—came stroking along the sails and whispering through rigging and block like a hand over strings. It was not silence they had lived with at sea, but one long monotone: the hush and slap of water, the creak of timbers, the constant grind of rope. Now the trade-wind had a different note, a promise in it, as if it had learned the human word for mercy.

Frank, leaning forward where the bowsprit thrust toward that blue bar, mouthed his own prayer into the salt air.

“Blow freshly… freshlier yet… Save her. Land me at her feet—if only to call the lost lamb home, and die.”

The sunset set fire to his thin cheeks; the bright fever-spot on either side looked like a cruel mockery of health. His eyes were too large in his wasted face, and they clung to the land as if it were not an island at all, but the one fixed point in a world that had become all water.

A few minutes, and the west’s jewel-box snapped shut. Emerald and ruby and amethyst melted into one pearl-gray band; overhead, the deepened sky took on that hard, clean blue of polished stone, and the Moon and Venus hung above the sea like two lamps in a chapel.

“That should be Barbados, your worship,” said Drew the master, with the quiet pride of a man whose figures have not betrayed him. “Unless my reckoning’s far out—which, Heaven knows, it has no right to be after such a passage. God be praised.”

“Barbados?” Amyas turned, shading his eyes. “I never heard of it.”

“Very like, sir. But Yeo and I were here with Captain Drake—and I was here after, too, with poor Captain Barlow. There’s good harborage on the south and west, as I mind.”

“And neither Spaniard, cannibal, nor other evil beast,” said Yeo, dry as ship’s biscuit. “A very garden of the Lord, hid away in the seas for an inheritance to those who love Him. I heard Captain Drake talk once of planting it, if ever he had a chance.”

Amyas’s face softened. “I mind now Sir Humphrey speaking of an island here. Would God he had gone thither instead of Newfoundland.”

“Nay then,” said Yeo, not unkindly. “He is in bliss now with the Lord; and you would not have kept him from that, sir?”

Amyas sighed and shook his head, as if the wind could blow a dead man back. Then he looked over his company—scurvy-swollen gums, hollow eyes, men shuffling like old men though beardless—and the captain’s mind did its plain arithmetic.

“What say you, my masters? How can we do better than spend a few days here, to set our sick on their legs, before we make the Main and set to our work?”

All approved at once, a murmur that grew toward cheer; only Frank said nothing, as if any delay were a theft.

“Come, fellow-adventurer,” Cary said, clapping him lightly. “We must have your voice too.”

“To my impatience,” Frank answered low, so that only Cary heard, “there is but one place on earth. I am all day longing for wings to fly thither. But the counsel is right. I approve it.”

So it was settled. The decision ran through the ship like a draught of fresh water. Men who had been too weak to swear found strength enough to shout; and long before morning they had slid along the island’s southern shoulder and were feeling their way into the bay where Bridgetown would one day stand, though there was not yet so much as a cut wharf or a torch to tell of human hands.

The land slept in moonlight—low wooded hills, the palms standing like dark plumes against the pale sky. Fireflies stitched the shadows with dancing sparks, as if the ground itself were spangled with stars fallen and still restless. The air came off the shore thick with scent: crushed leaf, warm resin, something like honey bruised with green, and beneath it all the clean wet smell of soil. Nostrils that had lived on salted wind drank greedily.

And the sound—oh, the sound. The sea’s eternal whisper was not gone, but it was no longer alone. From the land came a swelling chorus: insects clicking and whirring, tree-toads snoring in comic solemnity, shore-fowl crying their plaintive notes across the shallows. The night had a body now, made of voices. Every man on deck listened as if his own mind were being tuned again after weeks of one harsh note.

At last the ship stopped. The cable rattled through the hawsehole with a music of iron; the anchor took hold; and, careless for the moment of Spaniard or Carib, a cheer burst from every throat.

Amyas had much to do to keep them aboard. They were like starving men sniffing bread through a door.

“Two hours of day, lads,” he cried. “Two hours, and then ashore. Will you go breaking your legs on coral in the dark?”

“Never were two such long hours,” complained a young lad, pacing like a caged thing.

“You never were in the Inquisition,” Yeo answered, sharp as vinegar. “Or you’d know better how slow time can run. Stand still, and give God thanks you’re where you are.”

“I say, Gunner,” piped another, eyes bright in a hollow face. “Be there goold to that island?”

“Never heard of none,” said Yeo. “And so much the better for it.”

A cripple with scurvy-swollen joints licked cracked lips. “But… be there oranges and limmons there?”

“Not of my seeing,” Yeo said, softening. “But plenty of good fruit down to the beach, thank the Lord. There comes the dawn at last.”

Up flushed the rose. The sun came in a single stride, and the day, sudden as a struck match, set everything glittering. Level light ran along smooth palm-stems, turned the fringe of reef-foam into broken rainbows, and gilded lonely uplands far inland—quiet now, though in another age they would be cut with roads, pricked with engine-houses, and filled with the harsh iron complaint of mills. For the moment there was no man-made din—only the sea, the birds, and the wind dying off into a glassy calm.

Long lines of pelicans went clanging out to sea, their wings beating the air with a heavy, workmanlike rhythm. The night’s insect-hum hushed as if someone had shut a door, and then a thousand birds burst into song—notes flung out bright and clean, each species with its own dialect of joy, none translated, none needing to be.

Boat-load after boat-load went over the side. The sick were lifted as gently as one might carry bread just risen and too delicate to jar. They were landed on the beach and laid in the shade of palms, where the sand was pale and warm and the air smelled of green life. In half an hour the shore was scattered with Englishmen like castaways in a painting—some sprawled, some sitting, some weeping outright for no reason they could have named but relief.

And then, as if the body’s oldest hunger spoke first and loudest: “Fruit! fruit! fruit!”

Men who could barely walk crawled from plant to plant. They tore at violet grapes of a creeping shore-vine, staining their mouths purple as if they had been drinking wine at a feast. They gulped down prickly pears, blistering lips and tongues in their greed, thorns sticking like needles in their fingers despite Yeo’s curt warnings.

Some of the healthier set to hewing at cocoa-nut trees, confident as fools that they could beat a palm into surrender with a ship’s hatchet. They blunted their edges and sweated, and got little for it but curses.

Yeo and Drew, having gathered half-a-dozen men with sense enough to listen, went inland with their knives and their eyes. They returned in an hour like messengers from a pantry no one had dared imagine: acid junipa-apples that made the mouth pucker and then beg for more; guavas heavy with perfume, their skins yielding under the thumb; and crowned ananas—pineapples—prickly as a mailed fist, but promising, even before the knife went in, a fragrance like spiced honey and sun-warmed straw.

There on sandy turf they made their banquet. No table but the ground, no cloth but the shade; and yet it was a kind of sacrament after the ship’s salted misery. Knives flashed; skins fell away; fibers parted under the blade with that clean tearing sound ripe fruit makes. The pineapple, when cut, gave off a sharp, intoxicating sweetness that seemed to clear the head like a hymn. Men chewed with their eyes shut, as if to taste more fully; juice ran down wrists and dripped into the sand; and laughter came easier, the way it does when the mouth is no longer dry with despair.

Around them the island watched. Old blue land-crabs sat in the doors of their burrows and brandished their fists, absurdly brave. Cranes stood solemn in the shallows, heads cocked, considering how long it had been since they had seen featherless bipeds break the solitude.

Frank wandered apart, not gloomy so much as struck dumb by the strangeness—by the way even the smallest fly and leaf seemed to speak a different mother-tongue than the hedges of Devon. He looked, and looked again, as if he could force these foreign forms into the words he carried.

“New,” he said at last, softly. “New—everything new. Oh, awful feeling! All things changed around us, even to the tiniest fly and flower; yet we the same—the same forever.”

Amyas, mouth full of junipa-apple, followed him with the air of a cheerful showman who has seen wonders before and refuses to be overawed.

“Look, Frank—that’s a colibri. You’ve heard of colibris?”

A humming-bird hung over a bloom like a living jewel—its throat flashing green, then copper, then fire, as the light shifted. The air around it trembled with its wings’ fierce music, a high, insistent hum like a tiny spinning wheel. It darted away, then back again, and another joined it; together they whirred and danced round the flower-starred bush, their flight a bright argument no human grammar could quite catch.

Frank watched long, solemn as a priest before an altar. Then he breathed, half to himself, in the learned tongue that came to him when English failed.

“Qualis Natura formatrix, si talis formata? Oh my God, how fair must be Thy real world, if even Thy phantoms are so fair!”

“Phantoms?” Amyas frowned, uneasy at the word. “That’s no ghost, Frank, but a jolly little honey-sucker, with a wee wife, and children no bigger than peas—solid greedy little fellows enough, I’ll warrant.”

“Not phantoms in thy sense,” Frank answered, gentler than his first cry, “but in the sense of those who know the worthlessness of all below.”

Amyas halted and looked at him squarely. “I’ll tell you what, brother Frank—you’re a great deal wiser than me, I know; but I can’t abide to see you turn up your nose at God’s good earth. See now: God made all these things; and never a man, maybe, set eyes on them till fifty years agone; and yet they were as pretty as they are now, ever since the making of the world. And why do you think God could have put them here, then, but to please Himself—” and Amyas took off his hat, sudden reverent “—with the sight of them? What’s good enough to please God is good enough to please you and me.”

Frank’s face softened; the hard, feverish brightness in his eyes dimmed for a moment into something like humility.

“Your rebuke is just, dear old simple-hearted fellow. God forgive me, if with all my learning—which has brought me no profit—and my longings—which have brought me no peace—I presume at moments, sinner that I am, to be more dainty than the Lord Himself. He walked in Paradise among the trees of the garden, Amyas; and so will we, and be content with what He sends. Why should we long for the next world, before we are fit even for this one?”

“And in the meanwhile,” Amyas said, recovering his grin, “this earth’s quite good enough—at least here in Barbados.”

Frank, as if to turn the talk away from his own heart, listened to the island’s noises again—the layered chorus, each creature keeping its own hour and pitch. The ship behind them creaked faintly; the sea sighed; the palms hissed when the breeze stirred. It was a kind of music that belonged to the place, not made for sale or show, and for that reason truer.

“Do you believe,” he asked, “in those tales of the Spaniards—that Sirens and Tritons are heard singing in these seas?”

Amyas shrugged. “I can’t tell. There’s more fish in the water than ever came out of it, and more wonders in the world than we ever dreamt of. I was never in these parts before; and in the South Sea, I must say, I never came across any—though Yeo says he has heard fair music at night up in the Gulf, far away from land.”

“The Spaniards report,” Frank went on, warming to his own learning as if it were a hearth, “that at certain seasons choirs of these nymphs assemble in the sea, and with ravishing music sing their watery loves. It may be so. For Nature, which has peopled the land with rational souls, may not have left the sea altogether barren of them; above all, when we remember that the ocean is as it were the very fount of all fertility, and its slime—so the most learned hold with Thales of Miletus—that prima materia out of which all things were one by one concocted. Therefore the ancients feigned wisely that Venus—the mother of all living things, whereby they designed the plastic force of nature—was born of the sea-foam, and rising from the deep, floated ashore upon the isles of Greece.”

“I don’t know what plastic force is,” Amyas said, laughing, “but I wish I’d had the luck to be by when the pretty poppet came up. The nearest thing I ever saw to that was maidens swimming alongside us in the South Seas, and would have come aboard too; but Drake sent them off again for a lot of naughty packs—and I verily believe they were no better. Look at the butterflies now! Don’t you wish you were a boy again, and not too proud to go catching them in your cap?”

So they wandered together through the tropic wood—through leaves that shone like polished leather, past blooms that smelled of spice and rot and honey all at once, past ants marching in dark processions, past lizards that clicked away into shade. The island spoke continuously: the scratch of a crab, the clack of a beak, the sudden dry rattle of seed-pods, the far boom of surf on reef. Frank listened as if he might learn the place’s language by ear alone; Amyas only drank it in, content, as he had said, with what God sent.

They returned to the beach to find the sick already changed. Men who at dawn could not lift themselves from their hammocks were now pacing under palms, slow but stubborn, gaining strength with each step. The fruit had done more than fill bellies; it had put color into faces, and hope into eyes.

“Well done, lads!” Amyas shouted. “Keep a cheerful mind. We’ll have music ashore after dinner—for want of mermaids to sing to us. And those that can dance may.”

And so four days passed.

They did not forget their duties: they washed foul clothes in fresh water till the fabric smelled less of ship and more of sun; they filled casks from streams; they gathered fruit enough to keep a while. Yet, like schoolboys on holiday, they gave themselves up to simple merriment. At first they went hunting for gold with the folly of men who think riches grow like berries—ranging inland, prying under roots, staring into gullies, convinced each glint in sand must be treasure, in spite of Yeo’s steady reminder that none had ever been heard of there.

When no gold came, they were fain to lounge about like full-grown babies. They picked up shells and sea-fans to carry home to sweethearts, holding them to the ear to hear the sea’s imprisoned whisper. They smoked agoutis out of hollow trees with whoops and laughter. They tormented every living thing they could catch or corner—till not a land-crab dared show his claws from his hole, nor an armadillo unroll himself, until the invaders were gone.

Then, the ship watered and victualed with what the island would give, they weighed anchor. The bay took them back with no complaint but the chain’s rattle and the canvas filling. The palm-fringed shore slid away, returning to its own voices.

They left Barbados as they had found it—no men therein—never guessing, in their hunger and their play, that they had been the unknowing forerunners of a future thick with noise and trade and engines; that this quiet garden, where the night had been all insects and frogs and shore-birds, would one day ring with other sounds and other tongues, and be called, in a world grown busy, the richest gem of the tropic seas.

They slipped by the southern horn of Grenada in the black hours before dawn, and by sunrise were deep within that ring of islands where the sea seems to have remembered every color it ever wore, and the land to have forgotten every scruple it ever learned. Here the trade-wind came steady and kindly, as if it too had been trained to household service; it laid a cool hand on the sweaty necks of the men, filled the canvas with a soft drum, and kept the ship’s timbers talking in their homely creaks and sighs. No hurricane voice worried these waters; the great roar went prowling elsewhere, far northward, while here the world kept a perpetual, dangerous smile.

Sky, sea, and islets melted into one another like a thin sherbet—blue upon blue, then sudden bars of green, then a bright dash of sand, and over all a haze that turned distance into a mirage-pantry where shapes wavered like heat above an oven-mouth. The sailors, practical as butchers and as hungry in spirit as they were in belly, cared less for the rainbow than for what might be salted and stowed: Spanish gold, and—more whispered, more coveted—pearls. Amyas, who had seen tropic beauty before and would not be bewitched into idleness, watched the coasts with a colder appetite: not for meat, but for conquest; he weighed in his mind, as another man might weigh flour, the chances of stripping these islands from Spain and laying them as a dish before Queen Elizabeth.

Yet even the hardest eyes on board could not look without a certain hush at seas so famous and so little known to English keels. Here a failure cost more than drowning. The thought of the Inquisition sat on their tongues like too much pepper—hot, bitter, not to be swallowed without water. The old mariners of Hawkins’s ventures were plied all day, as if they were cook-books for a strange kitchen: the names of isles and capes, the habits of fish and birds, where fresh water might be drawn, where a reef would rip a boat like a careless knife.

Frank listened most of all, grave and quiet. A sort of awe, not sorrowful but shining, grew on him; he stood long at the rail and seemed to drink light as if it could nourish. At times he murmured, half to himself, “This is the gate of heaven,” and forgot the very call to mess, as though bread and beef were poor things beside the spectacle. The islands hung in the air with inverted doubles below them; sand-hills rolled and weltered in the mirage; slopes blazed with yellow flower-beds and huge thorny cacti, like giant candelabra set out for some pagan feast. Even the sun, as he went down, was warped into odd oval and pear-shaped flames through crimson mist, as if the sky itself were a distorting glass.

While Frank wondered, Yeo rejoiced; for to the southward of the sinking sun rose a cluster of tall peaks out of the sea. Unless his reckonings lied, those were the mountains of Macanao at the western end of Margarita—the Isle of Pearls, whose very name had rung through Mediterranean ports and German fairs, and whose riches were spoken of with the same mouth that spoke of sin.

Next day they ran along the north side of the island, having passed—so far as their eyes could judge—undiscovered by the castle the Spaniards had built at the eastern end to guard the pearl-fishery. Toward afternoon they opened a deep, still bight wooded down to the water’s edge. There in the roadstead lay a caravel, and three boats by her.

At that sight the deck filled as if a bell had rung. Every man had a counsel, every mouth a plan. Some would have sailed straight in on the fresh afternoon breeze that always seemed to blow shoreward in those parts. But the white lines of breakers fretted here and there at the bay’s mouth; coral, they feared, and not in a mood to forgive. Better to run by quietly and send in boat and pinnace.

Yeo would have had Spanish colors shown, to keep the caravel from starting like a startled hare; but Amyas refused, square and stubborn. To lie with a flag, he said, was a mean thing, unless driven by extremity or commanded by great statecraft.

So they held on till the next point shut them from the roadstead, and then lowered away. Cary went in the largest boat with twenty men; Amyas took the smaller with fifteen. Among them, because he would not be denied, came John Brimblecombe in cassock and bands, and with an old sword of his uncle’s that he treasured as another man might treasure a carved spoon from his grandmother’s chest.

At the bight’s mouth they found the reef as thick as warned—coral heads everywhere, pale and treacherous under the clear water, like bones under skin. They edged along it a long way hunting a passage. Those new to the Indies leaned over and stared down into gardens no gardener on English soil could have imagined: living flower-beds, branching fans, soft waving fronds, and fish flashing through as bright as spilled spice—turmeric-yellow, saffron, a sudden pepper-red. Even the sea had its own music here: the hush of water sliding, the tick of the reef, the distant boom where surf struck the outer rocks like steady hand-drumming.

Then, below, the tone of the place changed. Shapes moved, long and thick, with an easy cruelty. A “school of sharks,” Yeo called it—some of them nearly as long as the boat—tilting up their wicked scowling eyes as if measuring what might be had for supper.

“Jack,” said Amyas, sitting near him, “look how that big fellow eyes thee. He hath surely taken a fancy to that plump hide of thine, and thinks thou wouldst eat as tender as any sucking porker.”

Jack went pale to the lips but said nothing.

Just then the biggest brute shot upward after a parrot-fish. The little creature, finding no other way, sprang clean into the air and almost aboard. Where it had burst from the water, three or four great brown shagreened noses clashed together within two yards of Jack. Each opened to show those horrible rows of saw teeth, and then sank again, sulky as hungry dogs, to wait.

Jack breathed very softly, “In manus tuas, Domine,” and turned his eyes inboard. Whatever wonders the Indies held, he had no lust to look at sharks.

At last they found their opening through the reef and ran in on the fair wind. The caravel lay now not a musket-shot away. Cary laid her aboard before the Spaniards could get to their ordnance. Standing up in the stern-sheets he shouted to them to yield. The captain answered boldly enough, asking, in whose name?

“In the name of common sense, ye dogs!” cried Will Cary. “Do you not see you are but fifty strong to our twenty?”

And up he scrambled. The Spaniard fired a pistol at him. Cary knocked him over rather than kill him; and the crew, seeing the thing done, yielded—some on their knees, some leaping overboard, splashing like frightened sheep.

Meanwhile Amyas had pulled round under the stern and boarded the second boat, the nearest being fast alongside and therefore a sure prize. In that boat the Spaniards cried “Misericordia” and yielded without a blow; the negroes leaped overboard and swam ashore like sea-dogs.

The third boat, not an oar’s length away, began to pull off. Here John Brimblecombe, fired by a need to be seen brave, seized a boat-hook and clawed it into her stern, shouting, “Stay, ye Papists! Stay, Spanish dogs!”

They were ten to his one. In a moment they hauled him over; he fell full-length, nose-first, into the sea, and the hook stayed fast behind them.

What seized him then no man could tell—perhaps the memory of those sharks, for fear breeds its own creatures and populates the water with teeth. He roared like a bull in a market, and instead of turning for the English boat struck out after the Spanish, cassock floating behind him so that he looked for all the world like some great black monk-fish. He howled and puffed with his mouth full of salt water, and his words came broken as if chewed.

“Stay, ye Spanish dogs! Help, all good fellows! See you not I am a dead man? They are nuzzling already at my toes! He hath hold of my leg! My right thigh is bitten clean off! Oh that I were preaching in Hartland pulpit! Stay, Spanish dogs! Yield, Papist cowards, lest I make mincemeat of you; and take me aboard! Yield, I say, or my blood be on your heads! I am no Jonah; if he swallow me, he will never cast me up again! It is better to fall into the hands of man, than into the hands of devils with three rows of teeth apiece. In manus tuas. Orate pro anima—!”

And so on, in a frenzy that might have made Panurge himself blush. The English, expecting every moment to see him snapped up by a shark or brained by an oar, fired a volley at the fugitives. The Spaniards, terrified, leaped overboard as the others had done. Jack scrambled into the empty boat, and drew sword with one hand while wiping water from his eyes with the other, and began to lay about him like a lion, cutting at the air and crying, “Yield, idolaters! Yield, Spanish dogs!” Only when his breath failed and his wits returned did he see there was no one left to strike. He sat panting, then began stripping off his hose, swearing his thigh was bitten through and his leg full of blood, and would have swooned for that imaginary wound had not the men, with much laughter and some anger, persuaded him at last that he was whole.

They overhauled the prizes. The caravel held hides and salt pork—heavy stuff, fit for stowage and barter, with a smell of brine and old animal oil that clung to the fingers. But there was more. In the captain’s cabin, and in the stern-sheets of the boat Brimblecombe had “boarded,” were frails of leaves packed neatly. When they opened them, the leaves crackled like dry bay, and within lay goodly pearls, somewhat browned. The Spaniards, in haste and greed, had damaged the color by opening the shells with fire, instead of leaving them to decay slowly after the Arabian fashion. Still, pearls were pearls. Though no one could tally their worth exactly, content spread through the men like warmth after a swallow of strong liquor.

Then some malicious fellow set the caravel on fire. Hides burn with an acrid, nauseous stench; it was no nosegay that rose over the bight, but a black reek like scorched hair and tallow. Amyas was very angry at that wanton waste—Drake, his model, had never made a pastime of destruction. Cary had his jest ready.

“Ah!” said he. “‘Lutheran devils’ we are, you know; so we are bound to vanish, like other fiends, with an evil savor.”

Once aboard again Amyas rounded on Mr. Brimblecombe and boxed his ear, telling him he would do better to play the man with less roaring—roar less before he was hurt, and keep his breath for his strokes, if he wished the crew to listen much to his sermons. Frank, hearing, bade Amyas leave the offender to him, and set upon Jack with a scholar’s whip.

“Come hither, thou recreant Jack, thou lily-livered Jack, thou hysterical Jack. Tell me now—thou hast read Plato’s Dialogues, and Aristotle’s Logic?”

Jack, meek as a scolded boy, answered, “Yes.”

“Then I will deal with thee after the manner of those ancient sages, and ask whether the greater must not contain the less?”

“Yes, sure.”

“And that which is more than a part contain that part, more than which it is?”

“Yes, sure.”

“Then tell me—is not a priest more than a layman?”

Jack, who was ever loud on the dignity of the priesthood, answered boldly, “Of course.”

“Then a priest containeth a man, and is a man, and something over—viz., his priesthood?”

Jack, seeing the snare, hesitated. “I suppose so.”

“Then if a priest show himself no man, he shows himself all the more no priest.”

“I’ll tell you what, Master Frank,” says Jack, sore but stubborn, “you may be right by logic; but sharks aren’t logic, nor don’t understand it neither.”

Frank pressed him, asking if it were manly to howl like a pig in a gate because he thought there was that which was not there; and still more, if there had been real peril, whether it were brave to keep his mouth open and swallow half the sea. Jack had no answer at first. Then, in plain sincerity, he confessed it was not Spaniards he feared. Spaniards were Heaven’s handiwork; he knew how to deal with them. But those “fiends’ spawn of sharks”—the sight of that great fellow taking fish alongside had upset him clean.

“Oh, Jack, Jack,” cried Frank, “behold how one sin begets another! Just now thou wert but a coward, and now thou art a Manichee; for thou imputest to an evil creator that which was formed only for a good end—namely, sharks, which were made on purpose to devour useless carcasses like thine. Moreover, as a brother of the Rose, thou wert bound by the vow of thy brotherhood to have leaped joyfully down that shark’s mouth.”

Jack retorted that perhaps if Mistress Rose had been in the beast’s stomach he might have shown more devotion; but he had wanted to fight Spaniards, not to be shark-bitten. Frank called it self-will, and with mock solemnity pronounced him cut off and deprived—no more chaplain, unless within seven days he did some deed fit for the Seven Champions or Orlando himself; and even then, no iron about him beyond what would mend his pen.

Jack protested, asking since when Frank had been Bishop of Exeter. Frank answered that Jack’s wit was blinded by gross vapors raised by fear, and that the bishop might deal with his Manicheeism; but his howling—scandalous in a preacher—deserved censure.

Then Amyas joined in, and between them they pounded him with blunt truths: that no wonder young men refused the Gospel when it was preached by those whom they could not help but look down upon; that for a priest of the English Church, whose work was to make men good Christians by making them good men and good Englishmen, to show the white feather in need was to unpreach in a minute what he had preached for years. Jack, overwhelmed, clapped hands to ears and fled, and Frank laughed so heartily that even Amyas’s sad steadiness softened for a moment, glad after all for the smile.

Next day was Sunday. After divine service—Jack could hardly be persuaded to read it, so shamefaced was he, and would not hear of preaching afterward—Amyas read aloud, as was their custom, the articles of their agreement. Then, seeing a sloping beach abreast with a shoot of clear water running into the sea, he resolved they should land, wash clothes, and water the ship again, for water had been scarce at Barbados.

To this party Jack Brimblecombe must needs go, taking sword and a great arquebuse. He had dreamed, he said, that Spaniards set upon him, and was sure the dream would come true; and moreover he cared little if it did, or if he ever got back alive. Better, he declared in a wild bitterness, to die than be made an ape and a scarecrow, laughed at by the men and badgered with Ramus’s logic and Plato’s dialectical devilries, till he was made to confess himself a Manichee, and for aught he knew a turbaned Turk or a Hebrew Jew. So he flung himself into the boat like a desperate man.

They went ashore under strict command from Amyas that no firearms should be let off, for fear of alarming Spaniards. The place was fair beyond telling: trees crowding down to the bright edge, shade cool as a cellar, and the thin music of the rivulet pouring into the sea with a constant, clean chatter that soothed the mind after the ship’s groaning and the men’s loud talk. They washed their clothes, stretching limbs with joy, and then shot the seine they had brought. The net came up alive and strange, heavy with creatures that flashed and wriggled like a spilled casket of jewels.

Among them they took a sea-cow—seven feet long—barnacled and limpeted on her back as if she had been drift-timber. She was a fond, foolish beast, and yet, men said, pious withal: if she found a corpse she would watch it day and night until it decayed or was buried. The Indians called her manati; she carried her young under her arm and gave it suck like a woman; and wounded, she lamented aloud with a human voice, and was said at seasons to sing melodiously—perhaps, some argued, the very melody that had given birth to tales of sirens and Tritons. They did not rashly deny it; for in those seas Nature bred wonders as a kitchen breeds steam, and the man who calls all strange things lies may prove only ignorant.

One of the men also brought in two great black fowls shot with a crossbow—bodied and headed like capons, but bigger than any eagle—the Spaniards called them curassos. Later these, with the sea-cow, made them good cheer, roast and sodden. The manati’s flesh was dainty meat, as good as a four-months’ calf, tender and fat; the smell of it as it browned—clean, sweet, with a mild richness—made the men forget danger for a little, and talk with their mouths full, as men will.

Then they set to filling casks and barricos, hauling the boat up to the outflow of the rivulet so the water ran clear and cold into their staves. Lucky for them it fell out that they were all close together at that work, and not abroad skylarking as they had been half an hour before.

John Brimblecombe, as soon as they landed, had gone apart with a shamefaced and doleful face. Under a great tree he sat, plucked a Bible from his bosom, and read steadfastly, girt with his great sword, his arquebuse laid by him. That too was well—for him and for the rest.

They had not finished watering when a cry rose sharp as a cracked bell: the enemy was upon them. Out of the wood, not twenty yards from the parson, came full fifty shot, with a multitude of negroes behind, and an officer in front on horseback, plume of feathers nodding in his hat, sword drawn in his hand. The suddenness of it broke the shoreline’s gentle music; the birds fled with startled clatter, the rivulet’s constant voice seemed to grow thin under the harsh new sounds—boot-thuds, shouted Spanish, the metallic cough of match and pan.

“Stand, for your lives!” shouted Amyas; and only just in time. Ten minutes were lost in running to and fro before he could get them into any order. But when Jack beheld the Spaniards—almost as if he had expected them—he plucked a leaf and set it in his Bible for a mark, laid the book down soberly, caught up his arquebuse, and ran like a mad dog straight at the Spanish captain. He fired once and shot him through the body, stark dead. Then, flinging the arquebuse at the head of the next man, he fell on with his sword like a very Colbrand, breaking in among the arquebusiers and striking right and left such ugly strokes that the Spaniards, who took him for a fiend—or Luther himself come to plague them—gave back in confusion.

They fired at him five or six at once, but whether for fear of him or fear of hitting one another, made such bad play that he took only one shrewd gall in his thigh, enough to make him limp for many a day. Still he pressed on. By then the English had run up in better order, near forty well armed. The Spaniards turned and fled as fast as they had come.

Cary, with breath to jest even then, hinted that the dogs had tasted the parson and had no stomach to wait for clerk and people.

“Come back, Jack! Are you mad?” shouted Amyas.

Jack, who had not spoken one word all this while, followed the Spaniards as fiercely as ever, until, reaching a great blow at one arquebusier, he caught his foot in a root. Down he went, struck his head, and knocked all breath out of himself—which between fatness and fighting was not much. He lay.

Amyas, seeing the Spaniards gone, would not pursue. He lifted Jack up. Jack stared about as if waking from a fever and cried, “Glory be! glory be!—How many have I killed? How many have I killed?”

“Nineteen, at the least,” quoth Cary, “and seven with one back stroke,” and then showed him the dead captain and two arquebusiers, and three or four more limping away wounded—some by their fellows’ shot.

“There!” said Jack, pausing and blowing, “will you laugh at me any more, Mr. Cary, or say I cannot fight because I am a poor parson’s son?”

Cary took him by the hand and asked pardon for his scoffing, owning that Jack had that day played the best man of them all. Jack, who never bore malice, began laughing too.

“Oh, Mr. Cary,” he said, “we have all known your pleasant ways, ever since you used to put drumble-drones into my desk at Bideford school.”

So they went to the boats and pulled off, thanking God—as well they might—for their deliverance. All the crew rejoiced over Jack; yet after a while he grew very faint, having bled more than he knew, and made as little of his real wound as he had made much the day before of his imaginary one.

That evening Frank asked him, with the curious seriousness of one who keeps a ledger of men’s souls and manners, how it came to pass that he had shown so cool and approved a valor in so sudden a mishap.

The men would willingly have lingered yet a while about Margarita and Cubagua, hoping for another stroke at the pearl-trade; for the thought of those milky beads—wrenched from brown throats by Spanish greed and Indian misery—had set every tongue wagging. But Amyas, who said grimly that he had “fleshed his dogs,” would not hang about the islands after the alarm. So they bore away south-west across the mouth of that vast bay that lies between Paria and Codera, leaving on their right Tortuga, and on their left the meadow-islands of the Piritoos—two long green blades laid flat upon a sea that seemed, in that place, almost tideless and too smooth for its own good.

Yeo and Drew knew every bend of the coast; they had earned that knowledge, as men earn it who trade where trading is half war, and war is never far from hunger. The ship’s world was all homely routine—hands at braces, the wet slap of rope-ends, the perpetual scrape and creak of timber—yet the horizon ahead began to rise into something unhomely: the Caracas mountains, lifting sheer out of the sea, higher and higher, till the very notion of height which most of the crew had carried from Devon and Cornwall was made ridiculous.
