Frank had seen the Alps; Amyas had looked on the Andes; but Cary’s imagination had been bounded by M’Gillicuddy’s Reeks, and Brimblecombe’s by Exmoor. Brimblecombe, to Cary’s infinite mirth, spent a whole day clinging to the rigging and staring upward with his chin higher than his nose, until his neck stiffened and he groaned as if the mountain had set a hand upon him.

Soon the water grew rough and chopping, though the breeze was fair and gentle. The ship met, as it were, an invisible river running eastward in the sea—one of those winter currents that mock a westering mariner. All night they struggled through broken billows, with the huge wall of Cape Codera a thousand feet above them on the left; and beyond it, bank upon bank of mountains, washed in yellow moonlight. Even the ship’s noises altered under that cliff: the wind sounded thinner, as if drawn through a narrow throat; the surf below the rock had a steady, animal breathing; and the men, hearing those new voices about them, grew strangely quiet between orders.

Morning showed them a large ship that had passed in the night upon the opposite course, and now lay a good ten miles to the eastward. Yeo, with that eager righteousness which made him smell prize as a hound smells blood, would have gone back and taken her; he spoke of the thing as if it were already done, and their guns already telling out a psalm of iron.

“Why, Yeo,” said Amyas, “you told me one day more would bring us to La Guayra.”

“All the more reason, sir,” said Yeo, “to do the Lord’s work thoroughly when He has brought us safely so far.”

“She can pass well enough,” said Amyas. “No loss.”

“Ah, sirs, sirs,” Yeo answered, shaking his head, “she is delivered into your hands, and you will have to give an account of her.”

Frank, whose mind was elsewhere and yet could not bear to have their purpose blurred, took it up quickly. “We shall give account enough of many a tall Spaniard before we return; but La Guayra—and the salvation of one whom we believe dwells there—was our first object.”

Yeo’s eyes had that old, sad look which seemed to listen not to men but to judgment. “A lady brought Captain Oxenham to ruin.”

“You do not dare compare her with this one?” Cary and Frank cried together.

“God forbid,” said Yeo; “yet no venture will prosper unless it have a single eye to the Lord’s work; and that is, to cripple the Spaniard and exalt her majesty. I had thought nothing was dearer than that to Captain Leigh’s heart.”

Amyas stood a moment between two duties, both sharp as points. The queen’s service urged him to turn; his vow drove him on. It sounds a scholar’s dilemma when read in quiet; on deck, with land under the lee and an enemy to windward, it was practical enough.

Frank’s counsel prevailed; they held on for La Guayra. Amyas half hoped the Spaniard would see and come down upon them, so that duty and desire might be made one by a cannon-shot. But the Spaniard went his way eastward; and if he had not, the tale that follows would have had another ending.

About mid-day a canoe—the first they had seen—came staggering towards them under a huge three-cornered sail. She rode the short seas like a gourd, and yet came on with a boldness that made Cary laugh.

“Metal floats in these seas, you see,” said he. “There’s a fresh marvel for you, Frank.”

“Expound,” Frank answered; for he was ready now to swallow marvels as a child swallows sweetmeats.

“Why, how else would those two bronze statues dare go to sea in such a cockle-shell? Have I given you the dor now, master courtier?”

“I am long past dors,” said Frank, smiling in spite of himself. “But what noble creatures—and how fearlessly they come alongside!”

“I suspect they take us for Spaniards and want to sell us cocoa-nuts,” Cary said. “See—she’s laden with vegetables.”

“Aye,” said Amyas; and, because he had learned in these seas that food was more than food—news, peace, bait, friendship—he leaned over the side as the canoe drew near. Baskets lay amidships—plantains, cassava bread wrapped in leaves, gourds of water, and what looked like little brown nuts with pale husks: cacao, perhaps, or some cousin of it, smelling faintly of earth and green bitterness even across the water.

“Hail them, Yeo. You speak the best Spanish, and I want speech of one of them.”

Yeo hailed. The canoe, without more ado, ran alongside and lowered her sail; and a splendid Indian scrambled aboard like a cat—full six feet high, naked save for a girdle, his limbs shining as if oiled, his carriage as proud as any gentleman’s. He smiled at first, showing white teeth; then the smile snapped. His eyes flew round, taking in a dozen small things—the cut of a cap, the fashion of a weapon, the very way a rope was coiled—signs, to such a man, as plain as printed letters. In an instant he sprang to the side and shouted to his comrade in Spanish:

“Treachery! No Spaniard!”

He would have leaped overboard; but a dozen hands caught him. It took trouble to master him, so strong he was, so slippery the naked limbs that twisted like eels. Amyas, between anger at the tumult and pity for the brave fellow, cried to the men to hurt him not, and to the Indian to be quiet and no harm should come. After five minutes’ confusion the stranger gave in sulkily, breathing hard, his chest rising and falling like a bellows.

“Don’t bind him,” said Amyas. “Let him loose, and make a ring round him. Now, my man—there’s a dollar for you.”

The Indian’s eyes glistened at the sight of the coin. He took it, weighing it on his palm as if the coldness spoke.

“All I want,” said Amyas, “is first to tell me what ships are in La Guayra; and next, to go thither aboard of me and show me which is the governor’s house and which the custom-house.”

The Indian set the coin down on the deck as if it were hot, crossed himself, and looked Amyas full in the face.

“No, señor. I am a freeman and a cavalier, a Christian Guayqueria, whose forefathers, first of all the Indians, swore fealty to the King of Spain; and whom he calls to this day, in his proclamations, his most faithful, loyal, and noble Guayquerias. God forbid, therefore, that I should tell aught to his enemies, who are my enemies likewise.”

A growl rose from those who understood him. More than one hinted what a cord round the head or a match between the fingers might do for memory.

“God forbid!” said Amyas, and his voice cut the muttering short. “A brave and loyal man he is, and as such will I treat him. Tell me, my brave fellow—how do you know us to be his Catholic majesty’s enemies?”

The Indian, with a shrewd smile, pointed about the deck at half-a-dozen different objects, saying to each, “Not Spanish.”

“Well—and what of that?”

“None but Spaniards and free Guayquerias have a right to sail these seas.”

Amyas laughed outright. “Thou art a right valiant bit of copper. Pick up thy dollar and go thy way in peace. Make room for him, men. We can learn what we want without his help.”

The Indian paused, incredulous, astonished; courtesy, in an enemy, was a kind of sorcery.

“Overboard with you!” Amyas added. “Don’t you know when you are well off?”

“Most illustrious señor,” began the Indian, falling into that drawling, sententious fashion of his race, “I have been deceived. I heard that you heretics roasted and ate all true Catholics—such as we Guayquerias are—and that all your padres had tails.”

“Plague on you, sirrah!” squeaked Jack Brimblecombe, hopping forward in earnest indignation. “Have I a tail? Look here!”

“¿Quién sabe? Who knows?” said the Indian through his nose, and the men laughed.

“How do you know we are heretics?” Amyas asked.

“Humph! But in repayment for your kindness,” the Indian went on, “I would warn you, illustrious señor, not to go on to La Guayra. There are ships of war there waiting for you; and moreover, the governor, Don Guzman, sailed to the eastward only yesterday to look for you; and I wonder much that you did not meet him.”

“To look for us?” Cary burst out. “Impossible. Lies! This is some trick of the rascal’s to frighten us away.”

Amyas’s face hardened. “Don Guzman came out but yesterday to look for us? Are you sure you speak truth?”

“As I live, señor—he and another ship, for which I took yours.”

Amyas stamped upon the deck. That, then, was the ship they had passed. Fool that he was—duty and opportunity had crossed within a cable’s length, and he had let them go by.

“Off with you,” he said shortly; and the Indian, with a bound, was over the side and into his canoe. They watched him, wondering at the stateliness and courtesy of that bold sea-cavalier, who could be loyal without being servile.

So westward-ho they ran, beneath the mighty northern wall—the highest cliff on earth—some seven thousand feet of rock, parted from the sea by a narrow strip of bright green lowland. Here and there a patch of sugar-cane, or a knot of cocoa-nut trees close to the water’s edge, reminded them that they were in the tropics; but above, all was savage and bare as an Alpine precipice. Deep clefts here and there let the southern sun pour a white blaze down to the very sea-marge; and through them they caught glimpses, far above, of stately trees lining the glens, and of a veil of perpetual mist shrouding the inner summits. Between ship and mountain-side hung white fleecy clouds, motionless in the burning air, as if nailed there; and the silence of that suspended world was broken only by the ship’s own living noises—block and tackle, the slap of sails, a cough, a laugh checked too soon—until even those sounds seemed impertinent against so vast a rest.

“Within those mountains,” said Drew, the master, “three thousand feet above our heads, lies Saint Yago de Leon—the great city the Spaniards founded fifteen years agone.”

“Is it a rich place?” Cary asked, as men ask of towns.

“Very, they say.”

“Is it a strong place?” Amyas asked, as captains must.

“No forts at all, they say. The Spaniards boast that Heaven has made such walls already that man need make none.”

“I don’t know,” said Amyas, looking up. “Lads—could you climb those hills, do you think?”

“Rather higher than Harty Point, sir,” one answered; “but it depends pretty much on what’s behind them.”

Then the last point was rounded, and they were full in sight of the goal they had sailed four thousand miles to reach. A low black cliff crowned by a wall; a battery at either end. Within, a few narrow streets of white houses, running parallel with the sea upon a strip of flat that seemed not two hundred yards broad; behind, the mountain wall, casting the whole place into a shade so deep it looked like ink poured behind the roofs. Drew, who had been off the place before, pointed out a narrow path that wound upward through a glen, seemingly sheer perpendicular: the road to the capital, if any man dared.

Even in shadow the town had a dusty, glaring look, as if the very heat bleached it. The breaths of air off the land were stifling; no wonder, for La Guayra, with that vast fire-brick of heated rock behind it, is one of the hottest spots on earth. The heat came with its own sound—or rather its own lack of sound: the air felt thick, as if it swallowed echoes, and the sea’s voice seemed dulled, as though the shore were a blanket.

Where was the harbour? There was none—only an open roadstead, where five vessels lay tossing at anchor. Two outermost were small merchant caravels. Behind them lay two long, low, ugly craft, and at sight of them Yeo gave a long whew.

“Galleys, as I’m a sinful saint! And what’s that big one inside them, Robert Drew? She has more than hawseholes in her idolatrous black sides, I think.”

“We shall open her astern of the galleys in another minute,” said Amyas. “Look out, Cary; your eyes are better than mine.”

“Six round portholes on the main deck,” Cary answered.

“And I can see the brass patararoes glittering on her poop,” said Amyas. “Will, we’re in for it.”

“In for it we are, captain.

‘Farewell, farewell, my parents dear.
I never shall see you more, I fear.’

Let’s go in, nevertheless, and pound the Don’s ribs, my old lad of Smerwick. Eh? Three to one is fair odds.”

“Not underneath those fort guns, I beg leave to say,” quoth Yeo. “If the Philistines will but come out unto us, we will make them like unto Zeba and Zalmunna.”

“Game-cocks are game-cocks,” Amyas answered, “but reason’s reason.”

“If the Philistines are not coming out, they are going to send a messenger instead,” said Cary. “Look out, all thin skulls!”

As he spoke, a puff of white smoke rolled from the eastern fort; a heavy ball plunged into the water between fort and ship, and the sound came a moment later—flat and brutal, like a door slammed in their faces.

“I don’t altogether like this,” said Amyas. “What do they mean by firing without warning? And what are these ships of war doing here? Drew, you told me the armadas never lay here.”

“No more, I believe they do, sir,” Drew answered, peering, “on account of the anchorage being so bad, as you may see. I’m mortal afeared that rascal’s story was true, and the Dons have got wind of our coming.”

“Run up a white flag, at all events. If they expect us, they must have known it long, or how could they have got their craft hither?”

“True, sir. They must have come from Santa Marta at the least—perhaps Cartagena. That would take a month, going and coming.”

Amyas suddenly remembered Eustace’s threat at the wayside inn, and for one instant his mind tasted the sourness of betrayal. Could he have warned them? It seemed impossible—yet here lay proof in black hulls and portholes.

“Let us hold a council of war, at all events, Frank.”

But Frank was fastened to another sight. Half a mile to the eastward of the town, two or three hundred feet up the steep mountain-side, stood a large low white house, embosomed in trees and gardens. There was no other house of that size near, no place for one; and before it, flapping and snapping in the land-breath, flew the royal flag of Spain. That must be the governor’s house; that must be the abode of the Rose of Torridge. Frank stood devouring it with his eyes until imagination grew cruelly sure, persuading him that he could see a woman’s figure upon the terrace, and that the figure was hers. Amyas could hardly tear him away to the council, which was sad, and only not peevish.

Amyas, Frank, Cary, with Brimblecombe, Yeo, and Drew, withdrew upon the poop; and each looked at the others awhile. Plans and hopes of months were brought to nothing in an hour.

“It is impossible, you see,” said Amyas at last, “to surprise the town by land while these ships are here; for if we land our men we leave our ship without defence.”

“As impossible as to challenge Don Guzman while he is not here,” Cary answered.

“I wonder why the ships have not opened on us already,” said Drew.

“Perhaps they respect our flag of truce,” Cary said. “Why not send in a boat to treat with them, and to inquire for—”

“For her?” Frank broke in sharply. “If we show that we are aware of her existence, her name is blasted in the eyes of those jealous Spaniards.”

“And as for respecting our flag of truce,” said Yeo, “if you will take an old man’s advice, trust them not. They will keep the same faith with us as they kept with Captain Hawkins at San Juan d’Ulloa—when we might have taken the whole plate-fleet, and did not, but asked license to trade like honest men. And yet, after they had granted it, and deceived us by fair speech into landing ourselves and our ordnance, the governor and all the fleet set upon us five to one, and gave no quarter. No, sir; I expect the only reason they don’t attack us is because their crews are not aboard.”

“They will be soon enough, then,” said Amyas. “I can see soldiers coming down the landing-stairs.”

And indeed boats full of armed men began to push off to the ships. Their oars made a soft, busy whisper on the water, like rats in a wall; and the sound, small as it was, set every nerve on edge, for it spoke of preparation, of nets drawing tight.

“We may thank Heaven,” Drew said, “we were not here two hours agone. The sun will be down before they are ready for sea, and the fellows will have no stomach to go looking for us by night.”

“So much the worse for us,” Amyas answered. “If they do that, we may give them the slip, and back again to the town, and there try our luck; for I cannot find it in my heart to leave without one dash at it.”

Yeo shook his head. “There are plenty more towns along the coast more worth trying than this, sir. But Heaven’s will be done.”

As they spoke, the sun plunged into the sea, and all was dark—darkness thickened by the mountain’s shadow, so that the town seemed a low line of lamps pinned under a great black cloth.

At last it was agreed: they would anchor and wait till midnight. If the ships of war came out, they would try to run in past them; and, desperate as it might be, attempt the first plan—land to the westward of the town, take it in flank, plunder the government storehouses close to the landing-place, and fight back to their boats and out of the roadstead. Two hours would suffice if the armada and galleys were but once out of the way.

Amyas went forward, called the men together, and told them. It was not cheerfully received; yet what else was there? The ship’s belly held salted beef and biscuit, the hard bread that smelled of weevils and old flour; but the men’s imaginations already tasted Spanish wine, fresh meat, fruit, and the sweet fatness of loot. Hunger is a counsellor that speaks with a loud voice, and makes gold seem as needful as bread.

They ran down a mile and a half to the westward and anchored.

The night wore on. There was no stir among the shipping; though they could not see the vessels themselves, the lights—easy to distinguish by their heights from those in the town—remained motionless. The men fretted and fumed for weary hours at seeing a rich prize (for of course the town was paved with gold) within arm’s reach and yet, for the moment, impossible. The sea made its own low talk about the cable; from shore came now and then a faint clatter—an oar in a thole-pin, a distant shout—and above all loomed the mountain, holding the sound down as a hand holds down a child.

Let Amyas and his men have patience. Some five years more, and the great Armada will have come and gone; and then that avenging storm—of which they, like Oxenham, Hawkins, and Drake, were but the avant-couriers—will burst upon every Spanish port from Corunna to Cadiz, from the Canaries to Havana; and La Guayra and Saint Yago de Leon will not escape. Captain Amyas Preston and Captain Sommers, the colonist of the Bermudas, will land with a force tiny enough, though larger far than Leigh’s, where Leigh dares not land; and taking the fort of Guayra will find, as Leigh finds, that their coming has been expected, and that the Pass of the Venta, three thousand feet above, has been fortified with huge barricades, abattis, and cannon, making the capital within its ring of mountain-walls impregnable—to all but Englishmen (or Zouaves). For up that seven thousand feet of precipice behind the town those fierce adventurers will climb hand over hand through rain and fog, while men lie down and beg their officers to kill them, for no farther can they go—yet farther they will go, hewing a path with their swords through woods of wild plantain and rhododendron thickets, over—so it seems, however incredible—the very saddle of the Silla, down upon the astonished Mantuanos of Saint Yago, driving all before them; and, having burnt the city in default of ransom, return triumphant by the right road and pass along the coast masters of the deep.

I know not whether any men still live who count their descent from those two valiant captains; but if such there be, let them be sure that the history of the English navy tells no more Titanic victory over nature and man than that now forgotten raid of Amyas Preston and his comrade, in the year of grace 1595.

But though a venture on the town was impossible, there was another venture which Frank could not bear to let slip. A light now shone brightly in one of the governor’s windows—steady as a star set in stone—and it drew his thoughts as a lodestone draws iron. While the ship lay humming softly under the night—timbers answering the swell, blocks ticking, men whispering and shifting like sleepers—Frank sat in the cabin with Amyas, Cary, and Jack, and opened his heart.

“And are we, then,” he asked mournfully, “to go without doing the very thing for which we came?”

All were silent. The air in the cabin held the mixed smells of men and tar and damp wood; some scrap of food—cheese-rind or onion-skin—gave a faint homely sharpness, like a memory of kitchens far away. Outside, the sea’s voice was as constant as breath.

At last John Brimblecombe spoke, small in body but with a will that seemed to ring like metal when struck.

“Show me the way to do it, Mr. Frank, and I will go.”

“My dearest man,” said Amyas, “what would you have? Any attempt to see her—even if she be here—would be all but certain death.”

“And what if it were?” Frank answered, and his voice did not tremble. “What if it were, my brother Amyas? Listen to me. I have long ceased to shrink from Death; but till I came into these magic climes, I never knew the beauty of his face.”

“Of death?” Cary said, half bitter, half wondering. “I should have said, of life. God forgive me; but a man might wish to live forever, if he had such a world as this wherein to live.”

“And do you forget,” Frank went on, “that the more fair this passing world of time, by so much the more fair is that eternal world whereof all here is but a shadow and a dream? by so much the more fair is He before whose throne the four mystic beasts—the substantial ideas of Nature and her powers—stand day and night crying, ‘Holy, holy, holy, Lord God of hosts; Thou hast made all things, and for Thy pleasure they are and were created!’ My friends, if He be so prodigal of His own glory as to have decked these lonely shores—almost unknown since the foundation of the world—with splendours beyond our dreams, what must be the glory of His face itself? I have done with vain shadows. It is better to depart and to be with Him, where shall be neither desire nor anger, self-deception nor pretence, but the eternal fulness of reality and truth. One thing I have to do before I die, for God has laid it on me. Let that be done to-night, and then—farewell!”

“Frank! Frank! remember our mother!” Amyas cried; for that name, in such a cabin at such an hour, was like bread broken to steady the heart.

“I do remember her,” Frank answered. “I have talked over these things with her many a time; and where I would fain be, she would fain be also. She sent me out with my virgin honour, as the Spartan mother did her boy with the shield, saying, ‘Come back either with this, or upon this.’ And one or the other I must do, if I would meet her either in this life or in the next. But do not mistake me: my life is God’s, and I promise not to cast it away rashly.”

“What would you do, then?” Amyas asked, low.

“Go up to that house, Amyas, and speak with her, if Heaven gives me an opportunity—as Heaven, I feel assured, will give.”

“And do you call that no rashness?”

“Is any duty rashness? Is it rash to stand amid flying bullets if your queen has sent you? Is it more rash to seek Christ’s lost lamb if God and your own oath hath sent you? John Brimblecombe answered that question for us long ago.”

“If you go, I go with you!” said all three at once—Amyas, Cary, and Jack.

“No. Amyas, you owe duty to our mother and to your ship. Cary, you are heir to great estates, and are bound thereby to your country and your tenants. John Brimblecombe—”

“Ay!” squeaked Jack, hurt in his pride. “And what have you to say, Mr. Frank, against my going? I, who have neither ship nor estates—except, I suppose, that I am not worthy to travel in such good company?”

“Think of your old parents, John, and all your sisters.”

“I thought of them before I started, sir, as Mr. Cary knows, and you know too. I came here to keep my vow, and I am not going to turn renegade at the very foot of the cross.”

“Some one must go with you, Frank,” said Amyas, and his voice faltered in spite of him; “if it were only to bring back the boat’s crew in case—”

“In case I fall,” Frank said quietly, with a smile that made the cabin seem colder. “I will finish your sentence for you, lad. I am not afraid of it, though you may be for me. Yet some one, I fear, must go. Unhappy me, that I cannot risk my own worthless life without risking your more precious lives!”

“Not so, Mr. Frank!” said John. “Your oath is our oath, and your duty ours. I will tell you what we will do, gentlemen all. We three will draw cuts for the honour of going with him.”

“Lots?” Amyas said. “I don’t like leaving such grave matters to chance, friend John.”

“Chance, sir?” John answered, lifting his small head as if he listened beyond the ship’s walls to something larger than the sea. “When you have used all your own wit, and find it fail you, then what is drawing lots but taking the matter out of your own weak hands, and laying it in God’s strong hands?”

When the sun sprang, as it did in those latitudes, at one bound from night into a glaring, merciless day, the Rose was already thundering close-hauled through a heaving sea. Amyas Leigh paced the deck like a man pursued—hair blown into ropes, coat torn, eyes raw with rage and salt tears—his grief not the quiet kind that sinks, but the kind that staggers and strikes at shadows. He spoke aloud to no one and to all: he would go back; he would burn the villa; he would take La Guayra and pay blood for blood.

“We can do it, lads!” he cried hoarsely. “If Drake took Nombre de Dios, we can take La Guayra.”

A roar answered him, fierce as hunger. “Yes!”

“We will have it, Amyas, and have Frank too, yet,” cried Cary; but Amyas only shook his head, as if some sure knowledge—he could not have told whence it came—had settled, cold and heavy, under his ribs: that all the ports of New Spain could never give him back one face.

He stood a moment staring toward the coast, where the green mountains rose sharp as knives behind the shore haze. Then his hand went out.

“There—there is the first crop of our vengeance.”

Between them and the now-distant shoulders of the Silla three sails showed, white as gulls’ wings and yet as ill-omened as carrion birds, creeping up to windward.

“There are the Spanish bloodhounds on our heels—the same ships we saw yesterday off Guayra. Back, lads, and welcome them, if they were a dozen.”

The older men answered with a cheer that made the younger swallow their first misgiving. Salvation Yeo’s great voice rolled over all, as steady as a bell in a storm.

“If there were a dozen, the Lord is with us, who hath said, ‘One of you shall chase a thousand.’ Clear away, lads, and see the glory of the Lord this day.”

“Amen!” cried Cary; and the Rose was kept closer yet into the wind, her timbers complaining as she climbed each sea and slid down again with a hiss of foam.

The sight of battle worked in Amyas like strong drink. He no longer felt wounds, nor the weight of the night behind him. Even Frank’s last look—so bright, so unearthly—went dim at the edges as the familiar needs of ship and fight came crowding in. In a quarter of an hour his voice rang firm again, as of old.

“Now, my masters, let us serve God, and then to breakfast, and after that clear for action.”

Jack Brimblecombe read the daily prayers and the prayers before fight at sea; and his honest voice shook when, in the Prayer for all Conditions of Men, he added—against the very grain of Amyas’s despair—“and especially for our dear brother Mr. Francis Leigh, perhaps captive among the idolaters.” They rose from their knees, the boards under them smelling of tar and wet rope and yesterday’s powder-smoke.

“Now then,” said Amyas, “to breakfast. A Frenchman fights best fasting, a Dutchman drunk, an Englishman full, and a Spaniard when the devil is in him—and that’s always.”

“And good beef and the good cause are a match for the devil,” said Cary. “Come down, captain; you must eat too.”

Amyas would not stir. He took the tiller from the steersman and sent him below with a sharp nod. But Cary, who knew how a body fails when the belly is empty, was down and up again in five minutes with a plate and a jack—brown bread already damped by the sea air, and great slices of salted beef, the fat edged with last night’s cold, and a draught of ale that smelled of malt and old oak. He coaxed it into Amyas as one forces food into a child that has cried itself sick. Then he scuttled below again, his own tears dropping into his beard where no man might see.

Amyas stood steering, chewing without tasting, the hard salt of the beef on his tongue like penance. His face had aged seven years in a night; the grief had set into it like a scar. A calm had come over him—not peace, but the stillness of a drawn sword.

“There are three of them, you see, my masters,” he said when the men were again on deck. “A big ship forward, and two galleys astern of her. The big ship may keep; she is a race ship, and if we can but recover the wind of her, we will see whether our height is not a match for her length. We must give her the slip, and take the galleys first.”

“I thank the Lord,” said Yeo, eyes bright beneath his weathered brow, “who has given so wise a heart to so young a general; a very David and Daniel, saving his presence. And if any dare not follow him, let him be as the men of Meroz and of Succoth. Amen! Silas Staveley—smite me that boy over the head, the young monkey; why is he not down at the powder-room door?”

And Yeo went to his guns as to a sacrament, with a terrible certainty that this, too, was work laid on him.

There was not much to mend; the Rose had slept, as it were, with her teeth bared. Yet there were always a hundred small tasks that made the difference between living and dying: decks cleared; nettings laced; bulwarks strengthened; waist-cloths fitted; tops armed; pikes tallowed so hands would not stick with salt and blood; yards slung; sheets and tacks doubled. The ship’s ordinary sounds—block and tackle creak, mallet taps, the rasp of rope—made their own rough music, and over it the sea’s breathing and the wind’s thin whistle through the rigging. In such a soundscape men held their hearts steady; silence bred imagining.

Amyas took the poop; Cary the forecastle; Yeo the main-deck; Drew the waist. All was ready, and more than ready, before the big ship was within two miles.

Her golden flag floated at her poop. Her trumpets shouted defiance up the breeze from a dozen brazen throats. The Rose answered from her own, and the flags told their tale plain: England; and at the fore, Leigh and Cary side by side, with the ship and bridge of Bideford above them, as if home itself had come to sea to watch.

“Now, silence trumpets,” cried Amyas. “Waits, play up! ‘Fortune my foe!’ And God and the Queen be with us!”

So the music rose—cornet and sackbut, fife and drum—and Parson Jack, planted among the players on the poop, worked away at his violin as if it were a weapon.

“Well played, Jack; thy elbow flies like a lamb’s tail,” said Amyas, forcing a jest across the hard place in his throat.

“It shall fly to a better fiddle-bow presently, sir, an I have the luck—”

“Steady, helm!” Amyas snapped. “What is he after now?”

The Spaniard, who had been driving down upon them under a press of sail, took in light canvas.

“He don’t know what to make of our waiting for him so bold,” said the helmsman.

“He does though, and means to fight us,” cried another. “See—he is hauling up the foot of his mainsail, but he wants to keep the wind of us.”

“Let him try, then,” quoth Amyas. “Keep her closer still. Let no one fire till we are about. Man the starboard guns; to starboard and wait, all small-arm men. Pass the order down to the gunner: fire high, and take the rigging.”

One of the Spaniard’s bow guns spoke; the shot went wide. Then another, and another—angry, impatient cracks, the sound carried off by wind and sea. The men of the Rose fingered primings and match-cords; the archers loosened arrows in the sheaves, the goose-feather fletching damp with spray.

“Lie down, men, and sing a psalm,” said Amyas. “When I want you, I’ll call you. Closer still, if you can. We will try a short ship against a long one. We can sail two points nearer the wind than he.”

He had read the Spaniard’s mind. The great race ship would gladly have crossed the Rose’s bows, but dared not, knowing the English hunger for a rake. If he did not mean to shoot past to leeward, he must come up close and wait on the same tack.

Amyas smiled without mirth. Hold, hold—there are more ways of killing a cat than choking her with cream.

They closed until the enemy filled the eye—timbers, men, painted sternwork, the flash of a breastplate. Then Amyas’s voice cut like an axe.

“Ready about!”

The Rose went about like an eel. Her yards creaked, her canvas shivered, and she ran upon the opposite tack—swift, clean, insolent—under the Spaniard’s stern. The Spaniard hesitated, astonished at the quickness; then tried to follow, lumbering as a cart in a narrow lane. Too late. His long body hung in the wind’s eye, helpless, while the Rose’s bowsprit all but scraped his quarter and she slid across his stern at ten yards.

“Now then!” roared Amyas. “Fire, and with a will! Have at her, archers! Have at her, muskets all!”

Bar and chain-shot, round and canister swept from stem to stern. Through the smoke musket-balls hissed, and the cloth-yard arrows—silent, cruel—drove their way into flesh and wood. The steersman and all who crowded the poop went down. The mizzen topmast snapped; stern-windows and quarter-galleries burst inward. The gorgeous painting of the Madre Dolorosa on the enemy’s stern—heart pierced with seven swords, gilded and bedizened—shivered into splinters. And, crowning all, the golden flag of Spain, which had flaunted so proudly an instant before, drooped and trailed in the sea.

Her tiller was shot away; her helmsman lay dead. She staggered like a blinded beast and fell up into the wind.

“Well done, men of Devon!” shouted Amyas as cheers tore the air.

“She has struck!” cried some.

“Not a bit,” said Amyas. “Hold on, helmsman. Let her patch her tackle while we settle the galleys.”

Away they shot, and long before the great ship could right herself, the Rose was two good miles to windward, with the galleys sweeping down fast.

They were venomous craft, those galleys—long and low, slicing the short chopping sea on forty oars apiece, their beaks like sword-fish snouts nosing forward. A strong square forecastle was packed with soldiers; cannon muzzles grinned not only from the sides but straight ahead, so that a ship before them might be hammered without respite. Down the waist were the slaves—five, six to an oar—half naked, sun-browned, their backs striped and shining. Between the banks ran a narrow gangway, and along it the drivers paced, whips in hand.

Now the Rose’s men heard them: the roll and rattle of oars, the hoarse “Ha!” that came with every stroke; the crack of lashes; the answering yells, not human so much as driven out of humanity; the curses of the drivers. And over all came a stench on the wind—musky, rank, like a kennel where hounds have been shut too long in heat and filth—so that more than one young sailor turned sick, not from fear but from pity and loathing. Even the sea’s own clean salt could not master it.

“Must we fire upon the slaves?” someone muttered, and more than one thought the same, for who knew what tongues were among those panting wretches—what English cries swallowed by the lash?

Amyas drew breath as if it hurt.

“Spare them all you can, in God’s name. But if they try to run us down, rake them we must, and God forgive us.”

The two galleys came abreast, forty yards apart. Amyas knew well he could not out-turn their oars as he had out-witted canvas; to run was to be caught between them and the big ship astern. So he chose, as he often did, the desperate road.

“Lay her head up in the wind, helmsman. We will wait for them.”

They opened with their bow-guns; but the sea’s chop threw their aim awry, and the balls went wide, slapping water and skipping off. Amyas held his own fire. The men stood at quarters, lips pressed tight, hearing every sound—the creak of leather, the clink of a buckle, the breathing of their mates—because when the guns are silent the world’s small noises grow loud and sharp.

The Spaniards, seeing him wait, shouted with joy. Mad Englishman! The galleys converged, meaning to strike him full, one on each bow.

Within forty yards—another minute and the shock would come—Amyas’s helm went up; the yards swung; the Rose gathered way and plunged straight upon the larboard galley.

“A dozen gold nobles to him who brings down the steersman!” shouted Cary from the forecastle, knowing his moment.

A flight of arrows hissed and rattled upon the galley’s quarter-deck.

Hit or no, the steersman flinched at the oncoming bulk. The galley sheered; her helm went up; her beak slid almost harmlessly along the Rose’s bow. There came a long dull grind, then crack on crack—hideous as breaking bones—as the Rose sawed through the bank of oars from stem to stern. Oars snapped like dry sticks; slaves were hurled in heaps; the yells rose in a wave that made English hearts clench.

Before the other galley could swing to strike in the Rose’s new position, Amyas had poured his whole broadside—great and small—into her at pistol-shot. The answer was a scream of rage and agony that seemed to tear the day.

“Spare the slaves! Fire at the soldiers!” cried Amyas; but the work was too hot for nice choosing. The crippled larboard galley, brave as a mad dog, swung across the Rose’s stern and hooked herself on.

It was bold, and folly with it; for it checked the other galley from returning without offering herself again to the English broadside. Spaniards tried to board through stern-ports and up the quarter; but they were met with such a storm of steel and shot that in three minutes they were flung back upon their own poop—and with them, to their disgust, came Amyas Leigh and twenty English swords.

There followed five minutes of hard cutting, hand to hand. The Rose’s lofty stern gave her men an advantage; arrows and muskets from above raked the galley’s open decks, and the forecastle soldiers could scarce lift a head without paying for it. Amyas fought as if grief had made him weightless—his blade rising and falling with a dreadful steadiness—until the poop was clear.

Then he drove along the central gangway, shouting in Spanish, his words cutting through the lash-cracks and the slaves’ “Ha!” like a new kind of thunder.

“Freedom to the slaves! Death to the masters!”

He clambered into the forecastle, his men swarming after him like wasps; and he showed them so hot an example how to sting that in three minutes more there was not a Spaniard aboard who was not dead or dying.

“Let the slaves free!” he shouted. “Throw us a hammer down, men. Hark—there’s an English voice!”

And indeed there was. From amid the wreck of broken oars and writhing limbs, a voice shrieked out—broad Devon, as plain as a hearth in winter—up to the Rose’s master looking over the side.

“Oh, Robert Drew! Robert Drew! Come down, and take me out of hell!”

“Who be you, in the name of the Lord?” cried Drew, staring as if at a ghost.

“Don’t you mind William Prust, that Captain Hawkins left behind in the Honduras, years and years agone? There’s nine of us aboard, if your shot hasn’t put ’em out of their misery. Come down, if you’ve a Christian heart—come down!”

All discipline went from Drew like a cloak flung off. Hammer in hand, he leapt down; and the two old comrades, amid blood, splintered oars, and the raw stink of the galley, caught each other fast in their arms as if the sea itself had turned in a circle and brought the past home again.

“Follow thee? Follow thee? Wha wad na follow thee? Lang hast thou looed and trusted us fairly.”

If ever a man might have been forgiven for falling sick, it was Amyas Leigh. The hot land had a way of seeping through cloth and skin alike, creeping in as noiselessly as damp into a ship’s biscuit. Yet he did not take the yellow fever then, and the one reason he gave Cary was reason enough for those who have carried others when their own knees shook.

“I have no leisure to be ill while my men lie ill.”

So long as there was work—lifting, hauling, ordering, watching—his blood kept its pace like a drum that will not slacken for pain. But he knew, as all who have driven themselves hard know, that the moment the labor ceased, the body might claim its due with interest.

Next morning he called, not so much a council of war as a desperate inquiry into how to keep men alive. Below-decks the air seemed thickened—close, sour, tainted as if a great pot had been left to stand and turn, and all the ship were forced to breathe its spoiled steam. Above, the crew were wild with fear, that sharp-edged fear which makes men talk of mutiny as readily as they talk of supper.

Amyas faced them first, plain and fierce.

“I cannot see what profit can come to you by killing me,” he told them, “or”—and he let his hand fall meaningly to his sword—“being killed by me.”

Then he went below, where the voices of his chief men rose and fell like different instruments playing tunes that would not meet.

The doctor had his learning ready, as if learning were broth that could be ladled hot into a dying man. He spoke of humors and complexions and animal spirits, naming invisible things while the visible men shook with ague.

Jack Brimblecombe, who could find a text for any disaster, called it God’s visitation.

Cary smiled and jested with a dryness that was almost despair; a man will sometimes crack a walnut on the edge of his own coffin.

Yeo stood like an old oak, fatal as winter, buttressing himself with Scripture as if words could nail down fate.

Drew, the master, would not be drawn beyond his own craft.

“My business is to sail the ship,” said he, “and not to cure calentures.”

Amyas, at his wits’ end, caught at his hair till he seemed like to tear it out, and then flung his anger round the room in a blaze.

“Doctor! a fig for your humors and complexions! Can you cure a man’s humors, or change his complexion? Can an Ethiopian change his skin, or a leopard his spots? Don’t shove off your ignorance on God, sir. I ask you what’s the reason of this sickness, and you don’t know.

“Jack Brimblecombe, don’t preach God’s visitation at me; this looks more like the devil’s visitation. We are doing God’s work, and He is not likely to hinder us. Down with the devil, say I.

“Cary, laughing killed the cat, but it won’t cure a Christian.

“Yeo, when an angel tells me it’s God’s will we should all die like dogs in a ditch, I’ll call it God’s will; but not before.

“And you, Drew—if your business is to sail the ship, then sail her out of this infernal poison-trap this very morning, if you can—which you can’t. The mischief is in the air, and nowhere else. I felt it run through me coming down last night, and smelt it like any sewer: and if it was not in the air, why was my boat’s crew taken first, tell me that?”

Silence answered him. There are moments when even the boldest tongues grow blank.

Then Amyas, as if he had seized the truth by the throat, spoke on, quick and hard.

“I’ll tell you why they were taken first: because the mist only rose five or six feet above the stream, and we were in it, while you on board were above it. And those that were taken on board this morning—every one slept on the main-deck; and every one was in fear of the fever. Whereby I judge two things—Keep as high as you can, and fear nothing but God, and we’re all safe yet.”

“But the fog was up to our round-tops at sunrise,” Cary answered.

“I know it. But we on the half-deck were not in it so long as those below, and we had freer air. Besides—I suspect the heat in the evening draws the poison out more, and when it gets cold toward morning, the venom goes off somehow.”

How that venom “went off” no man there could tell; they had no neat rule for it, only the harsh schooling of suffering. Yet common sense sat on Amyas’s side; and old Yeo, remembering words he had heard long ago, rose at last.

“As I have said before,” quoth he, “the Lord hath sent us a very young Daniel for judge. I mind me now how Spaniards say these calentures lie always in the low ground, and never come more than a few hundred feet above the sea.”

“Then let us go up those few hundred feet.”

They stared at him—at one another. They had expected orders about swabbing decks and boiling vinegar and bleeding men. They had not expected to be turned loose upon a jungle.

“Gentlemen,” said Amyas, “look the devil straight in the face, if you would hit him in the right place. We cannot get the ship to sea as she is; and if we could, we cannot go home empty-handed; and we surely cannot stay here to die of fever. We must leave the ship and go inland.”

“Inland?” the voices rose together, save only Yeo’s, who kept his assent in his eyes.

“Up to the mountains; stockade a camp, and get our sick and provisions thither.”

“And what next?” someone demanded.

“And when we are recruited, march over the mountains, and surprise St. Jago de Leon.”

Cary let fly an oath, half admiration, half incredulity.

“Amyas! You are a daring fellow!”

“Not a bit,” said Amyas. “It is the plain path of prudence.”

“So it is, sir,” Yeo replied, steady as a ploughman measuring a field, “and I follow you in it.”

“And so do I,” squeaked Brimblecombe, eager as if salvation lay up-hill.

“Nay then, Jack, thou shalt not outrun me. So I say yes too,” said Cary.

Amyas turned to Drew.

“At your service, sir, to live or die,” said the master. “I know naught about stockading; but Sir Francis would have given the same counsel, I verily believe, if he had been in your place.”

“Then tell the men we start in an hour. Win over the Pelicans, Yeo and Drew; the rest must follow like sheep over a hedge.”

The Pelicans, and the liberated galley-slaves, came in at once; they had tasted Spanish chains and feared fever less than another hand on the collar. But the Rose’s crew gave Amyas a stormy hour, fretting and accusing in the same breath. The mangrove thicket, dense as woven rope, shut out all sight, and the great question arose: where were the hills at all?

“The hills are not three miles to the south-west of you at this moment,” said Amyas. “I marked every shoulder of them as we ran in.”

“I suppose you meant to take us there?” one cried; and that question, like a spark, lit all the hidden tinder of suspicion.

Amyas stamped it out with sheer force.

“Fools! If I had not wit enough to look ahead farther than you do, where would you be? Are you mad as well as reckless, to rise against your own captain because he has two strings to his bow? Go my way, I say, or, as I live, I’ll blow up the ship and every soul on board, and save you the pain of rotting here by inches.”

They knew the tone; Amyas was not a man to threaten air. He did not mean to do it—he meant them to believe he would—and belief was enough. The sight of the Pelicans setting to work with willing hands shamed the rest into obedience.

So they went. And if there is any mercy in leaving misery undescribed, let it be here. For five or six hours under a sun that did not merely burn but stewed—like a cauldron kept always at the boil—they stumbled over mangrove roots slick as wet bone, hewed through thorn that caught clothes and skin alike, dragged sick men whose sweat had turned sour with fear, hauled casks and sacks that grew heavier with every step, and fought the small torments of that land—snakes that slid away with a whisper, mosquitoes that sang triumph at each bare wrist, false alarms of Spaniards that made the heart leap and the breath go thin. The forest was never silent: it clicked and creaked and murmured, a thousand little lives rasping and droning, and beneath them the steady, thick hush of heat, like a great ear pressed close to listen for weakness.

By sunset, they had gained a level place a full thousand feet above the sea, backed by a cliff so sheer it might have been God’s own wall, and guarded below by steep wooded slopes. A few trees felled in the right place would make it, to all but artillery, unassailable.

Amyas settled the sick under the arched roots of an enormous cottonwood—roots not so much roots as buttresses, like the ribs of a cathedral turned inside-out. The air up there was different; it moved. It tasted of green things crushed fresh, of water over stone, instead of the lowland reek that had clung to the men like an unwashed garment.

He made a second journey down toward the ship for hammocks and blankets, while Yeo’s quiet strength held the company together. It had been Yeo who found the brook, and then, like a man who has followed streams in many countries, drove them to keep its course, arguing they would find water and flatter ground where water chooses to linger. Again and again, when men begged to be let lie down and be done, he went back, speaking to them as one speaks to oxen that must plod another furrow: not cruelty, but necessity.

Cary brought up the rear like a cheerful tyrant—bullying, rallying, snatching burdens from faint hands, and singing on halts in a voice that made the weary lift their heads as if sound itself could feed them. Once or twice he drove back at sword-point those who tried to slink away downhill, and then laughed at them, calling them cowards to keep them from thinking themselves dying men. There is a kind of hopefulness which is not blindness but defiance, and Cary had it.

That night, with the camp quieting to groans and low talk, Amyas, beside himself with a fierce joy that the two men he trusted most were true, leaned close to Yeo and Cary and whispered what had grown in him all day like a seed in hot soil.

“Cortez burnt his ships when he landed. Why should not we?”

Yeo sprang upright as if struck by lightning, then sank down again, whispering, eyes alight under his grey brows.

“Do you say that, captain? ’Tis from above then, that’s certain; for it hath been hanging on my mind too all day.”

“There’s no hurry,” Amyas answered. “We must clear her out first, you know.”

Cary said nothing. He stared into the dark as if seeing ships burn there already, and other, stranger fires beyond.

By dawn Amyas had them fortifying. The place was strong by nature; high cliffs hemmed it, and the mountain-range behind was so vast and tangled that no enemy would come over it, and if he did, he would never find a handful of men hidden under that roof of trees. Attack must come from below.

Against that, Amyas had smaller trees felled and laid with boughs outward over the crest of the slope, a snarling hedge of timber that would stop man or beast in two steps. Trunks were sawn into logs, set lengthwise, steadied by stakes and packed earth; and in three or four hours’ savage labor they had a stockade that would laugh at anything but cannon.

When it was done, Amyas scrambled up into the boughs of the huge ceiba-tree that dominated the place and took his seat there like a watchman in a living tower. From that height the world spread out—forest like a green sea, mangrove-swamps like a darker stain, and beyond them the bright blue of open water, sheeted with white. The sounds came up too: the steady insect-hum like an unseen wheel, bird-calls sharp as whistles, distant surf with its unending breath. For a moment he let it fill him, as if sound itself might keep his thoughts from tearing him.

To stay long was impossible; to avenge himself on La Guayra was impossible; to go without knowing whether Frank was alive seemed at first impossible too. But was he to sacrifice Brimblecombe, Cary, and eighty men again to his private grief? That question wrung him until rage and prayer came one upon the other, and he wept for both.

At last he cried aloud, as if the forest must hear him: “God help him! for I cannot!” And his mind turned like a compass needle, fixing on duty.

He went down calm, and drew Cary and Yeo aside. They could not refit the ship without dying of fever in the doing; neither dared deny it. Even if they refitted her, they would likely fight Spaniards again; the Indian’s warning had been true enough, and there was no doubting now that the enemy had been forewarned. Nor was there any doubt—though Amyas spoke it with a bitterness that tasted like iron—that Eustace had been the traitor.

“Let us try St. Jago then,” said Amyas. “Sack it, come down on La Guayra in the rear, take a ship there, and so get home.”

“Nay, Will,” Cary answered, and for all his daring his voice had the tone of a man tasting a broth and finding poison. “If they have strengthened themselves against us at La Guayra, where they had little to lose, surely they have done so at St. Jago, where they have much. I hear the town is large, though new; and besides, how can we get over these mountains without a guide?”

“Or with one?” said Cary, with a sigh, looking up at the wall of wood and rock range upon range. “But it is strange to find you, at least, throwing cold water on a daring plot.”

Amyas’s eyes narrowed, and he spoke lower.

“What if I had a still more daring one? Did you ever hear of the golden city of Manoa?”

Yeo gave a grim, joyful laugh.

“I have, sir; and so have the old hands from the Pelican and the Jesus of Lubec, I doubt not.”

“So much the better.” And Amyas told Cary what he had learned from the Spaniard, while Yeo, like an elder repeating half-forgotten harvest-lore, set rumor upon rumor beside it—tales gathered in taverns and on decks at night, names passed mouth to mouth until the sound of them carried more weight than truth. Cary sat half aghast as that glittering phantasm grew—golden city, far rivers, strange nations—as if the jungle itself were making promises.

At last Cary shook his head, half laughing, half sober.

“So that was why you wanted to burn the ship. Well—after all, nobody needs me at home, and one less at table won’t be missed. So you want to play Cortez, eh?”

“We shall never need to play Cortez,” said Amyas, “because we shall have no such cannibal fiends’ tyranny to rid the earth of as he had. And I trust we shall fear God enough not to play Pizarro.”

So it dropped for the time; but none of them forgot.

In that mountain nook they lay ten days and more. Several died—some from fever laid atop wounds, some, likely, because the surgeon would have had blood for everything, as if a man were a cask to be tapped till the bad liquor ran out. He was all but mutinous when Amyas stayed his hand; but Amyas would not let him drain what little strength remained.

The others mended steadily, helped by certain herbs Yeo administered with the air of an old husbandman who knows what leaf cools a swelling and what bark steadies a shaking stomach. The doctor scowled, for it galled him to see plants do what his lancet could not.

By daily trips down to the ship, provisions were fetched up, so that they ate better than despair might have warranted. There were raccoons and monkeys and other small beasts which Yeo and Hawkins’s veterans knew how to snare. There was fruit, and vegetables; above all the tender mountain cabbage of the Areca palm, cut with care lest the heart be bruised, then boiled till it yielded like marrow, and eaten with salt like something stolen from a king’s own table. And there was the fresh milk of the cow-tree, white and thick, drawn not from beast but from bark, and warming in the mouth with a faint wild sweetness, as if the forest itself had learned the craft of dairying. They paid well for what they took, and the land, if it cannot love, at least did not refuse them.

All day a watch was kept from the branches of the mighty ceiba. And what a tree it was—an empire of wood. The hugest English oak would have looked a stunted hedge beside it. Its roots rose like twisted boards, walls twelve feet high, between which the whole crew might shelter with ammunition and stores. Above that, the trunk, forty feet in girth, soared smooth for a hundred feet, then burst into boughs so vast that each limb was itself a stately tree. The topmost twigs were two hundred and fifty feet from the ground.

Yet it was easy to climb, for the lianes hung down like ropes set there for men’s hands—smooth cords trailing to earth, often without knot or leaf. Once up, you were in a world between earth and heaven. Cary joked that it was like Jack’s beanstalk: somewhere amid that tangle a giant might keep his castle and his park.

Gardens there were, truly enough. Pendent cactuses drooped like chandeliers; orchises flamed in strange colors; wild pines clung and flowered. One half of the tree was in rich foliage; the other half, leafless, carried brilliant yellow blossoms on every twig, about which hummingbirds whirred like living sparks. Parrots peeped and screamed in the hollows. Lizards basked on bark like jewels made flesh. Gaudy finches flitted and chirruped. Butterflies hovered on the upper air. Insects hummed from morn till eve with that ceaseless music of the tropics—so lovely at first, and then so pressing, so inescapable, that a man might long for one honest silence. At sunset tree-toads came out to croak and snore till dawn, their chorus thick and throaty, as if the night itself were breathing.

There was more life round that one tree than in a whole square mile of English soil; and Amyas, lounging among its branches, felt sometimes he could stay there forever, fed by sound and sight alone. Then he would start, remembering that a few days might bring the foe, and force him to a choice at which the stoutest heart might falter without shame.

So he watched—often taking the scout’s place himself—over the forest at his feet, the flat mangrove-swamps below, and the blue sea beyond. No sail appeared for days. As fear of fever ebbed, the men began to ask when they would go down and refit the ship. Amyas put them off as best he could, keeping his deeper purpose in his own breast.

Until, one noon, he saw a large ship creeping along the shore from the westward under easy sail. He recognized her—or believed he did—the vessel they had passed on their way.

If it were she, she must have run past them to La Guayra by night and now returned, perhaps searching the coast.

He watched, hoping she might glide past the river mouth and be gone. But she lay-to close to shore. After a while two boats pulled in from her and vanished behind the mangroves.

Sliding down a liane, he told the camp. The men, sick of waiting, shouted with joy and set to work as if battle were a feast promised after fasting.

Four brass swivels they had brought up were mounted and fixed in logs to command the path. Musketeers and archers clustered round with matches and tackle ready. Half a dozen good marksmen volunteered up into the cotton-tree with their arquebuses, bragging that from there “a man might have very pretty shooting.” Prayers followed as a matter of course; dinner too, as if war and eating were the two hinges on which their days turned. Yet two weary hours dragged by with no sign.

Then a wreath of white smoke curled up from the swamp, and the crack of a caliver followed. Amid English growls, the Spanish flag rose above the trees—and floated, horrible to behold, at the masthead of the Rose. They were signaling their ship for more hands; and indeed a third boat soon pushed off and vanished into the forest.

Another hour of waiting, during which men paced, muttering loud enough to betray themselves. Amyas hushed them sharply. The Spaniards might never find the track; they might pass the stockade within yards and see nothing; if they missed it, they would likely return for the present. He exacted a promise: perfect silence until he gave the word.

Hardly had the promise been wrung from them when a helmet glanced in the path below—then another, then another.

“Fools,” whispered Amyas to Cary. “They come up in single file, rushing on their own death. Lie close, men!”

The path was narrow; two could seldom walk abreast. It was steep enough that men stumbled as they climbed, their armor clinking softly, the sound swallowed by leaves. They seemed half unwilling, halting more than once; but Amyas heard an authoritative voice behind. Presently a figure pushed forward, sword in hand, and at the sight of him both Amyas and Cary started as if the air had turned suddenly cold.

“Is it he?”

“Surely,” whispered Cary. “I know those legs among a thousand, though they be in armor.”

“It is my turn for him now, Cary, remember. Silence—silence, men!”

The Spaniards reached within forty yards of the stockade and paused, suspecting a trap, puzzled by the complete stillness. Amyas sprang atop the stockade with a white flag in his hand; but his heart hammered so fiercely at the sight of that hated man that the words fought him.

“Don Guzman! The quarrel is between you and me, not between your men and mine. I would have sent you a challenge at La Guayra, but you were away. I challenge you now to single combat.”

Don Guzman’s answer came like a lash.

“Lutheran dog, I have a halter for you, but no sword! As you served us at Smerwick, we will serve you now. Pirate and ravisher, you and yours shall share Oxenham’s fate, as you have copied his crimes, and learn what it is to set foot unbidden on the dominions of the king of Spain.”

“The devil take you and the king of Spain together!” shouted Amyas, laughing loud. “This ground belongs to him no more than it does to me, but to Queen Elizabeth, in whose name I have taken lawful possession of it as you ever did of Caracas. Fire, men! and God defend the right!”

Both sides answered with steel and flame. Amyas dropped behind the stockade just in time; a bullet whistled over the timber. The English line burst into one blaze of muskets and swivels, raking the Spaniards from front to rear. In that cramped file the front ranks fell over each other in heaps; the rear turned and ran, tumbling down the steep path, chased by bullets and arrows that struck men into sudden silence.

“Out, men—charge them! See, the Don runs like the rest!” Amyas cried.

He scrambled over the abatis with about thirty at his heels, for he hoped to snatch a prisoner and wring from him news of Frank.

But Amyas was unjust in that bitter taunt. Don Guzman, by what seemed miracle, had been only slightly wounded. When his men broke, he rushed back to rally them, but the flood of fugitives bore him away.

The Spaniards vanished into thick bush before the English could overtake them. Amyas, fearing a rally and an ambush against so small a party, withdrew against his will.

In the path lay fourteen Spaniards—dead. One of the wounded had fired as he lay, and Amyas’s men, maddened by desperation and by the ghastly tales of the rescued galley-slaves, had slain them all before their captain could stop them.

“Are you mad?” cried Amyas, striking up a sword as it rose again. “Will you kill an Indian?”

He dragged from the bushes an Indian lad of sixteen, slightly wounded, crawling away like a copper snake along the ground. Someone cursed.

“The black vermin has sent an arrow through my leg—poisoned too, most like.”

“God grant not,” said Amyas. “But an Indian is worth his weight in gold to us now.”

He tucked the lad under his arm like a bundle. The boy, seeing escape was gone, resigned himself with a stoic stillness that seemed older than his years. They brought him in and treated him kindly enough, but he refused to eat. After much questioning he gave his reason in broken signs and fearful words: he meant to make them kill him at once, for fatten him they should not.

Little by little they understood. The Spaniards, it seemed, had told him that the English always fattened and ate their prisoners like the Caribs. And until he saw the English go out and bury the Spanish corpses, nothing would persuade him that those bodies were not meant to be cooked for supper.

My next passage is a bitter one; I will make it no longer than the truth demands. Yet it must be set down, that those who read may judge, in fairness, what manner of enemies Englishmen had to meet in those iron years.

Three weeks have slipped away, and the world has shifted—as a pot is lifted from one hearth and set upon another—into a long, low corridor of cells in Cartagena, where the air is old, sour, and close, and every sound is magnified. The drip of moisture has a metronome’s patience; a rat’s scratch on stone is as loud as a file. Even the lamp’s small flame seems to make a noise in that darkness, with its thin, oily hiss and its smell of tallow and smoke.

The door of one cell stands open. Within are two cloaked figures. One we know: Eustace Leigh. The other is a familiar of the Holy Office, and in his hand he carries the lamp, holding it so that the light falls upon a bed of straw—flattened and fouled by long use—and upon the sleeping figure of a man.

The high white brow, the pale delicate features: we know them too. They are Frank’s.

Saved half-dead from the fury of savage negroes, he has been kept for cruelties more finely measured—cruelties practiced by civilized and Christian men, with pen, seal, and prayer. He had undergone the question that afternoon; and now Eustace, his betrayer, is come to persuade him—or to ensnare him. Eustace himself hardly knows which. And yet he would give his life to save his cousin.

His life? He has long since ceased to care for that. He has done what he has done because it was called duty; and now he is to do duty once more: to wake the sleeper, and argue, coax, threaten him into recantation while “his heart is still tender from the torture,” as Eustace’s employers phrase it, as coolly as if they spoke of softening beans in a pot, or kneading a stubborn dough.

And yet how calmly he is sleeping.

Is it only the lamp’s trick, trembling on Frank’s mouth, or is there a smile upon his lips? Eustace takes the lamp himself and bends over him to see; and as he bends he hears Frank whispering, in his dreams, his mother’s name—and then a name higher and holier still. The sound is so human, so homely, that for a moment the cell is not a cell, but a darkened room in England where a boy speaks in his sleep and the house listens, and the night wind outside is kind.

Eustace cannot find the heart to wake him.

“Let him rest,” he whispers to his companion. “After all, I fear my words will be of little use.”

“I fear so too, sir. Never did I behold a more obdurate heretic. He did not scruple to scoff openly at their holinesses.”

“Ah!” said Eustace; “great is the pravity of the human heart, and the power of Satan! Let us go for the present.”

“Where is she?”

“The elder sorceress, or the younger?”

“The younger—the—”

“The Señora de Soto? Ah, poor thing! One could be sorry for her, were she not a heretic.”

And the man eyed Eustace keenly, as if tasting his tone for any trace of softness, then added, quietly, “She is at present with the notary; to the benefit of her soul, I trust—”

Eustace half stopped, shuddering. He could hardly gather himself enough to gasp an “Amen!”

“Within there,” said the familiar, pointing carelessly to a door as they went down the corridor. “We can listen a moment, if you like; but don’t betray me, señor.”

Eustace knew well enough that the fellow was likely watching him even as he spoke, ready to report to his superiors the least sign of pity for a heretic. Still, a horrible curiosity overcame fear. He paused close to the fatal door.

His face burned; his knees knocked together; the ringing in his ears grew until it seemed the whole passage had become a shell held to the sea. His heart beat so violently that he felt it against the wall as he leaned there, hiding his convulsed face as best he could from his companion. In that place, even breath was loud. Even silence had a weight and a voice.

A man’s voice was plainly audible within—low, but distinct. The notary, patient as a butcher sharpening a blade, was trying that old charge of witchcraft, which inquisitors, whether to ease their consciences or to whiten their villainy in the eyes of the mob, so often laid upon their victims.

Then Eustace’s heart sank clean through him as he heard a woman’s voice reply—sharpened by indignation and agony, as a knife is honed on a stone:

“Witchcraft against Don Guzman? What need of that, oh God! what need?”

“You deny it then, señora? We are sorry for you; but—”

A confused, choking murmur from the victim, mingled with words that might mean anything or nothing, as if pain itself had broken language into scraps, into sounds that would not translate even to mercy.

“She has confessed!” whispered Eustace. “Saints, I thank you!—she—”

A wail rang out, and it seemed to strike the corridor like a blow, and rebound from stone to bone. It was not merely heard; it entered him—through ear, through teeth, through the very marrow—until his thoughts were nothing but that one sound.

He would have torn at the door to open it; but his companion forced him away. Another wail followed, and another, and the familiar hurried him on. Eustace stopped his ears with his hands in vain; those piercing cries chased him like avenging angels through the dreadful vaults.

At last he escaped—out into fragrant open air and golden tropic moonlight, into a garden that might have served as a model for Eden. Night insects kept their delicate percussion; palms whispered with a soft, continual brush; somewhere water fell, steady and bright, making a music fit to lull the sick. The scent of crushed leaf and bruised blossom rose as if the earth were offering comfort like warm bread.

But man’s hell followed into God’s heaven. The wails still rang through his ears, blotting out the garden’s gentler sounds, drowning the natural world’s clean song with a manufactured anguish.

“Oh, misery, misery, misery!” he murmured to himself through grinding teeth; “and I have brought her to this! I have had to bring her to it! What else could I? Who dare blame me? And yet what devilish sin can I have committed, that requires to be punished thus? Was there no one to be found but me? No one? And yet it may save her soul. It may bring her to repentance!”

“It may, indeed,” said the familiar, “for she is delicate, and cannot endure much. You ought to know as well as I, señor, the merciful disposition of the Holy Office.”

“I know it, I know it,” interrupted poor Eustace, trembling now for himself. “All in love—all in love.—A paternal chastisement—”

“And the proofs of heresy are patent, beside the strong suspicion of enchantment, and the known character of the elder sorceress. You yourself—you must remember, señor—told us that she had been a notorious witch in England, before the señora brought her hither as her attendant.”

“Of course she was; of course. Yes; there was no other course open. And though the flesh may be weak, sir, in my case, yet none can have proved better to the Holy Office how willing is the spirit!”

So Eustace departed; and ere another sun had set, he had gone to the principal of the Jesuits, and told him his whole heart—or as much of it, poor wretch, as he dared tell even to himself. He entreated to be allowed to finish his novitiate and enter the order, on the understanding that he should be sent at once back to Europe, or anywhere else.

“Otherwise,” he said frankly, “I shall go mad, even if I am not mad already.”

The Jesuit—kindly enough in manner, as men may be kindly while serving a pitiless machine—went to the Holy Office and settled all with the inquisitors, recounting to them, to set Eustace above suspicion, Eustace’s past valiant services to the Church. His testimony was no longer needed. He left Cartagena for Nombre that very night, and sailed the next week—I know not whither.

I say, I know not whither. Eustace Leigh vanishes henceforth from these pages. He may have ended as General of his Order. He may have worn out his years in some tropic forest “conquering the souls” (including, of course, the bodies) of Indians. He may have gone back to his old work in England, and been the very Ballard who was hanged and quartered three years afterwards for his share in Babington’s villainous conspiracy. I know not.

This book is a history of men—of men’s virtues and sins, victories and defeats; and Eustace is a man no longer. He is become a thing, a tool, a Jesuit: which goes only where it is sent, and does good or evil indifferently as it is bid; which, by an act of moral suicide, has lost its soul in the hope of saving it; without a will, a conscience, a responsibility (as it fancies) to God or man, but only to “The Society.”

In a word, Eustace, as he says himself, is “dead.”

Twice dead, I fear.

Let the dead bury their dead. We have no more concern with Eustace Leigh.

Nearly three years had gone by since they had knelt at evensong under the great tree of Guayra—three years that left little behind them but broken trails, scattered graves, and a few blotted names caught up by rumor and mis-spelt by strange tongues. They had ranged, like a needle hunting a lost thread, across a world of hill and forest—eight hundred miles one way and four hundred the other—always with Manoa in their minds: the Golden City that an Indian tale had set shining on the dark loom of the wilderness.

They had followed the long green banks of the Orinoco; they had labored beyond the roaring whiteness of Maypures where the river seemed to gnash its teeth; they had pushed into the upper waters that fed the mighty Amazon. They had climbed streams into Peru and walked under cinchona trees at Loxa, not knowing—as no one then knew—that the bitter bark held mercy against fevers. They had seen Chimborazo lift its virgin snows above thundercloud, and Cotopaxi brood like a black altar before it spilled fire. Foiled behind the Andes, they had turned again and plunged from cold cliff into the “green and misty ocean of the Montana,” and there, mile by mile, had wormed northward along the eastern foot of the inland Cordillera, until at last they lay camped on one of the many feeders of the Meta—rivers born of the Suma Paz and sliding down into forested plains.

There, under enormous trees and a tropic moon, the watch-fires threw a reddish tremble on the silent stream. Amyas and Will Cary sat with Jack Brimblecombe and old Salvation Yeo; near them, the Indian lad who had followed their fortunes as faithfully as a dog followed a master, alive still, lean and bright-eyed. They were as far from Manoa as ever—its fairy lake, its golden palaces, its wonders—though rumor had so often pricked their hopes that they had started a score of times in fresh directions only to meet fresh emptiness. Hope deferred had made their hearts sick, and yet they had gone on, because stopping felt too like dying.

Four-and-forty men sat there out of the four-score and four who had left Guayra.

The rest were scattered as the forest scatters what it has once taken. Drew the master lay by the Rio Negro with five brave fellows slain under poisoned arrows when they tried to force the gorges of Parima. Two had frozen among Andean valleys beneath hail that came down like slate shards from the condor’s height. Four had been swallowed at an Orinoco rapid. Five or six wounded men had been left among friendly Indians at another, to be fetched if they could—perhaps never. Fever had bitten them month by month; snakes and jaguars had taken their toll; alligators, cannibal fish, electric eels—every water and thicket had its tax. Of their march no path remained, only lonely mounds where English hands had dug into a foreign earth.

Yet the living were not broken. Sun-dried and spare, they were tough as seasoned oak, with the quiet fire of English courage unquenched, and that stubborn cheer which can make a jest of toil because it cannot afford to yield to it. Their beards lay on their breasts; their long hair was knotted like women’s to shade the nape from burning noon. Leggings of guazu-puti deer-skin hugged their shins; their shirts were patched with Indian cotton web; jaguar and puma, ape and deer, hung from their shoulders in ragged trophies. Their powder was long gone; their muskets, spoiled by the steaming vapor-bath of the woods, had been abandoned in a cave by some cataract of the Orinoco, rusty and useless. But their swords were bright still, and in the forest’s half-light flashed like teeth. They carried bows no Indian arm could bend, and arrows pointed with the remnants of their armor; many had learned the silent deadliness of the pocuna blowgun. So they had become, by sheer need, half-Indian in gear while remaining wholly English in temper—terrible to hostile tribes, but just and generous to those who dealt straight. Many a smooth-chinned Carib or Ature, Solimo or Guahiba, would recount with wonder the righteousness of the bearded strangers who called themselves the enemies of Spaniards, and spoke of a great queen oversea who would avenge and deliver the oppressed.

Most of the men slept among the trees: some on the ground, some in grass hammocks slung between trunks. The night had its own cookery of sound. Now the heavy plunge of a tapir broke the river as he tore up water-weeds for his supper; now a jaguar, climbing from tree-top to tree-top, disturbed the monkeys, and the monkeys roused the birds, and for ten minutes the forest turned into a madness of roars, howls, shrieks, and cacklings, like a lid lifted on pandemonium. Then it sank again into that listening hush which is never empty—only so crowded with small lives that the ear can scarcely sort them. In such a place a man learned that silence was not the absence of sound, but a balance of it: the difference between a mind soothed by river and leaf and insect-hum, and a mind driven raw by the clangor men make when they try to drown their own thoughts.

Beside the watch-fire, the four held their council of war. They had held a hundred such, and failed; likely this too would fail. Yet it was more solemn than most, because the two years for which they had vowed to seek Manoa were long spent. Another path must be chosen, unless they meant to die as forest-ghosts, drifting forever under green ceilings.

“Well,” said Will Cary, drawing his cigar from his mouth, “at least we have got something out of those last Indians. It is a comfort to have a puff at tobacco once more, after three weeks’ fasting.”

The tobacco had come as all food had come to them now: by barter, by courtesy, by cunning, by hardship—leaf bundled in palm-fibre, smelling of smoke and earth and a sweetness that made the mouth water, as if it promised meat. Cary’s fingers were careful with it as with a spice, and the glowing end breathed a warm, resinous perfume into the damp night.

“For me,” said Jack Brimblecombe, “Heaven forgive me! but when I get the magical leaf between my teeth again, I feel tempted to sit as still as a chimney and smoke till my dying day, without stirring hand or foot.”

“Then I shall forbid you tobacco, Master Parson,” said Amyas, “for we must be up and away again to-morrow. We have been idling here three mortal days, and nothing done.”

“Shall we ever do anything?” Jack answered, and the firelight touched the hollows in his cheek. “I think the gold of Manoa is like the gold that lies where the rainbow touches the ground—always a field beyond you.”
