Amyas was silent awhile, and so were the rest. The river’s small noises—fish kissing the surface, some unseen creature slipping among reeds—seemed suddenly loud for want of speech. There was no denying the truth: in the immense circuit they had made, they had met nothing but disappointment.

“There is but one more chance,” Amyas said at length, and his voice sounded older than his years, “and that is the mountains to the east of the Orinoco, where we failed the first time. The Incas may have moved on to them when they escaped.”

“Why not?” Cary said. “They would so put all the forests, beside the Llanos and half-a-dozen great rivers, between them and those dogs of Spaniards.”

“Shall we try it once more?” Amyas asked. “This river ought to run into the Orinoco; and once there, we are again at the very foot of the mountains. What say you, Yeo?”

Old Salvation Yeo sat with his great hands on his knees, the fire making iron of his weathered face. “I cannot but mind, your worship,” he said slowly, “that when we came up the Orinoco the Indians told us terrible stories of those mountains—how far they stretched, and how difficult they were to cross, by reason of the cliffs aloft and the thick forests in the valleys. And have we not lost five good men there already?”

“What care we?” Cary broke in. “No forests can be thicker than those we have bored through already. Why, if one had had but a tail, like a monkey, for an extra warp, one might have gone a hundred miles on end along the tree-tops, and found it far pleasanter walking than tripping in withes, and being eaten up with creeping things from morn till night.”

“But remember, too,” said Jack, “how they told us to beware of the Amazons.”

“What, Jack—afraid of a parcel of women?” Amyas said, and would have laughed, but the sound would not fully come.

“Why not?” Jack returned. “I wouldn’t run from a man, as you know; but a woman—it’s not natural, like. They must be witches or devils. See how the Caribs feared them. And there were men there without necks, and with their eyes in their breasts, they said. Now how could a Christian tackle such customers as them?”

“He couldn’t cut off their heads, that’s certain,” said Cary; “but, I suppose, a poke in the ribs will do as much for them as for their neighbors.”

“Well,” Jack persisted, half in jest and half in dread of the unnameable, “if I fight, let me fight honest flesh and blood, that’s all, and none of these outlandish monsters. How do you know but that they are invulnerable by art-magic?”

“How do you know that they are?” Amyas answered. “And as for the Amazons—woman’s woman all the world over. I’ll wager you may wheedle them round with a compliment or two.”

Cary’s eyes sparkled. “Pity I have not a court-suit and a Spanish hat. I would have taken an orange in one hand and a handkerchief in the other, gone all alone to them as ambassador, and been in a week as great with Queen Blackfacealinda as ever Raleigh is at Whitehall.”

“Gentlemen,” said Yeo, and there was no laughter in him, “where you go, I go; and not only I, but every man of us, I doubt not. But we have lost now half our company, and spent our ammunition, so we are no better men—were it not for our swords—than these naked heathens round us. Now it was, as you all know, by the wonder and noise of their ordnance—let alone their horses, which is a break-neck beast I put no faith in—that both Cortez and Pizarro, those imps of Satan, made their golden conquests, with which if we could have astounded the people of Manoa—”

“Having first found the said people,” Amyas laughed at last, the sound sharp with fatigue. “It is like the old fable. Every craftsman thinks his own trade the one pillar of the commonweal.”

“Well! your worship,” Yeo said, stubborn as an old oak root, “it may be that being a gunner I overprize guns. But it don’t need slate and pencil to do this sum—are forty men without shot as good as eighty with?”

“Thou art right, old fellow—right enough,” Amyas answered, and his voice softened. “I was only jesting for very sorrow, and must needs laugh about it lest I weep about it. Our chance is over, I believe, though I dare not confess as much to the men.”

“Sir,” Yeo said, and the crackle of the fire seemed to listen, “I have a feeling on me that the Lord’s hand is against us in this matter. Whether He means to keep this wealth for worthier men than us, or whether it is His will to hide this great city in the secret place of His presence from the strife of tongues, and so to spare them from sinful man’s covetousness—and England from that sin and luxury which I have seen gold beget among the Spaniards—I know not, sir; for who knoweth the counsels of the Lord? But I have long had a voice within which saith, ‘Salvation Yeo, thou shalt never behold the Golden City which is on earth, where heathens worship sun and moon and the hosts of heaven; be content, therefore, to see that Golden City which is above, where is neither sun nor moon, but the Lord God and the Lamb are the light thereof.’”

There was a simple majesty in the old man’s outbreaks—something that made even bold companions stare as if he had stepped aside, for a moment, into another air. In the wilderness a man’s words were often all he had left to keep his own story from being stolen by fear; and Yeo’s words, with their antique turns and Scripture-thunder, sounded like a dialect that refused to die. Brimblecombe, whose gentle piety had long ceased to be offended by the Anabaptist’s roughness, answered softly:

“Amen! amen! my masters all. And it has been on my mind, too, this long time, that there is a providence against our going east; for see how this two years past, whenever we have pushed eastward, we have fallen into trouble, and lost good men; and whenever we went Westward-ho, we have prospered—and do prosper to this day.”

“And what is more, gentlemen,” Yeo went on, as if a hand had turned his thought like a page, “if, as Scripture says, dreams are from the Lord, I verily believe mine last night came from Him. For as I lay by the fire, sirs, I heard my little maid’s voice calling of me, as plain as ever I heard in my life; and the very same words, sirs, which she learned from me and my good comrade William Penberthy to say, ‘Westward-ho! jolly mariners all!’—a bit of an ungodly song, my masters, which we sang in our wild days. But she stood and called it as plain as ever mortal ears heard, and called again till I answered, ‘Coming! my maid, coming!’ and after that the dear chuck called no more—God grant I find her yet!—and so I woke.”

Cary had long since ceased to laugh at Yeo about the “little maid.” In a forest where a man might lose not only his way but his very words, the sound of a remembered voice could be as solid as bread.

“So let it be, Yeo, if the rest agree,” Amyas said at last. “But what shall we do to the westward?”

“Do?” Cary’s grin returned, quick as a spark in dry leaf. “There’s plenty to do; for there’s plenty of gold, and plenty of Spaniards too, they say, on the other side of these mountains—so that our swords will not rust for lack of adventures, my gay knights-errant all.”

They talked on into the night, the plan shaping itself between firelight and shadow. Desperate enough, certainly—but what cared those hearts for desperate counsels? They would cross the Cordillera to Santa Fe de Bogota, of whose wealth Amyas and Yeo had heard on the Pacific. There they would try to seize either the town itself or a convoy of gold coming from it. Then they would strike for the nearest great river said to run northward, build canoes, and make for the Northern Sea once more; and if Heaven favored them, they might take a Spanish ship and win home to England—not with Manoa’s unimaginable treasure, but with a fair booty of Spanish gold.

It was a wild dream; yet no wilder than Drake’s had been before it, and not so wild as Oxenham’s might have proved, but for the man’s own fatal folly.

Amyas sat up late, watching the coals crumble, and feeling the weight of what must be abandoned. To give up the dream of years was hard; to face his mother with empty hands was harder still. But men’s lives were in the balance; he had no right to feed them on a hope that had turned to mist.

Next day the new plan was put before the company and received with a shout, as if any road were better than the slow, sick wandering after an ever-receding marvel. They would go up to the mountains and rest awhile; if it might be done, bring up the wounded left behind; then try a new venture with new hopes and, likely, new dangers. They were long since hardened to danger, as the hand is hardened to the oar.

They started next morning cheerfully enough. For three hours and more they paddled easily up glassy, windless reaches between two green, flower-bespangled walls of forest. Birds and insects jeweled the air. Long trailers hung from branches to the water’s edge and seemed to admire their own blossoms in the stream’s clear mirror. River, trees, flowers—fairyland, if one chose to call it so; only the fairyland was colossal, and long familiarity had dulled the men’s wonder.

What once would have been miracle had become routine. Trees two hundred feet high stood massed with yellow and purple blossom to their topmost twigs; every stem was a hanging garden of orchids in crimson and orange, of vanilla vines whose pods—when you bruised them—gave a warm, dark perfume that made a man think, for a moment, of some kitchen far away where sweetness and smoke belonged to peace instead of peril. Strange birds flashed like dropped banners; fish glittered like new-minted coin. Monkeys marched along the tree-tops, keeping pace and chattering in whistles and grunts and howls; their noise, once comic, had grown as ordinary as rain. The roar of jaguar and the rustle of boa stirred no more fear than the creak of a ship’s timbers stirs a sailor used to the sea.

So when a brilliant green and rose-colored fish—flat-bodied like a bream, flab-finned like a salmon, saw-toothed like a shark—leapt clean into the canoe to escape the rush of a huge alligator (whose loathsome snout, unable to check itself, actually rattled against the canoe within a foot of Jack Brimblecombe’s hand), Jack did not turn pale as he once had at sharks upon another voyage. He coolly seized the fish, weighed it by habit in his palm, and said, “He’s four pound weight! If you can catch ‘pirai’ for us like that, old fellow, just keep in our wake, and we’ll give you the cleanings for wages.”

Food, even in jest, was never far from their minds now. The forest fed them, but on its own terms: smoked monkey, roasted iguana, fish split down the back and laid over greenwood coals till the skin blistered and the flesh steamed, bitter roots grated and washed to take out poison, palm hearts cut pale and crisp as a child’s bone. The very act of cooking had become a kind of religion—measuring salt scraped from palm-nut ashes, turning meat so it dried without burning, keeping smoke low so it would not call enemies, listening for the wrong bird-cry that meant strangers near. Here, taste and smell were not luxuries; they were warnings, promises, memories.

They paddled on, hugging the shadow of the southern bank while on their right the full sun-glare lay upon the northern wall of mimosas, figs, and laurels, broken by bamboo tufts and decked with a thousand gaudy parasites. Bank on bank of bloom piled upward toward the sky until, where its outline cut the blue, flowers and leaves too lofty to be distinguished formed a broken rainbow of hues quivering in ascending streams of azure mist, melting at last into heaven itself.

As the sun climbed, a great stillness fell upon the forest. Jaguars and monkeys withdrew into darker hollows. Birds’ notes died one by one. Butterflies ceased their flitting and slept with outspread wings upon glossy leaves, so perfectly matched to blossom that the eye could not pick them out. Now and then a hummingbird whirred down toward the water, hummed for a breath around a pendent flower, and vanished again into the inner shade where trunks stood like the pillars of some heathen shrine. A parrot swung and screamed at them from an overhanging bough. A thirsty monkey slid lazily down a liana, dipped water in his tiny hand, and scrambled back chattering when he met the stare of an alligator peering up through the clear depths.

In shaded nooks, capybaras—rabbits as large as sheep—paddled sleepily among blue water-lilies; black and purple water-hens ran on rafts of floating leaves. A freshwater dolphin’s shining snout rose, a jet of spray whirred up with a small hiss, and for a moment a rainbow hung upon it before the snout sank again. On shallow pebbly shores, scarlet flamingoes stood knee-deep on one leg like figures cut from cloth; crested cranes pranced, admiring their own finery; ibises and egrets dipped their bills for prey. But before noon even those slipped away, and there reigned a stillness that might be heard: not emptiness, but a delicate weave of sound—far-off water, near insect-hum, leaf’s faint rub on leaf—so that a man’s mind, if it had been bruised by harsher clamors, could find itself again in the balance of living noise. There, at least, the world was not shouting at itself for profit, nor selling music in order to drown the quiet truths that rose in quietness.

At last a soft, distant murmur began to grow through the air, swelling by degrees into a heavy roar. They were nearing some cataract. Turning a point where deep alluvial soil rose into a low cliff fringed with delicate ferns, they came full upon the sight—and every paddle paused, not in wonder, but in something like disgust.

“Rapids again!” grumbled one. “I thought we had had enough of them on the Orinoco.”

“We shall have to get out, and draw the canoes overland, I suppose. Three hours will be lost—and in the very hottest of the day, too.”

“There’s worse behind; don’t you see the spray behind the palms?”

“Stop grumbling, my masters, and don’t cry out before you are hurt,” Amyas called. “Paddle right up to the largest of those islands, and let us look about us.”

Humboldt tells, somewhere in his travels, of stumbling on a knot of Indians huddled round a fire, their skins clotted with grease and paint, crunching ants and kneading clay between their teeth as if it were bread. He confesses—almost in spite of himself—that but for the lesson-book of science, which bade him call them the raw beginning of mankind, he should sooner have believed them the last poor shreds of a nobler people, fallen and perishing. It was an honest temptation; and had he yielded to it outright, he would have spared himself a doctrine which, for all its boldness, has often less fact than fancy, and must shut its ears to a thousand plain witnesses in order to keep its story straight.

Amyas and his comrades were not of the sort to trouble their heads with such new theories. They took the old tale as they found it, and saw in these woodland folk not clever beasts on an upward ladder, but immortal souls astray—men and women, as themselves, yet “taken captive,” and likely enough to be lost, in this world and the next, if no hand brought them light.

And the place they came to seemed at first to plead against despair. Their canoes grounded on a bank where the forest opened into a scattered village—palm-leaf sheds, hammocks slung from trunk to trunk, and, in little clearings, patches of cassava and indigo showing a careful hand. There was a homely air of order: the kind of comfort that comes of knowing where one’s food grows, and tending it day by day in the same damp heat, under the same bird-cry and insect-whirr.

Then the sound began.

On either side of the landing stood four or five stout fellows, each planted behind his instrument: tall drums and long earthen trumpets that swelled into hollow bulbs along their length. The first foot of an Englishman on shore was the signal. Out burst a din of thumps and groans, of hoots and howls, a din that slapped the ear like a wet cloth and made the very leaves seem to shake with pain. The cataract’s own roar had a steadiness and majesty in it; this was the noise of something mean and eager, as if it fed on confusion.

“They be calling on their devil, sir,” muttered Yeo between his teeth.

Cary, wincing, had still wit enough to answer, “Then they’re like to get him; for none else would ever come to listen to such a racket.”

“And mark you,” said Yeo, looking past the instruments, “there’s some feast or sacrifice toward. I’m not overconfident yet.”

“Nonsense,” Amyas returned, with the stubborn cheer of a man who has been hungry and wet too long to be frightened by mere ceremony. “We could kill every soul of them in half an hour, and they know it as well as we.”

Yet there was no denying that something solemn—by their measure of solemnity—was forward. The people were drawn up in two lines along an open space: men in front, women behind, all bedizened as bravely as they could manage with arnotto and indigo, strings of seeds, and feathers that flashed red, blue, and oily green where the sun broke through.

With a yell that seemed to tear itself out of their throats, a figure sprang into the midst—jaguar-skin on his shoulders, tail dragging, teeth grinning, horns set above his brow, black-and-yellow plumes nodding, and in his hand a great rattle.

“Here’s the Piache, the rascal,” Amyas said.

“Ay,” Yeo answered, “in Satan’s livery; and I’ll warrant his work matches his coat.”

“Don’t be afeard, Jack,” Cary called, edging behind Brimblecombe with mock concern. “It’s your trade to tackle him. Go at him boldly and he’ll run.”

The men laughed, and the Piache—who had dressed to strike dread—hung for a moment, as if his own ear misgave him. But impudence was his bread. He strode forward, cracked his rattle over one of the musicians’ heads to buy himself silence, and launched into a long harangue, while Amyas listened with a cigar between his lips as though he were hearing a plea in some Devonshire court.

“What’s the howl about?” Amyas asked their boy-interpreter.

“He asks if you have seen Amalivaca beyond the great water.”

Amyas had been asked that question up and down the rivers—after the mythic civilizer who, so these folk said, had carved the strange marks upon inland cliffs and then gone home again over the sea. As usual, Amyas answered by praising Queen Elizabeth, setting her up as the wonder of the world and the true lady of the sunrise.

The Piache replied at once that she must be one of Amalivaca’s seven daughters—those he carried away, while he broke the legs of the rest so they should not run after him, and left them to people the woods.

“My queen’s legs are not broken,” Amyas returned, amused, “for she is a pattern of grace and activity, and the best dancer in all her realm. But tell your master this: will you give us cassava bread, and let us rest peaceably on this island awhile, before we go on to fight the clothed men beyond the mountains?”

At that the Piache began capering and turning head over heels with much howling, then beckoned them to follow. The Indians were unarmed and in high good humour; so Amyas went on, his men at his heels, their ears still buzzing from the racket.

The Piache stopped at a hut whose door had been carefully closed. He dropped to all fours like a beaten cur, crawled up, and began whining to someone within.

“Ask what he’s at, boy,” Amyas said.

The lad questioned the old cacique who had come with them, and got for answer that the Piache was consulting the Daughter of the Sun.

“Here’s our mare’s nest at last,” Cary whispered, as the Piache’s whines rose to screams and wild gestures, then to violent convulsions—foam at the mouth, eyes rolling, his whole body jerking as if a cord pulled it—till he suddenly sank and lay as one dead.

“As good as a stage play,” Cary added.

“The devil’s played his part,” Jack Brimblecombe said gravely; “and now, by all play-rules, Vice should come on.”

“And a fair Vice it’ll be, I warrant,” Cary murmured, leaning his head to listen. “A right sweet Iniquity, Jack. Hark!”

From the hut’s darkness floated a low, sweet song. At once the village bowed, faces to earth or hands, as if the very air had grown holy. The Englishmen, too, fell still—astonished—because the voice was not shrill and broken like an Indian’s, but round and clear and rich as a European’s; and it moved with such compass and surety that more than one of those rough wanderers, who could hold his part in a psalm at home, felt his throat tighten. The melody rose and fell like a bird’s call across water—almost without words, yet perfect in its turnings. It did not fight the cataract’s thunder; it threaded through it, and made the roar seem for a moment like the deep drone beneath a choir.

At last came one triumphant burst, sharp enough to make every ear ring—and then dead silence.

The Piache sprang up as if he had never been still, and began preaching again at Amyas.

“Tell the howling villain to make an end,” Amyas said. “His tune won’t hold after that last note.”

The boy translated, grinning: the Piache declared that the Daughter of the Sun accepted the strangers as friends; her friends were theirs, her foes theirs.

A scream of delight went up, and Amyas, rolling a fresh tobacco leaf in a strip of plantain with the neat fingers of habit, answered as if he were bargaining for ale at an inn.

“Then let her give us cassava,” he said, and lit his cigar.

The hut-door opened. The Indians flung themselves flat.

Out came the fair apparition the English had seen before upon the island—only now clothed in feather-robes and plumes of every hue the forest can lend: scarlet like fresh arnotto, blue like indigo stain, yellow as maize, black with green sheen, and white like her own strange skin. She walked slowly, stately as one who has never been contradicted, her gaze sweeping over her prostrate adorers with a cold pride; then she pointed, with arms that moved as gracefully as reeds in a stream, toward trees and gardens and huts, making plain without a syllable that all they had was at Amyas’s service. Taking his hand, she lifted it gently to her forehead.

At that sign a shout of rapture broke out, and as she withdrew again into her hut the crowd pressed round the English, laughing, touching, staring—at swords, at bows and blow-guns, at the wild-beast trophies on their shoulders. Women hurried off and returned with fruit and flowers, with cassava cakes hot from the ashes—white bread of the roots they had grated, washed, pressed of their bitter juice, and baked thin upon flat stones—and, to Amyas’s great anxiety, calabashes of a drink that smelled sweet and sour together, like over-ripe fruit warmed in sun. They sat under the trees and feasted, while the drums and trumpets resumed their hideous din, and lithe youths and girls danced uncouth dances whose stamping and shrill cries set Brimblecombe’s teeth on edge and made Yeo’s brow darker every moment.

Between bites Amyas could not help tasting the cassava as a sailor tastes bread after long want—dry, a little smoky, with that faint, clean bitterness which tells of the poison washed away by patience. He watched the hands that had made it: the women’s fingers moving quick and certain, as if kneading and pressing were prayers; the smell of warm root and crushed leaf mixing with sweat, damp earth, and river-mist. Yet the drink, and the noise, and the leering dance set him on edge. He was willing enough to get back to his island while his men were still sober, and before the village’s good humour curdled.

So there were many leave-takings and promises of return on the morrow, and the English paddled back to their island-fortress, racking their wits as to who and what the mysterious maid might be.

Amyas, lying afterwards in his hammock, had shaped his own answer: she must be of the lost Inca blood—perhaps descended from that fair girl, wife of the Inca Manco, whom Pizarro had once stripped, scourged, and shot with arrows, not to win a war but to torture a fugitive king’s heart. The thought clung like smoke in the nostrils.

That evening, as always, they gathered for prayer—hardly a day had passed since they left England when they had not done so. The service ended, they must needs sing a psalm, and then a catch or two, as Englishmen will when food is in the belly and danger for the hour seems asleep. Till the moon climbed high, twenty mellow voices rang above the cataract’s unwearied thunder. The island’s small noises—crickets, frogs, the occasional slap of a fish—were lost under the great continual sound; yet to ears that had lived with it for weeks, the torrent was like a steady bass, and anything new cut through it at once.

Once or twice, in the midst of a tune, they fancied an answer out of the darkness, but took little heed until Cary, who had gone apart, came back and touched Amyas’s arm.

“The sweet Iniquity is mimicking us, lad.”

They went to the river-brink. Across the black water the forest stood up like a wall. There, plain now, floated that same clear voice, repeating snatches of the airs they had sung—true and sure, as if the singer had learned them long ago and only waited to show her skill. Strange and solemn it was: the men’s deep notes on the island answered from the unseen wood by sweet treble, as if the dark itself had a tongue. Amyas and Cary stood a long while, listening, staring out across eddies that swirled down golden in the moonlight; but they saw nothing but shadow, and the steady shine where water turned.

After a while the voice ceased. They went back to sleep, and dreamed of Incas and nightingales.

They visited the village again next day, and every day for a week and more. The maiden appeared rarely, and when she did she kept her distance—haughty as a queen, granting favour by the very act of withholding it.

As soon as Amyas could make himself understood a little better, he questioned the cacique about her. The old man made an owl’s face at the name, and shook his head with mysterious warning. She was a child of the Sun; it was enough. The less said, the safer.

“Tell him,” Cary whispered wickedly to the boy, “that we are children of the Sun by his first wife, and sent to see how they’ve used our step-sister; for the Sun can’t spy all their tricks down here, the trees be so thick. If he won’t tell, all their cassava shall be blighted.”

“Will, Will, don’t play with lying,” Amyas said sharply; yet the threat had its effect. The cacique, frightened, took them in his canoe a full mile down-stream, as if afraid the maiden might overhear through leaf and water, and there, in a kind of rhythmic chant—like a tale that must be remembered by sound because words shift and die—he told them what he knew.

Many moons ago—how many he could not tell—his tribe had been a mighty people and dwelt in Papamene, until the Spaniards drove them forth. Wandering northward, far away under the mountain spurs where Cotopaxi burned like a lamp against the sky, they found this fair creature alone in the forest, no bigger than a child of seven. Her skin was white; her limbs delicate; her eyes had a look that did not belong to the woods. The simple folk took her for a god and led her with trembling joy. When they found she was human, their wonder hardly lessened. How could such a tender thing live among jaguar and snake? She must be protected; she must be of the Sun; she must belong to that mighty Inca race whose dreadful fall had travelled even into these green solitudes. The elders had heard that many Incas had haunted for years the eastern slopes of the Andes—about Ucalayi and Maranon—and that one day they would rise again, when bearded white men should come over the sea to restore them to their throne.

So, by the conjuror’s command, they tended her with royal honours to win the Sun’s favour, and the Inca’s favour, in that day of coming glory. As she grew, she became to them not only a fetish to be feared and adored, but a prophetess: more prudent in council than their elders, valiant in war, cunning in the chase. Her songs—those strange sweet songs—were full, they said, of wisdom about birds and beasts and flowers and rivers, taught her by the Sun and the Good Spirit from above. Unmarried she remained, partly from scorn of all Indian youths, but chiefly because the conjuror had declared it profane to mingle with the Sun’s race. He had given her a hut near his own, where she was served in state, and where she gave oracular answers to the questions he put to her—answers which, no doubt, kept his cassava-bin and coca-pouch pleasantly full.

Cary, when the tale was done, said drily what any man might have thought: that the conjuror “made a very good thing of it.” But Amyas was silent, his mind running, like the river itself, back into the south—back to rumours of the Inca remnant, to the vexatious thought that he might have been close to them and yet could not follow with his diminished band. If only he could speak with the maiden herself. If only he could carry home some hint that would be worth gold to a future venture. Manoa had not yet let go its hold upon him. If he got safe home, there were gallant gentlemen enough—Raleigh’s face rose at once before him—who would try again, not by these upper waters, but by the mouth of the Orinoco.

They paddled back while the cacique begged them, in his poor trembling way, to tell the Sun in their daily prayers how well the Omaguas had treated his descendant, and not to take her away, lest the Sun forget them and their manioc and fruit ripen no more.

Amyas had no wish to linger longer than needful to bring up the sick men left behind toward the Orinoco. That, he knew, would take months and carry danger in every mile.

Cary offered at once to go, if he could have half a dozen men and an Indian crew to help work the canoe. The last was hardest. The Omaguas feared the fierce Guahibas whose country must be passed; and every tribe, as Amyas had learned, looked on any tribe of another tongue as natural enemies—hated, and made only to be destroyed when met. The English, in their fashion, called this too the work of the devil, the divider and accuser; and perhaps they were right—only, as any man should, Amyas might do well to watch that, in hunting the devil in others, he did not make room for him in his own breast.

Whether pride or shyness had kept the maiden aloof, she conquered it after a while—perhaps from mere woman’s curiosity, perhaps from sheer hunger for something new in a life so narrow as a forest village, where the days were measured by pounding roots and the nights by the same drum-thump and frog-cry. She made it plain, however, that though all the bearded men might be great, none should be her companion but Amyas.

Before a month was past she was often hunting with him in the neighbouring woods, with a train of chosen nymphs—girls who, emboldened by her, had begun to spurn the dusky suitors round them. To the English it was proof that they were near the land of the famous Amazons, of whom Indians told such tales. Amyas, for his part, was sure that in her he saw the tradition of the Virgins of the Sun, and the austere rule of Peruvian superstition. Had not George of Spires and Jeronimo Ortal, fifty years before, found convents of the Sun upon these very waters?

So a friendship grew—harmless enough at first—and soon turned to practical use. No sooner did she understand that Amyas needed a crew than she went to the Piache, called the tribe together, retired to her hut, and began that singing which held them like a spell. When she came out, it was given out (unless the Piache lied) that the song had been a command: young men must go with Cary, under pain of the sovereign displeasure of an evil spirit whose name was not to be spoken. The argument, being of the sort that bites quickest into fear, succeeded at once, and the canoe set out on its perilous errand.

John Brimblecombe doubted much whether any venture begun under such patronage could prosper. Amyas too, though he used what came to hand, disliked the jugglery, and told Ayacanora it had been better to say the deed was good, and pleasing to the Good Spirit.

“Ah!” she answered, with a child’s simplicity and a woman’s shrewdness. “They know better than that. The Good Spirit—he big and lazy. He smiles, and takes no trouble. But the little bad spirit—he so busy—here, and there, and everywhere.” And she fluttered her pretty hands up and down among the leaves. “He the useful one to have for friend.”

The Piache approved mightily—as became his trade. Once he told Brimblecombe, with a sneer sharp as a thorn, that he was a meddlesome fellow to teach the Indians that the Good Spirit cared for them. “For if they ask the Good Spirit for what they want, who bring me cassava and coca to keep the bad spirit quiet?”

The argument—felt by devil’s priests in all ages to be very forcible—did not stop Jack’s preaching; and still less the morning and evening service in the island camp. That service, with its ordered psalmody, drew Indians in such numbers, lured by the strange clean harmonies cutting through the cataract’s perpetual roar, that the Piache saw his offerings dwindle. The forest folk, who lived by sound as much as sight, seemed soothed by the English voices—notes that did not shriek and pound like the trumpets, but moved in measured courses like water in a channel. The conjuror raged, and vowed to end Jack’s Gospel with a poisoned arrow.

Blinded (as Jack would have phrased it) by his master, he even took it into his head to tell Ayacanora—thinking her still the partner of his tithes—of his plan. He was amazed to receive a box on the ear and a storm of abuse. After which Ayacanora went straight to Amyas, told him all, and proposed that the Piache should be thrown to the alligators, and Jack set up in his place; declaring that whatever the bearded men said must be true, and whoever plotted against them should die.

Jack, however, forgave his foe with a steadiness that was more than pride, and preached on—with fresh zeal, but with little success. For the conjuror, though his chief treasure had slipped him, had still a reserve: a certain holy trumpet hidden in a cave on the neighbouring hills, which no woman might look on under pain of death. It was known—had been known for generations—that unless that trumpet, after fastings and flagellations and solemn rites, was blown by night through the woods, the palm-trees would bear no fruit. So great was its fame that neighbouring tribes sent, in season, to hire both trumpet and blower, paying in precious trumpery, that they might share in its supposed power to fatten and ripen.

One day the Piache proclaimed aloud that, because of the Omaguas’ impiety, he would go to a neighbouring tribe of more religious mind; and taking with him the precious instrument, he would leave their palms to blight and themselves to the evil spirit.

Then the village’s mood turned as swiftly as milk in heat. There was wailing—women’s cries rising above the river—and wrath. Jack’s words were good words, they allowed; but what was Gospel compared to a trumpet that promised fruit? The rascal saw his advantage and poured out a furious harangue against the heretic strangers. As he maddened, his hearers maddened. The savage nature, capricious as a child’s, flashed out into suspicion and rage. Women screamed; men scowled and ran to their huts for bows and blow-guns.

The case grew sudden and deadly. Amyas had with him no more than a dozen men, and they carried only their swords. The Indian men could muster near a hundred. Amyas forbade his men either to draw or to retreat; but poisoned arrows are weapons before which the boldest may well quail, and more than one cheek whitened that had seldom been pale in England or on the Spanish Main.

“It is God’s quarrel, sirs all,” Jack Brimblecombe said, his voice steady in the thickening tumult. “Let Him defend the right.”

As he spoke, from Ayacanora’s hut rose her magic song—low at first, then gathering strength—quivering aloft among the green heights of the forest, threading through drum-thump, cataract-roar, and angry cries like a bright blade drawn in the dark.

A fortnight and more had worn itself away in hard labor—hard, yet not harder than the stubborn work these men had so often taken for their daily bread. They had bidden farewell, once and for ever, to the green, heaving emptiness of the eastern plains; had climbed the Cordillera till the air thinned and rang clearer about the ear; and from a height had looked longingly toward Santa Fe, lying like a white patch among rich gardens on its lofty mountain table-land. It was, as they had foreseen, too great a place for any handful of desperate English to meddle with. They passed it by; but they had not passed idle.

Their Indian lad had found, by those subtle threads of rumor which run through every forest and valley quicker than any horse, that a gold-train would go down from Santa Fe toward the Magdalena. For that they waited—crouched beside a miserable rut that called itself a road, camped in a forest of oaks which for an instant might have cheated the heart into thinking of Devon or of home, had not the undergrowth been tree-ferns, damp and glossy as if forever newly washed; had not the earth fallen away at their very feet into gorges so deep that, while their brows were swept by the cool breath of the temperate zone, they could look down into a continual bath of steaming vapor, and through it, dimly, see the gigantic shapes and wet colors of the tropic wood below.

They had chosen their lair above a place where the path crept along a steep side, with a sheer cliff beneath, dropping many hundred feet. There had been a road there once—perhaps in the days when these hills belonged to a kingdom that knew order, and measured its labor, and set stones where stones were needed. All that Spanish rule had left was a few broken steps slipping from their beds at the bottom of a narrow ditch of mud. The rest had gone the way of aqueducts and bridges, post-houses and garden-terraces, llama-flocks and husbandry; for where men run mad for gold, they let everything rot that does not glitter. Only the great stones of churches endured—splendid cathedrals lifted above the ruins of older temples; and even that “milder” worship, as it named itself, had shown these English before now how it could demand blood.

Now the vegetation had taken back its own. A vine would seize the fallen column as readily as it seized a living throat; ferns sprang where men had trodden; the place had the damp, sweet smell of leaf-mould and bruised green, with here and there the sharpness of resin and the sour breath of hidden rot. Within a few miles of a Spanish settlement they might as well have been in the middle of the Orinoco.

Their search for sulphur and nitre had failed them. No yellow crust on stone, no white bloom in cave or crevice—nothing to please Yeo’s fierce longing for powder and ball. Much to his disgust they must trust, as they had trusted before, to steel and yew.

“Be it so,” Amyas had said. “Drake took Nombre de Dios and the train with no better.”

So they felled a great tree across the road above them, wedging its trunk into the bank; and they sat among flowers which seemed to have been painted into life and then drenched, chewing coca in default of honest meat and drink—green, bitter mouthfuls that numbed hunger and made the tongue thick, while the body, cheated of its rightful complaint, went on. It was no meal for men who remembered bread’s warm smell, or the crackle of fat on a pan; but it served, and served too well, deadening more than the belly’s craving.

At night, as they lay, another thing had troubled them: a mysterious roar, heard nightly in their wake ever since they left the Meta. Not jaguar, nor monkey, nor any sound they knew. Always behind, always following, as if some huge throat in the dark were calling them by name. In such a land of wonders a man might learn to mistrust even his own ears; yet to these sea-dogs, used to reading weather by the smallest shift of sound upon water, the noise bit into the mind. Still, the gold-train mattered more than any phantom.

At last, from below, came a sharp crack and a loud cry—neither snapping branch nor woodpecker, neither parrot scream nor monkey howl.

“That was a whip’s crack,” said Yeo. “And a woman’s wail. They are close, lads!”

“A woman’s?” whispered Amyas. “Do they drive women in their gangs?”

“Why not, the brutes? There—did you see the basnets glitter?”

Amyas’s voice dropped till it was little more than breath. “Men! not a bowstring drawn till I do. Then one arrow—then out swords, and in among them. Pass it.”

Up they came, slowly, grinding their way step by broken step, and the English hearts beat harder for every jingle of metal and every shuffle of bare foot on stone.

First came about twenty soldiers—only half of them walking. The rest were borne, each in a chair on the back of a single Indian; while those who marched had shoved their heavier pieces—arquebuses and the like—into the hands of attendant slaves, pricked on at will by the pike of the soldier behind.

“The men are mad to let their ordnance out of their hands,” whispered Amyas.

“Oh, sir,” murmured Yeo, with a bitter curl, “an Indian will pray to an arquebus not to shoot him. They be sure their artillery’s safe enough.”

“Look at the proud villains,” breathed another. “To make dumb beasts of human creatures so!”

“Ten shot,” counted Amyas, coldly, as if he were counting barrels aboard ship. “And ten pikes. Will can tackle them above.”

Last of that troop came an inferior officer, also in his chair. As he went he turned his face toward the laboring file behind, and every other second drew the cigar from his lips to fling back a volley of oaths—thick, sticky blasphemies that seemed to cling to the air like smoke to hair and clothes.

“The blasphemous dog!” hissed Yeo, fingers worrying his bowstring as if the cord would snap with his restraint. Amyas laid a hand on his arm; and in that instant another procession came into view, and anger hardened into something worse.

A line of Indians, Negroes, and Zambos—naked, emaciated, scarred with whips and the bite of irons—were chained by the left wrist and driven upward. Each carried a basket held by a strap across the forehead, the leather biting into skin already raw. Sweat ran down their ribs; breath came in short, rasped pulls; some moved like men in sleep, their eyes open and yet not seeing. Among them were old men and youths, and women too—slender girls, and mothers with children running at their knees, the little ones stumbling in the mud. Three or four drivers, armed with whips of manati-hide, lingered along the file and paid for every lagging step, every slip of toe on stone, with a crack like a pistol-shot across bare flesh. The blows made a sound that stayed in the ear like a snapped string, and after each crack came the smaller sounds: a stifled grunt, a sob swallowed, a child’s startled breath.

A low murmur rose from the ambushed Englishmen—no mere anger for robbed gold, but the indignation of free men at the sight of bondage.

Yet the first forty bore a burden that dragged every eye forward. Each basket held a square package, carefully corded in hide. Amyas knew that look as a sailor knows the shape of a cloud that brings storm.

“What’s in they, captain?” whispered one.

“Gold,” said Amyas.

At that word there ran through the hidden men a rustle like wind through dry reeds—greed waking, quick and shame-faced. Amyas’s whisper cut it like a knife.

“Be men—be men—or you’ll spoil all!”

The last twenty Indians carried larger baskets, more lightly loaded—manioc, maize-bread, and food for the party. Then came twenty soldiers more, and after them the officer in charge, smiling in his chair and twirling two huge moustachios as if the world were made only to stroke his vanity. The ambush was complete; only the moment was wanting.

Amyas felt that old shrinking from drawing in cool blood on men who had no thought of danger—even though he saw cruelty at work with every step. His mind sought for any cleaner beginning, some act that would make the fight not murder but judgment.

Judgment came of itself.

The last but one in the chained line was an old gray-headed man; behind him, a slender, graceful girl of eighteen or so. Amyas’s heart tightened at the sight of their weakness under the load. Just then the foremost of the file rounded the corner above; there was a bustle and a shout.

“Halt, señores! There is a tree across the path!”

“A tree across the path?” bellowed the officer, and poured out fresh oaths. The Indians, told to halt above and driven on by blows below, surged helplessly on the broken steps. The old man went down, grovelling on his face.

The officer leaped from his chair and hurried up. Of course he came upon the fallen wretch.

“Sin pecado concebida! Grandfather of Beelzebub! Is this a place to lie worshipping your fiends?” And he pricked the prostrate body with his sword-point.

The old man strove to rise; the load and the strap across his forehead held him down. He fell again and lay still.

The driver laid the manati-hide across his loins once—twice—each blow sounding sharp in the narrow pass, echoing off rock as if the cliff itself repeated the cruelty. Still the body did not stir.

“Gastado, Señor Capitan,” said the driver, shrugging. “Used up. He has been failing these three months.”

“What does the intendant mean by sending me worn-out cattle?” roared the officer. “Forward there! Clear away the tree, señores, and I’ll soon clear the chain. Hold it up, Pedrillo!”

The driver lifted the chain fastened to the old man’s wrist. The officer stepped back, flourishing a Toledo blade whose beauty—its cold, bright line—made Amyas, in spite of himself, covet it as a craftsman covets a perfect tool.

The Spaniard was tall and handsome, broad-shouldered, with the look of birth and breeding that makes a villain more hateful. Amyas thought he meant to show his strength by severing the chain at a stroke.

He did sever something with one stroke. The blade flashed once—twice—and fell, not on iron but on living flesh.

There was a shriek, a crimson spurt; the hand dropped away, and the chain and its prisoner were parted indeed.

For one instant Amyas’s arrow yearned toward the murderer’s throat; the next, vengeance came from a swifter source.

The girl sprang like a tiger-cat. With the whole strength of passion in her thin arms she clasped the officer, and together they went—one dark body and one glittering—over the narrow ledge and out into the abyss.

A rush, a shout; all faces bent over. The girl hung by her chained wrist, caught for a moment on the edge. The officer was gone. There was a dreadful pause, and then, far below, the sound of his body crashing through tree-tops.

“Haul her up! Hew her in pieces! Burn the witch!” cried the driver, seizing the chain and pulling with all his might, while soldiers, springing from their chairs, stooped over the brink.

Now was Amyas’s time. Heaven had delivered them into his hands, and his mercy was already spent.

At ten yards his arrow went clean through the driver. Amyas leapt with a roar into the midst of the astonished Spaniards.

His first thought was for the girl. He jerked her up by sheer strength and flung her safe upon the road, while the Spaniards recoiled, as if some mountain-giant or ghost had burst among them. His Devon hurrah undeceived them; a cry rose—“English! Lutheran dogs!”—but too late.

A storm of arrows answered. Five Spaniards dropped dead at once; a dozen more went down or staggered bleeding. Down leapt Salvation Yeo, white hair streaming, with twenty good swords behind him, and then the narrow path was a shambles.

The Spaniards fought like lions; but they had no time to set arquebuses on their crutches, no room on that cramped ledge to use their pikes. The English had the wall of them; and to have the wall there was to hold a man’s life between stone and steel. Five desperate minutes, and not a living Spaniard stood upon those steps; and certainly no living one lay in the green abyss below. Two only, who had chanced to be behind the rest and in full armor, escaped mortal wound and fled down the hill.

“After them!” cried Amyas. “Michael Evans and Simon Heard—catch them, though they run a league.”

The two long, lean Clovelly men ran like deer, trained by cliff and forest, and in ten minutes they returned, their faces set, having done what they were sent to do.

Amyas and the rest hurried past the chained file to help Cary and the party above, where shouts and musket-shots told of another sharp affray. Their coming ended it. Spaniards fell; three or four scrambled down the crannies of the cliff.

“Let not one escape! Slay them as Israel slew Amalek!” cried Yeo, bending over; and before the wretches could find shelter, arrows found them. Their bodies rolled lifeless down the rocks.

“Now then,” said Amyas, breath coming fast, “loose the Indians!”

They found armorers’ tools on one of the dead and went to work. Iron rang; links gave way; shackles fell with dull thuds into mud. Amyas spoke to them through signs and what words their lad could lend him.

“We are your friends. All we ask is that you help us carry this gold down to the Magdalena, and then you are free.”

A few of the younger fell at his knees and kissed his feet, staring up as at something fallen from the sky, calling him the child of the Sun. But most sat where they stood when the irons came off, staring into vacancy as if the very act of freedom were beyond understanding. Their bodies had been trained to obey; their minds had been trained not to imagine. Even the mountain’s cleaner air and the birds’ bright calls could not enter them. The iron had bitten deeper than flesh.

Only the girl moved as if she still had blood in her. Once she was loosed, she sprang to the old man’s body. Without a word she lifted him—how that thin frame did it none could tell—laid him across her knees, kissed the fallen lips, stroked the furrowed cheeks, and murmured low, inarticulate sounds like the cooing of a woodland dove. None knew what she said but she—and he who could not hear, for his soul had fled.

Then truth struck her. She drew one long, heaving breath, rose erect with the body in her arms, and before any could grasp her intent, leaped with him into the abyss.

They watched her dark limbs, twined about the corpse, turning over and over in the green air until a crash among leaves and a scream of startled birds told that she had struck the tree-tops. The living roof hid her.

“Brave lass!” shouted a sailor, awed.

“The Lord forgive her,” said Yeo. “But, your worship, we must have these rascals’ ordnance.”

“And their clothes too,” said Amyas, forcing his mind back to necessity. “If we mean to go down the Magdalena unchallenged, we must pass through the heart of Spanish settlements and by Saint Martha itself. Listen, my masters! We have won, by God’s good grace, gold enough to serve us the rest of our lives, and without losing a man. May we win more, if we be wise and He thinks good. But remember Mr. Oxenham and his crew. Do not make God’s gift our ruin by faithlessness, greediness, or mutinous haste.”

“You shall find none in us!” cried several. “We know your worship. We can trust our general.”

“Thank God,” said Amyas. “Now—no shame nor sin to make these men carry it, saving the women—God forbid we should burden them. How many lie here?”

“Thirteen here, and about ten up above,” said Cary.

“Then near twenty are missing. Who will go down over cliff and bring up their spoil?”

“I—and I—and I—” A dozen stepped out at once, as always when Amyas asked for volunteers; for they knew he meant to set his own hand to the work.

“Follow me,” said he. “Sir John—take the Indian lad for interpreter. Try to comfort these poor souls. Tell them they shall all be free.”

Then Sir John, looking down the road, cried out, “Why, who is that comes up?”

All eyes turned. Up came—wonder of wonders—Ayacanora herself, blow-gun in hand, bow upon her back, and all her feather bravery about her, though travel had dulled and torn it. She saw Amyas, gave a cry that was half joy and half pain, quickened into a run, and at last fell panting at his feet.

“I have found you,” she said. “You ran away from me, but you could not escape me.” She fawned round him like a dog that has found its master, then flung herself upon the bank and burst into wild sobs.

“God help us,” muttered Amyas, clutching his hair as he looked on the beautiful, weeping creature. “What am I to do with her, over and above all these poor heathens?”

There was no time. Over the cliff he scrambled with the volunteers, searching among branches and rock-shelves for where the fallen had lodged. Ayacanora, seeing the main body of English stayed, perched on a point of rock to watch him, still as a hawk, the forest’s soundscape shifting around her—the drip of water, the far call of some unseen bird, the sudden flap and scold of frightened wings.

After half an hour of hard work they hauled up weapons, clothes, and armor of the fallen Spaniards, and bundled them among the men. The remaining corpses were flung over the precipice, to make an end; and they started again toward the Magdalena, while Yeo snorted like a war-horse at the joy of once more handling powder and ball.

“We can face the world now, sir! Why not go back and try Santa Fe, after all?”

But Amyas thought enough was as good as a feast, and held on downward. The freed slaves followed—no thanks, no laughter of deliverance; only meek obedience to new masters, and now and then a sign or grunt of surprise that no blow fell, that no pike prodded them to hurry. Some caught sight of the little calabashes of coca in the Englishmen’s hands. That woke them. They began coaxing abjectly for a taste of the miraculous herb—bitter green pellets that could silence the belly and lend breath to lungs in the keen air, and, worse kindness, for a while at least drive away the fallen Indian’s most unpitying foe: the malady of thought.

As the cavalcade turned the mountain corner, they paused for one last look at the place of that fierce triumph. Lines of vultures were already sliding out of the vastness, as if the sky had bred them for this purpose alone. In a few hours there would be no trace but a few white bones among untrodden beds of flowers, and the forest would close its mouth over the story.

And now Amyas had leisure to question Ayacanora. He wished her anywhere but where she was; yet now she was here, what heart could quite harden against her? He spoke with a tenderness born partly of pity, partly of fear of wounding her; and that gentleness, new to his voice, made her cling closer.

She told him—eager, breathless—how she had followed their track day and night, and every evening made sounds, as loud as she dared, hoping they would hear and wait, or come back to see what caused the noise.

Amyas remembered at once the nightly roaring.

“Noises? What did you make them with?”

Ayacanora lifted a finger with a proud little air of mystery. Then from under her feather cloak she drew an object at which Amyas had hard work to keep his countenance.

“Look!” she whispered, as if half afraid the thing itself could listen. “I have it—the holy trumpet!”

There it was—the bone of contention: a handsome earthen tube near two feet long, neatly glazed, painted with quaint grecques and figures of beasts—a relic of some dead civilization, its colors dulled by travel, yet still bright enough to catch the eye. Brimblecombe rubbed his small fat hands.

“Brave maid! You have cheated Satan this time,” quoth he.

Yeo’s face darkened. He advised that the “idolatrous relic” be “hove over cliff” without more ado.

“Let be,” said Amyas. “What means this, Ayacanora? And why have you followed us?”

She poured out a long tale; and from it Amyas gathered, as best he could, that the trumpet had for years been the torment of her life—the one thing in the tribe held higher than she, the one thing she was forbidden even to see because she was a woman. Therefore she had sworn to prove a woman as good as a man; and hence her hatred of marriage, her Amazonian pride. Yet still the Piache would not show her the trumpet nor tell where it lay hid; and to seek it openly she had feared the tribe’s superstitious fury.

But the day after the English left, the Piache, it seemed, chose to celebrate their departure; and, as might be expected, master and pupil fell to quarreling. The quarrel ended—she confessed it without shame—in her burning the old rogue’s hut over his head. He escaped, losing his conjuring gear, and fled raging into the woods, vowing he would carry the trumpet to a neighboring tribe. At that, by sudden impulse, she took plenty of coca, her weapons, and her feathers, and ran him down just as he was unveiling the precious mystery. The sight terrified her; she had been half inclined to flee. But she remembered how the white men laughed at the whole matter; and so she rushed upon the old conjuror and bore off her prize in triumph.

“I hope you have not killed him?” said Amyas.

“I did beat him a little,” she said simply; “but I thought you would not let me kill him.”

Amyas was half amused—half troubled—at her frank confession of his authority over her. She went on quickly:

“And then I dare not go back to the Indians; so I was forced to come after you.”

“And is that your only reason for coming after us?” asked Amyas—stupidly, busy still with roads and risks, and deaf to the finer meanings that cannot be translated into plain words.

He had struck some secret string. The girl drew herself up, her cheeks flushing scarlet beneath the paint and travel-stain.

“You never tell lies,” she said. “Do you think I would tell lies?”

And with that she fell back to the rear and followed them steadfastly, speaking to none, yet fixed as fate on following them to the world’s end—while in the high air above the gorge the vultures drifted, and below, far under their feet, the tropic forest steamed and murmured on, swallowing screams, prayers, and the last unspoken words of those whose tongues had never been allowed a name for freedom.

One last look—so a man might tell himself, and pretend it were merely piety—at that tropic sea which seemed to have been poured from a crucible. Evening came down on the bay of Santa Marta like warm oil, and the land-breeze combed the surface into a million tiny flames. Beyond, the forests crackled with their own quiet light: fireflies winking and drifting as steadily as embers in a kitchen-dusk, when a cook rakes the coals and the room answers with a soft, contented hiss. Mist lay along the inner hills, lazy as steam from a great pot left to settle; and above all, the peak of Horqueta, nineteen thousand feet of frozen white, caught the last rose of sunset and then, when the color died, took up again under Venus a pale gold—ice becoming treasure by mere change of lamp.

Gold. That word had a smell to it here, if a man were willing to swear so in mixed company: a metallic sweetness in the damp air, as if the earth itself sweated coin. It was that very fancy—half poetry, half appetite—that warmed the Bishop of Cartagena as he sat in the state cabin of the great galleon, The City of the True Cross, and gazed shoreward with a scholar’s melancholy.

His stoutness had been arranged to advantage: his body in one easy-chair, his stout ankles on another; before him a table spread as if the ship were not a floating fortress but a banquet-room. Oranges and limes lay split and shining, their oils sharp as a struck match; guavas with their bruised, musky perfume; pine-apples exhaling that fermented, honeyed bite which can make a hungry man angry with his own hunger. All the fruits of Ind crowded together, their skins sweating in the cabin heat. An Indian girl, scarved and chained with gold as with a second bondage, moved a fan of feathers in a patient rhythm to keep the flies from settling on his holiness’s mouth and hands. At his side, in a pail, stood ice hacked from Horqueta—ice bought with Indian backs and delivered as a pious gift—and in the ice more than one flask of Alicant, virtuous and strong.

But the bishop was not, this evening at least, to drink and nibble alone. At his especial request Don Pedro, colonel of the soldiers aboard; Don Alverez, intendant of his Catholic majesty’s customs at Santa Marta; and Don Paul, captain of mariners of The City of the True Cross, had come to keep him company. Two friars sat lower down the table, and between sips and peels and the endless buzz of tropical insects they were doing their best to coax his mind away from the latest ruin of his cathedral town—Cartagena, sacked and burned by Sir Francis Drake.

“We have been great sufferers, señores—ah, great sufferers,” snuffled the bishop, quoting Scripture with that glib irreverence which takes holy phrases and salts them like meat for a traveling table. He complained of Tita and the mosquitoes in the same breath as he spoke of Providence, and yet no one contradicted him. “Great sufferers, truly; but there shall be a remnant—ah, a remnant like the shaking of the olive tree and the gleaning grapes when the vintage is done.—Ah! Gold? Yes, I trust Our Lady’s mercies are not shut up, nor her arms shortened.—Look, señores!”

He pointed with a broad hand out of the stern-window where the bay lay like a poured ingot.

“It looks gold! it smells of gold, as I may say, by a poetical license. Yea, the very waves, as they ripple past us, sing of gold, gold, gold!”

“It is a great privilege,” said the intendant smoothly, “to have comfort so gracefully administered at once by a churchman and a scholar.”

“A poet, too,” said Don Pedro. “You have no notion what sweet sonnets—”

“Hush, Don Pedro—hush! If I, a mateless bird, have spent an idle hour in teaching lovers how to sing, why, what of that? I am a churchman, señores; but I am a man and I can feel, señores; I can sympathize; I can palliate; I can excuse. Who knows better than I how much human nature lurks in us fallen sons of Adam? Tita!”

“Um?” said the girl—more a throat-sound than a word, as if her native tongue had been pressed down under Spanish syllables and would not rise.

“Fill his excellency the intendant’s glass. Does much more treasure come down, illustrious señor? May the poor of Mary hope for a few more crumbs from their Mistress’s table?”

“Not a pezo, I fear. The big white cow up there”—and he jerked his chin toward Horqueta—“has been milked dry for this year.”

“Ah!” The bishop looked up as if he could drain the mountain with his eyes. “Only good to cool wine with, eh? and as safe for the time being as Solomon’s birds.”

“Solomon’s birds? Explain your recondite allusion, my lord.”

“Enlighten us, your excellency, enlighten us.”

“Ah! thereby hangs a tale. You know the holy birds who run up and down on the Prado at Seville among the ladies’ pretty feet—eh? with hooked noses and cinnamon crests? Of course. Hoopoes—Upupa, as the classics have it. Well, señores, once on a time, the story goes, these hoopoes all had golden crowns on their heads; and, señores, they took the consequences—eh? But it befell on a day that all the birds and beasts came to do homage at the court of his most Catholic majesty King Solomon, and among them came these same hoopoes; and they had a little request to make, the poor rogues. And what do you think it was? Why, that King Solomon would pray for them that they might wear any sort of crowns but these same golden ones; for—listen, Tita, and see the snare of riches—mankind so hunted, and shot, and trapped, and snared them, for the sake of these same golden crowns, that life was a burden to bear. So Solomon prayed, and instead of golden crowns, they all received crowns of feathers; and ever since, señores, they live as merrily as crickets in an oven, and also have the honor of bearing the name of his most Catholic majesty King Solomon. Tita! fill the señor commandant’s glass. Fray Gerundio, what are you whispering about down there, sir?”

Fray Gerundio had done no more than murmur to his brother something which sounded like a prayer uttered through clenched teeth.

“O si sic omnia!—would that all gold would turn to feathers in like wise!”

“Then, friend,” replied the other friar, a Dominican too but darker and sterner in the face, “corrupt human nature would within a week discover some fresh bauble, for which to kill and be killed in vain.”

“What is that, Fray Gerundio?” asked the bishop again.

“I merely remarked, that it were well for the world if all mankind were to put up the same prayer as the hoopoes.”

“World, sir? What do you know about the world? Convert your Indians, sir, if you please, and leave affairs of state to your superiors.” He turned to the Dons and lowered his voice with the conspiratorial relish of a man who enjoys his own authority. “You will excuse him, señores. A very worthy and pious man, but a poor peasant’s son; and beside—you understand. A little wrong here; too much fasting and watching, I fear, good man.” And he tapped his forehead to signify that Fray Gerundio’s wits were not, in his judgment, properly salted.

The friar saw and heard and only smiled—one of those quiet men whose charity had been sharpened, like a blade honed against cruelty, by watching his countrymen grind the weak. He was clumsy of court-speech; he could not knit three fine sentences together to please a bishop’s ear. But among Indians and negroes, crucifix in hand, he could make words live. He had been used to rebuffs; he took them like hard bread and swallowed without complaint, sipping his wine in silence while the talk rose and fell around him like surf.

“They say,” observed the commandant, “that a very small Plate-fleet will go to Spain this year.”

“What else?” said the intendant, and his voice cracked like a dry stick. “What have we to send, in the name of all saints, since these accursed English Lutherans have swept us out clean?”

“And if we had anything to send,” said the sea-captain, “what have we to send it in? That fiend incarnate, Drake—”

“Ah!” cried the bishop, as if the name itself were a bad smell at table. “Spare my ears! Don Pedro, you will oblige my weakness by not mentioning that man—his name is Tartarean, unfit for polite lips. Draco—a dragon—serpent—the emblem of Diabolus himself—ah! And the guardian of the golden apples of the West, who would fain devour our new Hercules, his most Catholic majesty. Deceived Eve, too, with one of those same apples—a very evil name, señores—a Tartarean name.—Tita!”

“Um!”

“Fill my glass.”

“Nay,” swore the colonel, slamming his cup down so that the fruit trembled, “this English fellow is of another breed of serpent from that, I warrant.”

“Your reason, señor; your reason?”

“Because this one would have seen Eve at the bottom of the sea, before he let her, or any one but himself, taste aught which looked like gold.”

“Ah, ah!—very good! But—we laugh, valiant señores, while the Church weeps. Alas for my sheep!”

“And alas for their sheepfold!” said Don Alverez. “It will be four years before we can get Cartagena rebuilt again. And as for the blockhouse, when we shall get that rebuilt, Heaven only knows, while his majesty goes on draining the Indies for his English Armada. The town is as naked now as an Indian’s back.”

“Baptista Antonio, the surveyor, has sent home by me a relation to the king, setting forth our defenceless state. But to read a relation and to act on it are two cocks of very different hackles, bishop, as all statesmen know. Heaven grant we may have orders by the next fleet to fortify, or we shall be at the mercy of every English pirate!”

“Ah, that blockhouse!” sighed the bishop, and his sigh had in it both wounded dignity and the thin whine of a man remembering the taste of other men’s wine. “That was indeed a villainous trick. A hundred and ten thousand ducats for the ransom of the town! After having burned and plundered the one-half—and having made me dine with them too, ah! and sit between the—the serpent, and his lieutenant-general—and drunk my health in my own private wine—wine that I had from Xeres nine years ago, señores—and offered, the shameless heretics, to take me to England, if I would turn Lutheran, and find me a wife, and make an honest man of me—ah! and then to demand fresh ransom for the priory and the fort—perfidious!”

“Well,” said Don Pedro, “they had the law of us, the cunning rascals, for we forgot to mention anything but the town, in the agreement. Who would have dreamed of such a fetch as that?”

“So I told my good friend the prior, when he came to me to borrow the thousand crowns. It was Heaven’s will. Unexpected like the thunderbolt, and to be borne as such. Every man must bear his own burden. How could I lend him aught?”

“Your holiness’s money had been all carried off by them before,” said the intendant, who knew, and none better, the exact contrary.

“Just so—all my scanty savings! desolate in my lone old age. Ah, señores, had we not had warning of the coming of these wretches from my dear friend the Marquess of Santa Cruz, whom I remember daily in my prayers, we had been like to them who go down quick into the pit. I too might have saved a trifle, had I been minded: but in thinking too much of others, I forgot myself, alas!”

“Warning or none, we had no right to be beaten by such a handful,” said the sea-captain. “And a shame it is, and a shame it will be, for many a day to come.”

“Do you mean to cast any slur, sir, upon the courage and conduct of his Catholic majesty’s soldiers?” said the colonel, stiffening.

“I?—No; but we were foully beaten, and that behind our barricades too, and there’s the plain truth.”

“Beaten, sir! Do you apply such a term to the fortunes of war? What more could our governor have done? Had we not the ways filled with poisoned caltrops, guarded by Indian archers, barred with butts full of earth, raked with culverins and arquebuses? What familiar spirit had we, sir, to tell us that these villains would come along the sea-beach, and not by the high-road, like Christian men?”

“Ah!” said the bishop, “it was by intuition diabolic, I doubt not, that they took that way. Satanas must need help those who serve him; and for my part, I can only attribute—the misfortune which occurred to art-magic. I believe these men to have been possessed by all fiends whatsoever.”

“Well, your holiness,” said the colonel, “there may have been devilry in it; how else would men have dared to run right into the mouths of our cannon, fire their shot against our very noses, and tumble harmless over those huge butts of earth?”

“Doubtless by force of the fiends which raged with them,” the bishop put in eagerly, glad to have the universe arranged into a simple catechism of good and evil.

“And then, with their blasphemous cries, leap upon us with sword and pike? I myself saw that Lieutenant-General Carlisle hew down with one stroke that noble young gentleman the ensign-bearer, your excellency’s sister’s son’s nephew, though he was armed cap-a-pie. Was not art-magic here? And that most furious and blaspheming Lutheran Captain Young, I saw how he caught our general by the head, after the illustrious Don Alonzo had given him a grievous wound, threw him to the earth, and so took him. Was not art-magic here?”

“Well, I say,” said the captain, “if you are looking for art-magic, what say you to their marching through the flank fire of our galleys, with eleven pieces of ordnance, and two hundred shot playing on them, as if it had been a mosquito swarm? Some said my men fired too high: but that was the English rascals’ doing, for they got down on the tide beach. But, señor commandant, though Satan may have taught them that trick, was it he that taught them to carry pikes a foot longer than yours?”

“Ah, well,” said the bishop, and his voice sagged into self-pity, “sacked are we; and San Domingo, as I hear, in worse case than we are; and St. Augustine in Florida likewise; and all that is left for a poor priest like me is to return to Spain, and see whether the pious clemency of his majesty, and of the universal Father, may not be willing to grant some small relief or bounty to the poor of Mary—perhaps—(for who knows?) to translate to a sphere of more peaceful labor one who is now old, señores, and weary with many toils—Tita! fill our glasses. I have saved somewhat—as you may have done, señores, from the general wreck; and for the flock, when I am no more, illustrious señores, Heaven’s mercies are infinite; new cities will rise from the ashes of the old, new mines pour forth their treasures into the sanctified laps of the faithful, and new Indians flock toward the life-giving standard of the Cross, to put on the easy yoke and light burden of the Church, and—”

“And where shall I be then? Ah, where? Fain would I rest, and fain depart. Tita! sling my hammock. Señores, you will excuse age and infirmities. Fray Gerundio, go to bed!”

The Dons rose to go, while the bishop maundered on, his words thickening as the wine did its work.

“Farewell! Life is short. Ah! we shall meet in heaven at last. And there are really no more pearls?”

“Not a frail; nor gold either,” said the intendant.

“Ah, well! Better a dinner of herbs where love is, than—Tita!”

“My breviary—ah! Man’s gratitude is short-lived, I had hoped—You have seen nothing of the Señora Bovadilla?”

“No.”

“Ah! she promised:—but no matter—a little trifle as a keepsake—a gold cross, or an emerald ring, or what not—I forget. And what have I to do with worldly wealth!—Ah! Tita! bring me the casket.”

When the guests were gone, the cabin’s sounds changed. Their boots and spurs faded into the ship’s creaks; the table’s fruit sat in its own sweet breath; the fan stopped and the flies returned. The bishop, alone now with the girl and his God, mumbled prayers out of his breviary and fingered jewels and gold with the dull, greedy eyes of covetous age—his lips forming Latin while his hands counted in a language older than Rome.

“Ah!—it may buy the red hat yet!—Omnia Romae venalia! Put it by, Tita, and do not look at it too much, child. Enter not into temptation. The love of money is the root of all evil; and Heaven, in love for the Indian, has made him poor in this world, that he may be rich in faith. Ah!—Ugh!—So!”

He clambered into his hammock. Tita drew the mosquito net over him, wrapped another around her own head, and curled on the cabin floor like a shadow cast down and forgotten. Soon master and slave snored a merry bass to the treble whine of mosquitoes—a whole choir of hunger and heat.

It was long past midnight, and the moon was down. The sentinels above, who had tramped and challenged until they believed their officers were deep asleep, had slipped away from what they called the unwholesome rays of the planet to find what they considered their right—health and peace—in slumber. The ship, full-fed with plunder and self-satisfaction, slept too.

Out beyond the galleon’s stern, from behind the isolated rocks of Morro Grande, two long lines glided into the bay. They were almost invisible on the glittering water, for they were perfectly white; only the faint, cold sparks of phosphorescence along their sides betrayed their passage, like fish-fire under a knife.

The bishop woke and shifted, uncomfortable. The wine was dying in him, leaving headache and sour mouth; his shoulders had slipped down, his heels up. He sat upright in his hammock and peered out.

“Tita!”

“Um?” She lifted her head, eyes open and blank.

“Put another pillow under my head, child! What is that? a fish?”

Tita looked. She did not think it was a fish. But she did not choose to say so; an argument with a bishop in the night was like trying to hush a pot that has boiled over—you only burn yourself and make a mess.

The bishop squinted again, decided it must be some white whale or shark or other monster of the deep, crossed himself, prayed for a safe voyage, and snored once more, clutching the casket as if it were a reliquary.

Presently the cabin-door opened without a creak, and the head of the señor intendant appeared. His face shone with sweat though the night was not hot, and his eyes moved as if listening for every small noise the ship made—the click of a spar, the far slap of water against the hull, the nearer, intimate rasp of a rat.

Tita sat up. Then she began to crawl, snake-smooth, among chairs and table-legs toward him, lit by the weak yellow of the cabin lamp.

“Is he asleep?” whispered the intendant.

“Yes: but the casket is under his head.”

“Curse him! How shall we take it?”

“I brought him a fresh pillow half-an-hour ago; I hung his hammock wrong on purpose that he might want one. I thought to slip the box away as I did it; but the old ox nursed it in both hands all the while.”

“What shall we do, in the name of all the fiends? She sails to-morrow morning, and then all is lost.”

Tita bared her white teeth, not in a smile but in the show of an animal cornered too long. She touched the dagger at his side.

“I dare not!” he hissed, and shuddered as if the blade were already in his own flesh.

“I dare!” she breathed. “He whipt my mother, because she would not give me up to him to be taught in his schools, when she went to the mines. And she went to the mines, and died there in three months. I saw her go, with a chain round her neck; but she never came back again. Yes; I dare kill him! I will kill him! I will!”

The señor felt his mind ease as a coward’s does when another offers to carry his sin. He had no wish to do the murder himself; he feared the devil and valued his own soul. But Tita was an Indian, and in his accounting Indians’ souls were cheap, like their bodies. If she were lost, it would be an acceptable loss.

“But we shall be discovered!”

“I will leap out of the window with the casket, and swim ashore. They will never suspect you, and they will fancy I am drowned.”

“The sharks may seize you, Tita. You had better give me the casket.”

Tita’s eyes flashed; she saw his hunger for the box as clearly as she smelled the bishop’s wine on his breath.

“You would not like to lose that, eh? though you care little about losing me. And yet you told me that you loved me!”

“And I do love you, Tita! light of my eyes! life of my heart! I swear, by all the saints, I love you. I will marry you, I swear I will—I will swear on the crucifix, if you like!”

“Swear, then, or I do not give you the casket,” she said, and held out the little crucifix at her neck. Her gaze devoured him—wild, unreasoning, the fierce love of one raised where words of tenderness have been taught in a foreign tongue and paid for in bruises.

He swore, trembling and pale.

“Give me your dagger.”

“No, not mine. It may be found. I shall be suspected. What if my sheath were seen to be empty?”

“Your knife will do. His throat is soft enough.”

She slipped away, soundless as a cat, toward the hammock. The intendant stood shivering at the far end of the cabin and turned his back, so that he might not see the deed and have the picture haunt him at confession.

He waited. One minute. Two. Five. An hour, perhaps. Cold sweat bathed him; his blood hammered in his temples until the whole world seemed to ring. Was that a bell? No—only his own pulse, tolling like a warning no priest could absolve.

There was a struggle—she was at it now—then a stifled cry. The intendant’s gut clenched; of all sounds he dreaded that most, the old man’s voice breaking the night. Another struggle; and Tita’s voice, muffled as if beneath cloth, called for help.
