“I cannot help you. Mother of Mercies! I dare not help you!” he hissed. “She-devil! you have begun it, and you must finish it yourself!”

A heavy arm locked his throat from behind. For an instant he believed the bishop’s ghost had risen and taken him, or some fiend had come to drag him down alive. He tried to scream—to wake the ship, to buy his life with others’—but a handkerchief was jammed into his mouth, choking the sound into a wet grunt. In another minute he was bound hand and foot and flung upon the table. The cabin filled with armed men as if the shadows had hatched them: wild figures with bright blades, armor catching starlight from the stern-gallery.

Two men were lashing up the bishop in his hammock; two more had seized Tita, pinning her wrists as easily as one pins a struggling hen for the knife. Over them all loomed a gigantic enemy, whose voice, when it came, cut through the cabin’s tight air like a command in a storm.

“Now, Will,” he whispered, “forward and clap the fore-hatches on; and shout Fire! with all your might. Girl! murderess! your life is in my hands. Tell me where the commander sleeps, and I pardon you.”

Tita looked up into the huge speaker’s face, and something in her—fear, calculation, a sudden sense of power offered and withdrawn—made her obey without a word. She nodded, turned her chin, and pointed.

The intendant, gagged, could only hear. He heard the giant’s steps. He heard the door of the colonel’s cabin open. Then a short scuffle—boots thudding, a strangled oath—and silence.

But only for a moment. For already the alarm had been given, and confusion tore through the ship like fire itself. Feet ran. Doors banged. Voices rose: one man shouting “Fire!” as if flames were at his heels; another “Wreck!” as if the sea had opened its mouth; another “Treason!” as if the word could ward off steel.

Amyas—for it was none other—had gained the poop. The sentinels were gagged and bound before they could find their breath; and every half-naked wretch who came trembling up on deck in his shirt by the main hatchway was hurled into the scuppers and there secured, as briskly as fish are flung into a basket. The ship’s own sounds—the creak of timbers, the slap of water, the high thin singing of rigging—were swallowed by human panic, that ugliest noise of all: a crowd of men who do not know which story to believe, only that their lives depend on believing the loudest.

“Lower away that boat!” shouted Amyas, in Spanish, to his first batch of prisoners.

They were unarmed, bare-legged, blinking like men dragged from ovens into cold air. They could do nothing but obey.

“Now then, jump in. Here, hand them to the gangway as they come up.”

It was done. Each Spaniard who appeared was kicked to the scuppers and bundled down over the side, splashing into the boat with curses and prayers tangled together. The boats filled fast—white shirts in heaps, hands clawing gunwales, mouths open for breath and forgiveness.

“She’s full. Cast loose now and off with you. If you try to board again we’ll sink you.”

“Fire! fire!” shouted Cary, forward. “Up the main hatchway for your lives!”

The ruse worked perfectly. Before half-an-hour was over, all the ship’s boats that could be lowered were crammed with Spaniards in their shirts, pulling for shore as if the sea behind them were boiling.

“Here is a new sort of camisado,” quoth Cary. “The last Spanish one I saw was at the sortie from Smerwick: but this is somewhat more prosperous than that.”

“Get the main and foresail up, Will!” said Amyas. “Cut the cable; and we will plume the quarry as we fly.”

“Spoken like a good falconer. Heaven grant that this big woodcock may carry a good trail inside!”

“I’ll warrant her for that,” said Jack Brimblecombe. “She floats so low.”

“Much of your build, too, Jack. By the by, where is the commander?”

And so Ayacanora came to lodge in Lucy’s narrow cabin as though she had always belonged there—as duly shipped, as truly part of the company as any man who ate the ship’s biscuit and answered the boatswain’s call.

Yet she proved the most perilous of all their prizes.

For when those who have lived by hunting and hunger come first among folk who can draw sweetness from a chest with a key, there falls upon them a kind of sickness—of the spirit first, and then of the body. The old labors which had kept the limbs honest are gone; the old pride which had braced the heart is bruised by new craft and stronger tools. What was once won by patient tracking is now had for the snatching; and so the hand learns to take before the tongue has learned to ask. Amyas, watching her, felt that he held a soul on the edge of a knife; and had he been less steady, less simple in his steadfastness, the poor wild creature might have ended in a misery such as men afterward told of in docks and garrets—one more marvel broken and mislaid among the refuse of London.

At first it truly seemed she was slipping fast.

The warrior-prophetess of the Omaguas dwindled into a wayward child. After her first wonder at the great floating house—after she had ranged from deck to deck, nosed into every locker, tried every latch with fingers quick as a crab’s—she began to steal and hoard any trifle that glimmered and pleased her. She was too proud, or too shy, to beg; and so she took. When Amyas forbade it, she flung back her head with that sudden, savage royalty of hers, threatened to drown herself, and shut herself away sulking for hours, as if the very planks had offended her.

Still, she obeyed him—except where sweetness was concerned.

Perhaps her body, used to plantains and forest fruit, craved what seemed to her nearest kin: the preserved guavas and candied peels in the bishop’s store, the sticky comfits, the dried figs, the honeyed dainties. Those dwindled with a speed that made the purser’s face lengthen. Worse, the sweet Spanish wine which Amyas had set apart for Lucy’s daily cordial began to sink in the bottle as if the sea itself drank it.

Amyas’s rebuke then was sharp enough to cut. He told her—plainly, as he would have told a cabin-boy—that to rob a sick woman was mean; that it was the act of a coward, not of a brave. Ayacanora’s lip quivered; her eyes flashed; she cried out she would drown herself, sprang for the deck, and would have cast her bright head over the side then and there, had not Amyas caught her by the wrist and drawn her back with a strength that was gentleness only because he willed it so.

Afterward came storms of weeping and a flood of penitence; promises poured out in broken English and swift Indian speech. And a week later Amyas found she had slipped past her own conscience by bullying the Portuguese steward into giving her other wine instead. The thing might have gone worse; for she drank one evening, and the drink made her foolish and restless, laughing too loudly, stirring from place to place as if the very air pricked her. In the morning her head ached and her eyes were dull; and Amyas saw his warning proved before him like a lesson written in pain.

The steward, hauled up, swore in his terror that she had threatened to kill him. Amyas, hot with wrath, ordered him a sound flogging. But Ayacanora, with a pride that had its own rude honor, flung herself between the man and punishment, begging him off, offering herself instead, confessing that she had indeed frightened him into obedience.

Her headache and Amyas’s cold looks taught her more than stripes could have done. She made one more rush toward drowning herself, as if it were her last refuge from shame; then, like a creature that has found the fire burns, she shrank from the thing thereafter. From that day forward—to her dying day, it was said—she could hardly be persuaded to touch fermented drink.

Yet Amyas lay awake more than once, beating his brains. How was he to govern a girl who flew to suicide whenever crossed? To punish her he dared not, even if he had the heart; to put her ashore was impossible. There was in him an instinct—no idle superstition—that her strange clinging to the English was not mere caprice, but some charge laid upon him; and he prayed, with that downrightness of his which made prayer a kind of work, to be shown what he should do before she harmed herself—or, what seemed as likely some days, harmed a man who vexed her.

At last he thought it best to set Parson Jack to teach her the beginnings of Christianity, that she might be baptized when they came home.

But here rose a new trouble. She would learn of none but Amyas. Parson Jack, with all his honest zeal, was to her as a crow to a hawk. Amyas himself had reasons enough for refusing the office of schoolmaster; yet for a week or two she remained untaught in that matter, though in English she made astonishing speed, catching words as a cook catches scents—by instinct, by hunger, by daily nearness. She learned the names of things first—bread, knife, salt, fire—things that could be held and tasted and felt; and with the words came a sharper desire to be understood.

Then, as if her ill-humors must needs spend themselves on persons, she took a violent dislike to John Brimblecombe, mimicking his walk and his voice upon deck to the men’s delight; and she hated Will Cary too, never suffering him to approach or speak to her. Whether jealousy of his intimacy with Amyas, or the subtle woman’s knowledge that Cary alone might dare to make love to her, no one could tell; for Cary, to do him justice, was as innocent of that intention as Amyas himself. When remonstrated with, her only reason was childishly imperial: Cary, she said, was a cacique as well as Amyas, and there ought not to be two caciques. Once, in sober earnest, she proposed that Amyas should kill him and take the ship for himself; and when Amyas, amid shouts of laughter, repeated her counsel to its intended victim, she sulked for days as though betrayed.

The negroes fared worst of all. At first she hid from them as from some unclean beast; after, she flung at them whatever lay to hand—bits of rope’s-end, small tools, once a belaying-pin—till the poor Quashies complained to Amyas that their skulls would be cracked for no fault save their skin.

Over the common sailors she reigned like a petted princess with a knife in her sleeve—calling them from their work to run her errands, make her toys, twist her bright little fancies into cord or wood; enforcing her commands now and then with a shrewd box on the ear. Yet the good fellows, especially old Yeo, bore it and even fed it, as sailors will—humoring her much as they might humor a tamed leopard whose claws might show at any moment. She amused them, and she amused Amyas too; and the constant fret of watching her kept his mind from settling too heavily on what waited him in England—his mother’s face, and the dark tale he must tell her, which otherwise would have sat like lead upon his triumph and deliverance.

At last matters came—like most matters—to a head.

The ship had slipped along for many days under a favoring breeze. She had passed the ring of West Indian islands and crawled, safe from pursuit, through the great weed-beds of the Sargasso Sea, where the ocean lies thick and still as a meadow flooded. There, for the first time, they judged it safe to relax the discipline which had held them tight as a clenched fist, and to rummage their noble prize.

What they found of gold and silver, jewels and merchandise, need not be told. There was enough, when all claims were paid—Cary’s and the crew’s, and Mr. Salterne’s third as owner—to make Amyas rich for life. But in the captain’s cabin they discovered two chests: one stuffed with gorgeous Mexican feather-work, and the other with Spanish and East Indian finery that had come by way of Havana and Cartagena, bound, it seemed, to some señora at Caracas.

At Cary’s proposal, and amid the acclamations of the men, both chests were voted to Ayacanora as her share of the pillage—in reward, they said, of her Amazonian prowess and service.

She seized upon them with a greedy joy, had them carried into Lucy’s cabin, and knelt before them for hours, stroking and fingering as if the silks were living things. The Mexican feather dresses she sniffed at as savage; but the Spanish gowns—ah! those were enchantment. For two or three days she walked the quarter-deck in Seville mantillas and Madrid hats, in brocaded farthingales stiff as armor, in glittering trinkets hung about her throat and ears; and heaven knows what else, and heaven knows how wrongly worn. She spread herself in the sun like a peacock, turning for Amyas’s eye, hungry for praise.

The crew tittered. Amyas felt more inclined to weep.

There is something more pitiful than hunger in a child: vanity—that bright, trusting hunger to be admired. And most pitiful of all is the moment when it finds itself laughed at instead. Amyas would have spoken, but feared to crush her; and the thing healed itself as illnesses sometimes do—by breaking.

On the third day, unable to hold in her triumph longer, she came straight to Amyas.

“Ayacanora quite English girl now; is she not?”

A titter rose behind her. She turned, saw a dozen honest faces in broad grin, understood in a flash, and her pride—so tender under all its fierceness—bled at once. She darted down the companion, vanished below.

Amyas, thinking she would throw herself overboard, followed. But she had locked herself in with Lucy. Through the door he heard her sobbing, great convulsive sobs; and Lucy’s faint voice, pleading to know what was wrong.

He knocked. She would not open. All day she kept the door fast; and toward evening they were forced to break it, lest Lucy be starved.

There sat Ayacanora amid the wreck of her finery—half torn from her in spite, scattered across the floor like shed feathers—crying as if her heart would crack. Lucy cried too, half from fright and hunger, half because another’s grief is contagious in a close cabin where two women breathe the same thin air.

Amyas tried to comfort her, promised the men should not laugh again; but then, stumbling over the right words, he added, “You must not be so—so—”

“So what?” she demanded, and cried harder.

“So like a wild girl, Ayacanora.”

At that, her hands dropped to her knees. A spasm shook her throat and breast. She slid down, not in rage now but as if struck through, and knelt before him, looking up with an imploring face that made him feel as though he had wounded a child with a sword.

“Yes,” she whispered, “wild girl—poor, bad wild girl. But I will be English girl now!”

“Fine clothes will never make you English, my child,” Amyas said, very gently.

“No! Not English clothes—English heart! Good heart, like yours! Yes, I will be good, and Sir John shall teach me!”

“That’s my good maid,” Amyas answered, and his own throat tightened. “Sir John shall begin to-morrow.”

“No! Now—now! Ayacanora cannot wait. She will drown herself if she is bad another day! Come, now!”

And she made him fetch Brimblecombe then and there. The honest fellow, though still sore in dignity from her mockeries, bore himself patiently for an hour or more; and afterward she told Lucy, that very night, all that he had said—fumbling for words, translating into her own strange sense what she could not yet wholly understand.

From that day, when Jack went in to read and pray with the poor sufferer, Ayacanora no longer fled on deck. She stood by, trying to make it all out. She knelt when he knelt; she tried to pray too—that she might have an English heart. And if the prayer was dumb, it was not therefore unheard.

So they went on for a few days, more hopefully, with no great outbreak—until one morning, just after they had cleared the Sargasso-beds.

The ship was taking care of herself. The men sat under the awning mending and cobbling, their tools clicking softly—needle through canvas, awl into leather, hammer tapping metal—so that the whole deck had a homely, workshop music to it: the scrape of a knife on a spar, the creak of rope, the low talk, the occasional laughter. The sea’s hush around them held it all the more plainly, as a chapel holds a psalm. Even the wind seemed to walk on tiptoe; and Amyas, hearing how cleanly the little sounds rose and fell, thought how a ship at peace has its own kind of sanity—how different from the racket of guns and the shriek of iron, that modern men, he fancied, might one day call glory.

Brimblecombe was catechising his pupil below. Amyas and Cary, cigars in mouth, lounged and talked of all heaven and earth, and chiefly of how best to set on foot a fresh adventure against the Spaniards so soon as they reached England. And as they spoke, Amyas found himself pouring out to Will that dark hatred of the whole nation—his purpose of revenge for his brother and for Rose—which had settled like a murky cloud into every corner of his heart.

Suddenly a noise broke up from below—a scuffle, a shout—and both men sprang to their feet.

Up on deck rushed Jack Brimblecombe, holding his head with both hands as if to keep it from falling off.

“Save me! save me from that she-fiend! She is possessed with a legion! She hath broken my nose—torn out half my hair!—and I am sure I have none to spare! Here she comes! Stand by me, gentlemen both! Satanas, I defy thee!”

And Jack, with a courage that knew how to take cover, planted himself behind Amyas and Cary, while Ayacanora whirled upon deck like a very Maenad; then, seeing Amyas, stopped short, chest heaving, eyes bright with wrath.

“If you had defied Satan down below,” Cary said with a laugh, “I suspect he would not have broken out on you so boldly, Master Jack.”

“I am innocent—innocent as the babe unborn! Oh, Mr. Cary, this is too bad of you, sir!” Jack cried, indignant, while Amyas asked, sharp and anxious, what had happened.

“He looked at me,” Ayacanora said sturdily.

“Well,” said Amyas, “a cat may look at a king.”

“But he shall not look at Ayacanora. Nobody shall but you, or I will kill him!”

Jack protested, spluttering, that he had not so much as lifted an eye. The fancy—if fancy it was—had seized her like a fit. She refused to return below to her lesson. Jack went off grumbling, minus some hair, and wore a black eye for a week.

“At all events,” Cary said, relighting his cigar, “it is a fault on the right side.”

“God give me grace,” Amyas muttered, “or it may prove one on the wrong side for me.”

“He will, old heart-of-oak,” said Cary, and laid an arm about Amyas’s neck—an easy comradeship that made Ayacanora’s nostrils flare with visible disgust. She stalked away to the ship’s side, found a fishing-line, and began amusing herself, laughing and clapping her hands when flying-fish skittered from the bonitos, their silver bodies hissing through the air like thrown knives.

At last, weary of idleness, she went forward to the poop-rail and listened to John Squire the armorer, who sat tinkering a headpiece—metal ringing lightly under his hammer—and humming a song, changing the name in it to suit his own home:

“Oh, Bideford is a pleasant place, it shines where it stands,
And the more I look upon it, the more my heart it warms;
For there are fair young lasses, in rows upon the quay,
To welcome gallant mariners, when they come home from say.”

“’Tis Sunderland, John Squire, to the song, and not Bidevor,” said his mate.

“Well, Bidevor’s so good as Sunderland any day,” John Squire answered, “for all there’s no say-coals there blacking a place about; and makes just so good harmonies, Tommy Hamblyn—

“Oh, if I was a herring, to swim the ocean o’er,
Or if I was a say-dove, to fly unto the shoor,
To fly unto my true love, a waiting at the door,
To wed her with a goold ring, and plough the main no moor.”

Here old Yeo broke in, as if the very tune were a temptation.

“Aren’t you ashamed, John Squire, to your years, singing such carnal vanities, after all the providences you have seen? Let the songs of Zion be in your mouth, man, if you must needs keep a caterwauling all day like that.”

“You sing ’em yourself then, gunner.”

“Well,” said Yeo, “and why not?” And out came his psalm-book, and he began in that deep, twanging voice of his, which seemed made to carry above wind and water:

“Such as in ships and brittle barks
Into the seas descend,
Their merchandise through fearful floods
To compass and to end;
There men are forced to behold
The Lord’s works what they be;
And in the dreadful deep the same,
Most marvellous they see.”

“Humph!” said John Squire. “Very good and godly: but still I du like a merry catch now and then, I du. Wouldn’t you let a body sing ‘Rumbelow’—even when he’s heaving of the anchor?”

“Well, I don’t know,” Yeo answered; “but the Lord’s people had better praise the Lord then too, and pray for a good voyage, instead of howling about—

‘A randy, dandy, dandy O,
A whet of ale and brandy O,
With a rumbelow and a Westward-ho!
And heave, my mariners all, O!’”

“Is that fit talk for immortal souls? How does that child’s-trade sound beside the Psalms, John Squire?”

Now it befell that Salvation Yeo, for the very purpose of holding up that time-honored melody to ridicule, put on the true nasal twang and rang it out as merrily as ever he had done, perhaps twelve years before, when he got up John Oxenham’s anchor in Plymouth Sound.

And it befell also that Ayacanora, standing near Amyas, heard it and started as if stung. Then, half to herself, she took up the strain, and sang it over again—word for word—in the very same tune and tone.

Salvation Yeo started in his turn and turned deadly pale.

“Who sung that?” he asked quickly.

“The little maid here,” said Amyas. “She’s coming on nicely in her English.”

“The little maid?” Yeo whispered, paler still. “Why do you go about to scare an old servant, by talking of little maids, Captain Amyas? Well—” and then he spoke aloud to himself, as men do when their hearts run away with them—“as I am a sinful saint, if I hadn’t seen where the voice came from, I could have sworn it was her; just as we taught her to sing it by the river there, I and William Penberthy of Marazion, my good comrade. The Lord have mercy on me!”

A silence fell. All knew that whenever Yeo, by chance or by pain, came near any remembrance of that lost child, something in him shook like a mast in a squall. Only Ayacanora, pleased with Amyas’s praise and not knowing why men stared, went humming on, light as a bird:

“And heave, my mariners all, O!”

Yeo leapt up from the gun where he sat.

“I can’t abear it! As I live, I can’t! You, Indian maiden—where did you learn to sing that there?”

Ayacanora looked at him, half frightened by his vehemence; then at Amyas, to see if she had done wrong; and then, taking refuge in sauciness, turned away, stared over the side, and hummed again, provoking as a child can be when it is cornered.

“Ask her, for mercy’s sake—ask her, Captain Leigh!” Yeo cried, trembling.

“My child,” said Amyas, speaking in Indian, “how is it you sing that so much better than any other English? Did you ever hear it before?”

Ayacanora looked up, puzzled, and shook her head.

“If you tell Indian to Ayacanora, she dumb,” she said sharply. “She must be English girl now, like poor Lucy.”

“Well then,” said Amyas, coaxing her as he would coax a skittish horse, “do you recollect—do you recollect—anything that happened when you were a little girl?”

She paused, and lifted her hands above her head, as if the memory were a roof of leaves.

“Trees—great trees like the Magdalena—always nothing but trees—wild and bad everything. Ayacanora won’t talk about that.”

“Do you mind anything that grew on those trees?” Yeo asked eagerly.

She laughed. “Silly! Flowers and fruit, and nuts—grow on all trees, and monkey-cups too. Ayacanora climbed up after them—when she was wild. I won’t tell any more.”

“But who taught you to call them monkey-cups?” Yeo asked, his voice breaking with hope.

“Monkey’s drink; mono drink.”

“Mono?” Yeo repeated, baffled, and tried another path. “How did you know the beasts were called monos?”

“She might have heard it coming down with us,” Cary put in, joining them.

“Ay, monos,” she said, defending herself. “Faces like little men, and tails. And one very dirty black one, with a beard, say Amen in a tree to all the other monkeys, just like Sir John on Sunday.”

That thrust at Brimblecombe and the preaching ape made the sailors grin; but it did not move old Yeo at all.

“But don’t you recollect any Christians?—white people?” he pressed.

Ayacanora fell silent.

“Don’t you mind a white lady?” Yeo asked, and his hands worked as if kneading air.

“Um?” she said, half unwilling, half straining.

“A woman—a very pretty woman—with hair like his?” and he pointed to Amyas.

“No.”

“What do you mind, then, beside those Indians?” Yeo asked in despair.

She turned her back peevishly, tired of being hunted through her own thoughts.

“Do try to remember,” Amyas said, softly; and at his tone she made the effort, as if his voice were a leash that held her.

“Ayacanora mind great monkeys—black, oh, so high,” and she lifted her hand above her head and made a gesture of loathing.

“Monkeys? What, with tails?” asked Amyas.

“No, like man. Ah! yes—just like Cooky there—dirty Cooky!”

And that hapless son of Ham, crossing the main-deck at the wrong instant, saw a marlingspike—unlucky that one lay at hand—whistle past his ears.

“Ayacanora, if you heave any more things at Cooky, I must have you whipped,” Amyas said sternly, though there was no such intent in his heart.

“I’ll kill you then,” she answered, in the most matter-of-fact tone, as if it were only another choice of conduct.

“She must mean negurs,” Yeo muttered. “I wonder where she saw them now. What if it were they Cimaroons?”

“But why should any one who had seen whites forget them, and yet remember negroes?” Cary asked.

“Let us try again,” Amyas said. “Do you mind no great monkeys but those black ones?”

“Yes,” she said at last, after turning it over as if the thought were bitter on her tongue—“devil.”

“Devil?” all three echoed, for none of them were free of the belief that the fiend might take shapes and show himself to heathen conjurers.

“Ay, him Sir John tell about on Sundays,” she said.

“Save and help us!” Yeo groaned. “And what was he like unto?”

She made signs for a monkey’s face, and a gray beard like Yeo’s. That was plain enough. But then she began a series of motions about her pretty little neck—fingering and spreading, as if she wore a stiff ring there—which set them all at fault.

Cary burst out laughing.

“I know! Sir Urian had a ruff on, as I live! Trunk-hose too, my fair dame? Stop—I’ll make sure. Was his neck like the señor commandant’s—the Spaniard’s?”

Ayacanora clapped her hands, delighted to be understood, and nodded eagerly.

“The ‘devil’ appeared like a monkey,” Cary said, still half laughing, “with a gray beard, in a ruff—humph!”

“Ay!” Ayacanora cried, and in good enough Spanish now, as if the right word had been kept in that tongue all along—“Mono de Panama; viejo diablo de Panama.”

Yeo flung up his hands with a shriek.

“Oh Lord of all mercies! Those were the last words of Mr. John Oxenham! Ay—and the devil is surely none other than the devil Don Francisco Xararte! Oh dear! oh dear! oh dear! my sweet young lady! my pretty little maid! And don’t you know me? Don’t you know Salvation Yeo, that carried you over the mountains, and used to climb for the monkey-cups for you, my dear young lady? And William Penberthy too, that used to get you flowers; and your poor dear father, that was just like Mr. Cary there, only he had a black beard, and black curls, and swore terribly in his speech, like a Spaniard, my dear young lady?”

And the honest fellow dropped on his knees and covered Ayacanora’s hands with kisses, babbling as if words could build a bridge back over twelve lost years. The crew, seeing him on his knees to the savage girl, and hearing his cries, fancied him suddenly mad and crowded aft.

“Steady, men, and don’t vex him!” Amyas called. “He thinks he has found his little maid at last.”

“And so do I, Amyas, as I live,” Cary said under his breath.

“Steady, steady, my masters all!” Amyas added. “If this turn out a wrong scent after all, his wits will crack. Mr. Yeo—can’t you think of any other token?”

Yeo stamped like a man whose heart is too full for patience.

“What need then? ’Tis her, I tell ye, and that’s enough! What a beauty she’s grown! Oh dear! where were my eyes all this time, to behold her, and not to see her! ’Tis her very mortal self, it is! And don’t you mind me, my dear, now? Don’t you mind Salvation Yeo, that taught you to sing ‘Heave my mariners all, O!’ a-sitting on a log by the boat upon the sand, and there was a sight of red lilies grew on it in the moss, dear, now, wasn’t there? and we made posies of them to put in your hair, now?”

He ran on, pleading and suggesting, as if he could coax her memory into the shape he needed—into the child he had lost.

Ayacanora watched him—first angry, then amused, then attentive; and at last with such fierce earnestness that her face seemed to change before them. Suddenly she grew crimson. She snatched her hands away from the old man’s kisses, hid her face in them, and stood rigid as if the deck had turned to ice beneath her bare feet.

“Do you remember anything of all this, my child?” Amyas asked, very gently.

It was toward Candlemas-tide, on the fifteenth day of February in the year of grace 1587, when the dusk came early and the west wind smelled of salt and wrack, that Mistress Leigh walked, as she had walked at every evening high-water for three full years, the terrace at Burrough.

Below her the river uncoiled, slate-colored under the failing light; beyond it the sand-hills lay hazed and low, and farther still the broad western sea, which had swallowed so many brave men as quietly as a housewife takes in her breath and goes on kneading. The same sight, night after night; the same patient watching, until watching had become as much a household duty as setting out bread, or warming a little broth for the sick in the cottages.

Three years and more, and no word—neither of Frank nor of Amyas, nor of the good ship which had borne them out. In Bideford and Appledore, Clovelly and Ilfracombe, eyes had grown hollow with the same hunger. Once there had been a flare of hope: Sir Richard, unknown to her, had sent a rider hard to Plymouth when Drake and the rest came home from the Spanish Main; but the news, though great—Cartagena and San Domingo, Saint Augustine, and Raleigh’s poor Virginians relieved—brought no whisper of the Rose. Then Mistress Leigh had bowed her head as she did when bread failed or fever came, and spoken the old words as if they were a prayer learned before she had teeth: the Lord gave; the Lord hath taken away.

Her hair, once dark and orderly under her coif, was now grey as sea-fret. Her cheeks had lost their orchard-color. She moved more slowly; but she never missed the tide, any more than she missed church on a Sunday, or the round of cottages where she carried simples and kind speech. She did not speak her sons’ names. She would not let them be spoken in her hearing, as if to name them were to season a wound and make it smart anew. Yet every day, when the tide rose high and the red flag upon the sand-hills told there was water over the bar, she went out under the wind-clipped trees and stared westward with eyes that ate the horizon.

Ships still came and went as of old. White sails hovered off the bar, waiting; and many a time, year after year, men on the terrace had been deceived by a trick of light, or by longing. But in her there was an instinct as sure as a baker’s hand that knows, without looking, when the loaf is proved. She knew the cut of that ship, every line of her; she could have plucked her out miles away among a fleet—and the sail she sought never came.

So she went to and fro among the poor, and they, seeing her steadfast gaze and her quiet mouth, whispered that God had brought her very near to Himself. Lone women, bed-ridden men, took strength from the calm of her presence; for though she spoke little of her own sorrow, they felt she had walked deep in the furnace and was not burned. And all the while she blamed herself at night for that “earthly” pull which drew her, each high tide, back to the terrace as faithfully as a bell draws folk to prayer.

But this evening, Northam would not be quiet.

Down on the pebble ridge the surf thundered as it always did, like a cartload of stones poured without end; yet the village had noise enough without the sea’s voice. The tower shook with pealing bells. Men and boys were in the streets, singing and shouting about bonfires, drinking to the Queen’s health, crying, “So perish all her enemies!” Effigies were burned with a stink of pitch and singed cloth. The hills answered with their own red blazes from every hamlet, and far off Bideford’s bells replied, as they had replied years ago when Amyas had come home, brown from the sun and bright with the madness of the great sea-road.

For this day the tidings had run through England like fire through dry heath: Mary of Scotland had been beheaded at Fotheringay. After seventeen years of dread and plots and whispering treasons, the land sprang up as from a nightmare and shouted itself hoarse.

Mistress Leigh, Protestant as she was, found no relish in the shouting. She breathed a prayer—brief as the crossing of oneself, and as involuntary—that God would have mercy on that soul, clear as diamond and as hard, as Mary had once said of herself. The woman in Mary—whatever else had been in her—rose for a moment, pitiful and haunting, among the smoke and bells and zeal; and Mistress Leigh, who had learned in sorrow what no court could teach, shuddered to think of what training a girl might have had amid the Medicis and the Guises, and how little is asked where little is given. Still the bells would not cease.

Then, cutting across their clangor, came a sound from the outer dark—a flash, and after it the deep voice of a gun at sea.

Mistress Leigh stopped short upon the terrace.

The flash had been close outside the bar. It was no distress-signal; the wind was light and westerly, and the spring tide was making full. Another flash, another gun. At once the revel in Northam faltered, as if a hand had clapped over a mouth. Men ran, the way men run when fear sobers them: up into the churchyard that looks down upon the flats and the river.

Out beyond the bar, riding the dim water, was a tall ship running in with all her canvas spread. Near a thousand tons she might be, and yet the rig did not look English. For a breath the old dread bit: a Spanish cruiser come to strike for Drake’s mischief on the Cadiz shore? And yet—no. Spaniards did not play at such lone ventures if they could help it. If the Don came, he came with his proper order and ceremony: admiral and vice, transports and avisos, a whole book of war made flesh.

The strange ship slipped between the sand-hills on the flowing tide, bold as a hawk, and still she did not veil her topsails. That alone made men’s hands itch for match and powder; for every foreigner knew what it meant to show full canvas within sight of an English port. There were old seamen at Appledore who had not forgotten John Hawkins’s lesson to a proud Spanish admiral at Plymouth Sound—how a shot between the masts had taught manners, and a second had near “lackt” him through and through, until down came the flag and apologies with it. And if Hawkins had been so jealous in peace, then now—now—there was more than one sea-dog who would have found an iron pot to burn powder in.

The ship passed behind the hill of Appledore and vanished for a while; and then, up from the river came a cheer, sudden and great, like geese rising. Mistress Leigh stood as if turned to stone, listening. Another gun boomed among the hills. Another cheer answered it.

Time passed—twenty minutes, it might be; no clock in that moment had any use. She never moved, her eyes fixed where the dark rock of the Hubbastone should show.

At last the vessel came again, rounding the Hubbastone as she turned up the Bideford river. Music floated from her decks—drums and fifes, shawms and trumpets—waking echoes from slate and wooded knoll, so that the very land seemed to answer. And as she opened full upon Burrough House, her crew sent up a cheer that rolled along the silver water and climbed the hills.

Mistress Leigh turned, walking quickly toward the house.

“Grace,” she called, and her voice was not steady, “bring me my hood. Master Amyas is come home.”

Her maid, a faithful soul whose hands knew all household tasks from scouring to salving, stared and then cried out with a broken laugh.

“No, surely? O joyful sound! Praised be the Lord! But, madam—how ever did you know?”

“I heard his voice on the river,” Mistress Leigh said; and then the truth in her struck like cold iron. “But I did not hear Mr. Frank’s with him, Grace.”

“Oh, madam,” Grace hurried on, clinging to hope as to a warm pan in winter, “where the one is, the other is. They’d never part company. Both come home or neither, I’ll warrant. Here’s your hood.”

Hooded, with Grace behind her, Mistress Leigh went down toward Bideford at a pace her feeble step had not kept for years.

Was it truth? Or a dream wrought by long watching—some mercy, or some cruelty, of the mind? Grace asked herself the same, and crossed herself once in spite of being a Protestant, as folk do when fear is too old for doctrine.

On the quay it answered itself.

Down Bridgeland Street they came—where afterward tobacco-warehouses would stand for the Virginia trade, but where then rope-walks ran and sailmakers’ shops smelled of tar, hemp, and wet canvas. They saw the strange ship already at anchor. And as they reached the lower end of the street, a great mob swept round the corner—sailors, women, ’prentices—crying, questioning, laughing, weeping in the same breath.

Mistress Leigh stopped, and the crowd stopped too, as if a wave had met a rock.

“Here she is!” someone shouted. “Here’s his mother!”

His mother. Not their mother. The thought turned her skin white; but her heart, long trained to endurance, did not break.

Then the giant head and shoulders of Amyas rose above the press and came round the corner, moving like a man who has marched through worse crowds than this.

“Make a way! Make room for Madam Leigh!” he cried; and in the next moment he was on his knees at her feet.

She put her arms about his neck and bent her grey head over his dark one. Rough harbor-women, loud lads, weather-bit sailors—all were hushed as if a psalm had begun. A ring formed round them in silence.

Mistress Leigh asked no question. She saw, with a knowledge that needed no words, that Amyas was alone.

At last he whispered, close to her so that none but she might hear, “I would have died to save him, mother, if I could.”

“You need not tell me that, Amyas Leigh, my son.”

Silence again, heavy as a shut door.

“How did he die?” she whispered.

“He is a martyr. He died in the—”

The word would not come.

“The Inquisition?” Mistress Leigh said, and though her voice was low it had a hard edge, as a knife has even when laid down.

“Yes.”

A shudder went through her frame as if winter had passed inside her; and then she lifted her head, forcing her face to steadiness.

“Come home, Amyas. I little expected such an honor—such an honor—ha! ha! and such a fair young martyr, too; a very St. Stephen! God have mercy on me, and let me not go mad before these folk, when I ought to be thanking Thee for Thy great mercies! Amyas—who is that?”

She pointed. Close behind Amyas stood a girl, dark-eyed, watchful, slight as a young doe, looking upon the whole scene as one listens to an unknown forest: alert for danger, for kindness, for snares.

“She is a poor wild Indian maid,” Amyas said. “My daughter, I call her. I will tell you her story hereafter.”

“Your daughter? My grand-daughter, then. Come hither, maiden, and be my grand-daughter.”

The girl came obediently and knelt, because she had seen Amyas kneel.

“God forbid, child; kneel not to me. Come home, and let me know whether I am sane or mazed, alive or dead.”

She drew her hood closer, as if the cloth could hold in what her face might betray, and turned to go back, taking Amyas by one hand and the girl by the other.

For some twenty yards the crowd let them pass in a reverent hush; then, as if breath could be held no longer, it burst into a cheer that made the old town ring from slate to slate.

Mistress Leigh stopped suddenly.

“I had forgotten, Amyas. You must not let me stand in the way of your duty. Where are your men?”

“Kissed to death by this time,” Amyas answered, with a sound that tried to be mirth and failed, “all of them, that is, who are left.”

“Left?”

“We went out a hundred, mother, and we came home forty-four—if we are at home. Is it a dream? Is this you? and this old Bridgeland Street again? As I live, there stands Evans the smith at his door, tankard in hand, as he did when I was a boy!”

The brawny smith did indeed come striding across; but when he saw Amyas and no Frank, he stopped, and his comfort was clumsy as a sailor’s knot.

“Better one than neither, madam,” he said.

Amyas gripped his hand as he passed. Mistress Leigh neither heard nor saw him nor any man there.

“Mother,” Amyas went on, when they were past the causeway and the noise fell behind them like surf, “we are rich for life.”

“Yes,” she said, and the word cut, “a martyr’s death was the fittest for him.”

“I have brought home treasure untold.”

“What, my boy?”

“Treasure untold. Cary has promised to see to it to-night.”

“Very well. I would that he had slept at our house. He was a kindly lad, and loved Frank. When did he—?” Her voice faltered on the unspoken seeing of Frank, as if her mouth could not shape the past without tasting it.

“Three years ago and more. Within two months of our sailing.”

“Ah. Yes. He told me so.”

“Told you so?” Amyas stopped, bewildered.

“Yes. The dear lad has often come to see me in my sleep; but you never came.” She spoke as one speaks of bread rising or a cow calving—plain, without ornament, as if the world of spirits were only another room in the house. “I guessed how it was—as it should be.”

“But I loved you none the less, mother!”

“I know that too. But you were busy with the men, sweet; so your spirit could not come roving home like his, which was free.” She turned her head slightly toward the girl, as if drawing her into the circle of speech. “My maid—do you not find it cold here in England after those hot regions?”

The girl’s English was broken and small, but her meaning came clear.

“Ayacanora’s heart is warm. She does not think about cold.”

“Warm?” Mistress Leigh said softly. “Perhaps you will warm my heart for me, then.”

“Would God I could do it, mother!” Amyas answered, and there was a half-reproach in him, as of a man who has carried a burden too long and cannot set it down gently.

Mistress Leigh looked up full into his face. Something in it—what he had suffered, what he had feared from her—broke the last crust of her long-held composure, and she wept, violently, as if the tears were a flood at last allowed to run.

“Sinful! sinful that I am!”

“Blessed creature!” Amyas cried, and his own voice cracked with the strain. “If you speak so I shall go mad. Mother, mother—these months I have dreaded this meeting. It has hung over me like a black thunder-cloud; like a great cliff miles high, with its top hid in the clouds, which I had to climb and dared not. I have longed to leap overboard and flee from it like a coward into the depths of the sea—the thought that you might ask me whether I was not my brother’s keeper; that you might require his blood at my hands—and now, now, when it comes, to find you all love, and trust, and patience—mother, mother, it is more than I can bear!” And he wept as one who has held himself iron through battle and is broken only by kindness.

She knew him too well not to hear, in that outburst from a usually quiet nature, the sound of long wrestling with despair. At once she forgot her own pain in the one old habit of a mother: to soothe.

And she did soothe him. Home they went, arm in arm. Ayacanora clung like a child to the skirt of Mistress Leigh’s cloak; the fearless forest creature had shrunk into trembling modesty, cast suddenly upon a shore where every face was strange and every sound carried meanings she did not yet know.

“Will your mother love me?” she whispered to Amyas at the threshold.

“Yes,” he whispered back. “But you must do what she tells you.”

Ayacanora pouted, quick as a flame.

“She will laugh at me, because I am wild.”

“She never laughs at any one.”

“Humph,” said Ayacanora, and glanced sideways, measuring. “Well, I shall not be afraid of her. I thought she would have been tall like you; but she is not even as big as me.”

It did not sound promising for obedience. Yet before a day was gone, Mistress Leigh had won her utterly—not by threats, nor even by soft indulgence, but by that quiet power which rules a household without noise. Ayacanora, trying to explain in her own fashion, said she had thought so great a man must have a great mother; she had expected some dreadful princess with a frown like Juno, and had found instead—so her halting words made it—one who healed.

Her story was soon told; and Mistress Leigh, woman-like, would suffer no doubt of the girl’s right to the name Amyas had given her. And never did she imprint a prouder, tenderer kiss upon her son’s forehead than when he said simply that he had kept, unspotted like a gentleman and a Christian, the soul which God had put into his charge.

“Then you have forgiven me, mother?” he asked, like a boy again.

“Years ago,” she answered. “I said in this same room, What shall I render to the Lord for having given me two such sons? And in this room I say it once again. Tell me all about my other son, that I may honor him as I honor you.”

And then—with that iron nerve which belongs to good women—she made him give her every detail: Lucy Passmore’s tale; the long wandering; and all that had befallen them from the day they sailed until that luckless night at La Guayra. She did not flinch; she asked where she must; she forced the words, and when they were spoken she gathered them up and set them away inside her, as a housewife lays up grain against famine.

When it was done she led Ayacanora out and began to busy herself, quietly, with the girl’s comforts, as if Frank and Amyas had been sleeping in their cribs in the next room and the world had never altered. She saw to fire and water; she called for clean linen. The house filled with homely sounds—the clack of a pot-lid, the scrape of a trencher, the soft hurry of feet. The very scents of a lived-in place rose up: smoke in the rafters, wool warmed by a body, rosemary crushed when one brushed past it. After the great sea, the small household noises fell like blessings.

She had hardly gone up when the door below was struck with a loud knock—no timid visitor’s tap, but a command. It opened hastily; and into the hall burst, regardless of all manner, Sir Richard Grenville.

Tall, stately, his face cut as if from iron and his eyes like a winter sky, he came in as a storm comes. Amyas dropped on his knees by instinct, as he might have done before his captain on a deck.

But the stern warrior was unmanned. He bent over his godson; and a tear dropped from that iron cheek onto the weathered cheek of Amyas Leigh.

“My lad! my glorious lad! Where have you been?” he cried. “Get up and tell me all. The sailors told me a little, but I must hear every word. I knew you would do something grand. I told your mother you were too good a workman for God to throw away. Now—let me have the whole story. Why, I am out of breath! To tell truth, I ran three parts of the way hither.”

So they sat down, and Amyas spoke far into the night. Sir Richard, his stateliness returning as he listened, gave a grim smile here, a stern nod there, at each deed of daring; and when all was ended he answered with something like a sigh that betrayed the hunger of action in him.

“Would God that I had been with you every step! Would God, at least, that I could show as good a three-years’ log-book, Amyas, my lad!”

“You can show a better one, I doubt not.”

“Humph! With the exception of one paltry Spanish prize, I know not that the queen is the better, or her enemies the worse, for me, since we parted last in Dublin city.”

“You are too modest, sir.”

“Would that I were. I got on in Ireland no better than my neighbors, and so came home again to find that while I had been wasting time in that land of misrule, Raleigh had done a deed to which I can see no end. For he has found—his two captains, Amadas and Barlow, found for him—between Florida and Newfoundland, a country the like of which, I believe, is not on earth for climate and fertility. Whether there be gold there, I know not, and it matters little; for there is all else on earth that man can want: furs, timber, rivers, game, sugar-canes, corn, fruit; every commodity which France, Spain, or Italy can yield, growing wild in abundance. The savages civil enough for savages. In a word, a jewel fit for her majesty’s crown. The people call it Wingandacoa; but we—after her majesty—Virginia.”

“You have been there, then?” Amyas asked, and the new name tasted strange and promising in his mouth, like a spice brought from oversea.

“The year before last; and left there Ralf Lane, and Amadas, and some twenty gentlemen, and ninety men—and money of my own and of old Will Salterne’s, which neither of us may see again. For the colony, I know not how, quarrelled with the Indians—though I fear I was over-sharp with some for stealing; if I was, God forgive me!—and could not keep themselves alive for twelve months. Drake, coming back from his last West Indian voyage, after giving what help he could, had to bring the whole faint-hearted party home. And if you will believe it, they had not been gone a fortnight before I was back again with three ships and all they could want. Never was I more wroth than when all I found was the ruins of their huts, already full—so rich is the growth—of great melons, and wild deer feeding thereon. Pretty sight enough; not what I wanted then. So back I came, and being in no overgood temper, vented my humors on the Portugals at the Azores and had hard fights and small booty. So there the matter stands—but not for long. Shame it were if such a paradise, once found by Britons, should fall into any hands but her majesty’s. We will try again this spring, if men and money can be found. Eh, lad?”

“And the prize?” Amyas asked, for he knew Grenville would not have called it paltry if it had not cost him.

“Ah! That was no small make-weight to our disasters. I sighted her six days’ sail from the American coast; but ere we could lay her aboard it fell dead calm. Never a boat had I on board—they were all lost in a gale—and the other ships were becalmed two leagues astern. There was no use lying there and pounding her till she sank; so I called the carpenter, got up old chests, and with them and some spars we floated ourselves alongside—only just in time. The last of us had hardly scrambled into the chains when our crazy Noah’s ark went all aboard and sank at the side, so that if we had been minded to run away, Amyas, we could not. Whereon, judging valor the better part of discretion—as I usually do—we fell to with our swords and had her in five minutes; and fifty thousand pounds’ worth in her, which set up my purse again, and Raleigh’s too—though I fear it has run out again since as fast as it ran in.”

So ended Sir Richard’s tale; and the night wore on with the kind of talk that binds men who live by wind and steel, while in the house above, the household settled into uneasy sleep, and the river outside made its soft sucking sounds against the mud like a creature breathing.

The next day Amyas went to Salterne.

The old man had heard the bare outlines already; yet he bade Amyas sit down and tell all from the beginning, and listened with chin upon hand, elbows on knees, as if he were weighing coin. His cheek did not blanch; his lips did not quiver. Only when Amyas came to Rose’s marriage, he drew one long breath as if a weight had been lifted from his heart.

“Say that again, sir,” he said.

Amyas said it again, and then went on; and at the manner of her death his voice faltered. He hinted, as if any gentleness could soften truth.

“Go on, sir!” Salterne snapped. “Why are you afraid? There is nothing to be ashamed of there, is there?”

So Amyas told the whole with downcast eyes, and then dared a glance at the old man’s face. There was no sign of grief in it—only a proud curl at the corner of the iron mouth, as if something in him had been satisfied.

“And her husband?” Salterne asked at last, after a pause.

“I am ashamed to have to tell you, sir, that the man still lives.”

“Still lives, sir?”

“Too true, as far as I know. That it was not my fault, my story bears me witness.”

“Sir, I never doubted your will to kill him. Still lives, you say? Well, so do rats and adders.” He leaned back a little, and his eyes narrowed. “And now, I suppose, Captain Leigh, your worship is minded to recruit yourself on shore a while with the fair lass whom you have brought home—as I hear—before having another dash at the devil and his kin!”

“Do not mention that young lady’s name with mine, sir,” Amyas answered, and there was a stiff pride in him which even grief could not soften. “She is no more to me than she is to you; for she has Spanish blood in her veins.”

Salterne smiled grimly, as if that suited him well enough.

“But I am minded at least to do one thing, Mr. Salterne,” Amyas went on, “and that is, to kill Spaniards, in fair fight, by land and sea, wheresoever I shall meet them. And therefore I stay not long here, whithersoever I may be bound next.”

And now Amyas Leigh sat down again under his own roof-tree at Burrough, with the salt of many seas still in his blood, and for twelve months there was little which men would call great; yet the house itself, with its daily bread and its nightly prayers, became a kind of chronicle, and the smallest matters had in them the seeds of what was to come.

Salvation Yeo had, as if by right of storm and plank and cordage, taken up his berth among them for good. He called himself nothing so fine as steward; he asked no wages and no title; but he was everywhere, moving after Amyas like a long gray wolf-hound that has found its master again, silent-footed and watchful. By day he mended what was broken, measured what was short, and made order of what had been left to chance; by night he sat in the chimney-corner with the Bible open on his knee, while his hands, trained to rope and sail and the small contrivances of shipboard, shaped and whittled and stitched. Out of scraps of wood, bits of horn, a broken clasp, a strip of hide, he conjured up endless trifles—some of use, some merely to please the eye and occupy the fingers—buttons for Mrs. Leigh’s work-basket, a spoon with a handle carved like a fish, a little box that shut with a cunning spring, and, above all, toys for Ayacanora.

Each week, as surely as the Sabbath came round, he offered her some new trifle with a humility that was half devotion and half seaman’s awkward tenderness; and each week she received it as if he had laid a rattle in the palm of a grown woman. Her lip would go forward; she would flash those long black lashes of hers as if to say she was no child to be bribed with gewgaws; then Mrs. Leigh’s steady eye would rest on her, and the pout would soften into obedience. Ayacanora would take the gift, set it away with a strange solemnity, and—so far as any could tell—never look on it again.

For the whole heat of her heart was set upon one thought: she would be an English maid.

She ran all day after Mrs. Leigh as if the older woman’s apron-string were the thread that led out of some dark labyrinth. The kitchen and the still-room drew her like music. She would stand over the bread-trough, breathing in the warm, living smell of yeast as it rose—an odor at once sour and sweet, like new ale and bruised apples—and watch Mrs. Leigh’s hands fold and press and turn the dough until it shone. She begged to learn how to judge the oven’s temper by the sound of the draught and the color on the brick; how to lay rushes for a slow fire; how to scald milk without scorching it; how to hang herbs—sage, sweet marjoram, tansy, dried lavender—so that they kept their virtue through winter. In the still-room she was all wonder, for there the very air seemed thick with scents: rose-water cooling in glazed pans; rosemary and angelica lying cut and tied; the sharp, clean bite of juniper; the faint honey of meadowsweet. She listened to the drip from the worm of the still as if it were a language, and would ask again and again why the first run must be thrown away, and how the heart of the liquor was known by taste, not by sight.

But beyond all that, the needle held her. She would prick her fingers raw learning the tightness of a seam, the secret of a gathered sleeve, the sober patience by which linen is turned and turned again until it serves two lives. Soon she was begging cloth, not for herself only, but to make shifts for the old women in Northam, and little coats for children whose elbows shone through their rags.

This was a new thought to her, and it came, not like an argument, but like a smell caught on the wind. She had been bred among a people whose hospitality was boundless so long as it ran in the old channels—feast answered by feast, gift paid back with gift; the guest sat down to meat because some day the host would sit down to his. But to give to those who could give nothing back; to spend one’s strength on a stranger’s need with no hope of return—this was a mystery, as strange to her as the turning of milk to cheese or grape to wine.

She saw Mrs. Leigh, who might well have sat at ease after all her fears, spending each spare hour in quiet labour for the poor, and going from cottage to cottage with a basket that smelt of bread and smoked bacon and dried beans. She saw Amyas, after the public thanksgiving in church for his safe return, giving away money and food in Northam, Appledore, and Bideford; buying cottages and setting them apart for worn-out mariners, so that old men with knotted hands might die not in the ditch but by a hearth. When she asked why, she was told, simply, that this was his thank-offering to God.

A thank-offering, in her mind, had been something far other: blood, terror, skulls and bones set up to please a spirit that demanded fear. She could understand, indeed, the old instinctive English rule that something should be given to the Church itself. The vicar of Northam soon strutted, with comely pride, in a new surplice; and the altar shone with a splendid flagon and salver of plate, taken in the great galleon—a fair spoil, and holy, as men counted then. That, at least, made sense to her: if the Great Spirit was to be honoured, let his house be made brave.

But the alms still puzzled her, until Mrs. Leigh, one afternoon when rain fretted softly on the casement and the house was full of the homely hush that comes with wet weather, told her—without any flourish, as if naming the weight of flour for a loaf—that whoever gave to the poor gave to the Great Spirit; for the Great Spirit was in them, and in Ayacanora too, if she would be quiet and listen, instead of stamping and pouting and doing only what she liked.

The words sank in. The poor child took them in as children do—whole, without picking and choosing—and went at them with her hands first. She worked until her fingers ached, and her shoulders stiffened, and yet she would not stop: darning stockings, stitching seams, kneading and shaping, carrying broth in a covered pipkin so that it did not slop, learning how to thicken it with barley without lumps, and how to sweeten it for the sick with a spoon of honey when there was honey to spare. She went about with Mrs. Leigh, lovely and beloved now, because she was no longer merely a wonder from far seas but a help; and, from under her lashes, she would steal glances at Amyas, watching for the smallest sign.

On the day when she won a smile from him, she was good from morning to night, as if the sunshine had sunk into her bones. On the day when she did not, she was thoroughly naughty, and would have worn out any patience less chastened than Mrs. Leigh’s. Yet even her storms were beginning, little by little, to break themselves against that quiet woman’s constancy, as wind breaks itself against a cliff and must at last fall into the sea.

As for her finery, there was no schooling it down. Each Sunday she swept into church bedizened in Spanish bravery—silks that rustled like dry leaves, jewels that caught and threw back the thin Devon light—so that heads turned and eyes wandered and minds strayed from prayer. The good vicar, driven at last to humble remonstrance, spoke to Mrs. Leigh of the disturbance.

Ayacanora answered for herself, with the proud straightness of one who had been obeyed once: she was not thinking about them, she said; they need not think about her. And if the Piache—in plain English, the conjuror, as she took the vicar to be—wanted a present, he might have all her Mexican feather-dresses. She would not wear them; they were wild Indian things; she was an English maid. With that she darted upstairs, brought them down in a blaze of emerald and azure and crimson, and insisted so stoutly on arraying the vicar that the poor man escaped as if from a net. Yet he carried off one mantle, handsomest of all; and instead of selling it, he turned it cleverly into an altar-cloth. For years afterward, the communion at Northam was celebrated upon a splendour of colour which had once adorned the sinful body of some Aztec prince.

So she flaunted on, half child, half queen; while Amyas watched her half amused, half proud in the simple vanity of a man who has brought home a rare bird, and looked round at all gazers as much as to say, See what I have saved.

If she vexed the vicar, she vexed the neighbourhood more. The ladies came, of course, in proper troop, to congratulate Mrs. Leigh and to stare at the fair savage. The fair savage snubbed them all impartially—from the vicar’s wife to Lady Grenville herself—so that few tried a second visit.

Mrs. Leigh reasoned with her, and was met with tears. They came to look, she sobbed, as at some beast in a booth; she would not be made a show. She had been like a queen once, and all obeyed; here every one looked down on her. But when Mrs. Leigh asked, very gently, if she would rather go back to the forests, Ayacanora clung to her like a frightened child and begged not to be sent away. She would sooner be a slave in the kitchen here, she cried, than go back to the bad people.

So the months went by—foolish storms and foolish sunshine—but always under the shadow of one in whom there was neither storm nor sunshine, only a perpetual genial calm, like soft grey weather that does not dazzle and does not chill, but makes all things bearable. Under that influence the outbursts grew more rare; Ayacanora grew more rational, though no more happy; and small hints began to fall from her, as nuts fall when the bough is shaken, which made her story clearer to Mrs. Leigh and to Yeo.

When she had become used to houses, she let them see that she had seen such things before. The red Devon cattle did not startle her as much as they should have done; she watched them long, frowning, as if matching them to some older picture in her mind. The sheep puzzled her more. At last she made Mrs. Leigh understand, with a disdainful gesture, that they were too small.

“Ah, madam,” said Yeo eagerly, catching at every straw that might bind her to their guesses, “it is because she has been used to those great camel-sheep—llamas they call them—in Peru.”

But nothing woke her like a horse. The use of tame beasts at all, the very notion that a creature might labour with you rather than flee you, was daily miracle enough; but that a horse could be ridden—could carry a human as the sea carries a ship—was beyond belief. She would have a horse. Amyas, fearing for her pretty neck, put her off; she plagued him till his patience, tried by all things else, began to thin. Foiled with him, she went confidentially to Yeo and proposed they should steal one, as simply as she might have proposed snaring a deer. Foiled there, she went to the vicar and offered to barter all her finery for his broken-kneed pony. The vicar, too honest to take advantage of such madness, refused; and the matter ended with Amyas buying her a jennet.

In a fortnight she rode as if she had been born with saddle-leather under her thighs, her body answering the beast’s motion as a dancer answers a tune. She learned by ear as much as by hand: the cluck and whistle that steadied; the soft hiss that checked; the very rhythm of hoofbeats on turf that told her whether the ground was safe. Often, as she cantered, the sound seemed to draw something from deep within her—an older music, half forgotten.

That spring, at Lady Grenville’s invitation, the whole household went over to Stow. Mrs. Leigh rode soberly on a pillion behind the groom, her skirts pinned close, her hands folded; Ayacanora ranged and circled on the moor like a hound let loose, the wind combing her hair, trying to make Amyas race her. He would not. He watched her with eyes that would not confess all they felt.

That night, in the same chamber with Mrs. Leigh, Ayacanora woke shrieking. The cry tore through the quiet of the house like a gull’s scream in fog. When Mrs. Leigh caught her, she sobbed out a tale with such terror in it that it shook the listener: the “Old ape of Panama,” her especial abomination, had come to her bedside and dragged her into a courtyard; she had been forced onto a horse and ridden with an Indian over great moors and high mountains down into a dark wood. There the Indian and the horses vanished, and she found herself suddenly changed once more into a little savage child.

So strong was the impression that she could not be persuaded it had not happened, if not that night, then some night long ago. Mrs. Leigh, piecing it to other scraps, began at last to believe that the Lord had, in mercy, shown the poor child in a vision who she was; that she had been exposed in the forests by a jealous step-father; and neither Sir Richard nor his lady could doubt that this was the likeliest thread. Don Xararte—Panama was his home—might well have been at Quito; Yeo had seen him come aboard the Lima ship at Guayaquil, nearest of ports. It would explain her being found beyond Cotopaxi, the great peak of the Eastern Andes, if the old man, believing her to be Oxenham’s child, had taken so fearful a revenge as to cast her out to die.

Other facts surfaced slowly, and always by way of creature or tree or wind—as is natural in one whose mind had long been emptied of words and filled instead with hunger, cold, and the watching of beasts. Whatever her morals or affections had once been taught, had been rubbed out by the long spiritual death of the forest wandering. Mrs. Leigh could draw from her no clear tenderness for her mother, no recollection of early prayers.

Yet at last, strangely, the lost link was supplied—not by argument, but by smell and sound.

Sir Richard Grenville had brought home an Indian from Virginia. Of his first name none there were sure; but men whispered it might be that “Wanchese” whose name goes in company with “Manteo.” He was to be baptized at Bideford, and given the name of Raleigh, his sponsors being most like Sir Walter himself and Sir Richard. All Bideford came out, as folk will to any wonder: mayor and corporation-men in full robes; maces and tipstaffs bright; gossips elbowing for a sight. The church filled with the rustle of clothes and the scrape of shoes on stone, and above it all the small, living noises of a crowd that tries to behave.

Mrs. Leigh went, as a matter of course; and Ayacanora insisted on going too. She was eager to know what they would do with the “Carib.”

“To make him a Christian,” Mrs. Leigh said.

“Why did they not make her one?” Ayacanora pouted, for she could not bear that an “ugly red Carib” should be given something denied her.

Because, Mrs. Leigh told her, they were sure she was one already, and had been christened when she was born. Ayacanora was not sure; she stood there, watching, listening, trying to remember.

The stately son of the forest—now tamed into the livery of a great house—stood at the font. The service had gone on half through when a heavy sigh, almost a groan, broke from Ayacanora. She swayed and fell fainting into Mrs. Leigh’s arms.

They carried her out into a neighbouring house. When she came to herself, her face was white under its warm brown, and her eyes had the far look of one who is still half elsewhere. She told a strange story, with a minuteness that made the hair stir at the back of Yeo’s neck.

As she stood trying to recollect, the church, she said, had grown larger. The priest’s dress had become richer. The walls were covered with pictures. Above the altar, in jewelled robes, stood a lady with a babe in her arms. Soft music sounded—she insisted on that, as if the very notes still trembled in her skull. And the air—most of all the air—was full of fragrance, thick as mist: not the plain smell of bodies and damp wool, but a sweetness of incense and flowers, heavy and clinging, like crushed petals warmed. Through that scented cloud she saw not one Indian but many standing by the font; and a lady held her by the hand; and she was a little girl again.

After much questioning, so exact was her recollection, not only of the scene but of the building itself, that Yeo struck his hand on his knee.

“A christened woman she is, madam,” he said, “if Popish christening be worth the name; and she has seen Indians christened too in the Cathedral Church at Quito, the inside whereof I know well enough, and too well. For I sat there three mortal hours in a San Benito, to hear a friar preach his false doctrines, not knowing whether I should be burnt the next day.”

So Ayacanora went home to Burrough, with a new certainty laid like a seal upon her past; and Raleigh the Indian went to Sir Richard’s house.

The entry of his baptism still stands in the old parchment register at Bideford, crooked-lettered and plain, among the baptisms for 1587:

“Raleigh, a Winganditoian: March 26.”

And his name occurs once more, a year and a month after:

“Rawly, a Winganditoian, April 1589.”

But this time it is not among the baptisms. The free forest wanderer had pined in vain for cedar scents and deer-hunts, for lazy paddlings through still lagoons where water-lilies sleep under magnolias and vines hang in clustered ropes. The town’s constant clatter—carts on stone, hammers, the shut-in echo of narrow streets—had hemmed him round like a cage. He went away to what his own people might have called happier hunting-grounds, and all that was left below slept in a cramped churchyard, blocked in with dingy houses, whose tenants would never waste a sigh upon an Indian’s grave. Those two entries stand unto this day; and they are a kind of first-fruit and emblem of the sad fate of that worn-out red race, to whom what men call civilization came too late to save, and not too late to hasten decay.

Yet while Amyas lay quiet, England did not.

That spring Raleigh sent out another and larger colony to Virginia under one John White. Raleigh wrote more than once, entreating Amyas to take the command; and if he had gone, who can tell what might have been born sooner across the sea? But Amyas’s mother had bound him by a solemn promise to wait at home with her twelve months at least; and he, who could face guns and hunger, could not face her lonely fear. So he stayed, and sent instead five hundred pounds, which, for aught I know, lie in Virginia still in some fashion; for they never came back to him.

Soon a sharper trial came upon that promise—one that wore his temper thin and made him, for the first time in his life, moody, peevish, and restless. While other men fought Spaniards, he sat by his own hearth. The sound of distant drums, though he could not hear them, seemed to beat in him. And over all his thoughts there gathered a sullen malice against Don Guzman. The “single eye” began to cloud; his whole body was not full of light. He had stepped into that darkness in which every man walks who hates his brother; and it lay on him like a black shadow, in the bright mornings and at candlelight too.

Nor was there any company more fit to deepen that shadow than Salvation Yeo’s. The old man grew more stern in his fanaticism day by day, and found in Amyas too willing a listener. Mrs. Leigh, perhaps for the first and last time in her life, was seriously angry when she heard them coolly debating whether they had not committed a grievous sin in not killing the Spanish prisoners aboard the galleon. Her anger was quiet; but it was like the hard edge of a knife. There are gentlenesses that will endure much, and there are gentlenesses that will not endure cruelty, however it dresses itself in texts.

Yet, to speak plain truth, if such was the temper of Englishmen then, the Spaniards had done much to provoke it, and were attempting to do more.

For now the year 1588 drew near—the year which an astronomer of Königsberg, a hundred years before, had foretold would be admirable, and which German chronologers had called the climacterical year of the world. Men may make what they will of prophecies; but this at least came true. That year was indeed a turning, and decided once and for all the fortunes of European nations and of a whole continent across the sea.

In every great crisis there wakes in men some dim instinct that the Day of the Lord is at hand; that there is war in heaven; that fiends of darkness and angels of light are set in array, struggling for the souls of men. In that age such a feeling took readily the shape of astrological dream and planetary house, as if the stars must needs ring like bells when the world below was shaken.

But the wise needed no conjunctions to tell them what was coming. The long duel between Spain and England, fought hitherto by fits and starts—now in the Netherlands, now in the West Indies, now along the African shore—was drawing at last toward a death-grapple. Spain itself had been respected, and England likewise; trade to Spanish ports went on till, in 1585, the Spaniard, without warning, laid an embargo on all English ships coming to his European shores. They were seized, it seemed, to form part of an enormous armament, meant, so Spain said, to crush the rebellious Netherlanders; but the Queen, her council, and—when it was almost too late—the people of England believed otherwise. England was the destined victim. So, instead of negotiating to avoid fighting, they fought to produce negotiation. Drake, Frobisher, and Carlisle swept the Spanish Main with fire and sword, stopping the Indian supplies; while Walsingham, craftiest and yet most honest of mortals, by some dark turn of money-work, kept the Venetians from lending Spain the sums that would have repaired her losses. No Armada came that year.

Meanwhile the Jesuits, at home and abroad, made little secret among their own of what the Spanish armament was meant to do. The impious heretics—Drakes and Raleighs, Grenvilles and Cavendishes, Hawkinses and Frobishers—who had dared to violate that hidden sanctuary of half the globe which the Pope had bestowed on the defender of the true faith, were, so they cried, to meet shameful ruin and terrible death, when their sacrilegious barks should sink beneath the thunder of Spanish cannon, blessed by the Pope and sanctified with holy water and prayers to “God and his Mother.” England would fall with them. The proud islanders, rebels against St. Peter, should bow once more beneath the yoke of the Gospel. Their queen—illegitimate, excommunicate, contumacious, abettor of free-trade, defender of the Netherlands, pillar of false doctrine—should be sent in chains across the Alps to sue for her life at the feet of the long-suffering father of mankind, while his nominee took her throne.

They spoke of a crusade. They called on saints whose altars the English had rifled. They cried on the Virgin Queen of Heaven, whose gifts the English spurned. They set “Mary” against Elizabeth, and urged all manly hearts and swords to fight in her cause, against the impious harlot who had assumed her titles and received, from idolatrous flatterers, homage due to Mary alone; and Father Parsons, with more heat than charity, asserted that Elizabeth had even caused her own name to be substituted for Mary’s in the Litanies of the Church.

So Parsons and Allen and dozens more spoke, and spoke worse; and were somewhat surprised to find that their hearers, though granting the premises, were too dull—or too human—to follow the conclusion. The English lay Romanists, almost to a man, had hearts sounder than their heads, and could not help holding to the strange superstition that, being Englishmen, they were bound to fight for England. The Jesuits, who had boasted for years that the persecuted faithful throughout the island would rise as one man to welcome Spain and Rome, found instead that the faithful, like Demas, loved this present world: they had no objection, in the abstract, to the restoration of Popery, but they preferred some method less likely to strip them of ancestral lands and seat needy Castilians in their old houses, to treat tenants as Spain had treated Indians, and gentry as she had treated caciques.

Yet if men would not be moved by the supposed sorrows of the Queen of Heaven, there was another Mary nearer at hand, whose woes and gifts were more palpable to the carnal sense: Mary of Scotland. The English government kept her in comfort and luxury at a cost which would now be twenty thousand a year; she could employ her French jointure—perhaps fifty thousand more—in plotting the government’s ruin and the queen’s murder. If she prospered, there would be dukedoms and earldoms and lands and castles to scatter among friends. And with an angel face, a serpent tongue, and a heart—by her own confession—hard as diamond, she made weak men fall in love with her, and fancy, worse still, that she loved them.

Of her the Jesuits were not unmindful. Her sorrows made excellent stock-in-trade. She was Romanist; she was “beautiful and unfortunate,” and that virtue, like charity, hides a multitude of sins. So the poor card was played and played again, until it tore in the hand.

To all which England made at last a practical and terrible answer. From the highest noble to the lowest peasant there rose one simultaneous cry, like the sound of the sea when the wind shifts into storm: “We are weary of these seventeen years of chicanery and terror. This woman must die, or the commonweal of England perish.”

And we all know which of those two was chosen.

“Oh, where be these gay Spaniards,
Which make so great a boast O?
Oh, they shall eat the gray-goose feather,
And we shall eat the roast O!”

If some curious soul—one of those who in later centuries will come down to Plymouth for an hour’s idle looking, and then whisk home again before the meat is cold—had been turned about by a trick of time, and found the nineteenth century wiped away as clean as crumbs from a board, he would have stood blinking in the bright July of 1588, and scarcely known the place.
