Mount Edgecombe still rose green and courteous above the Sound; the same slopes took the same sunlight as if no years could touch them. But all the great iron-roofed docks were not; Devonport was a dream not yet dreamt; the breakwater that will one day lie like a giant’s arm across the water had not been laid there. Drake’s Island showed only a small raw battery, newly bitten out of the turf; Mount Wise was a solitary gentleman’s house among fields where the wind could still be heard moving through grass instead of through rigging and chains. The citadel was a toy fort, a child’s rattle beside the thunder that later engines will learn to make.

And for shipping—where a later age will choke the Hamoaze with floating towns—there were only a few lime-hoys creeping, and, packed thick in the Catwater, the lean-bellied wooden creatures that must to-morrow begin the greatest sea-fight the world has seen. Paltry enough they would look to eyes schooled by iron and steam: hulls no larger than a modest frigate of later days; metal that could not be flung a quarter so far; ropes and timber that asked for men’s muscles and men’s courage, not the turn of a valve.

Yet if the beholder were no mere gape-and-go, but one who knew by his own hands what “progress” costs—slow learning, sour disappointment, the patient tasting and re-tasting of failure until it becomes knowledge—he would not smile with contempt. He would feel, rather, that filial tenderness which the wise bear toward rough beginnings. For there was in those small ships the germ of a mightier sea-power; and to fashion even these, with the tools and charts and half-lit science of the time, demanded minds as inventive and spirits as stubborn as any that will boast afterward.

But if he were a student not of machines but of men—of voices, tempers, loyalties—he would have found no nobler field than the little terrace bowling-green behind the Pelican Inn on the afternoon of the nineteenth of July.

The Armada had been scattered by storm. Lord Howard had run out as far as the Spanish coast to seek it; then the wind shifted south, and he returned to Plymouth, uncertain whether the Dons would come on at all or slip by. The captains waited as lions might wait in their lair for the herd’s passing—yet they did not wait in silence. The Sound below gave back its living music: the clack and sing of blocks, the slap of small waves on timber, the low thunder of surf beyond, and, over all, the thin, constant piping of wind through cordage. No harsh din of industry dulled it—only the true sea-sounds that keep a man’s thoughts braced and wake.

Slip on, for a moment, the drawer’s apron, and come in through the rose-clad door from the tavern with a tray of long-necked Dutch glasses and a silver tankard of wine. The inn’s breath meets you at once—stale smoke, ale-sweetness, sweat of tarry coats drying in sun, and the sharp clean bite of salt blown in at every opening. On the terrace, men lounged over the low wall that looked down on the ships, or gathered in rings, talking earnestly, then breaking into sudden laughter as if laughter itself were a weapon.

There, in the center of one such ring, stood Walter Raleigh—soft long eyes, pointed chin, the look of a man who can taste words as others taste wine. A fair young lord in a flame-colored doublet—Lord Sheffield—had his arm about Raleigh’s neck with boyish warmth. Opposite them, stately as if a mast had learned to walk, stood Sir Richard Grenville; beside him, with a grave sailor’s authority that did not need finery to prove itself, Lord Charles Howard of Effingham, Lord High Admiral of England. Near him was his son-in-law, Sir Robert Southwell, captain of the Elizabeth Jonas.

And a little apart—short, sturdy, plainly dressed—stood the man to whom all turned when he spoke: Francis Drake. He held his cap in his hands, and the bullet head of crisp brown hair and wrinkled forehead showed plain. The cheekbones were high; the square face, broad-templed; thick lips set firm as rock. There was a coarse plebeian stamp; yet the stance—legs planted a little apart, hands clasped behind his back—said boundless determination and a self-possession that could outstare wind and cannon both. When at last he gave a few blunt words, the ring fell quiet to hear; even the gulls’ cries seemed to thin out above them.

Then, like a gust that brings laughter with it, a burly elder in greasy sea-stained garments came waddling up as if he had been born in a gale and never once reached calm water. The upper half of his sharp, dogged face looked like brick-red leather; the lower, like badger’s fur. A huge gold chain lay absurdly splendid against his tarry coat. He clapped Drake on the back and shouted in a broad Devon twang, “Be you a coming to drink your wine, Francis Drake, or be you not?—saving your presence, my lord;” and Lord Howard only laughed and bade Drake go and drink, for John Hawkins, Port Admiral, patriarch of Plymouth seamen, was in a temper of overflowing good-humor such as even his rival Drake could not resist. The prospect of Armageddon on the morrow had shaken him out of crabbed reserve, and made him loud and merry as a man who has already tasted victory.

They pushed through the crowd; and there were faces enough to have made a later historian forget his own name with longing. Martin Frobisher and John Davis sat on a bench, smoking tobacco from long silver pipes, the smoke hanging blue and sweet in the warm air. Near them were Fenton and Withrington—men who had tried to follow Drake round the world and failed, through no cowardice. George Fenner, Leicester’s pet, pledged them better luck next time. That short prim man in the huge yellow ruff, with sharp chin, minute imperial, and self-satisfied smile, was Richard Hawkins, Admiral John’s son, hereafter famous and hapless. He talked with his uncle William—of whom one day a monument will speak fair words in Deptford Church: “a worshipper of the true religion, an especial benefactor of poor sailors, a most just arbiter in most difficult causes, and of a singular faith, piety, and prudence.”

There, too, was John Drake, Sir Francis’ brother; there George, his nephew, not overwise, who had been round the world with Amyas. And there was Amyas himself, speaking low in a corner to Captain Barker of Bristol—answering him in fierce curt sentences, as a man answers when he counts injuries like coins and means to spend them. Barker, brother to hapless Andrew Barker who perished in Honduras twelve years ago because mutiny made him weak, now captained the Victory, one of the queen’s best ships; he had accounts with the Spaniards to settle. Amyas had as many, and more personal. While others jested and drank, those two growled together under the vine’s shade as dogs do before a fight, saving their teeth.

Against the sunny south wall of the tavern sat an aged man on a bench, as much an object of wonder as any ship in harbor. His long white beard flowed almost to his waist; his hands lay on his knees; his palsied head moved slowly from side to side to catch scraps of talk. A little maid of six, his great-grandchild, had laid her curly head on his knees; and his granddaughter, a buxom black-eyed woman of thirty, stood by tending him—half nurse, half showman—fending off well-meaning hands with the same plea again and again: “Bless you, sir, please now, don’t give him no liquor, poor old soul, the doctor says.”

It was old Martin Cockrem, father of the ancient host—aged beyond the common years of man—who could remember the bells of Plymouth ringing for Henry the Eighth’s coronation, and who had been, perhaps, the first Englishman to set foot in the New World. He sat there like a primeval stone tor among rich crops: the living memory of a vanished tongue of time. Around him the captains drifted and stared, as men look at a relic and yet cannot quite read the inscription.

Old Martin seemed uneasy. His rheumy eyes peered through the groups; his head tilted, listening, as if his life depended on a particular sound.

“There ’a be again! Why don’t ’a come, then?”

“Quiet, gramfer,” said the granddaughter, smoothing his shoulder, “and don’t trouble his worship.”

“Here an hour, and never speak to poor old Martin! I say, sir—” and the old man feebly plucked at Amyas Leigh’s cloak as he passed. “I say, captain, do ’e tell young master old Martin’s looking for him.”

“Marcy, gramfer, where’s your manners?” the woman murmured, laughing and blushing with the embarrassment of affection. “Don’t be vexed, sir; he’m a’most a babe, and tejous at times, mortal.”

“Young master who?” asked Amyas, bending, smiling to the nurse to let the old man have his way.

“Master Hawkins; he’m never been a-near me all day.”

Amyas, of course, caught at young Richard Hawkins’ sleeve; but the nurse caught at his, too, laughing now outright.

“No, sir, not Mr. Richard, sir; Admiral John, sir—his father. He always calls him young master, poor old soul!” And she pointed to the grizzled beard and weather-bitten face of the Port Admiral.

Amyas went to Hawkins and gave the message.

“Mercy on me! Where be my wits? Iss, I’m a-coming,” cried the old hero in his broadest Devon. He waddled off to Martin Cockrem, fumbling in a pocket with comic helplessness. “Here, Martin, I’ve got mun, I’ve got mun, man alive; but his Lordship keept me so. Lookee here, then! Why, I do get so lusty of late, Martin, I can’t get to my pockets!”

Out came, one after another, as if the pocket were a pantry stuffed without order: a piece of tarred string; a bundle of papers; a thimble; a lump of pudding-tobacco; and at last—prized above all—a little paper of Muscovado sugar, then a delicacy fit to make a poor mouth think itself in heaven. He thrust it into old Martin’s trembling hand.

Old Martin dipped his finger, rubbed the brown sweetness on his toothless gums, smiling and nodding his thanks; and the little maid at his knee, unrebuked, took her share with serious devotion, as children take sacrament when they do not know the word for it.

“There, Admiral Leigh,” Hawkins said, looking from the old man to the child, “both ends meet—gramfers and babies! You and I shall be like to that one day, young Samson!”

“We shall have slain a good many Philistines first, I hope,” said Amyas.

“Amen! so be it; but look to mun! so fine a sailor as ever drank liquor; and now greedy after a hit of sweet trade! ’Tis piteous like. But I bring mun a hit whenever I come, and he looks for it. He’s one of my own flesh like, is old Martin. He sailed with my father, Captain Will, when they was both two little cracks aboard of a trawler; and my father went up, and here I am—he didn’t, and there he is. We’m up now, we Hawkinses. We may be down again some day.”

“Never, I trust,” said Amyas.

“’Tain’t no use trusting, young man: you go and do. I do hear too much of that there from my lad. Let they ministers preach till they’m black in the face—works is the trade!” And he nudged Amyas in the ribs. “Faith can’t save, nor charity nether. There, you tell with him, while I go play bowls with Drake. He’ll tell you a sight of stories. You ask him about good King Hal, now—just—”

And off he went, leaving behind him the soft, granular scent of sugar and the quieter music of old age breathing.

“You have seen good King Henry, then, father?” Amyas asked, sitting down beside Martin Cockrem.

The old man’s eyes lit as if someone had opened a shutter in his head; he stopped mumbling his sugar.

“Seed mun? Iss, I reckon. I was with Captain Will when he went to meet the Frenchman there to Calais—at the Field, the Field—”

“The Field of the Cloth of Gold, gramfer,” the granddaughter supplied.

“That’s it. Seed mun? Iss, fegs. Oh, he was a king! The face o’ mun like a rising sun, and the back o’ mun so broad as that there—” and he held out his palsied arms—“and the voice of mun! Oh, to hear mun swear if he was merry—oh, ’tas royal!—Seed mun? Iss, fegs! And I’ve seed mun do what few has; I’ve seed mun christle like any child.”

“What—cry?” Amyas said, startled.

“I shouldn’t have thought there was much cry in him.”

“You think what you like—”

“Gramfer, gramfer, don’t you be rude, now—”

“Let him go on,” said Amyas gently; for there was a gravity in the old man’s voice beneath the rustic words, as if the very soundscape of memory—cries, splash, wind—had been preserved in him when other things had rotted away.

“I seed mun christle; and, oh dear, how he did put hands on mun’s face; and ‘Oh, my gentlemen,’ says he, ‘my gentlemen! Oh, my gallant men!’ Them was his very words.”

“But when?”

“Why, Captain Will had just come to the Hard—that’s to Portsmouth—to speak with mun, and the barge Royal lay again the Hard—so; and our boot alongside—so; and the king he standth as it might be there, above my head, on the quay edge, and she come in near abreast of us, looking most royal to behold, poor dear! and went to cast about. And Captain Will, saith he, ‘Them lower ports is cruel near the water;’ for she had not more than a sixteen inches to spare in the nether overloop, as I heard after. And saith he, ‘That won’t do for going to windward in a say, Martin.’ And as the words came out of mun’s mouth, your worship, there was a bit of a flaw from the westward, sharp like, and overboard goeth my cap, and hitth against the wall; and as I stooped to pick it up, I heard a cry, and it was all over!”

“He is telling of the Mary Rose, sir,” the granddaughter said softly, as if naming a dead person.

“I guessed so,” said Amyas.

“All over: and the cry of mun, and the screech of mun! Oh, sir, up to the very heavens! And the king he screeched right out like any maid, ‘Oh my gentlemen, oh my gallant men!’ and as she lay on her beam-ends, sir, and just a-settling, the very last souls I seen was that man’s father, and that man’s. I knowed mun by their armor.”

He pointed—shaking finger—toward Sir George Carew and Sir Richard Grenville.

“Iss! Iss! Drowned like rattens. Drowned like rattens!”

“Now; you mustn’t trouble his worship any more,” the granddaughter murmured.

“Trouble? Let him tell till midnight; I shall be well pleased,” Amyas answered; and called, “Drawer! ale—and a parcel of tobacco.”

The drawer brought ale that smelled of malt and cask-wood and the faint sour of yesterday; and tobacco whose leaf, when crumbled, gave a pungent green-brown odor that clung to the fingers. Amyas settled himself to listen while old Martin, comforted by attention and sweetness, purred half to himself:

“Iss. They likes to hear old Martin. All the captains look upon old Martin.”

“Hillo, Amyas!” cried Cary, coming up. “Who’s your friend? Here’s a man been telling me wonders about the River Plate. We should go thither for luck there next time.”

“River Plate?” said old Martin, lifting his head like a dog that hears its name. “It’s I knows about the River Plate; none so well. Who’d ever been there, nor heard of it nether, before Captain Will and me went, and I lived among the savages a whole year; and audacious civil I found ’em if they’d had but shirts to their backs. And so was the prince o’ mun, that Captain Will brought home to King Henry; leastwise he died on the voyage; but the wild folk took it cruel well, for you see, we was always as civil with them as Christians, and if we hadn’t been, I should not have been here now.”

“What year was that?” Amyas asked.

“In the fifteen thirty: but I was there afore, and learnt the speech o’ mun; and that’s why Captain Will left me to a hostage, when he tuked their prince.”

“Before that?” Cary said. “Why, the country was hardly known before that.”

Old Martin’s eyes flashed in triumph, as if some deep, half-forgotten dialect of pride had been spoken to him.

“Knowed? Iss, and you may well say that! Look ye here! Look to mun!” He waved his hand round at the captains. “There’s captains! and I’m the father of ’em all now, now poor Captain Will’s in gloory; I, Martin Cockrem! Iss, I’ve seen a change.”

His voice wandered, catching at memories by their sounds: bells, battle-cries, the crackle of burning ships.

“I mind when Tavistock Abbey was so full o’ friars, and goolden idols, and sich noxious trade, as ever was a wheat-rick of rats. I mind the fight off Brest in the French wars—Oh, that was a fight, surely!—when the Regent and the French Carack were burnt side by side, being fast grappled, you see, because of Sir Thomas Knivet; and Captain Will gave him warning as he ran a-past us, saying, says he—”

“But,” Amyas said, gently steering him back, “what do you mind about America?”

“America? I should think so! But I was a-going to tell you of the Regent—and seven hundred Englishmen burnt and drowned in her, and nine hundred French in the Brest ship, besides what we picked up. Oh dear! But about America.”

“Yes, about America. How are you the father of all the captains?”

“How? you ask my young master! Why, before the fifteen thirty, I was up the Plate with Cabot (and a cruel fractious ontrustful fellow he was, like all they Portingals), and bid there a year and more, and up the Paraguaio with him, diskivering no end; whereby, gentles, I was the first Englishman, I hold, that ever sot a foot on the New World, I was!”

“Then here’s your health, and long life, sir!” said Amyas; and Cary echoed it.

“Long life? Iss, fegs, I reckon, long enough a’ready!” Old Martin chuckled, and the sound was like dry leaves. “Why, I mind the beginning of it all, I do. I mind when there wasn’t a master mariner to Plymouth, that thought there was aught west of the Land’s End except herrings.”

He spoke now like a man tasting old words—words that belonged to a Plymouth that was gone, a Plymouth that spoke a rougher music, with turns and vowels that no courtier could swallow.

“Why, they held them, pure wratches, that if you sailed right west away far enough, you’d surely come to the edge, and fall over cleve. Iss—’Twas dark parts round here, till Captain Will arose; and the first of it I mind was inside the bar of San Lucar, and he and I were boys about a ten year old, aboord of a Dartmouth ship, and went for wine, and there come in over the bar he that was the beginning of it all.”

“Columbus?” said Amyas.

“Iss, fegs, he did, not a pistol-shot from us; and I saw mun stand on the poop, so plain as I see you. No great shakes of a man to look to nether; there’s a sight better here, to plase me. And we was disappointed, we lads, for we surely expected to see mun with a goolden crown on, and a sceptre to a’s hand, we did, and the ship o’ mun all over like Solomon’s temple for gloory.”

He licked the last grains of sugar from his finger, as if the sweetness helped the memory along.

“And I mind that same year, too, seeing Vasco da Gama, as was going out over the bar, when he found the Bona Speranza, and sailed round it to the Indies. Ah, that was the making of they rascally Portingals, it was! And our crew told what they seen and heerd: but nobody minded sich things. ’Twas dark parts, and Popish, then; and nobody knowed nothing, nor got no schooling, nor cared for nothing, but scrattling up and down alongshore like to prawns in a pule.”

His voice rose into a kind of rough chant, half Scripture, half seaman’s exultation, and the sounds of the harbor seemed to answer him—the wind in the vines, the distant hammering on shipboard, the creak of leather as a captain shifted his stance.

“Iss, sitting in darkness, we was, and the shadow of death, till the day-spring from on high arose, and shined upon us poor out-o’-the-way folk—The Lord be praised! And now, look to mun!” His hand swept round again, trembling with joy. “Look to mun! Look to the works of the Lord! Look to the captains! Oh blessed sight! And one’s been to the Brazils, and one to the Indies, and the Spanish Main, and the North-West, and the Rooshias, and the Chinas, and up the Straits, and round the Cape, and round the world of God, too, bless His holy name; and I seed the beginning of it; and I’ll see the end of it too, I will!”

He leaned forward, peering seaward as if his eyes could reach beyond the Sound to the very edge of Spain.

“I was born into the old times: but I’ll see the wondrous works of the new, yet, I will! I’ll see they bloody Spaniards swept off the seas before I die, if my old eyes can reach so far as outside the Sound. I shall, I knows it. I says my prayers for it every night; don’t I, Mary? You’ll bate mun, sure as Judgment, you’ll bate mun! The Lord’ll fight for ye. Nothing’ll stand against ye. I’ve seed it all along—ever since I was with young master to the Honduras. They can’t bide the push of us! You’ll bate mun off the face of the seas, and be masters of the round world, and all that therein is. And then, I’ll just turn my old face to the wall, and depart in peace, according to His word.”

Then, as if the whole prophecy were no more than steam off a pot-lid, he blinked, looked down, and the childlike bathos of hunger and sweetness took him again.

“Deary me, now, while I’ve been telling with you, here’ve this little maid been and ate up all my sugar!”

“I’ll bring you some more,” Amyas said; and though some laughed, his own throat tightened, for he heard in the old man’s speech the sound of a whole lifetime that could not be translated into any other mouth—not into court English, not into Latin, not into the brave printed words of chronicles. It was Martin Cockrem’s alone, and it was vanishing even as it was spoken.

“Will ye, then?” said Martin, brightening. “There’s a good soul, and come and tell with old Martin. He likes to see the brave young gentlemen, a-going to and fro in their ships, like Leviathan, and taking of their pastime therein. We had no such ships to our days. Ah, ’tis grand times, beautiful times surely—and you’ll bring me a bit sugar?”

“You were up the Plate with Cabot?” Cary asked, after a pause. “Do you mind the fair lady Miranda, Sebastian de Hurtado’s wife?”

“What! her that was burnt by the Indians?” Old Martin’s face changed as if a shadow had passed over sun. “Mind her? Do you mind the sun in heaven? Oh, the beauty! Oh, the ways of her! Oh, the speech of her! Never was, nor never will be! And she to die by they villains; and all for the goodness of her! Mind her? I minded naught else when she was on deck.”

“Who was she?” Amyas asked Cary.

“A Spanish angel, Amyas.”

“Humph!” Amyas said. “So much the worse for her, to be born into a nation of devils.”

“They’em not all so bad as that, yer honor,” old Martin muttered, with a stubborn fairness that had outlived storms. “Her husband was a proper gallant gentleman, and kind as a maid, too, and couldn’t abide that De Solis’s murderous doings.”

“His wife must have taught it him, then,” Amyas said, rising. “Where did you hear of these black swans, Cary?”

“I have heard of them, and that’s enough,” Cary answered, and would not stir the sad recollection further.

“And little enough,” Amyas said sharply. “Will, don’t talk to me. The devil is not grown white because he has trod in a lime-heap.”

“Or an angel black because she came down a chimney,” Cary returned; and the talk might have died there, as such talks do, with the taste of bitterness left on the tongue.

But across the terrace it was cut short by a sudden explosion from old John Hawkins—like a culverin fired too close, startling birds from the vines.

“Fail? Fail? What a murrain do you here, to talk of failing? Who made you a prophet, you scurvy, hang-in-the-wind, croaking, white-livered son of a corby-crow?”

“Heaven help us, Admiral Hawkins—who has put fire to your culverins in this fashion?” Lord Howard called, half laughing, half warning.

“Who? my lord! Croakers! my lord! Here’s a fellow calls himself the captain of a ship, and her majesty’s servant, and talks about failing, as if he were a Barbican loose-kirtle trying to keep her apple-squire ashore! Blurt for him, sneak-up! say I.”

“Admiral John Hawkins,” said the offender, stiff with affront, “you shall answer this language with your sword.”

“I’ll answer it with my foot; and buy me a pair of horn-tips to my shoes, like a wraxling man. Fight a croaker? Fight a frog, an owl! I fight those that dare fight, sir!”

“Sir, sir, moderate yourself,” Lord Howard said, stepping in with the easy authority of a man used to quarrels among brave men. “I am sure this gentleman will show himself as brave as any, when it comes to blows: but who can blame mortal man for trembling before so fearful a chance as this?”

And now began that long sea-fight, twelve days of iron weather and salt smoke, in which men’s souls, like their victual, were tried by fire; and it was to decide, not only who should command the Narrow Seas, but what tongue should be spoken, and what prayers should be said, from half Europe to the unmeasured coasts beyond the Atlantic.

The first gun was a proclamation. Lord Howard sent ahead the little pinnace called the Defiance, and she, like a sharp-tongued herald, denounced war with the crack and cough of her ordnance. Then the Ark Royal—some called her the Triumph—came in, closing to musket-shot, her broadside answering the Spaniards’ in a steady thunder, and fell upon what was taken for the Spanish admiral, though it proved to be Alfonso de Leon’s ship. Drake and Hawkins and Frobisher played on the hindmost squadron under Recalde; and soon the Spaniards learned what nimbleness meant when it was built of English oak and handled by men who had grown up with tide-rips in their ears.

Medina Sidonia drew his ships into a half-moon, striving to go forward in stately order, as if sheer bulk and sanctity could thrust aside the smaller craft that ringed him. The Armada moved like a herd with consecrated banners—solemn, stubborn, and blind to the quick teeth at its flank. But those “wolves” were hunters, and they had wind for a horse and powder for a spur. In the first short hours, pride leaked away from many a Spanish deck like wine from a split cask.

One of the great galliasses was already riddled; shot had splintered the trim “pulpits” and chapels set upon her, and the friars who had come to bless and boast were made suddenly mortal in a spray of oak and cordage. The fleet must close round her, or Drake and Hawkins would sink her outright. In the confusion the “principal galleon of Seville,” with Pedro de Valdez and a crowd of blue-blooded Dons, ran foul of her neighbor, carried away her foremast, and—Spanish chivalry notwithstanding—was left behind, wallowing helpless, the sea already beginning to take possession of her.

It did not look like victory. Yet the Spaniards still had Our Lady, and saints in plenty, and the dreadful paper-bull that thundered doom; and it was held blasphemy to doubt that Heaven was a Spaniard. Meanwhile, if this was their showing against a third of the English fleet, what would they do when the belated ships whitening the blue between the Mewstone and the fight came up to add their mouths of fire?

So ended the first day. Hardly an English ship, hardly a man, was hurt. Something broke in English minds that day—the old spell of Spain’s invincibleness—and it did not mend again. Lord Howard’s running fight, urged by Raleigh and Drake, was justified to the last splinter; for had they “clapped ships together without consideration,” the lesser and quicker must have been crushed under sheer weight of men.

Amyas Leigh, with the Bideford ships, had been busy for two hours knocking holes in a huge galleon which bore, high on her poop, the painted figure of a maiden with a wheel, and on her sprit a coat of arms that was shot away early. She was called the Sta. Catharina. Whether those arms had been De Soto’s or no Amyas could not tell; but he did not need heraldry to fix his hate. When at last the admiral’s signal called him off, he went to his hammock and slept as soundly as a boy, his body well spent with honest labour, his mind full of a darker appetite.

But before he had been an hour below, Cary came down and shook him up.

“We were to follow Drake’s lantern, Amyas; but where it is, I can’t see—unless he’s been taken up aloft among the stars for a new Drakium Sidus.”

Amyas turned out grumbling. There was no lantern to be seen—only, far off, a sudden explosion and a great fire on board some Spaniard, that flared like a kitchen chimney when fat catches, and then was gradually got under. All that night they lay-to with the fleet, the sea full of creaking yards and the low complaining of cordage, and, underneath, the eternal hush of the deep listening.

Next morning found them off Torbay. A pinnace came alongside, and brought a letter from Drake. It ran, with spelling as free as the man’s fortune, somewhat thus:

“DEAR LAD,—I have been wool-gathering all night after five great hulks, which the Pixies transfigured overnight into galleons, and this morning again into German merchantmen. I let them go with my blessing; and coming back, fell in (God be thanked!) with Valdez’ great galleon; and in it good booty, which the Dons his fellows had left behind, like faithful and valiant comrades, and the Lord Howard had let slip past him, thinking her deserted by her crew. I have sent to Dartmouth a sight of noblemen and gentlemen, maybe a half-hundred; and Valdez himself, who when I sent my pinnace aboard must needs stand on his punctilios, and propound conditions. I answered him, I had no time to tell with him; if he would needs die, then I was the very man for him; if he would live, then, buena querra. He sends again, boasting that he was Don Pedro Valdez, and that it stood not with his honor, and that of the Dons in his company. I replied, that for my part, I was Francis Drake, and my matches burning. Whereon he finds in my name salve for the wounds of his own, and comes aboard, kissing my fist, with Spanish lies of holding himself fortunate that he had fallen into the hands of fortunate Drake, and much more, which he might have kept to cool his porridge. But I have much news from him (for he is a leaky tub); and among others, this, that your Don Guzman is aboard of the Sta. Catharina, commandant of her soldiery, and has his arms flying at her sprit, beside Sta. Catharina at the poop, which is a maiden with a wheel, and is a lofty built ship of 3 tier of ordnance, from which God preserve you, and send you like luck with.

“Your deare Friend and Admirall,

“F. Drake.

“She sails in this squadron of Recalde. The Armada was minded to smoke us out of Plymouth; and God’s grace it was they tried not: but their orders from home are too strait, and so the slaves fight like a bull in a tether, no farther than their rope, finding thus the devil a hard master, as do most in the end. They cannot compass our quick handling and tacking, and take us for very witches. So far so good, and better to come. You and I know the length of their foot of old. Time and light will kill any hare, and they will find it a long way from Start to Dunkirk.”

“The admiral is in a gracious humour, Leigh, to have vouchsafed you so long a letter,” Cary said, eyeing Amyas, who read the lines about Don Guzman twice, as a man tastes a bitter herb to be certain of its sting.

“St. Catherine! Why, that was the galleon we hammered all yesterday!” Amyas stamped, as if the deck itself had cheated him.

“Of course it was. We shall find her again,” Cary answered. “That cunning old Drake—how he has contrived to line his own pockets, even while keeping the whole fleet waiting.”

“He has given the lord high admiral the dor,” Amyas growled.

“Lord Howard is too high-hearted to stop and plunder, Papist though he is.”

Amyas answered with a sound that was not assent. He worshipped Drake; and for Papists he had little justice.

They did not find Lord Howard till nightfall. He and Lord Sheffield had held on the whole night after the Spanish lanterns with two ships only; and whatever men had muttered before, there could be no doubting now the loyalty of English Roman Catholics, for the Howards fought as if to wash suspicion away in blood. A great Spaniard drifted by, deserted and partly burnt, smelling of scorched pitch and wet ash. Some of Amyas’s men looked at her as at a larder thrown open by Providence; but Amyas refused.

“I have come out to fight, and not to plunder. Let the nearest ship take her luck without my grudging.”

They passed on. The men made long faces when they saw the next neighbour snap her up and tow her toward Weymouth; and she proved to be Miguel d’Oquenda’s, the vice-admiral’s, which they had seen the night before all but blown up by a desperate Netherland gunner, who, being “misused,” had chosen to pay old scores in a flash and roar.

So ended the second day, and Portland rose clearer each hour.

The next morning they were off the island. Would the Spaniards try Portsmouth, though they had spared Plymouth? The wind shifted to the north and blew clear and cool off Weymouth Bay, smelling of chalk and short grass, and—between the gun-smokes—carrying plainly the cries of gulls and the thin piping of shore-birds. The Spaniards turned and faced the English, meaning to stand off and on till the wind changed and then to try for the Needles. At least, they should have work to do before rounding Purbeck.

The English went westward—only to come back on the opposite tack; and then began the chess-play for the weather-gage. It did not last long. Before noon the English slipped close-hauled between the Armada and the land, and came down upon them right before the wind.

Now began a fight most fierce and fell. It went confusedly, fortunes changing with every shift of sail and heave of sea. English ships rescued the ships of London when Spaniards hemmed them in; Spaniards delivered Recalde when he was hard pressed. The Spaniards’ shot flew for the most part over the English without harm, while English gunnery, leveled and deliberate, tore into towering sides as a cook’s knife lays open a great fish, straight to the bone. One Cock, an Englishman—Devon claims him—died with honour in the midst of enemies in his small ship, swallowed by the smoke as by a sudden night.

Lord Howard fought amid the enemy’s fleet; and seeing Fenner far off, cried out, “O George, what doest thou? Wilt thou now frustrate my hope and opinion conceived of thee? Wilt thou forsake me now?” The words stung like vinegar in a wound. Fenner came in and did the part of a most valiant captain; as did all, for shame and love of England are sharp seasonings, and make poor fare rich.

Night fell on that floating volcano. Morning found them far past Purbeck, the white peak of Freshwater ahead. Now, from all havens, flocked the chivalry of England in vessels fitted at their own cost, as to a set field where immortal fame might be won—Oxford, Northumberland, Cumberland, Pallavicini, Brooke, Carew, Raleigh, Blunt, and many another name that had been spoken by hearths and in halls. Spain too had staked her noble houses: not a great family of Arragon or Castile but had lent a brother or a son—and would have cause to mourn.

Lord Howard sent light craft into Portsmouth for ammunition; but the wind fell dead, and all the evening both fleets drifted helpless with the tide and shouted idle defiance, trumpet answering trumpet, fife and drum calling across the water. Those sounds mattered. A sea without wind becomes a great ear; every beat of a drum goes farther than it should, and the nerves of men tighten to it. The Spaniards’ music, bright with bells and measured chants, tried to make a market of devotion; the English answered with their own rougher cries and the hard clink of tackle, as if the very ships refused to be lulled.

The sun went down on a glassy sea and rose on a glassy sea again. It was the twenty-fifth—St. James’s-day, sacred to Spain’s patron. Would nothing be attempted in his honour? The galleys should attack; and in their van three of the great galliasses thrashed the sea to foam with three hundred oars apiece. If any saint rode before them, he rode in the mind, not on the water.

Out were towed Lord Howard’s Triumph, his brother’s Lion, Southwell’s Elizabeth Jonas, Lord Sheffield’s Bear, Barker’s Victory, and Fenner’s Leicester, to meet them; and they greeted the galliasses with chain-shot that smashed oars and cut rigging till those proud engines of Rome’s confidence became crippled creatures, wallowing and helpless. Had not the wind sprung up again toward noon and the Spanish fleet come up to rescue them, they had shared the fate of Valdez and the Biscayan.

Then the fight became general. Frobisher beat down the Spanish admiral’s mainmast. Mexia and Recalde fell on him, and Lord Howard rescued him; and Howard, endangered in turn, was rescued in turn, while the afternoon thundered under Freshwater’s virgin cliffs. Sea-fowl rose screaming from every ledge, dark wings fretting the chalk; a lone shepherd hurried down the slopes, forgetting the wheatear fluttering in his snare, to peer over the dizzy edge. What he heard first was not the sight but the sound: a long rolling concussion that turned the air into a drum, and then the smaller, crueler noises—splintering wood, shouts snatched away by wind, the hiss of shot. Smoke lay in a league-broad veil, sulphurous and greasy; through it, masts and flags pierced and vanished like thoughts men cannot hold.

So fared St. James’s-day, as Baal’s did on Carmel. If the saint answered, it was only with English cannon. The Armada gathered itself into a roundel and would fight no more, but made the best of its way to Calais, where perhaps French help might be ready, and then to Dunkirk to join Parma and the Netherlands flotilla.

A fair Etesian gale followed clear out of the south-southwest. Past Brighton cliffs and Beachy Head, Hastings and Dungeness, the two great fleets glided. Was it battle or triumph? For Lord Howard, by sea, seemed almost to reward rather than to fight; and after Lord Thomas Howard, Lord Sheffield, Townsend, and Frobisher had received knighthood, old Admiral Hawkins knelt and rose Sir John, and shook his shoulders after the accolade, saying the “old woman will hardly know herself again, when folks call her My Lady.”

Along the shore the cliffs were lined with pike-men and musketeers, and with every countryman and groom who could bear arms, led by squires and sheriffs, marching eastward toward Dover. Nor were they alone. From miles inland came women and children and aged folk in wagons, to join their feeble shouts—and their prayers, which were not feeble—to that great cry of faith and fear that rose from Britain’s spectators.

Let them pray on. The danger was not over. Howard had news from Newhaven that the Guises would not stir; and Seymour and Winter had left their watch on the Flemish shore to swell the fleet to a hundred and forty sail—slightly more than the Spaniards in number, but not half the tonnage nor a third the men. The Spaniards were battered and dispirited, but not broken. When they slid to anchor in Calais Roads on the Saturday evening of that memorable week, prudent men knew England’s hour had come, and that the bells which would call all Christendom to church upon the morrow would sound either death-knell or triumph.

Solemn was that Sabbath, in town and country. Many a light-hearted coward who had scoffed at the Armada because he dared not face the thought gave himself now to abject fear, seeing and hearing what he would not believe. Many a brave man, kneeling beside wife and daughters, felt his heart sink to the pavement at the thought of what those beloved might endure from a profligate and fanatical soldiery, or from the cold, patient cruelty of the Inquisition. St. Bartholomew’s, Smithfield’s fires, the immolation of the Moors, the extermination of West Indians—these were not dim histories then, but recent wounds, still raw in memory. Men knew what language might be forced into their mouths, what prayers might be forbidden them, what names might be erased, as if a nation could be silenced like a parish whose old words are outlawed by a conqueror’s lexicon.

And none knew it better than she who guided that devoted land, the especial mark of the invaders’ fury; and who, by what men then not unwisely held for a Divine inspiration, devised the daring stroke that would anticipate the coming blow.

But where was Amyas Leigh, in all this great hazard and glory?

Day after day he sought the Sta. Catharina in the thickest press, and could not come at her, could not even hear of her. One moment he dreaded she had sunk by night and balked him of his prey; the next, that she had repaired her damages and would escape him after all. He grew moody, discontented, restless—peevish even with his men, for the first time in his life. He could speak of nothing but Don Guzman. At every spare moment he took his sword from the sheath and handled it—fondling it, talking to it, bidding it not fail him in the day of vengeance, as if steel could understand the dialect of a man’s wrath when the man himself scarcely could.

At last he sent to Squire the armorer for a whetstone, and, half-ashamed of his own folly, whetted and polished the blade in bye-corners. The stone rasped with a dry, hungry sound; the rhythm of it, back and forth, became his private litany, harsher than any chant from the Spanish fleet. He muttered to himself, words that were scarcely words—more like the broken syllables of hate, the one tongue he would not lose. That fixed thought of selfish vengeance possessed his whole mind. He forgot England’s need, her triumph, his own safety—everything but his brother’s blood.

And yet this was the day for which he had been longing since he brought home the magic horn as a boy of fifteen: the day when he should stand face to face with an invader, and that invader, as he believed, Antichrist himself. He had held for years with Drake and Hawkins and Grenville and Raleigh that he was sent into the world to fight the Spaniard; and he was fighting now, for such a stake as he would never see again—and yet he was not content.

While throughout the fleet whole crews received the Communion side by side and rose with cheerful faces to shake hands, rejoicing that they were sharers in Britain’s Salamis, Amyas turned away from the holy elements.

“I cannot communicate, Sir John. Charity with all men? I hate, if ever man hated on earth.”

“You hate the Lord’s foes only, Captain Leigh.”

“No, Jack. I hate my own as well.”

“But no one in the fleet, sir?”

“Don’t try to put me off with the same Jesuit’s quibble which that false knave Parson Fletcher invented for one of Doughty’s men, to drug his conscience when he was plotting against his own admiral. No, Jack. I hate one of whom you know; and somehow that hatred keeps me from loving any human being. I am in love and charity with no man, Sir John Brimblecombe—not even with you. Go your ways in God’s name, sir, and leave me and the devil alone together, or you’ll find my words are true.”

Jack departed with a sigh. While the crew received the Communion on deck—bread broken, wine passed, the sea-wind carrying the low responses—Amyas sat below in the cabin sharpening his sword. The smell down there was of oil and steel and the closed warmth of timber, as different from the open, briny air above as a pantry is from a field. When he had done, he called for a boat and went aboard Drake’s ship to ask news of the Sta. Catharina. Across the water came the Spanish chants and the tinkling bells, neat and insistent, as if holiness could be made by sound alone and sold by the measure. Amyas listened scowling, his jaw set as tight as a clenched fist.

At last Drake was summoned by the lord admiral and returned with a secret commission that should bear fruit that night. Amyas, who had gone with him, helped him till nightfall; and then returned to his own ship as Sir Amyas Leigh, Knight, to the joy and glory of every soul aboard—save his moody self, who felt the new title sit upon him like a fresh chain.

So all that livelong summer Sabbath, before the little high-walled town and the long range of yellow sandhills, lay those two mighty armaments, scowling at each other, hardly out of gunshot. Messenger after messenger hurried toward Bruges to the Duke of Parma, begging for light craft that might follow these nimble English better than Spain’s floating castles, and, above all, entreating him to put to sea at once with all his force.

But the duke was not at Dunkirk. He was inland, on the future field of Waterloo, paying devotions to St. Mary of Halle in Hainault, to make all sure in his Pantheon; and already he saw, in visions of the night, Cardinal Allen placing England’s crown upon his head. He returned for answer, first, that his victual was not ready; next, that his Dutch sailors—kept many weeks at their posts by sword-point—had run away like water; and thirdly, that besides all, he could not come, so “strangely provided” were those five-and-thirty Dutch ships with great ordnance and musketeers, watching the entrance of Nieuwport and Dunkirk like terriers at a rat-hole.

Having ensured, as he thought, the private patronage of St. Mary of Halle, he would return on the morrow to make experience of its effects. But he heard, across the flats of Dixmude, the far thunder of fleets; and at Dunkirk he met the open curses of his officers. For while he had been praying and nothing more, the English had been praying—and something more; and all that was left for the Prince of Parma was to hang a few purveyors as peace-offerings to his sulking army, and then to chafe, as Drake said of him, “like a bear robbed of her whelps.”

“Full fathom deep thy father lies…” The old song went about the quays and church-porches, not as a stage-trick, but as folk-memory—salt on the tongue, and bell-sound in the ear—fitting enough for a year when so many men, Spanish and English, were already being turned by the sea into other substances: into weed-sway, into crab-meat, into coral-crumb, into the hard pearls of rumor.

It was over. The Armada, that vast pantry of war—casks, biscuit, horses, saints’ banners, brass guns, priests, and princes’ pride—had been beaten, and the long, high quarrel of Europe had, for a season, dropped from the sky into the plain talk of shot and splinter. A day came when even the simplest sailor could taste which side was light and which was darkness, as plainly as he could tell fresh water from bilge. And when it was done, and men counted the cost, it seemed again—as it always seems after such hours—that the loudest boast had been no more than a bladder blown up for a fair, bursting at one prick, leaving only stink behind.

Across England and the Low Countries the thanksgiving rose like a great oven-heat, that steady roar you hear in the bakehouse when the loaves are setting and the work is going well. There were appointed fasts and prayers; there were sermons that handled no other argument than praise; there were processions, and in London the Queen rode in a triumph that smelt of wet woollen liveries, pitch from the street-hangings, and the cold iron tang of captured ensigns. The colors of the vanquished hung out of St. Paul’s like fish on a line. The city shouted till the stones seemed to take the sound up and pass it along; for a people’s well-being is not only in bread and law, but in the very quality of the noises above their heads—whether they are the honest peals of bells and the measured chanting of a multitude, or the thin clatter of fear.

And whither had that great host fled? Northward—away and away, like deer breaking cover and splitting into scattered bodies, each beast for itself. Past Orkney and Shetland, where poor fishermen were caught up as unwilling guides; past Norway, where the old Viking blood refused them water and food; on toward the Faroes and the strange never-sleeping light near the Pole. Their water failed. Their cattle were heaved overboard, and the shrieks of drowning horses went up into that bleak air and were snatched away, as if even the wind was ashamed to carry them long. Medina Sidonia, with the soundest ships and best victuals, led his portion off and left the rest to grope homeward as they might, appointing Cape Finisterre like a market-cross where all should meet—if they ever did.

Meanwhile Parma, on the Dunkirk dunes, watched and waited and could not believe his idol would fail him, and spun fresh rigging and cast anchors as if patient industry could conjure back a sea that had chosen its own answer. His ladies might sit long with fans in hand.

As for Amyas Leigh and the Vengeance, they were not among those who turned home.

At the fifty-seventh degree the English fleet, short of provisions and long out of powder and ball, bore southward for Harwich, leaving the Spaniard to those “uncouth and boisterous northern seas.” But when the Lord High Admiral gave the word, Amyas asked leave to follow. He went to Sir John Hawkins and begged ammunition and victual such as might be spared, pledging repayment like an honest man—out of plunder if he lived, out of his estate if he died—and lodged bills in Hawkins’s hands. Hawkins, who could stow a kingdom’s hazard in the same pocket with string and tobacco, took them without ceremony.

Then Amyas called his men together. The smell of tar was in their clothes; salt had dried on their faces like a crust. He spoke to them again of the Rose of Torridge and Don Guzman de Soto, so that an old wrong might serve as yeast to make their courage rise.

“Men of Bideford, will you follow me? There will be plunder for those who love plunder; revenge for those who love revenge; and for all of us—(for we all love honor)—the honor of having never left the chase as long as there was a Spanish flag in English seas.”

And every soul aboard, with mouths that still remembered hard biscuit and beer gone sour in cask, answered that they would follow Sir Amyas Leigh round the world.

There were sixteen days of weary hunting after that—days whose hours were measured by tack and tide, by the scrape of rope through block, by the dull percussion of the sea against plank, by the cook’s calls and the thin comfort of a hot mess. They sighted the Sta. Catharina and struck at her and were forced off when help came down on them. When Medina Sidonia abandoned crippled ships, Amyas thrust straight into the thick of the Spanish mass rather than let the main quarry slip. He crippled her so she could not claw off to windward into the ocean, but must run south, past Orkney, down through the Minch between Cape Wrath and Lewis.

The younger hands snarled that he was mad, because in his stubborn haste he ran past noble prizes all but disabled—among them a great galliass, and the two great Venetians La Ratta and La Belanzara—golden mouths waiting to be picked clean. Those same ships would afterward, with thirty more, be wrecked on the west coast of Ireland, and men would scramble over their broken ribs like gulls over a carcass. Amyas took fresh water in spite of the “Hebridean Scots” of Skye, who reviled him in an unknown tongue—words that struck the ear like stones clacking in a bucket—then fought awhile, then embraced him and his men with howls of affection, half wild, half merry, and as nakedly honest as the wind. Cary christened a strange island, half black and half white, “the drowned magpie,” though the people called it Raghary, and the name sat in the air like a taste you cannot translate—something between peat-smoke and sea-fog.

Amyas let his crew pick the bones of a great Venetian going ashore upon Islay, where they got booty enough to sweeten their tempers and pay for the voyage; and then soured them again by refusing to land and plunder two great Spanish wrecks on the Mull of Cantire. He fought the Sta. Catharina off Castleton after she had taken refuge there—where Manx-men slew a boat’s crew with arrows—and shot away her mainyard after a day’s work. The Spaniard blundered down the coast of Wales like a man in a strange kitchen who cannot find the door, and both ships were nearly lost on Holyhead and again on Bardsey. In Cardigan Bay, with a lee shore under them and a heavy gale behind, the Sta. Catharina struck on Sarn David—those pebble-dykes drowned under water, said to be the remnants of lost Gwalior, ruined by Prince Seithenin the drunkard. Every loyal Welshman, they say, spits at his name. The galleon got off on the rising tide and fought again, then the wind shifted and she fetched round St. David’s Head.

So much for the long chase. The end was brewing, and the air itself began to taste of it.

On the fifteenth evening they had seen St. David’s Head, and the Welsh coast by Milford Haven looming black and sharp under the blaze of an inland thunder-storm. All through the fore part of the night lightning played—white, then violet, then that hard blue that leaves your eyes aching. A light south-western breeze carried the damp smell of land for a moment, then lost it again in the larger salt.

They strained their eyes through the dark for the Spaniard’s masts, glimpsed now and then by a flash; but the night swallowed all. Still, with the wind as it was, she could not have gone far west. To pass them and go north was more than she dared. She was likely lying-to ahead, perhaps between them and the coast.

A little after midnight the wind chopped west and blew stiffly till daybreak. If she had not tried the desperate trick of running past them in the dark, she was trapped in the mouth of the Bristol Channel. Dawn came slowly on a day like the aftermath of thunder: sunless, drizzling, roofed with low dingy cloud, netted and barred and hung with black fringes, as if the storm were only taking breath. The horizon lay narrow and spongy with vapor, drifting before a chilly breeze. Later the wind died, and the sea fell to a long glassy roll flecked with foam, while overhead the inky sky brooded and around them a leaden mist shut out both shore and chase.

Amyas paced the wet deck—sloppy with brine and rain—fierce as a man who has been starved at the very moment the roast is set down before him. He knew the Spaniard could not escape, and yet each idle minute was vinegar in his mouth. The men sat sulkily, whistling for a wind. The sails flapped and slapped the masts with that empty, scolding noise that can drive men mad at sea; the ship rolled till the yard-arms seemed almost to dip alternately into the gray.

“Take care of those guns,” Amyas growled. “You will have something loose next.”

“We will take care of the guns, if the Lord will take care of the wind,” said Yeo.

“We shall have plenty before night,” Cary answered. “And thunder too.”

“So much the better,” said Amyas. “Let it roar till it splits the heavens, if it does but let me get my work done.”

“He’s not far off, I warrant,” said Cary. “One lift of the cloud, and we should see him.”

“To windward of us, as likely as not,” Amyas said. “The devil fights for him, I believe. Sixteen days on his heels—and not sent this through him yet!” And he shook his sword like a hungry man shaking a knife.

Hour after hour—no gull, no fin, no life but their own ship’s creaks and the sea’s slow breathing. Even sound seemed dulled by the mist, as if the world’s ear were stuffed with wool. Amyas’s black mood deepened with the low sky, and the thought of losing the prey after all made his whole body shudder.

“No, God!” he cried aloud, as if the deck itself were a pulpit. “Let me but once feel this in his accursed heart, and then—strike me dead, if Thou wilt!”

“The Lord have mercy on us,” cried John Brimblecombe. “What have you said?”

“What is that to you, sir? There, they are piping to dinner. Go down. I shall not come.”

Brimblecombe went below, and his whisper carried through the mess like a draft under a door. Most took it for a bad sign, except old Yeo, who had the plain man’s courage of being neither grand nor delicate.

“Well, Sir John,” said he, “and why not? What better can the Lord do for a man, than take him home when he has done his work? Our captain is wilful and spiteful, and must needs kill his man himself; while for me, I don’t care how the Don goes, provided he does go. I owe him no grudge, nor any man. May the Lord give him repentance, and forgive him all his sins: but if I could but see him once safe ashore—as he may be ere nightfall, on the Mortestone or the back of Lundy—I would say, ‘Lord, now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace,’ even if it were the lightning which was sent to fetch me.”

“But, master Yeo, a sudden death?”

“And why not a sudden death, Sir John? Even fools long for a short life and a merry one, and shall not the Lord’s people pray for a short death and a merry one? Let it come as it will to old Yeo. Hark! there’s the captain’s voice!”

“Here she is!” thundered Amyas from above; and in an instant men scrambled up the hatchway, boots slipping on wet ladders, shoulders knocking in the press.

The cloud had lifted like a curtain jerked aside, and southward a ragged bore of blue sky opened, letting a long spear of sunshine fall upon tall masts and a stately hull. The Sta. Catharina lay rolling some four or five miles to the eastward, golded for a moment and then grayed again; but no land showed—only that false emptiness that makes a sailor’s stomach feel hollow.

“There she is; and here we are,” said Cary. “But where is here? and where is there? How is the tide, master?”

“Running up Channel by this time, sir.”

“What matters the tide?” Amyas said, devouring the ship with cold, terrible blue eyes. “Can’t we get at her?”

“Not unless some one jumps out and shoves behind,” Cary answered. “I shall down again and finish that mackerel, if this roll has not chucked it to the cockroaches under the table.”

“Don’t jest, Will! I can’t stand it.” Amyas’s voice quivered; Cary looked at him and saw the trembling that ran through him like wind through reeds. Cary took his arm and drew him aside to the bulwarks, where the ship’s wet wood smelt of salt and old tar.

“Dear old lad,” said he low, “what is this? You are not yourself, and have not been these four days.”

“No. I am not Amyas Leigh. I am my brother’s avenger. Do not reason with me, Will: when it is over I shall be merry old Amyas again.” He rubbed his brow as if to wipe off heat.

After a moment he said, “Do you believe that men can be possessed by devils?”

“The Bible says so.”

“If my cause were not a just one, I should fancy I had a devil in me. My throat and heart are as hot as the pit. Would to God it were done, for done it must be! Now go.”

Cary went away with a shudder. As he passed down the hatch, he glanced back. Amyas had drawn a hone from his pocket and was whetting his sword-edge again—stroke after stroke—like a man under a doom to sharpen forever, listening to that thin, exact rasp as though it were the only honest sound left in the world.

The day dragged on. The strip of blue sky was curtained over. The air grew sultrier; now and then a distant mutter rolled from the west. They could not lessen the distance—boats were gone, all but one too small to tow. The men went below to finish dinner; the smell of mackerel and hot fat rose for a moment through the hatch, sharp and comforting, then was swallowed again by damp air. Amyas stayed above, working the blade and looking up now and then at the Spaniard as if afraid she might vanish like a mirage.

About two, Yeo came up.

“He is ours safely now, sir. The tide has been running to the eastward for this two hours.”

“Safe as a fox in a trap. Satan himself cannot take him from us!”

“But God may,” said Brimblecombe simply, behind them.

“Who spoke to you, sir? If I thought that He—There comes the thunder at last!”

As if the sky answered, an angry growl rolled nearer and nearer, then crashed overhead against some cloud-cliff and ceased, leaving a silence that was almost louder. Men looked into one another’s faces. Amyas did not move.

“The storm is coming,” he said. “And the wind in it. It will be Eastward-ho now, for once, my merry men all!”

“Eastward-ho never brought us luck,” Jack muttered to Cary; but by then all eyes were on the north-west. A black line began to harden along the horizon, marking sea from air.

“There comes the breeze.”

“And there the storm, too.”

The thunder, which had been slow and distant, now began to ring peal on peal, as if someone were dragging cannon across the sky’s floor. The yards creaked round. The sea crisped. Hot air swept their cheeks, tightened rope, filled sail, and bent the Vengeance over. A cheer burst from the men as the helm went up; and they staggered away before the wind, straight down upon the Spaniard, who lay still becalmed, rich prey in a poor man’s sight.

“There is more behind, Amyas,” Cary shouted through the growing noise. “Shall we not shorten sail a little?”

“No. Hold on every stitch. Give me the helm, man. Boatswain, pipe away to clear for fight.”

In ten minutes they were at quarters, while the thunder rolled louder and the breeze freshened fast.

“The dog has it now. There he goes!” Cary cried.

“Right before the wind. He has no liking to face us.”

“He is running into the jaws of destruction,” said Yeo. “An hour more will send him either right up the Channel, or smack on shore somewhere.”

“There! he has put his helm down. I wonder if he sees land?”

“He is like a March hare beat out of his country,” Cary said, “and don’t know whither to run next.”

So it was. The Spaniard fell off again and fled dead down wind. The Vengeance gained fast; in two hours the four miles dwindled to one. The storm came nearer—lightning flashing like blue knives through the murk—and from a vast black arch of cloud poured a fiercer breeze. The hints to shorten sail grew to murmurs; Amyas yielded, unwilling.

They rushed on with scarcely lessened speed, the black arch following like a hunter’s cloak, curtained by a flat gray sheet of rain. Ahead of that curtain the water boiled in a long white line; behind it keen sparks leapt down into the sea or darted zigzag through rain so thick it looked like a net thrown over the world.

“We shall have it now, and with a vengeance; this will try your tackle, master,” Cary said.

The master only shrugged and turned up his collar as the first stinging drops struck ears and neck. Then the squall burst full upon them—rain that cut like hail, hail that lashed the sea into froth, and wind that tore the heads off surges and swept the waters into one white seething waste. Lightning ran and leapt, dazzling; thunder changed from deep roar to sharp cracks that hurt the ear like a whip.

“Get the arms and ammunition under cover, and then below with you all!” Amyas shouted from the helm.

“And heat the pokers in the galley fire,” Yeo added, “to be ready if the rain puts our linstocks out. I hope you’ll let me stay on deck, sir, in case—”

“I must have some one, and who better than you? Can you see the chase?”

No. The Sta. Catharina was wrapped in the gray whirlwind. She might be within half a mile for all they could tell.

Now Amyas and his old liegeman stood alone on deck, two men with one thought. The squall worsened. Rain poured heavier. The ship’s timbers groaned and sang—each rope with its own note, each block with its own clack—until the whole rigging seemed an instrument played by the storm. In such a chaos, language itself felt small; every shouted order was swallowed or broken, as if the wind refused to carry human words faithfully. What is untranslatable is not only a Gaelic curse or a Latin prayer, but the exact measure of terror in a man’s chest when the sea changes color.

“If he has laid-to, we may overshoot him, sir!” Yeo cried.

“If he has tried to lay-to, he will not have a sail left in the bolt-ropes, or perhaps a mast on deck. I know the stiff-neckedness of those Spanish tubs. Hurrah! there he is, right on our larboard bow!”

There she was indeed, two musket-shots off, staggering away with canvas split and flying, her greatness reduced to a thing battered and noisy, like a pantry-door torn from its hinges in a gale.

“He has been trying to hull, sir, and caught a buffet,” Yeo said, rubbing his hands. “What shall we do now?”

“Range alongside, if it blow live imps and witches, and try our luck once more. Pah! how this lightning dazzles!”

They swept in, gaining fast.

“Call the men up, and to quarters; the rain will be over in ten minutes.”

Yeo sprang forward to the gangway—and sprang back again, face white under its weathering.

“Land right ahead! Port your helm, sir! For the love of God, port your helm!”

Amyas jammed the helm down with bull-strength while Yeo shouted below. The Vengeance swung. The masts bent like whips. Crack went the foresail like a cannon.

What matter? Within two hundred yards was the Spaniard; and in front of her, rising through the dense hail, a huge dark bank heaved up from the sea and mingled with cloud—at its foot pillars and spouts of leaping foam.

“What is it, Morte? Hartland?”

It might have been anything—any deadly mile of coast.

“Lundy!” Yeo cried. “The south end! I see the head of the Shutter in the breakers! Hard a-port yet, and get her close-hauled as you can, and the Lord may have mercy on us still! Look at the Spaniard!”

Yes—look.

On their left, as they broached-to, the granite wall sloped down from cloud toward an isolated peak of rock, two hundred feet high. Then came a hundred yards of roaring breaker upon a sunken shelf, across which the tide-race poured like a cataract; then, amid a column of salt smoke, the Shutter rose—huge, black, fang-like—waiting. And between that fang and the land, the great galleon loomed dimly through the storm.

He, too, had seen his danger and tried to broach-to. But his clumsy bulk refused the helm. He struggled a moment, half hidden in foam; fell away again, and rushed upon his doom.

It was the first of October; one of those mornings which seem to hush themselves, as though the year, tired of thunder and blood, had drawn a cool cloth across its brow. The air lay bright and still upon the Taw, and the sky, from east to west, was gently mottled with pale, gray autumn cloud—as if heaven had folded its hands and listened.

Silently, almost with shame, the Vengeance slid over the bar and past the sleeping sand-hills, and dropped her anchor off Appledore. Her flag hung half-mast; for Salvation Yeo lay dead aboard.

A boat put off, heading for the western end of the strand; and Cary and Brimblecombe helped Amyas Leigh ashore and led him slowly up the hill toward his home. The people clustered round with cheers and blessings, with sobs too from women whose hearts were quicker than their tongues. All Appledore and Bideford knew by now what had befallen him.

“Spare me, my good friends,” Amyas said. “I landed here that I might go quietly home, without passing through the town and being made a gazing-stock. Think not of me, good folks, nor talk of me; but come behind me decently, as Christian men, and follow to the grave the body of a better man than I.”

As he spoke, another boat came off; and in it, covered with the flag of England, was the body of Salvation Yeo. The folk took Amyas at his word. A man was sent on to Burrough to tell Mrs. Leigh her son had come.

When the coffin was lifted, Amyas and his friends took their place behind it as chief mourners; the crew followed in order; and the crowd fell in after, swelling every moment, until before they had reached Northam town the train was near five hundred souls. The sexton had been warned the day before; and at the churchyard gate the parson waited ready.

Mrs. Leigh stayed quietly at home. She had no heart for a crowd; and though her heart yearned for her son, she was well content—when was she not content?—that he should do honor to the old servant who had been faithful through youth and war and wandering. So she sat in the bay-window with Ayacanora beside her; and when the tolling ceased she opened her Prayer-book and began the Burial-service.

“Ayacanora,” she said softly, “they are burying old Master Yeo, who loved you, and sought you over the wide, wide world, and saved you from the teeth of the crocodile. Are you not sorry for him, child, that you look so gay to-day?”

Ayacanora blushed and hung down her head. Her thoughts were not on Yeo—nor on death, nor on England’s flag—but only on Amyas.

The Burial-service was read; the blessing said. The parson drew back; yet the people lingered, crowding to look down at the coffin. Amyas stood at the head of the grave, very still.

By his command it had been dug at the west end of the church, near the foot of the tall gray tower that stood wind-swept, watching like a sentinel for beacon far and wide over land and sea. Perhaps, Amyas thought, the old man would like to lie where he might still be near the water—to “look,” as it were, on the ships coming in and out across the bar, and to hear the wind in winter nights roaring through the belfry overhead. It was but a fancy; and yet it clung to him, because it was homely and seamanlike, and because now—blind—he too felt what it was to need the world’s sounds to make a place real.

The crowd waited, looking first at the grave, then at the great blind giant who stood above it, as though by instinct they knew something more was due.

And something more came.

Amyas drew himself up to his full height and lifted his hand. Twice he tried to begin, and twice the words were choked upon his lips; and then at last he spoke.

“Good people all, and seamen, among whom I was bred, and to whom I come home blind this day, to dwell with you till death—here lieth the flower and pattern of all bold mariners; the truest of friends, and the most terrible of foes; unchangeable of purpose, crafty of council, and swift of execution; in triumph most sober, in failure—God knows I have found it—of endurance beyond mortal man.

“Who first of all Britons helped to humble the pride of the Spaniard at Rio de la Hacha and Nombre, and first of all sailed upon those South Seas, which shall be hereafter, by God’s grace, as free to English keels as is the bay outside.

“Who, having afterwards been purged from his youthful sins by strange afflictions and torments unspeakable, suffered at the hands of the Popish enemy; learned therefrom, my masters, to fear God and to fear naught else; and having acquitted himself worthily in his place and calling as a righteous scourge of the Spaniard, and a faithful soldier of the Lord Jesus Christ, is now exalted to his reward, as Elijah was of old, in a chariot of fire unto heaven: letting fall, I trust and pray, upon you who are left behind the mantle of his valor and his godliness; that so these shores may never be without brave and pious mariners, who will count their lives as worthless in the cause of their Country, their Bible, and their Queen. Amen.”

Then, feeling for his companions’ hands, he walked slowly from the churchyard, across the village street, and up the lane to Burrough gates. The crowd made way for him in solemn silence, as for something awful and solitary—strength and fame shut in, all at once, within a dark prison-house. He seemed to know each turn. At the gates he found the lock with his own hands and opened it, and went boldly forward up the gravel path; while Cary and Brimblecombe followed trembling, for they feared some violent breaking loose of grief, from him or from his mother. Their own tender hearts fluttered like girls’.

Up to the door he went as if he saw it. He felt for the entrance, stood in it, and called quietly, “Mother!”

In a moment his mother was on his bosom. Neither spoke awhile. She sobbed inwardly with tearless eyes; he stood firm and cheerful, his great arms clasping her.

“Mother,” he said at last, “I am come home, you see, because I needs must come. Will you take me in, and look after this useless carcase? I shall not be so very troublesome, mother—shall I?” And he smiled down toward where her face must be, and kissed her brow.

She answered nothing, but slipped her arm gently round his waist and led him in.

“Take care of your head, dear child,” she whispered; “the doors are low.”

They went in together.

“Will! Jack!” Amyas called, turning; but the two good fellows had already walked briskly off.

“I’m glad we are away,” said Cary. “I should have made a baby of myself in another minute, watching that angel of a woman. How her face worked and how she kept it in!”

“Ah, well!” said Jack. “There goes a brave servant of the Queen’s cut off before his work was a quarter done. Heigho! I must home now, and see my old father, and then—”

“And then home with me,” said Cary. “You and I never part again! We have pulled in the same boat too long, Jack; and you must not go spending your prize-money in riotous living. I must see after you, old Jack ashore, or we shall have you treating half the town in taverns for a week to come.”

“Oh, Mr. Cary!” said Jack, scandalized.

“Come home with me, and we’ll poison the parson, and my father shall give you the rectory.”

“Oh, Mr. Cary!” said Jack again; and so the two went off to Clovelly together that very day.

And Amyas sat alone.

His mother had gone out for a little to speak with the seamen who had brought up his luggage, and to set them to eat and drink; and he sat in the old bay-window where he had sat when he was a tiny boy, reading his old books—King Arthur, Fox’s Martyrs, and the Cruelties of the Spaniards. He put out his hand and felt for them: there they lay side by side, as they had lain twenty years before. The window stood open. Cool air came in, bearing the scents that made the house itself seem to have a memory—four-season roses and rosemary, and the sharp clove-sweetness of autumn gilliflowers.

On the table stood a dish of apples. He knew them before he touched them; their smell told him—round, clean, with that bruised-green, sun-warmed perfume that belongs to orchards and childhood. He took one and turned it in his hands, feeling the waxy skin and the little dent where the stem had been. Another followed; he weighed them, rolled them, set them down and drew them back again, as though, by their small honest shapes, he could persuade time to fold itself and come full circle.

As he fingered them, his whole past rose up before him—as men say it flashes before the drowning: places, voices, blows, blessings; the clank of arms, the hiss of arrows, the sea’s long grind against ship’s sides; and then, suddenly, that fairy island on the Meta. The cataract roared again in his mind; he saw palm-tops sleeping in sunlight far above the spray; he felt the smooth trunks beneath his hands as he stepped among boulders fringed with flowers; he leaped down to the gravel beach beside the pool—and there, from fern-grown rock, rose Ayacanora’s vision.

Where was she?

He had not thought of her till now. How he had wronged her. Let be; he had been punished. The account was squared. Perhaps she did not care for him any longer. Who would care for a blind ox like him, who must be fed and tended like a baby for the rest of his lazy life?

He listened: the house was full of small sounds—the faint mutter of voices outside, a creak of timber, the garden’s leaves stirring. Yet in that hush, his own helplessness rang loudest. How long his mother was away.

To steady himself, he began playing again with the apples, thinking of nothing but them and of climbs with Frank in the orchard long ago—hands sticky with juice, pockets heavy, the chatter of boys, the bees’ steady music in the grass. One apple slipped through his fingers and fell to the floor. He stooped and groped; his hands swept boards and found only emptiness.

Vexatious! He turned hastily to search another way and struck his head sharply against the table.

Was it pain? A child’s disappointment? Or the sudden, ridiculous way in which blindness will mock a man—by an apple on a floor and a blow from a table, as though the world had turned clown to show him his doom? Whatever it was, it broke through the last tight holding of his courage. He stamped once, pettishly, and then, checking himself, burst into a violent flood of tears.

A quick rustle passed close to him; the apple was set back into his hand.

“There! there it is!” Ayacanora’s voice sobbed out. “Do not weep! Oh, do not weep! I cannot bear it! I will get you all you want! Only let me fetch and carry for you, tend you, feed you, lead you, like your slave—your dog! Say that I may be your slave!”

She fell on her knees at his feet, seized both his hands, and covered them with kisses.

“Yes!” she cried. “I will be your slave! I must be! You cannot help it! You cannot escape from me now! You cannot go to sea! You cannot turn your back upon wretched me. I have you safe now—safe!”

She clutched his hands with a triumph that turned on itself in an instant.

“Ah! and what a wretch I am,” she sobbed, “to rejoice in that! to taunt him with his blindness! Oh, forgive me! I am but a poor wild girl—a wild Indian savage, you know: but—but—” And she broke down into tears.

A great spasm shook Amyas—body and soul. He sat silent a full minute; then said, very solemnly, “And is this still possible? Then God have mercy upon me a sinner!”

Ayacanora looked up into his face, searching it—trying, as always, to read in English what her own heart could not translate cleanly into any tongue. Before she could speak, he bent down; and lifting her as a lion lifts a lamb, he pressed her to his bosom and covered her face with kisses.

The door opened. There was the rustle of a gown. Ayacanora sprang from him with a little cry and stood half trembling, half defiant, as though to say: He is mine now; no one shall part him from me.

“Who is it?” Amyas asked.

“Your mother.”

“You see that I am bringing forth fruits meet for repentance, mother,” said he, smiling.

He heard her come nearer. Then there was a kiss and a sob between the women; and he felt Ayacanora sink once more upon his bosom.

“Amyas, my son,” said Mrs. Leigh’s silver voice—low, dreamy, like far-off chimes heard across a still moor—“fear not to take her to your heart again; for it is your mother who has laid her there.”

“It is true, after all,” Amyas said within himself. “What God has joined together, man cannot put asunder.”

From that hour Ayacanora’s power of song returned to her. Day by day, year after year, her voice rose within that happy home—sometimes soft as rosemary brushed between the fingers, sometimes keen as the sea-wind in the tower, always living—and it carried the blind giant’s thoughts back toward the West, along the wake of those heroes who from that time forth sailed out to plant another and vaster England, to the heaven-prospered cry of Westward-Ho!
